## B: B Ech.–black molly

### B Ech.
 
 <i>abbr.</i> B-Echelon cf Lotus Eaters [WW2]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He is really getting on my nerves as he natters on and on, a lot of nonsense about getting out of B Ech and back into the sharp end with the boys. Plenty of room at the other end of the paddock. A bunch of D-Day Dodgers and if they're going to hang around in their own half ... they might as well ... have a brew-up. <i>(1970,On the Ball,136</i>)

>  contraction for B-Echelon -- reserve section of a unit -- Lotus Eaters <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in Italy,Glossary</i>)

### B&B
 
 <i>abbr.</i> brace and bit, a hypodermic syringe and needle [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  Examples [of acronyms] include <i>B&B</i>, which stands for <i>brace and bit</i>, rhyming slang for <i>fit</i>, drug-users' slang for a hypodermic syringe and needle. <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2): 2</i>)

### b-bows
 
 <i>n.</i> handshears

>  b-bows: Handshears. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/21</i>) [NOTES: Wallace Shearing Glossary]

### B-listers
 
 <i>n.</i> people on list 'B' [list in terms of need]

>  No A-list applicants were housed in Mangere last month - the average waiting time for Mangere B-listers is 86 days, and for C-listers 364 <i>(Dec 5, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

### B-train
 
 <i>n.</i> articulated truck and trailer unit

>  A south-bound B-train (articulated truck and trailer unit) bounced off the hill on the left side of the road and turned across three lanes of rush-hour traffic and smashed through the central armco barrier. <i>(Sep 10, 2002,Independent Herald (Wellington),1</i>)

### B.A.
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Bloody Awful; something disliked [ORIGIN: acronym, 1953 15-17 M A29 Thames DHS 3 B.A. [BLOODY AWFUL] [M5]]

### B.B.
 
 <i>abbr.</i> big boy(s) =school shark. [commercial fishermen's]

>  <b>SORENSON</b> SHARK, SCHOOL (a) Scientific name: Galeorhinus australis (principally). (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Flake Fillets. (c) Other common names: B.B., Big Boys; Soupfin; Grey Boys; Tope or Topi. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,42</i>)

### B.Ball
 
 <i>n.</i> basketball [ORIGIN: abbreviation, secondary schools. 1984 17 F E51 Pakuranga Coll. 21 B.BALL -->1984 16 F E10 Pakuranga Coll. 21 B-BALL [F1M2]]

### B.D.
 
 <i>abbr.</i> boiling down [farming hist]

>  <b>PINNEY</b> On 25 may, B.D. started on Benmore; boiling down, like other unpleasant things, was usually known by its initials. <i>(May 25, 1870,Early Northern Otago Runs,1981 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [1870 May 25 Benmore diary] in 1981 Pinney Early Northern Otago Runs]

### B.M.
 
 <i>abbr.</i> board measure

>  <b>MALFROY</b> B.M. .. Board measure - a term expressing the board foot content of round or manufactured timber, and for expressing the volume of logs, trees, or stands in terms of the estimated amount of timber which may be cut therefrom. `Board foot' is a unit of measurement represented by a board 1 ft. long, 1 ft. wide, and 1 in. in thickness, or its equivalent <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,38</i>)

### b/couta
 
 <i>n.</i> a shorthand form of barracouta. [commercial fishermen's]

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i> BARRACOUTA</i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Thyrsites atun.</i> (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Sea Pike; Couta; Snoek; Barra Fillets. (c) Other common names: B/couta; Bait. (d) Principal localities where taken: Gisborne, Motueka, Nelson, Timaru, Port Chalmers. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,11</i>)

### babaco
 
 <i>n.</i> A hybrid papaya, <i>Carica pentagona,</i> originating in Ecuador; with large yellow-skinned fruit up to 30 cm long and 10-12 in cross-section, with rather acid, almost white and distinctly fragrant flesh.

>  <b>BURCHFIELD</b> babaco /b |b k u/. N.Z. & Austral. [S. Amer. Sp. (Ecuador).] A bright yellow marrow-like fruit, C<i>arica heilbornii pentagona</i>, usu. about 30 cm. long, with a faceted exterior and with a texture like that of a coarse melon. <i>(1988,Lexicographical & Linguistic Studies,192</i>)

>  <i>Carica pentagona</i> is called `babaco' by the natives and is regarded as a great delicacy. <i>(1921,Arkiv for Botanik,XVII. XII.4</i>) [NOTES: O. HEILBORN]

>  Because of its large fruits .. and because of its habit of producng seedless fruits, the <i>babaco</i> is worthy of attention in other countries. <i>(1924,Contr. National Herbarium (US),XXV. V. 126</i>) [NOTES: W. POPENOE]

>  <b>T. TANAKA</b> <i>Carica pentagona</i> .., `Babaco'. The flesh of fruits are nearly white, acid; eaten cooked, preserved or used in sauce. Ecuador & adj. territory. <i>(1976,Tanaka's Cycl. Edible Fruits,135</i>)

>  Group three: Blueberries, feijoas, avocado, tamarillos, cherries, babaco, persimmon, Asian pears, macadamias, pepino etc. <i>(1981,Southern Horticulture (N.Z.),Spring 16/1</i>)

>  Babaco orchards will be limited to frost-free climate requirements. <i>(Mar 1981,Orchardist NZ,LIV. 61/1</i>)

>  <i>The babaco .. is a perennial shrub of the papaya family (Caricacae) indigenous to the subtropical mountainous regions of Ecuador</i>. <i>(1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,XXVI. 237/1</i>)

>  <i>Babaco</i>. Brought to Australia via New Zealand from Equador [sic]... It is a relation of the paw-paw and has been an exceptional crop in New Zealand. <i>(Jan 21, 1984,Weekend Australian,21-22 Jan. 9</i>)

>  Kay also uses babacos to make a "great fruit loaf" by adding them to her home made bread. <i>(Jun 1990,NZ Gardener,34</i>)

>  The babaco grows in the same climate range as the mountain pawpaw, but has much larger fruits, comparable in size with the tropical papaya. <i>(1974,Orchardist NZ,259</i>)

>  Of the high-altitude fruiting plants that are native of Ecuador, the Babaco is the most promising fruit to be introduced into New Zealand from that country. <i>(Feb 1978,Orchardist NZ,18</i>)

>  [Caption] First impression gained by visitors to this orchard of babacos has been : "Little green men from Mars." <i>(Mar 1974,Orchardist NZ,59</i>)

>  <b>BOLLARD</b> The babaco, which is grown in Ecuador, was introduced here in 1973. <i>(1981,Prospects for Horticulture,116</i>)

>  We tried babacos but they got too large and started falling over in their buckets. <i>(1987,Country Calendar (TVNZ),55</i>)

>  The babaco story began in 1977 when Dick Endt, a nurseryman from Oratia, near Auckland, introduced the first privately grown species into New Zealand........ The babaco belongs to the Caricaceae family, more commonly known as the papaya family. <i>(May 1982,NZ Gardener,24</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> BABACO... is a hybrid between two papaya (pawpaw) species, <i>Carica pubescens</i> (mountain papaya) and <i>C. stipulata</i>. . . first brought to the attention of the rest of the world by the botanist Heilborn, who named it <i>Carica pentagona</i>, meaning five-sided. The Ecuadorian name babaco (pronounced as in banana) has been retained in New Zealand. <i>(1985,Delectable Fruits Cookery,41</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Babaco juice is a favourite beverage in Ecuador where it is served in hotels. <i>(Mar 1974,Orchardist NZ,61</i>)

>  The Babaco industry is now 10 years old. <i>(1990,Exotic Fruit Forum,12</i>)

>  A major love in his garden was his babaco plants, which he grew in a glasshouse. These tropical melon-like fruits were an object of his pride - and we didn't mind eating them. <i>(Aug 31, 2000,Evening Post,16</i>)

### babbler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BRUNO</b> Arch, the cook - oh, how that bold `babbler' could curse. <i>(1944,Desert Daze,33</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The `babbler' was in his element. <i>(1949,High Country Days,63</i>)

>  <i>(Jun 5, 1963,Weekly News,37/1</i>) [NOTES: [OED]]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Another wellknown old character was an old `babbler', George Frizzel. If George.. wasn't at the Reserve, he'd be at the Muller cookhouse. <i>(1977,Big Country,60</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>babbler</i> cook; rhyming slang `babbling brook/cook', used by shearing gangs and WWI Anzacs. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,12</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a station or shearing gang cook.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> We worked it out that old babbler made 112,000 rock cakes during those four months. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,86</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Telling the old babbler we'd be back for smoko, across he hopped in the truck. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,143</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. another wellknown old character was an old 'babbler', George Frizzell. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,60</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> babbler cook; rhyming slang 'babbling brook/cook' used by shearing gangs and WWI Anzacs. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,12</i>)

>  Station cook - babbler. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### babbling brook
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a station or shearing gang cook. [ORIGIN: rhyming slang]

>  <b>STAPLES</b> He always referred to me as the Babbling Brook instead of the cook. <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,22</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> I gathered that he was the cook from whom the name 'babbling brook' must have originated. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,26</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> He introduced himself by saying "I'm Barb Lindsay da babbling brook (cook)". <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,30</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> As the gang's cook (or 'babbling brook') he was required to be extremely resourceful. <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,142</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Our "babbling brook", Mrs. Clark, and her husband Archie, a presser, had their own motorcar. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,100</i>)

### Babe Ruth
 
 <i>n.</i> the truth

>  Sporting stars.. are represented by such terms as.. <i>Babe Ruth</i> (the truth). <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 2</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BABE RUTH (n) Truth. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,244</i>)

### baby
 
 <i>n.</i> [nf P8] in collocation, a junior; an (innocent) new chum or new pupil [1984 16 F E15 Pakuranga Coll. 25 BABIES [F2M3] 1984 14 F E137Wgton Girls C. 25 BABIES [F2]]

### baby All Black
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Discarded `Baby All Black' fullback Greg Cooper put himself right on target to make a return to the big time with a stunning performance for North in their 40-19 win over South in the first of the new zone matches yesterday. <i>(Apr 23, 1987,Dominion,28</i>)

### baby beef
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a young cattle beast fattened for the beef trade. (See [beefy](../B#beefy))

>  Herefords are singularly adapted for the production of what is called 'baby beef', that is, with a little forcing, they can be turned out fat at from 2 to 2 and a half years. <i>(1919,Livestock in New Zealand,82</i>)

### Baby Blacks
 
 <i>n.</i> an All Black team that played in 1986 without any of the experienced players who were part of the rebel Cavaliers team that went to South Africa and who were suspended. [cf young or inexperienced team]

>  When the All blacks confront France in the one-off rugby international at Jade Stadium on Saturday, it will be 17 years to the day that the "Baby Blacks" were born. That was the name given to a New Zealand team stripped of All Blacks by the controversial and unauthorised Cavaliers tour to South Africa earlier in the year, and thrown against a French team brimming with caps and confidence. <i>(Jun 24, 2003,Press, The,B8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a young or inexperienced All Black team, or the junior New Zealand international men's rugby union team (from 2008 an under-20 team) [cf the particular touring All Blacks team of 1996]

>  New Zealand is through to the under-20 rugby world championship final on Sunday night. They will meet England in Tokyo, giving the Baby Blacks a chance to defend the title they won last year. <i>(Jun 19, 2009,Southland Times,A20</i>)

### baby blipper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A bulge of primary school age children - the baby blippers - will start moving into central Auckland's colleges by 2001. <i>(Dec 4, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

### Baby Bliss
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  When you [the watersider] show grey under your skull cap you find you are not selling so freely as formerly. Your closest friend then mostly is a stringer on a pile, to which you mutter your sorrows, as the young emigrant, the `Baby Bliss' of wharf parlance, take[s] your place in the hold and shed. <i>(Aug 25, 1911,Maoriland Worker,5/2</i>)

### baby farm
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "..a tale which became infamous as the "Newlands baby Farm Murders." <i>(Jun 1, 2005,Northern Courier,p7</i>)

>  Did she keep what is called a "baby" farm? <i>(Nov 13, 1890,Otago Witness,24</i>)

>  <b>SHEEHAN</b> As a foster mother to many unwanted children Mrs Dean's baby-farm had been the object of the constable's suspicions. <i>(1933,Famous Murders in NZ,22</i>)

>  [Hdg] A Baby Farm. Shocking Disclosures. <i>(Nov 14, 1890,NZ Mail,25</i>)

>  <b>NEWLANDS COUNTRY WOMEN'S INSTITUTE</b> In 1922/23 the Baby Farm scandal broke, and as a result of this people did not want to live in Newlands, so the area ceased to grow for the next 20 years. <i>(1991,Short History of Newlands,2</i>)

### baby farmer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The death of Dr. J.A. Harman, who was junior counsel for the defence of Minnie Dean, with Winton baby farmer, who was hanged at Invercargill in 1895, has recalled memories.. of the only woman ever to have been hanged in New Zealand. <i>(Apr 14, 1954,NZ Truth</i>)

>  In legal history [Minnie Deans] has been known, with approximate accuracy, as the Winton Baby Farmer. Under the guise of benevolent motive she received unwanted children and apparently destroyed them... For a premium, never very large, Minnie Dean adopted unwanted infants, but the payment of the fee invariably marked the disappearance of the child into the care of a "lady" whose name or abode was never disclosed. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,III/449</i>)

>  The trial of one of the most remarkable criminals that has ever lived in New Zealand was concluded here at 1 o'clock today, Minnie Dean, the now notorious baby-farmer, being found guilty and sentenced to death for the murder of Dorothy Edith Carter. <i>(Jun 27, 1895,Otago Witness,13</i>)

>  <b>SHEEHAN</b> Eager eyes were turned upon the infamous baby-farmer as she was escorted to the dock. <i>(1933,Famous Murders in NZ,27</i>)

>  <b>LAWLOR</b> Tom Long's most important job was the hanging of Minnie Dean, the notorious baby-farmer, at Invercargill on August 12, 1895. <i>(1962,More Wellington Days,31</i>)

>  <b>PINE</b> The story of Minnie Dean, Southland's murderous baby-farmer and the only woman ever hanged by law in New Zealand, has not been forgotten. <i>(1969,Famous NZ Murders,XI</i>)

### baby farming
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] Charge of Baby Farming. <i>(Nov 13, 1890,Otago Witness,24</i>)

>  There has been more than one case in New Zealand of what became known as "baby- farming" - the maintenance of illegitimate children for a period followed by their murder without the knowledge of their parents. <i>(Jul 3, 1953,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  Attention is called to what appears to be a growing evil in this colony - viz., "baby-farming." That this evil exists there can be no doubt; and it appears that children, either by advertisement or otherwise, are placed in most unsuitable homes, where it is perfectly well understood that the sooner the child dies the better pleased all concerned will be. <i>(1893,AJHR,H-26/3</i>)

>  Six years ago Mrs Dean appears to have started baby-farming at Winton. <i>(May 16, 1895,Otago Witness,28</i>)

>  It was held by Mr Justice Chapman that the Crown Prosecutor.. could bring evidence to prove systematic baby-farming on the part of both accused. <i>(May 18, 1923,NZ Times,9</i>)

>  <b>SHEEHAN</b> "The Larches," Mrs. Dean's home on the outskirts of Winton.. was the stage for the next act in New Zealand's famous - or infamous - baby-farming case. <i>(1933,Famous Murders in NZ,23</i>)

>  First put on the map of high public estimation by his epic defence, as a young practitioner, in the famous, or infamous, baby-farming case. Regina versus Minnie Dean, [Hanlon] shot across New Zealand's forensic sky leaving behind him a trail of shining successes. <i>(Jan 23, 1953,NZ Herald,13</i>)

>  A baby-farming case was before the police court today, when Myra Smith. an elderly lady, was separately charged with having wilfully ill-treated and neglected two children in a manner likely to cause unnecessary suffering and injury to health... There was a burial four months ago, and last year, and the year before two deaths had occurred. <i>(Nov 14, 1890,NZ Mail,25</i>)

### baby kiwifruit
 
 <i>n.</i> the small hairless <i>Actinidia arguta</i>

>  Next off the block is likely to be commercial cultivars of actinidia arguta, which produces small, hairless grape-sized fruit. Already sold in Japan as "baby kiwifruit" since 1995, it has been targeted by Hortresearch breeders who want to carry the thin skin, high sugar content and flavour over into new cultivars. <i>(Apr 29, 2004,Dominion Post,C11</i>)

### baby ox
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cattle beast of 12-15 months bred for beef.

>  <b>BARTON</b> Grade name: Baby ox or heifer - carcases of this grade are from beef-bred cattle slaughtered when 12 to 15 months of age. The carcasses are plump, short-legged <i>(1972,Sheepfarming Annual 1972,71</i>)

### baby powerco
 
 <i>n.</i> [also baby ECNZ]

>  [Hdg.] Baby powercos ripe for plucking... The re-evaluation of the government's "baby ECNZs"... means the government can force these relatively debt-free powercos to borrow, then milk them of millions of dollars. <i>(Nov 3, 1999,Independent (Auckland),1</i>)

### bach
 




 
 <i>n.</i> small holiday home (See [crib](../C#crib))

>  When the mater returned to Dunedin after her holidays in this city, I determined to try "baching," and after having been six months in my crib at the top of the hill, I am more than ever resolved not to descend again to town level... My "bach" is perched.. about 7000ft above sea-level. <i>(Oct 9, 1912,Otago Witness,79</i>)

>  Between ridges of the waves and a bach / like a pink snail <i>(Jun 1981,Landfall,6(4) Nos. 31-32/118</i>)

>  "It's the boat, bach, beamer syndrome." <i>(Sep 3, 2006,Sunday Star Times,D3</i>)

>  Mr. Baine .. directed the festivities at Mr. Jones' bach. <i>(Dec 1932,Tararua Tramper,3</i>)

>  From being a mere shed, the bach has become a place at the seaside, and today many a bach has a refrigerator and wall-to-wall carpets. <i>(Nov 22, 1957,Listener (New Zealand Listener),4</i>)

>  The bach, these days, tends to be the name given to any smallish holiday house by the beach, no matter how sophisticated. But, in the past, it meant a small home, built by the owner - away out on the edge - by sea or river. Usually it was occupied for just a few summer weeks each year... The great building boom for the archetypal bach was after World War I, with another boom of more gentrified, less interesting baches after World War II. <i>(Oct 19, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  I've never set foot inside a bach. If it was a common experience - the new coat of paint on the windowsill, the towels hung over the front porch to dry, the tattered books - then I have only known it as a myth, as a New Zealand that existed as an advertisement of itself. <i>(Jun 19, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  ..the bach: a simple, no-frills retreat where we can rediscover our conection with nature ... The architect-designed technology-filled modern bach is a refuge not from the city but the wilderness that surrounds it. <i>(Jun 26, 2006,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  <b>IRELAND</b> He would unscrew the door or prise open a window of the double-locked little bach - or sleep-out as it would now be called - at the back of our house, where the old man lived. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,18</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> This time, it will be my turn to lie on my belly on the cliff above the Black Bach. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,67</i>)

>  The bach is like a postage stamp on the outside of a letter. It's worthless, but there is $1.5 million inside the envelope. <i>(Jan 14, 2006,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  A man on his own could just scrape along.. if he had the use of a buckshee bach. <i>(1960,Mate,5/39</i>)

>  Tidy baches around Kaipara Harbour start from around $300,000. <i>(Dec 21, 2003,Sunday Star Times,D11</i>)

>  So, has the coastal bach boat sailed for most New Zealanders? <i>(Dec 21, 2003,Sunday Star Times,D11</i>)

>  Or when you promise yet another little weekend fishing at a bach in Riversdale the woman of your life says that she's really sorry, but she's off to Acapulco. <i>(May 2, 2000,Evening Post,Flair : 2</i>)

>  My girl friend and I had a batch at Takapuna for a fortnight. <i>(Dec 30, 1924,Otago Witness,71</i>)

>  Some suburban bloods lately gave a more or less "fancy dress" ball at a "bach," where six young men and a housekeeper manage to keep the wolf from the door. <i>(Apr 5, 1913,NZ Observer,16</i>)

>  Bach theft is not unusual, but mostly people are looking for food or somewhere to sleep. Bach vandalism is rare. <i>(Feb 1993,North & South,94</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Mrs Clements' and the Laings' baches are also wooden and sandy and smell of the dry sacking on the bottoms of the bunks like ours. <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,21</i>)

>  The ubiquitous bach may instantly spring to mind as expressive of NZ DIY handymanship and days in the sun or bush. <i>(Aug 13, 2000,City Voice,13</i>)

>  All six share similar design features, if that is what you call the elements comprising a traditional bach. Low-pitched roofs, corrugated iron walls and recycled timber fascias are standard construction. Some have tiny gardens of hardy coprosma, flax and ice plants scratched out of the rocks. Each has a water tank, various structures to hold firewood and fishing gear, some have a boat shed or a TV aerial and each has a chimney or flue of some kind. <i>(Feb 8, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  In those days (when the old baches were originally built) the 'environment', as they call it now, wasn't an issue. <i>(May 12, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),101</i>)

>  To get the full flavour of baching you hire a bach as big as a piano-case and fill it with three times as many people as it will hold. <i>(Jan 1937,NZ Railways Magazine,47</i>)

>  At the Egmont County Council meeting on Tuesday.. the popular subject - "Dirty Baches" was brought up. <i>(Dec 14, 1918,Stratford Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Probably because so many New Zealanders either spent childhood holidays at their own parents' bach or at their friends' parents' bach, this national institution has become infused with a nostalgic glow. <i>(Jan 19, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  We don't leave much in the bach, it isn't wise these days. <i>(Mar 23, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,62</i>)

>  <b>KEITH</b> The baches all smelt the same, a wonderful summer smell of Pinex wall board or the raw wood of converted Army huts. <i>(1991,Lovely Day Tomorrow, A,54</i>)

>  Sure, there's a family boat, and a bach, but visitors report that "it's just a nice Kiwi bach." <i>(Jul 22, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  Facilities at the Collins' bach include the tank water plumbed to the kitchen sink and an ancient kerosene fridge whose emanations have blackened the walls and renders [sic] the air astringent. A corrugated iron hut outside the back door provides an open fireplace for cooking. <i>(Jul 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 68/37</i>)

>  But the recurring memories of Kiwi dads are set in beaches and baches and bush - Eden indeed. <i>(Jul 11, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  The feeling is seaside-dairy-meets-flash-bach, but the kitchen is white-uniformed efficient, serving an accomplished brunch that can slide seamlessly into lunch, or even dinner. <i>(Oct 11, 2003,Dominion Post</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to survive as a motherless lamb.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Bach: (up a gully) a verb sometimes used when referring to the survival of a motherless lamb. [batch] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to live and cook for oneself [as a young male farm worker]. [verb.]

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> He batched in a tin shanty for years. <i>(1938,Me and Gus,53</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> Ernest bached with him when the first year's bush was burned ... <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,7</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> Single men bached in the house and saw to the basic farm maintenance. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,126</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> .. he had his own whare erected and could bach on the spot. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,183</i>)

>  <b>WATERSON</b> 'Certainly beats baching,' said Wart. <i>(1999,Some Buggers I've Seen,29</i>)

### bach boy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Among the "bach boys" - young bloods from the city who came for summer weekends filled with wild parties during the 1910s, 20s and 30s - were those who would become prominent citizens in time: Bernard Freyberg, leading public servant Joe Heenan and Claude Skelley, a future headmaster of Day's Bay's Wellesley College. <i>(Jan 18, 2002,Evening Post,15</i>)

### bach-holder
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The original bach-holders had the foresight to plant beech everywhere, but also decreed there would be no fences. <i>(Jun 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 52/26</i>)

### bachelor
 
 <i>n.</i> single v. married men looked upon as distinct, esp. in contests. [1881]

>  <b>PYKE</b> `As a bachelor you had, perhaps, some excuse for seclusion,' continued Miss Melrose, gaily, `but in your new character of benedict a hermit existence is very unbecoming. [his wife had gone for a holiday]] <i>(1881,White Hood and Blue Cap,118</i>)

>  Strangely for such a familiar phrase, `an old identity' was a local invention, like `bachelors v. benedicts.' <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: (McNab Slang)]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Benedict, benedict</i>. Any married man: catachrestic: mid-C.20. In NZ, contests between married and single men are described as being <i>bachelors and benedicts</i>. <i>(1984,Dictionary of Slang,70</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a young seal

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> In autumn the outward migration begins; the bachelors, the pups and the mothers ... set out for the ... abounding deeps. <i>(1945,Historical Southland,28</i>)

### bachelor's buttons
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (plant)

>  On the inner side .. of the bank there are prostrate shrubs of pohuehue [<i>Muehlenbeckia complexa</i>], [and] some bachelor's button (<i>Cotula coronopifolia</i>). <i>(1976,Hist. & Nat. Hist. of the Boulder Bank,21</i>)

>  One could find thousands of the little yellow "bachelor's buttons," a daisy-like plant (cotula) which is only found where the marsh or damp soil is distinctly salt. <i>(Nov 23, 1920,Otago Witness,52</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (recipe)

>  BACHELOR'S BUTTONS [ingredients] Sift flour and baking powder together, rub in butter, add sugar, then beaten eggs, roll into small balls, dip in sugar, and bake on shelf in quick oven (400F.). <i>(1961,Edmonds Cookery Book,12</i>)

### bachelorise
 
 <i>v.</i>



 
 <i>v.</i> to live alone as a bachelor, to `do' for oneself (often in the temporary absence of a spouse); to share living quarters, domestic chores and expenses, usu. with a person of the same sex. Often bachelorise with. [also bach Hence bachelorising ppl. aj. and vbl. n.] (See [also bach](../A#also-bach))

>  At one time, he and two other young men.. "bachelorised," together in Wellington. <i>(Dec 20, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> Cheyne and Withers then bachelorised together, and a few days later, the process was repeated. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,42</i>) [NOTES: Originated in Otago Daily Times 25 Feb. 1905]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>(1883,Facts Relating to NZ,8</i>) [NOTES: [see bachelorising]]

>  <b>HOPEFUL</b> They [Chinese] of course live very cheaply, because these communities are composed of men [133] bachelorizing together, they have no wives or children to support, as women are not allowed to leave China. <i>(1887,Taken In,132</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> Whom should I see but Mr - from Timaru, with whom, in days gone by, I had bachelorised. <i>(1985,Diary,9</i>)

>  Single men [at Cross Creek] had either to bachelorise in their whares or board with one or another of the married men. <i>(Mar 10, 1906,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> So he's bachelorisin', and I'm keeping house for him. <i>(1912,Brown Bread,129</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,43</i>) [NOTES: Evans Waikaka Saga 42 or 43 quoting earlier source]

>  <b>OLIPHANT</b> We had then been bachelorising, and it was decided to break up the establishment. <i>(1905,Bill the Namer,6</i>)

### bachelorising
 
 <i>n.</i> living independently. (See [baching](../B#baching))

>  Do you like bachelorising, Dot? <i>(Sep 2, 1903,Otago Witness,67</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> He was reduced to a most forlorn `bachelorising' existence in a one-roomed hut. <i>(1912,Brown Bread,191</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Bachelorising, to use a Colonial term, is not a disagreeable life. <i>(1883,Facts Relating to NZ,8</i>)

>  <b>GIBSON</b> He promised ... to show me a few more points in bachelorising. <i>(1924,That Gibbie Galoot,16</i>)

### bachelors' ball
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ball or dance, often held in a wool-shed or country hall.

>  The Bachelors' Ball will come off on Friday, the 22nd instant. <i>(Oct 9, 1880,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  Auroa annual bachelors' ball on June 17 in Auroa Hall. <i>(Jun 7, 1910,Hawera Star,4</i>)

>  The bachelors' ball, which is looked upon as the event of the year, took place here on Wednesday night and, needless to say, was a great success. <i>(Jul 7, 1911,Hawera Star,3</i>)

>  The annual bachelors' ball at Manutahi will take place on August 23. <i>(Aug 16, 1912,Hawera Star,4</i>)

>  This woolshed is the place where the bachelor balls used to be held so a lot of people have been here. It's a living, breathing eight-stand woolshed [bachelor ball] <i>(Oct 4, 2000,Capital Times,26</i>)

### bacher
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SMITH</b> The normal bacher makes his bed once. <i>(1947,From N to Z,129</i>)

### bachie
 
 <i>n.</i> a bach-dweller or owner.

>  A true bachie spurns holiday homes with their fittings and faucets, and walks a righteous path through the wildflowers and cobwebs to the paint-peeled front door. <i>(Dec 1, 2001,Press, The,C1</i>)

### bachiness
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The bachiness of the restaurant also comes from its inside nooks and crannies, its dark green walls dotted with rods and reels and other fishing paraphenalia. <i>(Apr 27, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,4</i>)

### baching
 
 <i>n.</i> [some citations below belong with bach v. TD]

>  When the mater returned to Dunedin after her holidays in this city, I determined to try "baching," and after having been six months in my crib at the top of the hill, I am more than ever resolved not to descend again to town level... My "bach" is perched.. about 7000ft above sea-level. <i>(Oct 9, 1912,Otago Witness,79</i>)

>  A rather good story is told of two young embryo lawyers who are at present 'baching' not too far from Newton. <i>(Jul 26, 1890,NZ Observer,6</i>)

>  <b>EDDIE ATTWOOD</b> Here we were made quite welcome and although the occupiers were baching they had a jolly good feed ready. <i>(Apr 13, 1932,Diary</i>) [NOTES: Eddie Attwood's Diary in NZ Memories No. 36, June-July, 2002, p. 32]

>  <b>SMITH</b> "Baching" is a universal sport for men of all ages. <i>(1947,From N to Z,129</i>)

>  A feeling for week-end "baching" is disturbing for all of us... Be practical and do a week-end "bach". <i>(Nov 20, 1935,NZ Free Lance,19</i>)

>  A New Plymouth resident who is baching bought half a dozen eggs on a recent morning. <i>(Dec 30, 1944,Bay of Plenty Times,2</i>)

>  To get the full flavour of baching you hire a bach as big as a piano-case and fill it with three times as many people as it will hold. <i>(Jan 1937,NZ Railways Magazine,47</i>)

### bachophile
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A website set up.. last year..lists 700 baches from the best-selling guide Baches and Holiday Homes... Bachophiles can check it out at : www.holidayhomes.co.nz. <i>(Nov 8, 1999,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

### bachville
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  About 60 percent of ratepayers don't live there, up almost nine percent in the past three years. Bachville. <i>(Dec 31, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

### bachy
 
 <i>adj.</i> [being like a bach: small, modest, residential - of buildings]

>  A few bachy places have been bulldozed and big, flash "insensitive" houses - not owned by arty types - have gone up, a worry to every sensitive creative person who calls Paekakariki home. <i>(Oct 24, 2009,Dominion Post Indulgence,4</i>)

### back
 
 <i>adj.</i> remote

>  Shearing usually commences on the farms near the coast somewhat earlier than in the back districts. <i>(1883,Brett's Colonists Guide,214</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [backing dog or woolwalker] to scamper along the backs of sheep to unblock bottlenecks in races etc.

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> Teaching a dog to back sheep is something for which provision must be made in the puppy's early tuition if success is to be certain. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,38</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> For the first practice in backing live sheep, the race used for fat lamb picking should be used. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,41</i>)

>  He is well bred, very forceful and will head, hunt and back. <i>(Jul 8, 1971,NZ Farmer,102</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> This beautiful dog would head, hunt and back with almost any dog I've seen. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,13</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Back: A dog backs when it leaps up and runs across on top of sheep.(wool-walking) Hence a backing dog or wool-walker. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,2</i>)

>  Handy - a dog that heads a bit, hunts with a bit of noise and possibly backs as well. <i>(Oct 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,24</i>)

### back bush
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) bushland in an isolated area.

>  <b>H R RICHMOND</b> I cannot feel that you will be wise to come into the back bush and give up your profession ... <i>(Dec 7, 1856,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,245</i>) [NOTES: letter in Scholefield (1960)]

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> The hunter declared that he wanted Glen for companionship while he was camped out in the back country bush. [back country bush] <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,47</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) bushland furthest away from a farm dwelling or homestead [as in back paddock or back block.]

>  <b>RICHMOND</b> Thus our neighbour Gilmour in the back bush wants GBP1 per acre for his uncleared land ... <i>(Mar 13, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,145</i>) [NOTES: letter in Scholefield (1960)]

>  <b>JANE RICHMOND</b> Henry's heart seems a good deal set on making a palatial residence in the back bush, but I don't know how funds will be found at present. <i>(Jun 24, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,147</i>) [NOTES: letter in Scholefield (1960 )]

### back carting
 
 <i>n.</i> the carrying of return loads or loads on a return journey

>  <b>ACTON-ADAMS</b> I'll provide the yokes and chains [for the bullocks]; you would have to get two wool trucks and covers. You would earn at least GBP.3 a week. as I pay 50/- a ton for wool with a lot of back carting at half price. <i>(1885,Letters</i>) [NOTES: Acton-Adams Letters in 1969 McCaskill Molesworth 57]

### back country
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the high country beyond S.I. river gorges, as opposed to front down and plains land in front of the gorges and ranges. (See [backblocks](../B#backblocks))

>  I left Christchurch in company with a sheep farmer, who owns a run in the back country, behind the Malvern hills. <i>(1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,47</i>) [NOTES: in BUTLER First Year (1863)]

>  <b>MONEY</b> .. his station, which was situated in what was called the back country i.e. close to the ranges which divide the east from the west coast of the island. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,21</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Backblocks or backcountry: In the South Island that country beyond the river gorges is termed back country, as differing from front country which consists of those hills through whose gorges the same rivers wend their way. Backblocks is the equivalent North Island term though the nature of the rivers does not define them so neatly. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,2</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Back-country. Land extending beyond and including the foothills; land beyond the river gorges. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) remote country. (See [backblocks](../B#backblocks))

>  In the North Island this type of farming [high-country farming] is confined to two small areas, on the high central plateau and on some of the high back-country in the Hawke's Bay-Poverty Bay area. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,50</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> In the heavy back-country, the soil was being swept off steep faces and down the big rivers [back-country] <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [attributive uses below]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Before I describe the river I may as well say a word on the nature of back country travelling in the Canterbury settlement. <i>(1860,Forest Creek MS,37,1960 ed.</i>)

>  The distant `back-country' ranges must have felt the storm more severely even than we have. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,173,1870 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [OED]]

>  <b>POTTS</b> Within the last few years [the kea] has discovered the out-stations of some of the back-country settlers. <i>(1882,Out in the Open,187</i>)

>  <b>OWEN</b> He considered it a luxury for a man who was often reduced to <i>rou-rou</i> on a back country block. <i>(1909,Philip Loveluck,82</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> And he saw from a new angle that these big sheep people - whether they owned their land or leased it, as many back-country squatters did .. were a distinct class. <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,262</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> Back-country runs were deserted and became mere breeding grounds [of rabbits]. <i>(1938,High Country,134</i>)

>  <b>SCOTTER</b> The back-country part of the original run had been renumbered 134A, some 22,200 acres in the Otekaike Riding. <i>(1948,Run, Estate and Farm,65</i>)

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> That first back country winter was quite an experience. We lived on meat, potatoes, bread, scones and occasionally some treacle. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,16</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Of many memory-pictures of back-country loveliness which we keep stored in the mind. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,41,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRUNDY</b> Twice each day the operators at the [Chatham Island] radio station called all the back-country people. Hence back-countryman, also back country man [nf AND] [OED 1796 US, 1942 NZ.], one who lives or works in the back-country, cf. backblocksman. <i>(1968,Who'd Marry a Doctor?,49</i>)

>  <b>MARSH & BURDON</b> The shepherds, musterers, shearers and drovers, the back-countrymen of New Zealand. <i>(1942,New Zealand (various authors/titles),9</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> They were the wool-growers, the run-holders, the sheep-cockies, the back-countrymen. <i>(1945,Died in the Wool,12</i>)

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> In those days [1883] there were no luxuries like butter, condensed milk or jam for back country men. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [The hinterland of settled districts: US 1746 (OED), AND 1802; of a part 1849; of a settled dist. or station 1887.] The remoter parts of a farm or other area, away from the sea or other means of access. Also called backblocks. Compare front country. a. Usu. with def art., a hinterland, beyond settlements, or sparsely settled, remote from coastal or other means of access, often regarded from the point of view of its farming use or potential. In Canterbury, country towards the Southern Alps (cf front country). In later use, backblocks. [a hinterland, beyond settlements, or sparsely settled, remote from coastal or other means of access, often regarded from the point of view of its farming use or potential. In Canterbury, country towards the Southern Alps (cf front country).] (See [up-country](../U#up-country),[cf. front country](../F#front-country))

>  Hobson is founding a town at the Bay of Islands, but we have an assurance that there is no back-country there. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/16/208</i>)

>  The land [at Wellington] rises suddenly into bold and rugged hills ... as to render the back country exceedingly difficult of access. <i>(1841,GBPP,184</i>) [NOTES: Mathew to Hobson in GBPP 1842 (No.569)]

>  A good back country up the Hutt. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/24/301</i>)

>  The back country is hilly and woody apparently with black birch <i>(1843,NZ Journal,136</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Thus a run situated in the plains over which sheep are being constantly driven ..., will be in more danger [from scab] than one on the remoter regions of the back country. <i>Ibid.</i> iv/48 [OED] At last I have been really in the extreme back country, and positively, right up to a glacier. <i>(1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iv/42</i>)

>  It is found at very considerable altitudes in `the back country'. <i>(1871,TrNZI,3/185</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> [The Canterbury settler] often spoke [of] ... some quartz rocks that he had observed .. in parts of his station, which was situated in what was called the back country, i.e., close to the ranges which divide the east from the west coast of the island. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,21</i>)

>  Musterers returned from back country [of the station] with mob of about 2000 mixed sheep. <i>(1879,Tutira,124</i>) [NOTES: 1921 ed.]

>  <b>BARLOW</b> There are great numbers of wild cattle in the back country [of Kaipara]. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,124</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Flax and cabbage trees belong more to the front country, they are not so characteristic of the back. <i>(1860,Forest Creek MS,54</i>) [NOTES: (MS) (1960)]

>  <b>POTTS</b> At present it is only in the 'back country' as it used to be called, that we can hope to find its breeding place. <i>(1882,Out in the Open,41</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> In back-country where the mills depend upon driving for the supply of [kauri] logs ... dry weather ... may prevent conversion. <i>(1889,Forest Flora,151</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> I hereby surrender to the Crown my licence of the Tarndale back country. <i>(1893,Molesworth,78</i>) [NOTES: Acton-Adams Letters in 1969 McCaskill]

>  But owing to the repressive measures adopted there has been a marked abatement of the pest, even on high back-country. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,32</i>)

>  <i>back country</i> -. The country beyond the river gorges: for instance, Craigie- burn and Mt. White are in the back country, Woodstock and Mt. Torlesse on the front-country. Two new-fangled literary expressions, <i>out back</i> and <i>backblocks</i>, are driving <i>back-country</i> out of newspaper use. <i>(Sep 9, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss.]

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> A good story is told by a friend who was going into the back country on a visit. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,111</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> This back-country [of Canterbury] was in all probability ...subject to severe winters. <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,178</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> Another wily plan was to run a fence up a valley, adjacent to a stream, and thus deprive any intending purchaser of water or the use of the back-country. <i>(1947,Story of Canterbury, The,128</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> One yarn I heard tell of but cannot vouch for, in connection with those troublous times [Hau Hau rebellion], concerned a couple of the general's shepherds who had been sent into the back country to try and trace some missing sheep. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,51</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Back country, suggesting big Canterbury sheepruns, is a term of approval. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,143</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Activities were once confined to the backcountry, but helicopters now buzz even the home paddocks in their impertinent search for deer. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,100</i>)

>  <b>PINNEY</b> In 1886 there was a bad drought ... Middleton boxed the 9,000 cull ewes with the wethers, and in February pushed them well into the back country, which was best in such a dry year. <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,26</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Back-country</i>. Land extending beyond and including the foothills; land beyond the river gorges. Remote stations are often referred to as being <i>up the back</i>. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> Hone Tuwhare best captures the essence of the backcountry in this poem. <i>(1989,Travelling Naturalist,49</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> I'd say that any woman who has spent a winter in at Mount White could easily qualify as a back-country woman. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,59</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a part of a property that is the most remote from the homestead; the back paddocks.

>  Burke Evans and Mannering came in from back country and his man, Hammond McMurdock, came, having left dray and bullocks on other side of pass. <i>(May 15, 1858,Haldon Station Diary,245</i>) [NOTES: Vance (1965)]

>  T.C.K., C.H.S. and Hughie went to the back country to erect mustering yards. <i>(Jan 9, 1879,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

>  Musterers returned from back country with mob of about 2000 mixed sheep (800 woolly.) <i>(Jan 20, 1879,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

>  Drafted sheep from back country ... <i>(Jan 21, 1879,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> Falling 150 acres of bush on back country. <i>(Jun 8, 1903,Diary</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> .. nothing had been done to the great residue of the property - the fifteen or sixteen thousand acres then known as the 'back' country. <i>(1921,Tutira,228</i>)

### back country man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer or musterer who has had long experience in working isolated hill country area/s.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Among back country men, no one is held in greater respect than a crack rough rider. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,107</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Mutton chops, or hockey sticks as they are generally known, are day after day and month after month, the back country man's breakfast ... <i>(1947,Wayleggo,114</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> There's a subtle difference between back-country men and those enslaved to industry and commerce. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,20</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> If anything could faze a back country man this was it ... <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,22</i>)

### back country run
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pastoral property located behind the river gorges or in a remote area.

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> I have lived among the ranges, on a rough back-country run. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,2</i>)

>  There are the back-country runs where the merino still reigns supreme <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,95</i>)

>  The question of the occupation of back country runs under the jurisdiction of the Land Board by tenants with insufficient capital was introduced by a member. [back-country run] <i>(Jul 2, 1923,NZ Farmer,947</i>)

### back country station
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large, isolated farm property.

>  <b>BARKER</b> .. it used not to be uncommon on back-country stations to get up the fine things with a flat-stone .. for an iron. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,184</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> After 'cutting out' the Waimate shed, they would go on to the back country station such as Morven Hills. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,128</i>)

>  <b>STAPLES</b> Every Friday evening he dressed up and was away to the gate to be picked up by a transport truck on its way back from the back country stations. [back-country station] <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,20</i>)

### back delivery
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a tilter or harvesting machine [used since the 1860s] which `delivers' its grain at the rear rather than at the side.

>  Their farming implements were limited, and the plough, harrows, roller and back delivery reaper comprised, in most cases, the working plant. <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,37</i>)

>  <b>WILSON & FRASER</b> In those early days the only reaping machine was the station back-delivery. James Anderson owned the first back-delivery in Otama, C O'Hara had a side-delivery. <i>(1936,Book of the Pioneers, The,79</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Later came the mowing machines, though the crop still had to be bound by hand. After them came the 'back-delivery' as it was called. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,309</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The 'tilter'- also known as the 'back-delivery' because it dropped the sheaves from the back of a tilting hinged platform - was drawn by two horses and was first used in the early 1860s. <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,123</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> Instead of the crops being cut by the hook with women and children sharing the work, back deliveries came into use. <i>(1937,Thomas,41</i>)

>  <b>SCOTTER</b> Then at the beginning of 1869 this firm demonstrated their reaping machine; ... Meek used as many as ten of these back deliveries in a paddock, seven men were required to each machine and perhaps thirty more were stooking... When the side delivery, the binder, took its place ... <i>(1948,Run, Estate and Farm,41</i>)

>  <b>NEAVE</b> [He] got a job binding sheaves behind a back delivery, an early [c1868] machine for cutting crops, and he got a job on a farm. <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,90</i>)

### back hut
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an outstation or mustering hut.

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> They live in the lonely back huts while they muster the high tops. <i>(1966,Over the River,132</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Taking sheep out of both valleys meant the end of an era for Makarora; no more camping out in back huts, for days at a time, with a six- to seven-man gang. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,179</i>)

>  <b>AXBEY</b> In one of the station back-huts was an old .577 Snider with a box of ammunition ... [back-hut] <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,113</i>)

### back of beyond
 
 <i>phr.</i> a remote area; an isolated hill-country farm or farm settlement. (See [cf. boohai](../B#boohai))

>  Of course today we see the Marangae as the back-of-beyond, but you have to remember that these people believed that, within a few years, they'd be surrounded by all the intended infrastructure of a viable rural community... <i>(2006,National Geographic Magazine,#77, Jan - Feb 2006, p82</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> Late that afternoon saw me over ten miles of up-hill road which seemed .. to be leading to the back of beyond ... <i>(1943,Pure Gold,57</i>)

>  <b>ELLMERS</b> This area really was the back of beyond. <i>(1993,Schoolhouse Under the Sky,15</i>)

>  It's not the back of beyond. We have reticulated water, electricity ... <i>(Dec 2, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  <b>David Geary</b> Somehow we got a job out the back of beyond. <i>(2008,Six Pack 3, The,114</i>)

>  [Hdg] Back o' Beyond... From isolated back country farms, from lighthouses and from the most inaccessible part [sic] of the Dominion, 170 young people arrived at New Plymouth today. <i>(May 6, 1939,Taranaki Herald,6</i>)

>  `Very good,' I said, `then you shan't go home. I'll wheel you to the back of beyond. I'll take you miles.' <i>(Sep 11, 1911,Triad, The,46 [18/6/46]</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> I shall never forget the small dark man who took up a run at the back of beyond, carted a piano up there on a bullock dray, and passed his solitary evenings playing Bach's fugues. <i>(1912,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1912 Williams Letter in 1919 Jones Samuel Butler 1/85]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The attraction of the old land of enchantment `the back of beyond' drew me. <i>(1913,Trade Hunting,12</i>)

>  Some, like the house-sparrow, cling tenaciously to the settled areas, while others, like the chaffinch ..., sometimes occur in the very `back of beyond.' <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/441</i>)

>  <b>LUSK</b> Far away in the `back of beyond' as you call it. <i>(1929,Love and Chiffon,20</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> Late that afternoon saw me over the ten miles of up-hill road which seemed ... to be leading to the back of beyond, and arrived ... not far behind him. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,57</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> I'd been shifted down to the bad boys' camp in the back of beyond. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,147</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> Far away in a remote area. .in 4;`The last I heard of him he was prospecting on his own, west of the Red Hills - miles further on, away out the back of beyond.' <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],25,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> Duffer rushes collapsed so quickly that storekeepers could find themselves in the back-of-beyond with a fully-stocked shanty and no sales to pay for the cost of packing-in and packing-out. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,281</i>)

>  <i>Whykickamoocow:</i> An imaginary town located in the back of beyond; HICKSVILLE, NEW ZEALAND. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A</i>)

>  <i>Whykickamoocow:</i> An imaginary town located in the back of beyond; HICKSVILLE, NEW ZEALAND. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Eketahuna itself enjoyed the dubious notoriety in the seventies as <i>the</i> back of beyond. Hence, in occas. predicative use, `very remote'. <i>(1985,G'day Country,78</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> This was so back of beyond they [ = the railways] still had a water tank at Whanga. <i>(1985,G'day Country,97</i>)

>  Just fancy the general body of ratepayers voting rates for the purpose of conveniencing deer stalkers "way out the 'back of beyond'". <i>(Apr 6, 1904,Otago Witness,31</i>)

### back of creation
 
 <i>phr.</i> orig. in fig. use for a remote (`godforsaken') place (heard Marlborough ca1940); henceas an intensifier in comparisons.

>  <b>ANZAC</b> A soothing liquid that licks `forty-rod', `chained lightning' or `Cape smoke' [illicit whiskeys] to the back of creation. <i>(1916,On Anzac Trail,77</i>)

### back paddock
 
 <i>n.</i> a paddock remote from the farm homestead, or the main access route.

>  <b>GRAY</b> Front paddock 80 acres Upper paddock 50 [do] Back paddock 10 [do] Middle paddock 20 [do]. <i>(1970,Quiet with the Hills,49</i>) [NOTES: Peache Journal in 1970 Gray Quiet with Hills 49]

>  <b>BELL</b> Of course, by this route he could have a look round the sheep in Ashwin's back paddock on the way in or out. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,43</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> A couple of shepherds ... were riding through one of the back-paddocks. <i>(1907,Return of Joe,131</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> So Uncle Ted ... goes off to catch the little brown pony ... in the corner of the back paddock. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,37,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> It was quite a walk from the hut to where they were working in the back paddock; we had to go along the road and through a Taranaki gate and along a clay road. <i>(1965,Stand in the Rain,58</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Sometimes he would ask us if we were coming out for a shot in the back paddock. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,32</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> Like the froth on the creamy buckets of milk ... when the handyman brought them in from the bails in the back paddock. <i>(1960,Word for Word,122</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> Mr Robinson was a share-milker on a local farm. He lived by himself in a bach on a back paddock. <i>(1980,Murder and Chips,153</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> He was playing in the back paddocks with his sisters and cousins. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,63</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a paddock far from the homestead where grazing animals, such as wethers, receive little attention.

>  <b>PEACHE</b> Front paddock 80 acres Upper paddock 50 acres, Back paddock 10 acres ... <i>(1876,Quiet with the Hills,49</i>) [NOTES: Journal in Gray (1970) Quiet With the Hills]

>  <b>BELL</b> .. he could have a look round the sheep in Ashwin's back paddock on the way in or out. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,43</i>)

>  The back paddocks are usually some way from the shed and are ideal for veal production. <i>(Jun 10, 1958,NZ Dairy Exporter,53</i>)

>  Any [Kaimanawa] horses that a veterinarian deemed unsuitable for domestic use would be sent to farms to live out their days in back paddocks. <i>(Jun 12, 2001,Dominion,7</i>)

### back porch
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> The back door opened not directly into the yard but into what we called the back porch - a sort of slot in the house, neither inside nor really outside, but just cold. <i>(1995,Mum,63</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> On the back porch the boy fingered his fishing rod while his father smoked and puzzled. <i>(1990,Strangers and Journeys,243</i>) [NOTES: Jim says p. 264 in 1972 edition]

### back ranges
 
 <i>n.</i> mountain ranges a distance from the coast.

>  <b>POTTS</b> Look about the slopes of our alpine back-ranges. <i>(1882,Out in the Open,76</i>)

>  After a number of years, when sheep were mustered in from the back ranges, it was noticed that several would die in the yards. <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/278</i>)

### back road
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp.use] an alternative route giving access to remote or back areas of a property or district.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They were now 'between the fences' and would travel by back roads where the feed would be good. <i>(1949,High Country Days,114</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> We had a hell of a job rounding them up, but got every one in the finish and travelled by a back road past Hanmer to the accommodation paddocks ... <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,67</i>)

>  <b>HARRISON</b> Sid's cousin, Harry, had a farm about ten miles from Sid, up the back road. <i>(1986,She's a Fair Cow,64</i>)

### back round
 
 <i>v.</i> of a bush fire, grass-line, etc. to change direction relative to the direction of the wind. To burn against the wind (of a bushfire). ??Orig. naut. of wind to shift in counter-clockwise direction; (See [cf. back-burning](../B#back-burning))

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> As a last resort, get under a culvert. But it is backing round this way, and the whare will probably go later. <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,66</i>)

### back run
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an isolated grazing station, usually behind several hills.

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> It was after a day on the muster at a back run out in the west. <i>(1975,More From the High Country,12</i>)

>  <b>BAGNALL</b> ... the partners still hoped to establish their claim to three runs in all, namely Hautotara of 5120 acres as a back run of 43,500 acres. <i>(1976,Wairarapa : An Historical Excursion,339</i>)

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> It's often I think of the time and the days, when we mustered the back runs for sheep. <i>(2000,Country Bloke and Other Verse, The,223</i>)

### back section
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Your poor, rough, back section. It has been forfeited twice, and you'll never make a do of it. <i>(1902,NZ Illustrated Magazine,5/381</i>)

### back settler
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer settling in an isolated area.

>  The back settlers still complain of Government neglect with regard to the roads. The weather has just suited the back settlers, who have managed to get off some splendid burns. <i>(Apr 1896,NZ Farmer,143</i>)

### back station
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [out-station](../O#out-station))

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> The cattle ... were half-wild, restless brutes, imported from Australian back-stations, or bred on the equally wild ranges to the north of our own island. <i>Ibid.</i> 208 One evening, I remember a neighbouring back-station holder, after a long day's journey, rode up late along the fence-side of our home-paddocks, amidst a chorus of challenge from the sheep-dogs. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,204</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an isolated station property.

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> They settled between Saltwater Creek and Leithfield, known in that part as great carriers to back-stations. <i>(1937,George Rhodes of the Levels and His Brothers,72</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> All the back stations had a store or shop attached to the homestead where they kept a supply of clothing, towels, soap, tobacco, papers, matches and the like. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,46</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> There were also the district get-togethers when the back station people would come together for dances, parties and dog trials. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,2</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> When the bushfelling was in full swing on the three back stations in 1912, it took eight muttons a day to satisfy the total needs. [back-station] <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,65</i>)

### back to back
 
 <i>phr.</i> (See [cf. backs](../B#backs))

>  The ore-shoots of all the approximately parallel Hauraki veins occurred in corresponding positions in the veins, and pitched in the same direction as the main shoot. This is the structure termed in the older mining phraseology `ore to ore', or shoots `back to back'. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,(NS) No.4 /106</i>)

>  [At Waihi] ... the shoots being disposed `back to back' in these parallel veins. <i>(1912,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,(NS) No.15 107</i>)

### back to the mat
 
 <i>phr.</i> in phrases: return/go back to the mat, get back on/to the mat: of a Europeanised Maori, to figuratively resume Maori dress; to return to Maori customs and way of life; to go native (cf. go back to the bush, bush) [-] (See [mat](../M#mat))

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> Some of the Maoris, educated in youth and brought up in British ways, returned in later life `to the mat'. <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,109</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> She spoke so pleasantly that Miss Brown decided she must have been somewhere to a Maori High School and then come back to the mat. <i>(1978,All Part of the Game,88</i>) [NOTES: 1947 Gaskell `School Picnic' in 1978 All Part of the Game 88]

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include: ... <i>get back on the mat</i>: to go native <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> She spoke so pleasantly that Miss Brown decided she must have been samewhere to a Maori High School and then come back to the mat. <i>(1947,Big Game,92</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> you pakeha say <i>There he goes, back to the pa.</i> You say <i>Back to the mat.</i> That's what you say to each other about us. <i>(1974,Maori Woman,168</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> `Seems to me people are right when they say Maori people head back to the mat,' Sam said rolling cigarette. <i>(1976,Pallet on the Floor,114</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> But eventually Aramati left us (`to go back to the mat', my grandmother said), and then we had Edwina who was quite different. <i>(1965,Memoirs of a Peon,17</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> MIRIAM [to Hingawaru]: Tribe's all right, but tribe's split up. Stick with the tribe, it's back to the mat. Action songs and a bottle of beer. <i>(1974,Hand on the Rail, The,12</i>)

>  <b>GIFKIN</b> All Pakeha Taurangians were superior to all Maori; even an educated 'Pakeha-fied' Maori would end by going 'back to the mat'. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,128</i>) [NOTES: Meikle]

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> '... They all go back to the mat in the end. What's bred in the bone comes out in the flesh ... Eating dried shark and sleeping on the mat ... Back to the mat. I've seen it time and again.' <i>(1976,Pallet on the Floor,49</i>)

### back to the pa
 
 <i>n.</i> referring to a Europeanised Maori's return to Maori ways

>  <b>HYDE</b> `You couldn't have a Maori for a boss, though, they don't know what time means. `Taihoa,' that's what they say, and they all go back to the <i>pa</i> in the long run.' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,189</i>)

### back tray
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a platform on the back of a tractor that can be used to transport stock or implements. (See [tractor tray](../T#tractor-tray))

>  Ferguson 24 petrol tractor, runs well, tidy condition, back tray, roll bar, $1,500. <i>(Mar 21, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,22</i>)

### back up
 
 <i>v.</i> to repeat, reoffend; back up on (a- person), to gang up on (for retaliation).

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> In our gang we all used to back up on Barclay because we'd all talked it over and decided to stick together. <i>Ibid.</i> 244 <i>back up.</i> (v) Repeat, reoffend, reinforce. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> of a tree being felled, to put in a back-cut with a saw; or, to open up a back-cut with a wedge, or with a second wedge driven in on the back of the first already in place. (See [back-cut](../B#back-cut),[back-scarf](../B#back-scarf))

>  `Don't you hear her talkin'?' as a gentle ticking would indicate the act of partition had commenced. Then the wedges were `backed up,' and a few solid blows from the heavy mauls would complete the operation. <i>(Dec 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,178</i>)

>  A very large tree is chosen, ... He has got what they call a great `bottle' on him, so after debate it is agreed to bring him down with an axe instead of backing him up with a cross-cut. <i>(1864,Weekly News</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Weekly News (Auckland) in 1957 Reed Story Kauri 159]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> I soon learned [c1890] how to chop a scarf in a standing pine, how to pull on ... a crosscut saw, to back up with hammer and wedges and how to handle horses. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,11</i>)

>  <b>MARRIOT</b> When we were well in with the saw-cut, Cyrus started backing the tree up - driving two wedges into the saw-cut and actually lifting the back of the tree, from the lean it had, to the perpendicular. <i>(1981,Life in the Gorge,95</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [rams] to draw back before charging a rival.

>  It is well-known that they always 'back up' to get a start to butt. Stop their backing-up and you disconcert them entirely .. but on the attempt to 'back-up' he is astonished by an effectual brake in the rear. <i>(Oct 1883,Northern New Zealand Settler,291</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [of rams establishing territory and status] draw back to charge another.

>  It is well-known that they always 'back-up' to get a start to butt. Stop their backing-up and you disconcert them entirely as his majesty goes straight ahead about his business ... but on the attempt to 'back-up,' he is astonished by an effectual brake in the rear. <i>(Oct 1883,Northern New Zealand Settler,291</i>)

### back whare
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an out-station or isolated hut far from a homestead.

>  <b>BIBBY</b> In 1910 we camped with Ralph in the back whare for a long spell, pulling thistles each day. <i>(1990,Lunesdale: A Bush Farm Community from 1871,27</i>)

>  <b>PLUMMER</b> In the early spring of both 1930 and 1931, I was given the job of going out to the back whare and camping for eight weekends to go round the ewes in the back paddocks ... <i>(1994,From Drover to Director,35</i>)

>  <b>PLUMMER</b> I remember once the packhorses were used to carry six foot lengths of corrugated iron out to the back of the station to build a lean-to on to the back whare. <i>(1994,From Drover to Director,39</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> I jacked up a tucker box as Wrey and I were intending an overnight stay at the back whare. <i>(1996,I Was So Lucky,148</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> The shepherds' quarters was a very old and dilapidated building having originally been built as a back whare when the station was run as one large block. <i>(1996,I Was So Lucky,160</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> He arrived only the night before the big muster was due to start out at the Back Whare. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,105</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> The Jam Tin had to assume 'back whare' status then. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,132</i>)

### back yard
 
 <i>n.</i> an enclosure at the back of a house which may include a vegetable garden or lawn (See [cf. back](../B#back))

>  <b>HOPEFUL</b> All the surroundings of squalid houses, filthy backyards, rough streets, ... are neither calculated to refine or soften [children]. <i>(1887,Taken In,120</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> I was conducted to the back yard. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,4</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> We always gave him the same presents - three sticks of Juno tobacco and three cakes of hokey-pokey. The presentation took place in the back-yard, and he sang us a wonderful Irish song. <i>Ibid.</i> 111 There were four doors - one, judging from the smell, let into the `Store', one onto the `back yard', through the third I saw the bedroom. <i>(1899,Stories,6,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> She was chopping up a candle-box in the backyard, and suddenly the tomahawk fell from her hand and she dropped. <i>(1974,Undiscovered Country,301</i>) [NOTES: 1913 Mansfield in 1974 Gordon Undiscovered Country 301]

>  So long as there are babies ..., there is washing to be done daily, ... lugged up and down steps, in and out of back-yards, ... Once a week ... there's a copper boil-up. <i>(Oct 1949,Here & Now,17</i>)

>  <b>GRUNDY</b> Our back yard rose steeply from the house to the fence line, where native flax had been planted as a wind break. <i>(1968,Who'd Marry a Doctor?,30</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A top blade-man in his day ... He's past using a pair of tongs now but I noticed he's still got a dog tied up in his backyard. <i>(1973,Big Country,20,1977 ed.</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> She wore footy jerseys and sometimes boots, and he'd seen her clomping about in the back yard after it was dark. <i>(1987,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,1,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COX</b> We live ... on the crown of a little hill in Mount Vic, Laurie in a whitewashed car-case bach against the back fence and me ten metres away across a narrow strip of unkempt backyard grass, in the back half of a large divided bungalow. <i>(1987,Dirty Work,27</i>)

### back-blading
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [land-contouring] the using of a back-blade on a tractor or bulldozer.

>  When filling is completed the area is roughly levelled and smoothed across by blading and back-blading until only a depression remains. <i>(Oct 15, 1962,NZ Journal of Agriculture,305</i>)

### back-blocksy
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Di Hooper,from Putini Press, hopes the book will challenge the bad press the West Coast gets. "It gets a lot of good press for its spectacular beauty but sometimes I think people consider the West Coast a back-blocksy kind of place." <i>(Sep 11, 2010,Your Weekend,43</i>)

### back-box
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [mustering] to mix mobs intentionally.

>  <b>STACEY</b> We then mustered the head of the Rainbow back-boxing the two mobs and putting them above the bull break before riding to Tarndale. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,92</i>)

### back-burn
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>JARDINE</b> As it was not possible to fight the fire directly in the six-foot-high bracken, it would have to be back-burned from specially cleared breaks well out on each side. <i>(1978,Shadows on the Hill,40</i>)

### back-burner
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  For 15 long years Verna Meads back-burnered her life and was excluded from official rugby functions because of her gender. <i>(Apr 7, 2001,Evening Post,2</i>)

### back-chain
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a chain used to hold a cow in position during milking. (See [backing chain](../B#backing-chain))

>  I consider there's no argument about leg ropes, but with back-chains, there's much more room for debate. With one milker working under pressure, the odd few seconds are saved if no back-chain is used. <i>(Jun 1, 1957,NZ Dairy Exporter,66</i>)

### back-country
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land and people behind South Island river gorges or in remote areas] isolated.

>  Although they are not intended for use as road maps, geologists will inevitably use them as such, so I looked closely at the roads, with memories of squinting at 4-mile maps trying to see the roads while bouncing along back-country roads. <i>(Nov 1997,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,114/69</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> The distant back-country ranges. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,173</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> .. even a back-country shepherd had a nice tin mould in which he could make a dozen candles ... <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,144</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> Back-country cookery in the early days was done with the most primitive equipment. <i>(1973,Petticoat Pioneers,I 248</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> In back-country areas such as the Mohaka region the transport cost greatly increased the price [of grass-seed.] <i>(1985,Farming in the Mohaka State Forest,7</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Liz was back-country through and through, hailing from Duntroon Station in the Awatere Valley. <i>(1993,Station Country I,40</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> Bob Griffen, a genuine back-country boy, married Hazel Sibley of Gisborne. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,49</i>)

>  As well, back-country farmers are reducing stock numbers to winter levels and some good yardings of stock are anticipated. <i>(May 10, 2002,Dominion,16</i>)

### back-cut
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [back-scarf](../B#back-scarf))

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Always make your back-cut a bit higher than the scarf and leave plenty of wood so if she leans back you can wedge her over. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,47</i>)

>  <b>FRANCES</b> He knew that, here in this confined space, Dinny would put in the back cut himself, one mistake crashing a tree on to house or barn. <i>(1964,Johnny Rapana,28</i>)

### back-end
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of home detention) granted near the end of a sentence [cf. front-end home detention, which is open to anyone sentenced to less than two years in prison... Supporters say front end home detention, which accounts for more than 90 per cent of the sentences, can be a valuable wake-up call for first and minor offenders.]

>  "Back-end" home detention allows anyone serving a fixed term sentence (which excludes murderers and serious sex offenders given preventative detention) to apply to the [parole] board five months before their parole eligibility date. If approved they can begin home detention three months before their parole date. <i>(Sep 13, 2003,Dominion Post,A14</i>)

### back-end loader
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  1 Belcher back end loader bucket <i>(Nov 10, 1993,Napier Daily Telegraph,00</i>) [NOTES: check page]

### back-fence
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to erect a fence behind stock as they graze.

>  By July the weather is getting wetter and the cows will pug up the breaks they graze on rainy days. Provided you back-fence and keep moving, these strips will recover quite quickly except around troughs where the poaching has gone on for several days. <i>(Mar 1967,NZ Journal of Agriculture,37</i>)

>  He backfences as much as possible, forcing the cows to move on for their feed ... <i>(Dec 1976,NZ Dairy Exporter,3</i>)

>  Every year we buy more electric fence standards and reels so we can backfence. <i>(Nov 9, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  ..the cows are back fenced with another electric fence so that they cannot go back and cause further hoof damage on the pasture already eaten. [backfence; back fence] <i>(Jun 1, 2001,Fencepost.com,Expert Farming</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fence erected behind stock as they move forward in block-grazing.

>  <b>MCMEEKAN</b> Strip-grazing without a back-fence can produce fertility damage ... <i>(1960,Grass to Milk,61</i>)

>  Fit the back fence in such a way that the stock can get water, or provide a portable trough. <i>(Mar 1967,NZ Journal of Agriculture,35</i>)

>  Strip-grazing without a back-fence is the best for his farm. <i>(Nov 1975,NZ Dairy Exporter,11</i>)

### back-fencing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the process in block-grazing where stock are fenced from behind to protect the recently-grazed area, encouraging them to move forward.

>  Mr Handley believes that back-fencing is worthwhile to save damage to pasture and allow a quicker recovery. <i>(Sep 28, 1961,NZ Farmer,03</i>)

>  Back-fencing helps to eliminate pugging by restricting stock movement. <i>(Dec 1964,NZ Journal of Agriculture,533</i>)

>  Benefits of Power fence systems .. make strip grazing and back fencing easy. <i>(1996,Gallagher Power Fence Manual,4</i>)

>  The Toplesses have also realised the benefits of backfencing ... [backfencing] <i>(Nov 9, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### back-gifting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Keep notes to prevent "back-gifting" - re-gifting to the person who gifted it to you. <i>(Dec 22, 2005,NZ Herald,D1</i>)

### back-graze
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to re-graze on crop or pasture previously grazed by the same stock.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> There have been cases when hoggets back-grazed over earlier-eaten breaks of supplementary feed have suffered severely from worm infestation. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,65</i>)

### back-land
 
 <i>n.</i> of a port rather than back-country;

>  I have most carefully examined every part of it, and although the back-land is rugged and precipitous there is a much larger portion of it level than I have yet seen in the Bay, and fully sufficient to afford space for a very pretty and convenient town. <i>(1842,GBPP,(No.569) 145</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mathew to Hobson in GBPP 1842]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an out-lying area of a station, possibly supporting an out-station.

>  <b>EVANS</b> And so this couple settled down in this back-land home [out-station] and no doubt loneliness was a problem. <i>(1975,Silver Tussock, The,103</i>)

### back-legs
 
 <i>n.</i>

### back-load
 
 <i>v.</i> to carry on a return or `back' trip.

>  <b>HISLOP</b> But the usual thing for the Styx settlers so far inland was to back-load with a year's supply of good quality coal picked up at Waipiata railway yards ... <i>(1943,Pure Gold,117</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Sometimes our trips to the Bend were profitable for there were wool and hides to be taken out and fertilizers and general cargo to backload. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,76,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Bush rats at night sometimes made their way into our schooners when they were loaded with general merchandise, or back-loaded with small quantities of farm produce. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,122</i>)

>  <b>PINNEY</b> They contracted [in 1881] to cart to Kurow for 34s 4d a ton, and to backload at 23s. Many of these backloads were of coal. <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,34</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a load or freight carried on the return journey from carrying a main load.

>  <b>BAINES</b> Reserving some 30lbs ... for my back-load home. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,205</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> He would get two or three back-loads of manuka. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,112</i>)

>  They contracted [in 1881] to cart to Kurow for 34s 4d a ton, and to backload at 23s. Many of these backloads were of coal. <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,34</i>)

### back-out
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an early design of cowshed, in which a cow must back out after milking [rather than walk out the front as in a walk-through.]

>  <b>POWDRELL</b> There are many types of cow-shed, for example the Race, the Double bail, the Single back-out, and the Walk-through type of shed. <i>(1920,Dairy Farming in NZ,22</i>)

>  <b>BELSHAW</b> In modern sheds the walk-through type of bail has completely superseded the old back-out type. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,456</i>)

>  Design of cowshed: back-out. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### back-scarf
 
 <i>v.</i> to scarf a tree on the side opposite the direction of intended fall. (See [cf. scarf v](../S#scarf-v))

>  He went out into the bush and backscarfed a big tree so that it would fall in one particular direction. <i>(1892,One Lady at Wairakei,36,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> He will `belly-scarf' and `back-scarf' the lot, that is to say he will cut about one third through on the lower and upper sides [of the tree]. <i>(1928,Pioneering in Poverty Bay,38</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a scarf or cut so made (See [also back-cut](../A#also-back-cut))

>  <b>WILSON</b> If you go too deep [with the belly-scarf], the tree might split and fall before you put in the back-scarf; ... <i>(1955,Land of My Children,24</i>)

### back-set
