<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a stock carcass, arranged in a particular manner for display purposes.

>  Back-set: a carcass arranged to stiffen in a certain way so that it can be used for display purposes. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,3</i>)

### back-shunt
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  There the waggons and van would be left on the 'main' while the others were hauled out of the back-shunt and set back to rejoin the train... <i>(Dec 2002,NZ Memories,No. 39/54</i>)

### back-slack
 
 <i>v.</i> to answer back, to cheek, to back-chat

>  <b>SMYTH</b> Think I'm going to sit here and let this -- this farm labourer back-slack us. <i>(1929,Bonzer Jones,237</i>)

### back-slappy
 
 <i>adj.</i> hearty mannered

>  Given the ethnic background, the history and introverted nature of the society, it was obviously not a place of happy back-slappy outward-goers, like say some of the coal towns on the West Coast, but then of course, what are, or were? <i>(Nov 1986,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,74/79</i>)

### back-up
 
 <i>n.</i> retaliation, revenge.

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> It was rare for a dispute to take place without at least talk of a back-up (retaliation) afterwards, ... <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,79</i>)

### back-up colony
 
 <i>n.</i> a tool of animal population conservation

>  Another six chicks were taken to Moutohora (Whale Island) to start a back-up colony. <i>(Sep 7, 2005,NZ Herald,A14</i>)

### backblock
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a remote farming area.

>  The condition of some roads in the 'back-blocks' still calls out loudly for ameliorating action. <i>(Jan 8, 1905,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  .. it is hoped to provide for the benefit of settlers in the backblocks, the government will be in as till better position to improve the lot of the primary producers ... <i>(Jul 1925,NZ Dairy Exporter,35</i>)

>  His research was helped by his wife, Diane, who comes from the backblocks of Hawke's Bay. <i>(Nov 8, 2001,Dominion,17</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The pioneering father-figure of contemporary New Zealand imagination was man who went out to the back-blocks ( a term which originally referred to blocks of land purchased from the Maoris, but came to mean remote farming districts.). <i>(1951,History of New Zealand,149</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [South Island ranges] the mountainous farming land behind the river gorges.

>  <b>MCDONALD</b> The Merino provides good wool returns in its natural quarters - the mountainous back blocks. <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,37</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Backblocks or backcountry: In the South Island that country beyond the river gorges is termed back country .. Backblocks is the equivalent North Island term though the nature of the rivers does not define them so neatly. [back blocks; back-blocks] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> sg. a (survey) block of land remote from the main station or homestead, or from the coast; also compounded as a collective, remote blocks of land.

>  The back Block here. <i>(1852,McLean Papers,10/156</i>)

>  The back Block here. <i>(1852,McLean Papers,10/156</i>)

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> [The house] was used as head-station for a sheep run, comprising several `back blocks' ... that extended for many miles over bleak and rugged country. <i>(1895,South Sea Siren,161</i>)

>  To facilitate the working of the station, there were three places where the hands resided. The Homestead, ... the chief one, was at the end of the station nearest civilisation. About eight miles further back was a cottage, known as the `Out-Station' ... [Further back] the Ruahine whare ... This whare was therefore the furthest back habitation ... It was erected for the accommodation of shepherds when mustering the back-blocks. <i>(Apr 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,48</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> The Merino provides good wool returns in its natural quarters - the mountainous back blocks. <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,37</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> In the fifties he early became aware of the efforts of the runholding class to open up the backblocks. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,117</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a section of a farm or station, far from the homestead.

>  This whare was .. the furthest back habitation. It was erected for the accommodation of shepherds when mustering the back-blocks. <i>(1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,48</i>)

>  A party of six rabbiters, isolated by snow in backblocks of St Helen's Station, 30 miles behind Hanmer Springs, were supplied by air yesterday. <i>(Jul 4, 1947,Hawera Star,06</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. from the road it seems only a stone's throw across to that station's back block right under Arowhana itself. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,245</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Otairi had a second woolshed in its backblock and Alf got sick of toast and marmalade so he pinned a scone recipe to the inside of a cupboard of the cookhouse there. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,172</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> In jocular use, the farther suburbs of a city

>  [headline] Bus visits backblocks of Karori. <i>(Jun 16, 1987,Karori and Western Suburbs News,16</i>)

### backblock(s)
 
 <i>n.</i> the sparsely settled hinterland of more closely-settled districts (See [back country](../B#back-country),[boohai](../B#boohai),[sticks](../S#sticks))

>  He also brought a knowledge of the bush and back country that was invaluable to young field geologists such as myself and David Kerr, translated from urban England to work in the back-blocks of New Zealand. <i>(Mar 1994,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,103/87</i>)

>  <i>(Jan 16, 1890,Otago Witness,31</i>) [NOTES: We had listened reverently to an elderly stranger from the back-blocks, who took upon himself to explain to us that the men clad in calfskins and goatskins represented the tanners and curriers' trades.]

>  Upon the plains of Buenos Ayres, and into the furthest back-blocks of Australia and New Zealand the Waterbury Watch has found its way. <i>(Jun 2, 1890,Taranaki Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>PETERSON</b> But to me those back-blocks were the epitome of Romance. <i>(1981,Glasshouses,12 [pl v]</i>)

>  Far away in the bush backblocks. <i>(1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,7/266</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> We sleep tonight at the Rangataiki & then the plains & the back blocks. <i>(1907,Letters,1/32,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GROSSMAN</b> The wildness ... made Evelyn shiver and fasten her marabout closely at the throat. `Now I know what back-blocks are,' she said. <i>(1910,Heart of the Bush,13</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> Neither had we a `township' ... racecourse, newspaper, lawyers and due proportion of loafers in every back block as you appear to have now. <i>(1910,Reminiscences,29</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> I will now refer to the outsettlements or back-blocks of the early New Zealand days. <i>Ibid.</i> 18 When I come to write of the outsettlements from Wellington, or as they are now called, the `back blocks'. <i>(1910,Reminiscences,19</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The back blocks of Southland are not inaccessible places. Until you are stuck up by the great West Coast mountains ... you can get round. <i>(1913,Trade Hunting,12</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Living out in the back-blocks, miles away from any town, a motor-bike is not to be despised. <i>(1927,Gus Tomlins,90,1977 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PINFOLD</b> But, in the `back blocks' and new places, tracks, through the bush or just formed over clay hills, were mostly what one might expect to find. <i>(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,14</i>)

>  <b>CLOKE</b> Onward then unto the backblocks, Where the sheep are thinly spread. <i>(1931,Songs of NZ,210</i>)

>  Two new-fangled literary expressions, <i>out back</i> and <i>backblocks</i>, are driving <i>back-country</i> out of newspaper use. `The out-back' was, I think, coined by the `Bulletin' writers, and `back- blocks' is from the North Island. <i>(Sep 9, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss]

>  Tokotoko says she is not going to Kawa. She can't be bothered going to the `backblocks' she says. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/97</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The pioneering father-figure of contemporary New Zealand imagination was a man who went out to the `back-blocks' (a term which originally referred to blocks of land purchased from the Maoris, but came to mean remote farming districts). <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,149</i>)

>  <b>ESCOTT</b> But I did all I could to show her that I wasn't trying to push the charmer from the back-blocks any more. <i>(1963,Show Down,12,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> I headed for my father's broker's place up the Taihape road. Real backblocks, I can tell you. Plenty of scrub, gorse, and pigs deer. <i>(1964,Watersiders,70</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Although living in the back-blocks, (North Islanders say `in the sticks', presumably referring to bush or scrub) station people seem to encounter more than their fair share of incident. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,100</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> You get a bit old-fashioned out in the backblocks like us here. <i>(1974,Maori Woman,259</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> They wanted my brother to go back and manage the show but he said he'd had enough, way out in the backblocks like that. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,25</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) characteristic of the back country, often conveying a meaning of undeveloped understanding or unworldliness. [aka back-block, back-blocks; [AND back-block 1868.] In early use usu. backblock; later backblocks.]

>  The backblocks woman looked at her very gravely.. <i>(Oct 26, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,Magazine Section 1</i>)

>  Waitress: "What will you take to drink, Sir - Tea or Beer?" Backblocks Bill (scathingly): "Now which do I look as though I'd take?" <i>(Nov 1, 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,159</i>)

>  The backblock settler's burden was out of proportion to his ability to pay. <i>(Jun 30, 1944,Hamilton Courier,2</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> The first thing I had to do [on arriving at Wellington] was to get a new rig-out, as my backblock clothes were not in keeping with my important duties [as a civil servant]. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,55</i>) [NOTES: 1935 ed.]

>  The clean-limbed hard backblocks-men mustering in the river arm. <i>(1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,3/254</i>)

>  Choicest back-blocks Billingsgate. <i>(1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,8/128</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> [get cit] [Frequently throughout] any backblock editor. <i>(1909,Ballads about Business</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> His back-block talk he can never forget. <i>(1900,Wisps of Tussock,24</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Lonely back-blocks country. <i>(1904,Tussock Land,35</i>)

>  Slowly perhaps, but surely, we hope the Back-blocks District Nursing Scheme is progressing... No back-block settler .. will be too far away to get help in their time of trouble. <i>(Jan 1910,Kai Tiaki,28</i>)

>  A real back-block race-meeting as you ever did see. <i>(1911,Kiwi on the Swag,6</i>)

>  I love the little backblock schools. <i>(Aug 1917,Princes Street,9</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> The apt cunning of Taihape's hotel-keeper, long inured to back-blocks men's customs.., saw to that. <i>(1926,Butcher Shop, The,86</i>) [NOTES: 1981 ed.]

>  <b>CLOKE</b> We will give our backblock brothers What the Lord to us imparts. <i>(1931,Songs of NZ,210</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> Each little group was dependent upon itself for amusement and entertainment, and visitors were a peg on which to hang some simple backblock festivities. <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,55</i>)

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> `Well, I don't think you have done enough backblock work yet, but I think you ought to go to the Thames.' <i>(1936,Robert Maunsell,181</i>) [NOTES: 1936 in 1938]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The traditional fare of the backblocks stations was the traditional colonial menu of tea, damper and mutton for breakfast. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,41</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> All the early runs and backblocks settlements could have much easier done without horses than without draught bullocks. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,41</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Several miles beyond this block, out across the rolling gumland hills .. lay a back-block section, a bush farm in the early stages of development. <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,38</i>) [NOTES: 1960 ed.]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> I grew to awareness rejecting as alien our scrubby backblocks farm. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,10</i>) [NOTES: 1986 ed.]

>  <b>ELLIOTT & ADSHEAD</b> A little house still standing today in the backblock settlement of Apiti, about 28 miles north of Fielding. <i>(1967,Cowshed to Dog Collar,14</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> A simple phrase ... as he entered the dining-room would amuse the guests who probably wondered from what backblock country he had come. <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,103</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> Well, it looked a mighty big town to my backblock eyes but you'd think it pretty quaint... `You can't go about the city looking like a little backblock Maori,' she said. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,128</i>)

>  Bushie, at a back-blocks store: "You keep things for washin' here, don't yer?" "Oh, yes, sir, all toilet articles." "Well, you might give me a pound o' soap an' a rasp." <i>(Dec 4, 1885,Taranaki Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>MCKEE WRIGHT</b> But his back-block talk he can never forget, He's the same as he was on the station yet. <i>(1900,Wisps of Tussock,24</i>)

>  <b>WESTMACOTT</b> He certainly had not the backblocks temperament, and failed to enjoy sleeping on the floor that night. <i>(1977,After-Breakfast Cigar, The,159</i>)

>  <b>MARRIOTT</b> But when a townie is suddenly transplanted from the city to a real backblocks farm. <i>(1981,Life in the Gorge,9</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> Bunny had had his taste of backblock farming and was able to sell his shares at a 350% increase in value. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,42</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> Te Kumi was, and still is, reputed to be the most isolated and backblock station in New Zealand. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,113</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> While Phil chatted, in a low-key backblocks fashion, his grey horse was keeping a surreptitious eye on me. [back-block/s] <i>(2001,Riding Country,53</i>)

>  [Caption] A back block observatory : preparing to view the recent solar eclipse at Wharehine. <i>(Oct 5, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,37</i>)

>  The heavy expenses incurred by many of the native school teachers in travelling to Auckland from remote backblock stations affords an illuminating commentary on the sacrifices that are made by so many teachers. <i>(Jan 5, 1949,Auckland Weekly News,22</i>)

>  The claim of the backblocks settlers were paramount [sic] and it was the duty of the Government to construct roads in the out country. <i>(Aug 21, 1912,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  The drought did not terminate until the back-blocks firm was hopelessly ruined. However, a time came when the back-blocks people arrived to recover their sheet. The frontage squatter sent out to collect and bring in lot No. 1 of his sheet, and from these were drafted out several hundred of back-blocks sheet. <i>(Jul 15, 1875,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

### backblockedness
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a state or quality of living in an isolated rural area.

>  Eketahuna .. is another place name often used to raise a smile, evoking as it does both quintessential rural middle-New Zealandness and the urban notion of the ultimate in backblockedness. <i>(2002,NZWords,VI 4</i>)

### backblocker
 
 <i>n.</i> a person from the backblocks

>  BackZ. blockZ.er (b&abreve.kZ. bl&obreve.kZ.&etilde.r). See under BLOCK. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2017</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> `Why is it that the Back-blockers of today are so much less contented and happy than were the Back-blockers of from 50 to 60 years ago?' <i>(1910,Reminiscences,21</i>) [NOTES: VUWTS]

>  A philanthropic lady ... had given me some useful articles ... for me to dispose of among my poor and needy back-blockers. <i>(Jan 1910,Kai Tiaki,29</i>)

>  Your back-blocker, who hasn't seen a woman for months, often keeps painful control of his tongue until he gets a beer or two in, when out it comes and the usual prosecution follows. <i>(Jan 8, 1910,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> To the back-blocker it means rest. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,26</i>)

>  back-blocker n. resident of the back-blocks. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),2</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> backblocker dweller in remote rural area. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,11</i>)

>  Our New Zealand backblockers are eager to show their love and loyalty to the Empire. <i>(Nov 5, 1914,Poverty Bay Herald</i>) [NOTES: Leading Article]

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> It is one of the back-blocker's pleasures, taking tea in restaurants in town. <i>(1930,Bushman Burke,32</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> Sometimes in the city they were treated like real backblockers <i>(1989,Then Upon the Evil Season,19</i>)

>  The troubles and sorrows of the back-blockers were all too true. <i>(Apr 10, 1919,Stratford Evening Post,3</i>)

>  In the backblocks, we backblockers have to lead a pretty quiet life. No picture shows, or even shops. <i>(Sep 30, 1927,NZ Dairy Exporter,43</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a resident of isolated country or country beyond the river gorges in Canterbury and Otago.

>  A novel method of raising the wind was adopted by a jovial teacher of a small country school in the Waiau district last week .. Needless to say, everybody contributed, and everybody recognised the originality of the young backblockers and their patriarchal dominie. <i>(Feb 23, 1915,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,4</i>)

>  ..I am sure that my town friend with all her interests and gaieties did not realise for one moment what a difference her visit would have made to this lonely backblocker. <i>(Feb 20, 1930,NZ Dairy Exporter,38</i>)

>  Many of the men had been backblockers like himself ... <i>(May 1933,NZ Farmer,367</i>)

>  <b>HOWITT</b> However, the backblocker of backblockers, the first from his district to ever take such a trip, stepped on to the Royal Albert Docks with a score of golden sovereigns in his pocket, a wayfarer indeed. <i>(1945,Pioneer Looks Back, A,52</i>)

>  <b>HOWITT</b> The early back-blocker lived more closely to Nature than any other person in the country. <i>(1945,Pioneer Looks Back, A,187</i>)

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> It was a once in a lifetime treat for many backblockers. [back-blocker] <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,101</i>)

### backblocking
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) living in an isolated rural area.

>  <b>MCDONALD & O'DONNELL</b> .. this being their first experience of backblocking, the difficulties and hardships of their position would make a deeper impression on them. <i>(1929,Te Hekenga,205</i>)

### backblocks
 
 <i>n.</i> the sparsely settled hinterland of more closely-settled districts [aka back blocks, back-blocks] (See [also back-country](../A#also-back-country),[the boohai](../T#the-boohai),[the sticks](../T#the-sticks))

>  Far away in the bush backblocks. <i>(1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,7/266</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> We sleep tonight at the Rangataiki & then the plains & the back blocks. <i>(1907,Letters,1/32</i>) [NOTES: 1984 ed.]

>  <b>GROSSMAN</b> The wildness ... made Evelyn shiver and fasten her marabout closely at the throat. `Now I know what back-blocks are,' she said. <i>(1910,Heart of the Bush,13</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> When I come to write of the outsettlements from Wellington, or as they are now called, the `back blocks'. <i>Ibid.</i> 19 I will now refer to the outsettlements or back-blocks of the early New Zealand days. <i>Ibid.</i> . 29 Neither had we a `township' ... racecourse, newspaper, lawyers and due proportion of loafers in every back block as you appear to have now. <i>(1910,Reminiscences,18</i>) [NOTES: VUWTS]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The back blocks of Southland are not inaccessible places. Until you are stuck up by the great West Coast mountains ... you can get round. <i>(1913,Trade Hunting,12</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Living out in the back-blocks, miles away from any town, a motor-bike is not to be despised. <i>(1927,Gus Tomlins,90</i>) [NOTES: 1977 ed.]

>  <b>PINFOLD</b> But, in the `back blocks' and new places, tracks, through the bush or just formed over clay hills, were mostly what one might expect to find. <i>(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,14</i>)

>  <b>CLOKE</b> Onward then unto the backblocks, Where the sheep are thinly spread. <i>(1931,Songs of NZ,210</i>)

>  Two new-fangled literary expressions, out back and backblocks, are driving back-country out of newspaper use. `The out-back' was, I think, coined by the `Bulletin' writers, and `back- blocks' is from the North Island. <i>(Sep 9, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss.]

>  Tokotoko says she is not going to Kawa. She can't be bothered going to the `backblocks' she says. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/97</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The pioneering father-figure of contemporary New Zealand imagination was a man who went out to the `back-blocks' (a term which originally referred to blocks of land purchased from the Maoris, but came to mean remote farming districts). <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,149</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Although living in the back-blocks, (North Islanders say `in the sticks', presumably referring to bush or scrub) station people seem to encounter more than their fair share of incident. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,100</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> You get a bit old-fashioned out in the backblocks like us here. <i>(1974,Maori Woman,259</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> They wanted my brother to go back and manage the show but he said he'd had enough, way out in the backblocks like that. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,25</i>)

>  <b>ESCOTT</b> But I did all I could to show her that I wasn't trying to push the charmer from the back-blocks any more. <i>(1963,Show Down,12</i>) [NOTES: 1973 ed.]

>  <b>DAVIS</b> I headed for my father's broker's place up the Taihape road. Real backblocks, I can tell you. Plenty of scrub, gorse, and pigs deer. <i>(1964,Watersiders,70</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> The apt cunning of Taihape's hotel-keeper, long inured to back-blocks men's customs.., saw to that. <i>(1926,Butcher Shop, The,86</i>) [NOTES: 1981 ed.]

>  In the backblocks, we backblockers have to lead a pretty quiet life. No picture shows, or even shops. <i>(Sep 30, 1927,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,43</i>)

>  The Colonial Treasurer.. saw that each line of railway that was run out into the 'back blocks' would mark a distinct advance in the colony's affairs. <i>(1906,Life & Work of Seddon,81</i>)

>  <b>WADMAN</b> We lived in the King Country and out on the western slope of the Pirongia range - the backblocks as they were called. <i>(1947,Yearbook of Arts in NZ,3/72</i>)

>  TEACHERS IN THE BACKBLOCKS. <i>(Aug 29, 1913,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  Laurie Cadman is back again in Auckland after a couple of years' stenuous toil in the backblocks. <i>(Apr 4, 1925,NZ Truth,10</i>)

### backblocksy
 
 <i>adj.</i> rough, makeshift; uncouth characteristic of a remote rural area; rough or unpolished in manner or appearance.

>  <b>CHURCH</b> I have been told of your sporting adventure ... It sounds rather backblocksy. <i>(1916,Tonks,271</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> On the downstream side the sloping walls of the dam were well braced with solid struts, called [c1900] back-legs. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,201</i>)

### backblocky
 
 <i>adj.</i> [nf AND, OED], rough, makeshift; uncouth characteristic of a remote rural area; rough or unpolished in manner or appearance.

>  <b>CHURCH</b> I have been told of your sporting adventure ... It sounds rather backblocksy. <i>(1916,Tonks,271</i>)

>  St Petersburg, said Nicholas.. was too "backblocky" to do the thing in style. <i>(1913,NZ Observer,Xmas Annual : 7</i>)

### backbone of the country
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) the principal supporter of the New Zealand dependent economy [an expression subscribed to by rural folk who declare that farmers and farm produce are the principal supporters of the New Zealand economy, i.e. the backbone of the country, keeping all citizens fed, clothed, and housed]. [earlier also backbone of the colony/Dominion]

>  The agricultural interest is the backbone of the country ... <i>(Mar 27, 1880,NZ Herald,4C</i>)

>  And now pardon me if I say a few words to the farmers; for I have always recognised that they are the backbone of the colony. <i>(May 12, 1888,Egmont Star,6</i>)

>  NZ Farmer April 295 I now come to sheep, the backbone of the Dominion. <i>(Apr 1908,NZ Farmer,295</i>)

>  The financier is still doing well. Big interest makes him fat, but he is bleeding the farmer, the backbone of the country ... <i>(Jun 27, 1922,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  The well-known statement that 'farmers are the backbone of the country' is often made. <i>(Sep 26, 1925,NZ Dairy Exporter,33</i>)

>  Yet, dairying is New Zealand's premier identity; 'the backbone of the country' as one of them admitted naively. <i>(Sep 20, 1937,NZ Dairyman,41</i>)

>  The farmer is acknowledged - rather grudgingly by many - as the backbone of the country (and I do not think that he is any more of a perpetual grizzler than any other section of our population.) <i>(Oct 1, 1940,NZ Dairy Exporter,27</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> .. March 2. 4.1. There was a strong belief that farmers were 'the backbone of this country'. <i>(1998,Report</i>) [NOTES: Report: Research report farm families]

>  .. the story of the New Zealand farming family, who were the economic backbone of this country through the 20th century. [backbone of the colony/Dominion] <i>(Nov 8, 2001,Dominion,17</i>)

### backbone of the economy
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) the agricultural sector (See [backbone of the country](../B#backbone-of-the-country))

>  Agriculture, the backbone of our economy, is suffering early-onset osteoporosis. <i>(Feb 7, 2009,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  Manufacturers have accused farmers of becoming the jawbone rather than the backbone of the economy. <i>(Aug 21, 1990,Dominion,2</i>)

>  In this week's Farm View, New Zealand Institute of Agricultural Science president Jacqueline Rowarth argues that agriculture has the record and the direction to show it will continue to be the backbone of our economy. <i>(Nov 4, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  NZ economy still has rural backbone: New buzz words in 1999 overlooked what is still the backbone of New Zealand's economy ... <i>(Dec 27, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,04</i>)

>  Farming in New Zealand is the backbone of our economy - where would be without our farmers? <i>(Apr 20, 2001,Dominion,6</i>)

>  Our single biggest money earner - 16% of export earnings - dairying is the backbone of the economy. Forget about the sheep's back - if the New Zealand flag is redesigned, it should have a cow on it, says BNZ economist, Tony Alexander. <i>(Jun 17, 2001,Sunday Star Times,E1</i>)

### backdoor pensioner
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog past his working days [ implying a deserved and honourable retirement.]

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Backdoor pensioner: A dog past his working days. The term implies an honourable retirement. A dog of similar years but just a 'bit of an old pooch' would be more likely referred to as a pot-licker. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,2</i>)

>  .. among them bonnet-finder .. backdoor pensioner (retired working dog) <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion,C6</i>)

### backdoorman
 
 <i>n.</i> A man who surreptitiously comforts a wife in the husband's absence; a married woman's fancy man. ca1950 p.c. at least: 1987 August (Wellington) `He's always been a bit of a backdoorman.'

### backer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KITTO</b> Take the end [of the line to be the headline] ..; make it fast to the backer or whatever you are going to fasten it to; return to the boat, ... and all is ready to start to lay [12] the line. [It is fastened then to bow of dredge.] <i>Ibid.</i> 34 Go ashore to where the [head-]line is fast to see whether it has drawn the backer. If so, sink another backer hole where you can put the backer in easily. <i>(1900,Practical Dredgeman's Manual,11</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> <i>TRAINING THE BACKING DOG</i> Teaching a dog to back sheep is something for which provision must be made in the puppy's early tuition. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,38</i>)

>  <b>DARE</b> I even managed to make him `back' the sheep, a job a great many dogs will refuse. When sheep are being driven into the woolshed, there is a great tendency for them all to get jammed up and no amount of barking will make them move, and it is at this moment that a `backing dog' is needed. <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,165</i>)

>  You wouldn't notice Dan, the yellow backing dog until the mob got boxed at a gate way ... He'd be up over their backs barking. <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,126,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>Backing and Yard Dog</i>: Usually this is a `huntaway' or `handy dog' trained to run over the backs of tightly packed sheep and to walk back through a mob in the yards to keep them moving ahead. It is a useful dog for loading and unloading sheep. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/493</i>)

>  <b>LETHBRIDGE</b> Instead of climbing out of the the yard and along the rails, a backing dog would jump up on top of the sheep and run along their [121] backs pushing the front one right up into the head of the pen. <i>(1971,Sunrise on the Hills,120</i>)

>  <i>Backing dog</i>: Dog that will jump on to the backs of sheep to help move them. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,24</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a dog

>  <b>LILICO</b> A bitch pup I sold ... went direct to freezing works to pen up and developed into a first class forcer and backer ... The finest backing dogs I have ever seen are in the saleyards at Addington. <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,26</i>)

### backing
 
 <i>n.</i> (tree-felling) (See [cf. back-cut](../B#back-cut),[back up](../B#back-up))

>  <b>CRUMP</b> During the next few weeks Jack learned about scarfing, backing, limbing, deeing, sniping, [etc] <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,42</i>)

### backing chain
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a chain used to keep cows in position during milking.

>  I made a rule then that if I couldn't milk without a backing chain or leg rope, I didn't want them. <i>(Sep 20, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,Farming Supplement 7</i>)

### backing dog
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog used to clear congestion of sheep in a gateway, race or into a pen by running along the backs of sheep; a dog trained to help move yarded sheep by running across their backs. (See [back v](../B#back-v))

>  I had this feeling he'd make a good backing dog, and I was right. He just loves running around on the sheep. <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,237</i>)

>  I am led to discourse on 'backing dogs' this month ... <i>(Feb 1, 1930,NZ Farmer,172</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> .. it will depend upon the amount of grit and spirit a dog has, whether he proves a great backing dog or just an ordinary one. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,39</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> Backing dogs should not be used on lambs, especially a mob from which the owner expects to get a draft of "fats" away. There are also certain breeds of sheep upon which backing dogs should not be used, notably Cheviots and Perendales. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,73</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> All three of these great backing dogs were out of Arthur's old Smithfield bitch. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,13</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> a dog backs when it leaps up and runs across on top of sheep (wool-walking). Hence a backing dog (wool walker). <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,2</i>)

>  Backing dog - a useful type of huntaway, trained to scamper along sheep's backs in jammed races and loading ramps to unplug bottlenecks. <i>(Aug 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,10</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> I had two great backing dogs, Jim and Duke, ideal for yard work. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,72</i>)

### backing gate
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a gate that closes close behind stock and does not allow movement backwards.

>  The backing gate is used to bring cows from the yard into the dairy. Its main purpose is to keep stock near the milking area. As each cow is taken in to be milked, another fills up the space it has left and there are no delays in milking. The gate is controlled by the milker, who can bring in the next cows without having to leave those already being milked. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,83</i>)

>  .. the race includes the special backing gate. <i>(Jul 22, 1971,NZ Farmer,6</i>)

>  The main item of this equipment is a backing gate or, rather, a barrier which allows only one-way traffic in the race. <i>(1971,NZ Farmer,41</i>)

>  The backing gate is operated by depressing a floor bar with the foot. <i>(Nov 13, 1975,NZ Farmer,17</i>)

>  The herringbone milking shed and circular yard with electrically-operated backing gate have increased output/milker. <i>(1977,NZ Dairy Industry Dairy Board,41</i>)

>  A high-pressure water system through nozzles along the length of the bottom of the backing gate is another method. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,69</i>)

>  Use the backing gate to tighten up the cows when about halfway down the pit. <i>(Oct 1999,NZ Dairy Exporter,26</i>)

### backline
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of systemic animal sprays] administered on the backs of stock.

>  Backline (spray-on) formulations are becoming increasingly popular for the prevention of flystrike and control of lice in sheep. <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,Oct/Nov  46</i>)

### backloading
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>JONES</b> We had to order a year's supply of provisions, etc., which came up as backloading when the wool was sent down <i>(1933,Autobiography of an Early Settler,52</i>)

>  <b>SUMMERS</b> `When Nanna ... first came up here [to the isolated sheep station] they only had backloading [to bring in stores], so they needed a storehouse this size.' `Backloading?' ... `Yes - once a year. The wagons that came in to take the wool out brought in the stores.' <i>(1960,Moon Over the Alps,34,1974 ed.</i>)

### backlog
 
 <i>n.</i> Orig. a large log placed at the back of an open fire; thence, any sizeable log for an open fire.

>  <b>EASTWOOD</b> At the back of the fire was always an unsplit log, we called a backlog. <i>(1982,Brief History of Gold Mining, A,107</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> Having thus spoken, he ... discharged a liquid shot [a tobacco spit] at an aggravating knot in the back-log. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/vi/93,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>TWISLETON</b> The back-log's yellow light. <i>(1895,Poems (various authors),28</i>)

>  He cut up a lot of maire backlogs. <i>(1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,4/596</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> As a firewood its badness is almost incredible, and truly none but the newest of chums would dream of using it when camped in the forest. `Bucket-of-water wood', it has been termed; and the rather tall story goes how a trunk, which had been used for a back log to a fire for a whole year, upon being finally cast into the open air as worthless, put forth green shoots, and grew again into a tree! <i>(1910,NZ Plants & Their Story,152</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> `I'm used to a fire, not a couple of small sticks crossed together smoking.' .. I was forced to go out into the wet again and cut off a couple of big back-logs <i>(1927,Gus Tomlins,174,1977 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> A good rata back-log ... [i.e. to hold the fire in] <i>(1945,Camping and Bushcraft in NZ,18</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> The log being drawn by bullocks on the other side right into the chimney for a back log. <i>(1937,Thomas,83</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Rata for heat and backlog. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,60</i>)

### backpackers
 
 <i>n.</i> a backpackers' hostel

>  Sometimes, in the most inoffensive backpackers in the most beautiful islands, at three in the morning, we are struck by a sudden, uninvited realisation of the absurdity of being there. <i>(Feb 14, 1999,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  Now owned by Massey Univertsity and used as student accommodation for most of the year and as a backpackers during summer holidays, the landmark 74 year-old building is on the market. <i>(Jun 17, 2004,Wellingtonian,52</i>)

>  Many backpackers offer their own kind of themes and attractions which are as much of a drawcard as the towns and cities they sit within. <i>(Aug 17, 2000,City Voice,9</i>)

### backrunner
 
 <i>n.</i> ? a horse which prefers to make its run from the back of the field

>  While he was well aware that many a derby backrunner has had its chances extinguished by weakening horses, he was a great believer in not changing racing styles. <i>(Dec 23, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B12</i>)

### backward
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of crops or grass] retarded or late.

>  Pastures are backward, but there is still more growth than there was last month. <i>(Jan 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,71</i>)

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> It [nitrate of soda] may be used to stimulate a backward crop. <i>(1961,Agriculture,209</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of farmland] undeveloped.

>  <b>MILLS</b> A company director had purchased a backward farm for his son. <i>(1966,Huntaway,187</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sale stock] poor in condition

>  The cows were mostly in backward condition and did not realise high prices. <i>(Aug 4, 1900,Hawera Star,4</i>)

>  All cows and heifers near profit caused keen competition whilst backward and doubtful calves were hard to quit. <i>(Aug 7, 1900,Hawera Star,03</i>)

>  There was a good attendance, but prices were on the low side, especially for furniture and cows, several of the latter being aged and others having the appearance of being backward calvers. <i>(Aug 25, 1900,Hawera Star,04</i>)

### backwash
 
 <i>v.</i> to reverse the flow of water in a pumping system. [The Macquarie sense was used by our contractor installing a pool in 1990, and subsequently by us. I have also heard it in relation to public water systems. <i>Backflush</i> is also used in the context of public water systems, with the same meaning. This may be international English but I have no evidence of this. It does not appear in OED(BJC)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sidestream or sidepool away from the current in a braided river.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> At one bend in the river, the men happened on top of a dozen or so in a backwash. <i>(1949,High Country Days,34</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Sometimes they spent the whole day in a backwash underneath the bank. <i>(1966,Over the River,119</i>)

### backyard
 
 <i>adj.</i> (often unlicensed) conducted from home [ca1946 p.c. Weir House, Kelburn The Hendies were running backyard car sales.]

 <i>n.</i> the police. [1984 16-17 F E11 Pakuranga Coll. 24C BACON [F3M5]]

### bacon bond
 
 <i>n.</i> Kiwi savings stock issue (KISS).

>  The Kiwi stock issue, dubbed `bacon Bonds' by some investment quarters, came on sale on March 21. <i>(May 10, 1983,Dominion,10</i>)

>  Redemptions of the [inflation adjusted] bonds are outstripping new applications and money is being diverted into the Kiwi [savings] stock, nick-named `bacon bonds'. <i>(May 10, 1983,Dominion,1</i>)

### baconer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I have a fair sized pig farm here, and turned off 1000 baconers last season. <i>(Jul 23, 1927,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,67</i>)

### bad doer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm animal that does not convert feed to meat or milk readily or does not perform well.

>  As a rule, too, they are narrow in the chest, and unthrifty, bad doers. <i>(Jan 1, 1877,NZ Country Journal,I i / 30</i>)

>  Cull out bad doers, sheep with any deformity ... <i>(1919,Livestock in New Zealand,17</i>)

>  It is said that the farmer ought only to buy a horse that is a good 'doer' - that is to say a horse that does justice to his food. If a horse, instead of putting on muscle under wholesome treatment puts on fat, that horse is not a good, but a 'bad doer.' <i>(Mar 1931,NZ Farmer,236</i>)

>  A sick sheep or cow - a bad doer. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### bad doing
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of livestock] unthrifty.

>  .. it is necessary that there should be plenty of grass to flush the milk, or troubles may be experienced through desertion and bad-doing lambs. [bad-doing] <i>(1919,Livestock in New Zealand,46</i>)

### bad French
 
 <i>phr.</i> bad language, swearing.

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The enraged headsman spares no `bad French' in explaining his motives. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/327</i>)

### bad tupping weather
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) extreme weather during tupping that causes rams to lose interest in their task.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Shepherds speak of good tupping weather, but more often of bad tupping weather, when, because of extreme heat or heavy rain, the rams lose interest and many heat periods pass neglected. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,37</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [patiki](../P#patiki))

### badin
 
 <i>adv.</i> soon

>  See you badin. <i>(Dec 21, 1942,NZEF Times,II/78 : 2</i>)

>  In the sweet badin and boukhra, they will. <i>(Jun 28, 1943,NZEF Times,III/105 : 7</i>)

>  Badin. The method by which all urgent affairs in this country are arranged. <i>(Jan 24, 1944,NZEF Times,III/135 : 11</i>)

>  I want to know / What sort of job I'm going to get in the sweet badin and boukhra. <i>(Jun 5, 1944,NZEF Times,III/154 : 4</i>)

### badland gully
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a narrow gouged area within a larger erosion-prone area.

### bag
 
 <i>n.</i> Usu. pl. esp. in hop the bags, over the bags, a military sandbag. In the phrases hop the bags, cf. top-hopping [check cits here]; over the bags, cf. top.

>  There's many a good New Zealand lad .. Who has learnt love's lesson while things looked bad, from girls with faces fair, Toasted their health while he vowed to fight, in the good old digger way, And then `hopped the bags' with the photo fair of the girl in the estaminet. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,69</i>)

>  The boys went over the bags at 3.15 a.m. <i>(1989,Behind the Lines,76</i>) [NOTES: 1917 in 1989 Boyack Behind the Lines 76]

>  <b>TREADWELL</b> `Did not this man go over the top?' I asked... It was a rule of our battalion that any man awaiting trial ... should go `over the bags' at zero hour, ... <i>(1936,Recollections of an Amateur Soldier,201</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> Three times I had been over the bags. Three times the blood of mates had spattered my khaki, seeped into my being. <i>(1976,Soldier,1</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> blame, attack

>  I"m not going to sit here and bag the school because I think it's done a fine job. <i>(Sep 10, 2004,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Nine to Noon - Cambridge School Boadr Member]

>  Whitemans Valley dairy farmer Phil Kidd didn't hold back when a lifestyler neighbour told him he was mistreating his cows. <i>(Sep 9, 2004,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  The worst thing is that I've been bagging it for the last two days and now it's in my brain... I've also been bagging the yawnfest that's Beatlemania and now I know how the song goes. <i>(Nov 26, 1995,Sunday Star Times,D9</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to inspect the udders of ewes.

>  We did this last weaning by running the ewes through the race and judging their bags visually, but do not think we made a first-rate job. Next time we will manually bag them. <i>(Jun 11, 1981,NZ Farmer,40</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> of a horse, to be certain to lose

>  The neddy was in the bag in the Cup; he was no trier. <i>(1900,Colonial Turf,33 ?or 3</i>) [NOTES: [check page no: OED 33]]



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  It said that if the driver got a special work licence (which he was granted yesterday), it would put him `on the bag' for two months, which means selling tickets from the side of the road. <i>(Nov 9, 1983,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  The driver got his special work licence on Tuesday and the department said he would go `on the bag.'.. The driver took some annual leave and did `on the bag' work. <i>(Nov 11, 1983,Evening Post,1</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i>

>  LENNY [a traffic cop]: Should we put the bag on him. HOUND: Why? LENNY: Well, he reckons he's drunk. <i>(Nov 22, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)



 
<b>6</b> <i>n.</i> usu. neg. constr.; choice, preference

>  <i>bag noun</i> ... 3.b),choice or preference: Horseriding is not my <i>bag. </i> <i>(1979,Heinemann Dictionary of NZ Quotations,72</i>)



 
<b>8</b> <i>n.</i> a hole dug under the grain-outlet of a threshing-machine to facilitate the filling, ramming, and sewing up of the grainsacks; bag-rammer, bag-sewer. (See [also baggie](../A#also-baggie))

>  <b>KERSE</b> c1900] Sheets were placed under the [threshing] mill to catch any seed that leaked out and the bag hole men would dig out the bag hole so that the bags could be rammed full. ... The usual gang for stack threshing [c1900] was 12 men. The boss kept steam up and attended to the mill adjustments and lubrication, three men worked in the bag hole, the bag-rammer, the bag-sewer, and the bag-carrier; two men were on top of the mill, a band cutter and a man to turn the sheaves to the band cutter. <i>(1984,Knapdale Run, The</i>)

### bag bag
 
 <i>n.</i> a bag made from bags

>  Armed with little more than a pair of knitting needles, a few shopping bags and a dozen empty hours, Jeanette painstakingly combines different coloured plastics to create the ultimate in recycled fashion - the supermarket bag bag. <i>(Aug 12, 2000,Evening Post,20</i>)

### bag moth
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GILLESPIE</b> The ones attracting most attention were the owlet moths, the caterpillars of which destroyed the first pastures and cereal and root crops. There were also porina moths (<i>Oxycanus spp.</i>) whose caterpillars also destroyed pastures; the bag moth (<i>Oeceticus omnivorus</i>) which left cigar-shaped cocoons hanging from shrubs and tree [sic]; and the ghost-like white moth ... of the flax grub (<i>Orthoclydon praefectata</i>) which eats longitudinal slits on the surface of [flax] leaves ... <i>(1958,South Canterbury,10,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Moth, bag .. .. kapa, pu a Raukatauri, whare atua .. .. <i>Oeceticus omnivorous </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

### bag up
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sp.use: shearing prior to wool pressing] to place fleeces in sacks.

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Went on clipping the rest out in the yard, could not bag the wool up as it was too wet. <i>(Jun 1, 1910,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Bagged up the wool and covered it over with tarpaulin. <i>(Jun 3, 1910,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [breeding stock] to make milk.

>  .. a single injection of the drug given about six weeks before a cow's due calving date would cause her to 'bag up' within a few days after treatment. <i>(Jul 22, 1971,NZ Farmer,25</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Once they start to 'bag up,' (make milk) they come into foaling paddocks beside the house ... <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,164</i>)

>  When most cows have stopped bagging up, start increasing feed levels to put on condition. <i>(Oct 20, 2001,RD1.com,Milk Quality Expert</i>)

### bag-in-a-box
 
 <i>n.</i> container for cask wine

>  ...news that [Cottesbrook] would again be selling wine for bag-in-a-box was not surprising after Cottesbrook's response last year. <i>(Jul 3, 2004,Dominion Post,A10</i>)

### bag-in-box
 
 <i>n.</i> cardboard cask wine

>  If gold-medal status signifies world-class standards, then only one in every 1.7 million bottles of New Zealand sauvignon blanc is world class, which might explain why it is appearing in bag-in-box in Britain. <i>(Dec 13, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

### bag-rammer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a threshing-mill team member who presses filled bags to capacity.

>  <b>MILLER</b> Beyond the Blue Mountains 112 .. the bag-rammer would look up and say 'Dugald's got it now'. <i>(1954,Beyond the Blue Mountains,112</i>)

>  <b>KERSE</b> .. three men worked in the bag hole, the bag-rammer, the bag-sewer, and the bag-carrier <i>(1984,Knapdale Run, The,148</i>)

### bag-sewer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a threshing-mill team member who sews bags filled with grain.

>  <b>EVANS</b> The bag sewer then sewed across the top of the bag with twine. <i>(1975,Silver Tussock, The,231</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> baggie - the bag-sewer of a threshing-mill team. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,4</i>)

>  <b>KERSE</b> .. three men worked in the bag hole, the bag-rammer, the bag-sewer, and the bag carrier. <i>(1984,Knapdale Run, The,148</i>)

### Bagel
 
 <i>n.</i> name suggested by Angus Morrison for the new Wellington stadium. (See [Cake Tin](../C#cake-tin))

>  There's been plenty of support for the Tank or the Colosseum. My favourite., though, is the Bagel. It's round, with a hole in the middle, and took a lot of dough to make. <i>(Jul 9, 1999,Evening Post,26</i>)

### baggage master
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "I played rugby for a long time," says the baggage master and masseur for the Hurricanes and the All Blacks... "You have to be passionate about it because you're not just a baggage master or a masseur. You're a friend, a mate, a dad, a psychologist." <i>(Jun 5, 2002,Capital Times,18</i>)

### bagged up
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of breeding stock] beginning to produce milk.

>  If the keratin plug becomes dislodged pre calving - shown in the "bagged up" heifers and cows dripping milk pre-calving - these teats are now exposed again. <i>(Oct 1999,NZ Dairy Exporter,51</i>)

### baggie
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a member of a thrashing-mill or header-harvester gang who sews up the filled grain-sacks.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> At the grain chutes perspiring "baggies" toiled with needle and twine, carrying the sewn bags to a growing pile. <i>(1971,Our National Game,63</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The grain emerged from one corner of the mill into sacks. These were stitched up by the `baggies', using, as with the wool packs, large curved needles threaded with twine. <i>(1985,Coldstream,128</i>)

>  <b>WALLIS</b> Not much room [on the header] for the baggie, is there? How many bags does the chute hold? <i>(1963,Point of Origin,80</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a threshing-mill team bag-sewer or bag-handler.

>  <b>EARLE</b> One man drives the machine, one sews the bags, and the 'baggie' unloads the full bags and puts on fresh ones. <i>(1965,Mixed Farm, A,29</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Baggies to one wheel, forkies to the other and walloper hand out the spikes. <i>(1976,Water Joey,24</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> baggie: the bag-sewer of a threshing-mill team <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a larger amount of cannabis than a tinnie, sold in a plastic bag

### bagging
 
 <i>n.</i> a reprimand, a telling-off

>  Bagging is an expression used for strongly criticising someone, especially by a group directing disapproval at a single victim ..it is thought to have come from Australia. <i>(Jan 19, 2005,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  "I just have to forget about it [the coach's bagging] and get on with it [performing well]." <i>Ibid.</i> B6 [Hdg] Coaches' bagging fails to inspire players. <i>(Aug 10, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B5</i>) [NOTES: NB: Square brackets in original citation.]

### bagging up
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the early producing of milk in cows or ewes.

>  Obvious signs of hyper-oestrogenicity [reddened and swollen vulvas, 'bagging up'] may be seen. <i>(Oct 20, 2001,RD1.com,Pastures Expert</i>)

### baggy
 
 <i>n.</i> a worker in threshing procedure (See [baggie](../B#baggie))

>  'A good deal was said in Court on Tuesday during the hearing of a case about threshing-machines, and no little amusement was caused by a list given by one witness of the 'technical' names of titles of some of the workmen employed about them. 'Chaffy' and 'baggy' raised a smile merely, but 'straw walloper' tickled everybody except the witness, who glanced around with an air of pity on the laughing faces of those who were ignorant of the designation of the man who attends to the straw elevator. Some machines, it appears, boast of two 'straw wallopers', others are content with one'. <i>(Sep 6, 1884,Otago Witness,18</i>) [NOTES: Otago Witness quotes Timaru Herald 3 Sept 1884 p 2.]



 
 <i>n.</i> an army recruit

>  <b>HUGHES</b> I was an Officer Cadet - a grand sounding title, but a cadet is generally acknowledged to be one rank lower than the Regimental cat. Worse yet, I was a baggy on basic. <i>Ibid.</i> 18 As new recruits beginning basic training (baggy-arse trousers gave us our nickname), we comprehended almost none of the myriad abbreviations, acronyms or slang. <i>(1999,Shooting from the Lip,15</i>)

### bagman
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [baggie](../B#baggie))

>  <b>CARR</b> The mills carry their own forkers, band-cutter, weigher and bagman (one man), and sewer and stacker. <i>(1913,Country Work,13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [swagger](../S#swagger))

>  <b>MEEK</b> We rarely heard a bagman's tale- the saddle made him sore, <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,10</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> The old Acheron woolshed was a great gathering place for these old bagmen ... One of the most noted of these old swaggers was Spud Murphy. <i>(1945,Shining with the Shiner</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> in parliamentary context, one who counts numbers of supporters and solicits support for an election bid on behalf of the candidate, known or unbeknown to the candidate

>  Mr English is accordingly able to succeed to the leadership without visible blood on his hands. His bagmen collected the numbers, reached a compelling majority and this was conveyed to Mrs Shipley. <i>(Oct 9, 2001,Dominion,10</i>) [NOTES: Not in OED or Green in this context]

>  Instead Lockwood Smith and David Carter - Dr Brash's bagmen - were sounding out MPs by telephone - the first all-telephonic coup in the history of the party. <i>(Oct 30, 2003,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  [Hdg] Kiwi bagmen not too down and out. Journalists reporting on the National Party leadership scuffle swooped on the oddly Kiwi word "bagmen" to refer to the candidates' chief lobbyists in caucus... Elsewhere a bagman can be lot of things, all pretty well removed from getting in the vote for Bill or Don. In Oz it's an alternative to swagman. Elsewhere, it is a commercial traveller. Elsewhere again, and more sinisterly, it is one who collects racketeers' money. In a list of Post Office workers, it appears with porters. The <i>New Oxford Dictionary of New Zealand English</i> reckons it's a man who helps fill sacks of grain. The nearest to our special meaning is a Canadian reference to "political fundraiser". <i>(Nov 5, 2003,Dominion Post,B9</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a harvesting gang member who is in charge of a harvest once it is bagged.

>  The number of hands to be employed on each [threshing] mill is as follows:- driver, feeder, three stackmen, two bagmen, one strawman, one waterman and in camp one cook. <i>(1910,NZ Farmer,559</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> One season I'd be bagman. The bagman weighed the bag and kept the tallies and helped the contractor to make up the men's times at the end of the season. <i>(1976,Water Joey,98</i>)

### bags
 
 <i>v.</i> claim for oneself (bags not: not for me) [cf. ODE bags/bags I]

>  I hear a united "bags not" from all the women of New Zealand even in the face of the most attractive financial remuneration. <i>(Dec 8, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A12</i>)

>  We children slept in bunks and I gleefully bagses the top bunk, <i>(Mar 4, 2002,Evening Post,15</i>)

### Bagush Box
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>JACKSON</b> Baguish Box, as it is appropriately called, is a flat coastal plain bordered by an escarpment, and resembles the ground floor of an outsize packing-case. <i>(1943,Passage to Tobruk,104</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> The Box ... that outpost of Egyptian empire fully a hundred and fifty miles from Alex. The first batch of Kiwis went there in the summer of '40 to do a spot of digging. I believe they were bombed once. Anyway, they strung a few strands of barbed wire around their diggings and called it the Bagush Box. They proceeded ... to wait for Graziani ... who never got that far. <i>(1944,Troop Target,56</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> 4 and 6 Brigades back in Baagush box. Since Bel Hamed and Sidi Rezegh he wasn't sure whether to be proud of being a New Zealander. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives</i>)

### Bahamas
 
 <i>n.</i> solitary confinement cell

>  Other terms for places and procedures include.. <i>Bahamas</i> for the <i>pound</i> (solitary confinement cell). <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 1</i>)

### bail
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  The boar actually bails the young dog. The dog doesn't know what's going on, and he starts to bail back, in defence. Within a few hours bailing becomes part of its makeup. <i>(Feb 1998,Mana,No. 20: 36</i>)

>  The dogs got on the scent of a large boar, that did not "bail" until it reached the thick undergrowth. <i>(Oct 5, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,47</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The byre itself is a new-fangled invention and I think it is the first of its kind in Edendale... It holds 16 cows at one bailing, and has double bails on each side. <i>(Oct 23, 1912,Otago Witness,78</i>)

>  All the animals looked so intelligent as they went into the stalls which were empty and put their heads between the bails. <i>(Nov 23, 1903,Otago Witness,74</i>)

>  <b>COWLEY</b> She was alone, chained into a bail in an empty yard still awash from the afternoon milking. <i>(1979,Growing Season, The,2</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> A heifer being taught to go into the milking-bail usually has a rope first passed over her horns by means of a long stick, so.. she can be drawn into the position required, where she is secured by fastening the bails, and then, her leg being tied, she can be milked safely. This being repeated a few times with each cow, is generally sufficient to teach them to walk in of themselves afterwards when driven up to the bail. <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,170</i>)

>  When the last of the cows are in the bails I gather up the jug of milk and set sail for home. <i>(Jun 30, 1944,Te Awamutu Courier,4</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> A strong bail was made at the end of the race to secure his head. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,50</i>)

>  The same cows usually came into the bails first, a chain was fastened round the rear end and the back leg nearest the milker was pulled back and fastened with a leg rope. <i>(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/11</i>)

>  The cows were all in the bails, teats washed, ready for the machines. <i>(May 28, 1927,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,52</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> A strong bail was made at the end of the race to secure his head. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,50</i>)

>  He spends an incredible number of hours in a draughty shed that he refers to as the "bails."... Several hours a day must be spent in the cow bails and in mustering the sheep. <i>(Feb 5, 1924,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Instead of going into a bail, as she usually did, she made for my tin of milk. <i>(1975,Follow the Call,10</i>) [NOTES: c1924]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a contained animal.

>  <b>BEACH</b> Reaching the bail they had him in a narrow creek with six foot high sides. <i>(1993,Over 30 Years Pig Hunting,89</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) containment of a feral animal by dogs.

>  <b>BEACH</b> We could tell it was a good pig by the commotion on the way to the bail. When we got about 25 yards away, the other blokes elected Russell to go on to the bail on his own. <i>(1993,Over 30 Years Pig Hunting,11</i>)

>  <b>BEACH</b> As I hurried down the face towards the bail, I heard one of the dogs let out a scream and I knew what caused that. <i>(1993,Over 30 Years Pig Hunting,89</i>)

### bail up
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [ bailers or pig dogs] to corner and hold wild pigs.

>  <b>JOHNSTONE-SMITH</b> Bailers are dogs which can bail up a wild pig. <i>(1975,Battles, Buggies and Babies,41</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> A Captain Cooker (a pig) would soon be bailed up, and father would have his gun and knife ready. <i>(1975,Silver Tussock, The,61</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> When bailed up in a corner by dogs, a big tusker would attack men, horses and dogs ... <i>(1978,Mimiwhangata Station,60</i>)

>  <b>WATERSON</b> They [dogs] were also used to finding and bailing up wild pigs. <i>(1999,Some Buggers I've Seen,13</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [unco-operative stock] to resist dog handling, to move into a corner where they can not be dislodged.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> A beast such as a wild pig, in adopting a position - as often as not inescapably so - to fight on the defensive, bails up. It has also been bailed up by its aggressor. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,(ii)</i>)

>  A dog may be excused for biting a sheep's head if the sheep charges it or bails up and refuses to move ... <i>(1991,Constitution of NZ Dog Trial Assoc,28</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  When the milker has bailed-up and leg-roped the cow.. he should.. wash the cow's udder and teats and his hands. <i>(1895,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,III/88</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> A shorthaired collie.. superb at bailing up wild cattle or Captain Cook pigs, plump from hinau berries. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,133</i>)

>  The Maoris were somewhere about ready to "bail us up." <i>(Aug 4, 1883,Auckland Weekly News,6</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> I had just got one tin full when I went out to bail up my quietest cow. <i>(1975,Follow the Call,10</i>)

>  Intent on following the sounds of the dogs which frequently bailed up the pig, the party lost its bearings. <i>(Jul 20, 1939,Te Aroha Times,4</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  A well-known barrister and solicitor.. was immediately "bailed up" by a couple of student policemen.. who charged him with being Powelka. <i>(Jul 2, 1910,NZ Free Lance,12</i>)

>  <b>O'Sullivan</b> She remembered enough of how young men thought to know the last thing he would want was to have some old bat bailing him up. <i>(2006,Pictures by Goya,43</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [dairy cows] to move or be moved into a bail for milking or inspection.

>  Provision is made for bailing up twenty-two cows. <i>(Apr 1908,NZ Farmer,291</i>)

>  Many farmers train the bull when young to 'bail up' like the cows. <i>(Dec 20, 1928,NZ Journal of Agriculture,414</i>)

>  His treatment was to confine the victim to the hospital paddock, away from the other cattle. When treating her, of course, she had to be bailed up ... <i>(Jun 9, 1934,Hawera Star,7</i>)

>  .. they are brought into the shed, bailed up, washed , stimulated, and milked in the same way each day. <i>(Oct 15, 1962,NZ Journal of Agriculture,306</i>)

### bail-out
 
 <i>n.</i> falling off a bike

>  "Falling off is nature's way of telling you you've found your limit," is a philosophical definition of a very pertinent problem to readers of <i>Classic New Zealand Mountain Bike Rides</i>..." Other expressions for the same event, listed in the glossary, are <i>airborne</i> and <i>bail-out. <i>(Jan 15, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

### bail-up
 
 <i>n.</i> a bailing-up of a cow

>  <b>SHARPE</b> It looked to me as if the bail-up [of cattle] was about ten chains in from the forest fringe. <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,57</i>)

### bailed
 
 <i>adj.</i> with a number, describing the size of a cow-shed, measured by the number of `bails' it contains.

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Two-roomed house and four-bailed cowshed on property. Will milk twenty cows. <i>(1924,Follow the Call,3,1975 ed.</i>)

### bailer
 
 <i>n.</i> a pig dog trained to bail wild pigs (See [cf. holder](../H#holder))

>  Pig-dog types are many ... The writer's [J.P.Lewin's] considered classification is straight finders, straight bailers, straight holders and straight killers... Inside it we have finder-bailers; bailer-holders; finder-bailer-holders; bailer-holder-killers and holder-killers. <i>(Dec 1951,Here & Now,15</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> All our sheep dogs had a go at Kooti at some time, but they were only bailers and far too cunning to go within range of Kooti's tusks. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,201</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Pigdogs are classified into three groups: finders, bailers, and holders. The name of each type indicates perfectly what they are expected to do. <i>(1982,Wild Pig,156</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> But a finder and bailer like Rusty was usually a much lighter animal and blessed with a few more brains. <i>(1984,Razorback,17</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pig dog trained to bail a wild pig until a hunter arrives.

>  <b>JOHNSTONE-SMITH</b> Bailers are dogs which can bail up a wild pig. Certain dogs being good at finding and having 'found' would give the signal when the bailers would join in, so confusing and worrying the pig that it would stand at bay. <i>(1975,Battles, Buggies and Babies,41</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Bailer: a pig dog trained to - or does so naturally - force a wild pig to stand at bay until the hunter arrives. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,9</i>)

>  <b>AXBEY</b> We were told he wouldn't hold but was a real 'hard' bailer. <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,54</i>)

### bailey
 
 <i>n.</i> a unit measure of wealth

### bailing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The byre itself is a new-fangled invention and I think it is the first of its kind in Edendale... It holds 16 cows at one bailing, and has double bails on each side. <i>(Oct 23, 1912,Otago Witness,78</i>)

### bailing dog
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bailer](../B#bailer))

>  <b>CARR</b> Now this is where I made a big mistake. Mine were holding dogs and, when asking Tony whether his dogs would help I was thinking along the lines of holding something like an ear, a leg, or a snout. Tony, however, had only bailing dogs with him; his catching dog, Rock, was tied up at the cowshed and in saying, "Oh sure", he was meaning his dogs would dive down and keep the mob bailed up. <i>(1986,Diary of a Pig Hunter,19</i>)

### bailing up
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bail up](../B#bail-up))



 
 <i>v.</i> (See [bail up](../B#bail-up))

### bailing-up
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> [The bush rangers'] mode of proceeding was something after this fashion: on arriving they collected all the inmates of the house .. and placed a sentry with a loaded gun over them, with the instructions to shoot, without compunction, any recreant individual who dared to stir `a --- inch', in the delicate language employed. This was termed `baling up'. <i>(1881,Poenamo,1/3/42</i>)

>  <b>ROCK</b> The process of `bailing up' consisted generally in tying the victim to a tree, robbing him of all his valuables, and leaving him to take his chance of being rescued. <i>(1888,Colonists,98</i>)

>  Our Outram correspondent.. supplied the following information with reference to the "bailing-up" of a station in the Strath-Taieri district. <i>(Dec 18, 1890,Otago Witness,16</i>)

### bait
 
 <i>v.</i> (informal) take whitebait, take whitebait from

>  Long-time local Kit Green claims to be one of the first people to bait the Okarito Lagoon in the 1950s. <i>(Oct 10, 2004,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,10</i>)

>  Did I really bait barefoot or in jandals? ... [64] However I never baited until I was 23 and had transferred to the West Coast as a senior postie. <i>(Sep 2005,Air New Zealand inflight magazine,?</i>) [NOTES: -]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HULME</b> One thing everybody does know about the Coast is bait. Whitebait. That succulent little fish, quick and lucent, likened in an old haiku to the `spirit of the waters'... When it's a good season, tons are lovingly packed into freezers against the lean, non-bait portion of the year. <i>Ibid.</i> 38 Take half a pound of bait per person, add an egg (two eggs, if you really like them), stir vigorously until fish and egg is a viscous froth full of strange little eyes. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,37</i>)

>  On a good day, when the bait was really running, a halt would be called when more than one kerosene tin was full. <i>(Nov 6, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),131</i>)

>  Watch the resident 'baiter dragging in one and two pint hauls with each scoop when the 'bait were running truly good. <i>(Jan 6, 1993,NZ Tablet,9</i>)

### bait and switch
 
 <i>phr.</i> US definition : an illegal tactic in which a seller advertises a product with the intention of persuading customers to purchase a more expensive product. [http://www.thedenverchannel.com/]

>  The ruling sent a strong message about "bait-and-switch" advertising. "It confirms that companies are not able to make bald claims and then modify them or in some way negate them in the small print." <i>(May 22, 2001,Dominion,8</i>)

### bait layer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bait layer</i> cook in army; originally station cook. NZA <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,12</i>)

### bait line
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAWKE & SCOTT</b> These furrows have been made by the rabbiters, they are the bait lines. <i>(1985,Early Farm Machinery in NZ,27</i>)

### bait station
 
 <i>n.</i> a tube or other device to hold poisoned bait to eradicate pest animals such as possums, rats, etc, without endangering birds and other Good Things. [Jim Cameron writes: Not in OED, DNZE, NSOD or Collins. Frequent use in books, articles etc relating to conservation.]

>  Work started this year, with the first of what will be 130 kilometres of tracks built, 1000 bait stations erected and 2000 traps used to kill more than 5000 rats each year, with 500 traps for stoats and ferrets. <i>(Dec 13, 2001,Dominion,8</i>)

>  Bait stations, requiring people on foot rather than aerial poison drops, are used to kill possums and stoats, and regular monitoring must be carried out. <i>(Apr 2000,North & South,86</i>)

>  Please contact Greater Wellington's Upper Hutt depot if you would like a bait station or a trap on your property... <i>(Nov 10, 2004,Sunday Star Times,4</i>)

>  Another product manufactured in Palmerston North..is KBL's possum bait station... The company's fourth product is a rat bait box, which sits on the ground next to the wall of a building. <i>(Oct 26, 2001,Dominion,22</i>)

>  <b>RALSTON ET AL</b> Localised control is attempted in some areas by use of bait stations. <i>(1996,Mountains of East Asia and the Pacific,191</i>)

>  Bait stations with non-toxic bait will attract possums and give a better indication of numbers. Landowners really serious about controlling TB often set a large number of bait stations. <i>(Aug 22, 1996,NZ Farmer,19</i>)

>  Private land owners cannot be expected to handle the 1080 drops, but they do have the opportunity to use ground based bait stations and traps for controlling noxious animals. <i>(Oct 1997,Growing Today,53</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> The work is ongoing with traps and bait stations and constant monitoring of the systems in place. <i>(1996,Hunting for DOC,27</i>)

>  .. the ground-hunting operation, using bait stations, proved about $1 a hectare cheaper than the aerial drop and was deadlier .. By using bait stations, damage to deer, native birds and other non-target species is eliminated. <i>(Nov 16, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,14</i>)

### bait stationing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the using of bait stations to trap possums.

 <i>n.</i>

### baiting
 
 <i>n.</i> the catching of whitebait

>  A trip kayaking up the lagoon may have to be rescheduled around the baiting tide. <i>(Oct 10, 2004,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,10</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> I've seen people 'baiting - I've been 'baiting - when there's a lightning storm raging. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,108</i>)

>  The aim of baiting is to catch bait of course, but surprises can turn up in the net. ... Baiting has been part of the country's culture for a very long time; the fishery is now under considerable pressure. <i>(Sep 2005,Air New Zealand inflight magazine,67</i>) [NOTES: -]

>  River baiting, where locations are claimed and permanent jetties, netting systems and huts built, tend to be where the arguments occur. <i>(Sep 1, 2007,Dominion Post Indulgence,10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> spec. the laying of poison bait for rabbits

>  Poisoned rabbit bait is to be spread in three areas of Christchurch this week. The Christchurch City Council recommends children to be advised [not] to enter areas where baiting is taking place or to handle carcases. <i>(Sep 26, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

### Bakano
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Tom McDonald.. became responsible for turning out the basic red and basic white that many of today's baby boomers - the Cold Duck generation - came to acknowledge as the first half-decent wines they had tasted: Bakano and Cresta Dore. <i>(Oct 1999,North & South,75</i>)

>  Big Tom McDonald, the poker player of some repute, the autocratic boss, the generous wine lover, the man with the dream, provided the commercial drive and Dennis Kasza, the Hungarian winemaker largely responsible for the early Cresta Dores and Bakanos, was bringing some technical consistency to the operation. <i>(Jun 15, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,5</i>)

### bake
 
 <i>v.</i> to become exhausted (also as be baked: be exhausted) (See [cf. knock(ed) up](../K#knock(ed)-up))

>  In connection with stock the following terms were used: - `Let the sheep draw down to the Taieri'; `stags,' `crawlers,' `stragglers,' `a horse with couples in his mouth,' `the bull baked on the road,' `cut and tailed,' `lagoon washing,' `spaying cattle,' `killed a pleuro,' `herding lambs,' `buisting weaners,' `a camp for the cattle,' ... <i>(1858,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: [1858-1869] Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 85]

>  In 1860 one [bullock] would not drive; in 1861 one `baked' (that is knocked up) at the Gimmerburn. <i>(1861,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Puketoi Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 82]

>  [done up, done brown], We had not 5 minutes spell all day. ... We were quite baked by the end of the day. <i>(Nov 2, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,108,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> My horse is already baked and could not carry two. <i>(1900,Colonial Turf,83</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> homebake

>  Then he met someone who knew how to bake. <i>(May 2, 1986,Evening Post,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> drug manufacture, homebake

>  Closer to home, there was a recent incident in Perth where two people were severely injured after an explosion, though Bedford said the technique used to do the "bake" was different from the practice generally used in New Zealand. <i>(Nov 24, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

>  You might go out on a Friday night to a house where someone is doing a bake ... It takes about two hours to complete a bake excluding `shelling time'. (Taking pills out of packets.) <i>(Apr 5, 1986,Evening Post,19</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> exhausted

### bakshee
 
 <i>adj.</i> usu. senses in NZ `free'; `unofficial' f. orig. military <i>baksheesh</i>, `a tip', popularised in WW1 and WW2. .bds;1. (WW1) bakshee lance-jack <i>a lance-corporal.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bakshee lance-jack</i>. A lance-corporal: military (esp. Australians' and New Zealanders'): in G.W. 2. Spg <i>Bakchsceach</i> [? F. WW1 : reference here to 1920s.] bakshee leave unofficial or unapproved leave. <i>(1938,Dictionary of Slang,28</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> The ride down [on business] was uneventful, and though the `Electricity Station' [where a pair of good-looking girls lived] was only a few miles off the bridle track, Jim like a good Anzac, faithfully refrained from taking `Bachsceach Leave' and held to the paths of duty, that led in this case `not to glory, but nearly to the grave'. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,122</i>)
