### baldy
 
 <i>n.</i> pax

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Baldy! </i>I refuse (cf English schoolboy's `fain I'): NZ juvenile: late C19-20. B., 1941. No hairs on one's head: nothing to offer. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,983</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... the way to ask for a truce in a game is to call Pax ... At the school I went to, the word was <i>Fritz</i>, and the truce was terminated by the call <i>Fritz all over</i>, but we knew that some schools used <i>Fans</i> (from south-west English dialect <i>fain I</i> or <i>fainits</i>?. Apparently some New Zealand schools use or have used<i> baldy</i> which sounds like a variant of English <i>barley. </i> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,158</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Other twentieth century New Zealand expressions of varied use include <i>baldy!</i> a schoolboy term synonymous with the English <i>fain I </i> implying refusal. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,56</i>)

### bale
 
 <i>v.</i> to depart hurriedly

>  <b>SHARPE</b> Hearing the dogs, we had bailed out of that hut pretty lively ... <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,34</i>)

>  <b>HELMER</b> Thought we might bale our way down to the Central Waiau. It's only about two hours easy walking.' <i>(1964,Stag Party,44</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a hay-bale

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> In mid-afternoon ... the baler clanked and jiggled along the rows biting up the grass, pressing, tying, poking the bales out behind. <i>(1969,Green River,9</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> in NZ usu. to pack or press wool into standard packs.

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> Under his hospitable roof I took my first lesson in `dumping wool,' for he was busy `baling his clip' to send down to Sydney for shipment home. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk1/3/41</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> [sc wool], Gilbert was kept busy assisting to bale the wool, which was removed from the bins to the wool-press, where it was carefully packed and tightly pressed into the compact bales which might be adopted as emblematic of the wealth of the Australian colonies, as the woolsack was that of Britain. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,173</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BATTYE & EAKIN</b> baling the sump <i>emptying part of the mine's drainage system of collected water</i>. <i>(1980,Shadow of the Valley,72 (also 15)</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a package of wool in a regulation wool pack, weighing at least 100 kg., bale, verb; baler, noun.

>  Each bale carries her number and reputation. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/84</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> [The Minerva brought up] 4 bales wool, shorn from the back of sheep at Mayna [Mana] N.Z. <i>(1913,Old Taranaki,224</i>) [NOTES: 1838 Aug 16 Cargo Statements in 1913 McNab]

>  <b>McNAB</b> [The Minerva brought up] 4 bales wool, shorn from the back of sheep at Mayna [Mana] N.Z. and the first imported into this Colony [N.S.W.]. <i>(1913,Old Taranaki,224</i>) [NOTES: 1838 Aug 16 Cargo Statements in 1913 McNab]

>  <i>Bale</i>: Package of wool in a regulation wool pack, weighing at least 100 kg. Maximum packed weights allowed under regulations are 181 kg for fleece and lambs wool and 204 kg for oddments. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,52</i>) [NOTES: MAF]

>  <b>BREMNER</b> Bale pack made of jute, usually held 350lbs greasy or 300lbs scoured wool although other weights were recorded. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> This grey goddess was in for a bit when work began round the Donald's woolpress, this very press where.. dad.. having compressed laboriously a bale of wool, knocked off the top of the press without sewing down the top of the bale. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,81</i>)

>  [Hdg] The Wool-Dumping Charges... That the present charge for dumping wool be reduced at once from 2s to 1s 9d per bale. <i>(Nov 28, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  All the wool clip, about 17 bales, was lost. <i>(Jun 23, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> [The <i>Minerva</i> brought up] 4 bales wool, shorn from the back of sheep at Mayna [Mana] N.Z. and the first imported into this Colony [N.S.W.]. <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,224</i>) [NOTES: 1838 Aug 16 Cargo Statements in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 224]

>  When these bales were tightly packed, pressed, sewn up, weighed and marked with the station stencil, they were rolled off to the side of the shed. <i>(Sep 23, 1924,Otago Witness,54</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> I worked out a year's profits on paper, making a rough guess at.. the number of bales of wool. <i>(1975,Follow the Call,119</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> He would truss the body, then clear the tramped wool out of the pack,.. then open the front of the press and get the body into the bale. <i>(1945,Died in the Wool,147</i>) [NOTES: 1948 edition]

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Kath was sitting on a bale of wool beside Mr Smith. <i>Ibid.</i> 154 A detective was looking round a wool and skin buyer's.. and happened to notice the Algidus brand on two bales. <i>(1963,River Rules My Life, A,88</i>)

>  The use of the jute bale.. does not seem merely inadvisable. <i>(Oct 28, 1924,Otago Witness,13</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sp use] to press and pack wool into bales.

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> He was busy 'baling his clip' to send down to Sydney for shipment home. <i>(1881,Poenamo,41</i>)

>  In addition to three shearers, there are two baling, two at the wool table ... <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,502</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area, usually along a fenceline, where wrapped hay, silage or baleage is stored.

>  A bale park is a place where plastic-wrapped hay etc is kept. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [Jim Cameron writes: Known and used in rural NZ for 10+ years... Neither baleage (also <i>balage</i>) nor haylage is in the OED or any other dictionary available to me.  <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BOWMAN</b> His seventy years as a yachtsman commenced as "baling boy" for Wm. Innes.. <i>(1948,Port Chalmers,206</i>)

### Balkan Tap
 
 <i>n.</i> a wartime condition of madness (facetious)

>  In this land two infections assail all comers and vie with one another for first place. These are known as Malaria and Balkan Tap respectively. On the first we need not dwell, but a little information about the latter may be of interest. A similar infection has been met with elsewhere, and in Gyppo Land is known as `magnune', whilst away in our own sunny island I think `dippy' about covered it. Here we have a more or less insidious form of the disease, and no one with six months' residence to his credit may hope to escape. There are two varieties of the disease, malignant and benign. In the latter form the symptoms are easily recognised, and are amenable to treatment; but with the malignant type the victims, quite unconscious of infection, blindly affirm their condition to be normal, This form of the malady has several unfortunate features and is incurable. The percentage in our unit is very high. <i>(Sep 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,Second Series 3/12,1981 ed.</i>)

<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

### ball of fire
 
 <i>phr.</i> (WW2, N. Africa) ball of fire [So P8: 1941-43: Army in Nth Africa; WF; nf W3.] The Second New Zealand Division.

>  <b>CHURCHILL</b> For the purpose of turning the breach to fullest account, an entirely new corps, the 10th, was formed, consisting of two British armoured divisions and the New Zealand Division - that `ball of fire' as it was described to me by those who had seen it at work. <i>(Nov 11, 1942,War Speeches,1952 War Speeches 2/357</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> In the great Eighth Army attack of November 1, the New Zealand Division, with two British armored [sic] divisions, comprised the Tenth Corps. This `ball of fire', as Montgomery described it, broke through on a front of four miles ... <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),129</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Ball of fire. 2. As the Ball of Fire: the 2nd New Zealand Division: Army in North Africa: 1941-3. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,983</i>)

>  How does that help us who roll the old ball of fire? <i>(Dec 4, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/180 : 5</i>)

>  When you whose union see the world ablaze / New Zealand's "Ball of Fire." <i>(Dec 23, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/183 : 5</i>)

>  In England, not long before I left, I heard someone say that the New Zealanders were a 'ball of fire'. It was said by someone quite impartial. [Winston Churchill speaking to the NZ troops]. <i>(Aug 31, 1942,NZEF Times,II/62 : 1</i>)

### ball of muscle
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <i>(phrase)</i>. descriptive of vigour, good health e.g. to feel like a ball of muscle. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ball: <i>a ball of muscle</i>, fit and energetic. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,297</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>ball of muscle</i>: usually referring to children: (A) a BALL OF FIRE (complimentary) (B)a hyperactive child (derogatory) Which of the two meanings is employed, depends more on the circumstances than the child. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,11</i>)

### Ballarat lantern
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. manrope lantern](../M#manrope-lantern))

>  <b>WOOD</b> He lit a candle, and dropped it into the Ballarat lantern, and away they went. [fn] For the benefit of the unitiated, I may explain that a `Ballarat lantern' is formed by knocking off the bottom of a bottle, and putting a candle in its neck. <i>(1875,Waiting for the Mail,21</i>)

>  <b>BOREHAM</b> [Reference 1850s] More than once he [Selwyn] rushed, half-clothed, into some little bush settlement at dead of night. In his hand was a ballarat - an impromptu lantern, consisting of an inverted bottle with the bottom broken off, and a [104] candle fixed in the neck. <i>(1911,Selwyn,103</i>)

### ballast bag
 
 <i>n.</i> a gastrolith in a seal's stomach

>  <b>BEGG</b> At the Auckland Islands in 1864 Captain Thomas Musgrave noticed female sealions disgorging small pebbles which he thought must have been picked up inadvertently while catching fish on the sea floor. ... The practical sealers believed that the stones served to adjust the buoyancy of the animals and referred to the stone-filled stomach of the fur seal as the `ballast bag'. <i>(1979,World of John Boultbee, The,155</i>)

### ballast pit
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. gravel pit](../G#gravel-pit))

>  ... a hole left after excavation of metal for railway, quarter-acre gorse-infested old ballast-pit sections are to be sold by auction shortly. [check] <i>(Apr 5, 1913,Hutt Valley Independent,3</i>)

### ballhead
 
 <i>n.</i> [slang]

>  <b>MARRINER</b> ...I have to wonder how much face Joe stands to lose traipsing through the party with four 'ballheads' in tow. <i>(2001,Stonedogs,62</i>)

### ballies
 
 <i>n.</i> a ball-bearing used as a marble

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> Marbles were the currency of childhood and there were an astonishing number of names for them, some referring to the material or colour, others to value or function in the game. There were agates, aggies, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cats-eye, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, mollies, peelies, peewees, pisswees, pretties, Scotties, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers and whities. The march of technology added ball bearings - ballies or steelies - which were unbeatable when it came to smashing an opponent's miserable glassie. World War II brought `clayeys' often imperfectly spherical they were pathetic terracotta substitutes for real marbles. <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)

### balloon
 
 <i>n.</i> A metal object on a staff above the totalisator to indicate whether it was open for betting; the all ready signal for a race to begin: ? orig. a balloon hoisted.

>  However, the balloon is down for both stocks. In the days before modern communications at the races, a balloon signalled that the tote was open. When it went up it was a sign that the tote was closed and the horses were ready to race. And as very punter knew, when the balloon fell your money remained at risk until the race ended and the winner was declared. <i>(Mar 10, 2003,Dominion Post,C2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a race is about to begin

>  <b>SARGESON</b> balloon: <i>The balloon came down.</i> The balloon is raised above the number-board by the finishing-post to indicate to the starter that the Tote is closed. It is lowered when the Tote is balanced to indicate that the race can begin. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,297</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (confused with the general sense associated with beginning).

>  <b>BAXTER</b> The tote closed. The balloon went up. The light low sulkies flew round the course. <i>(1962,Horse,82,1962 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Bulls and Cows)]

### balloonfish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GLOBE-FISH <i>Contusus richei</i> (Freminville) ... It has several other popular names such as Balloon-fish and Mouse-fish. In America it is known as Rabbit-fish and Stink-fish. The most humorous name is that of Publican-fish. The names Globe-fish and Balloon-fish were given because of the animal's habit of inflating itself with air to such an extent that the body was like a lamp globe or an inflated balloon. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,378</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BALLOONFISH: <i>see Globefish</i> <i>Ibid.</i> BALLOONFISH: <i>see Globefish</i> <i>Ibid.</i> 115 [GLOBE-FISH] ... Other names include Toado, Balloon-fish, Mouse-fish, (N.Z.) Rabbit-fish and Stink-fish (U.S.); and Publican-fish (Aust). Gives off an offensive odour when handled. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,28</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293], balloonfish 2357 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### Balmoral oak
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pinus radiata</i> logs or timber; aka Pinus horizontalis [ca1954 (R.Mason) used by A.L. Poole, a humorous name used in the NZ Forest Service for <i>P. radiata</i> timber, esp. in Canterbury. The Balmoral State Forest in Canterbury was heavily damaged by wind in the early 1950's, and the pine windfalls provide a plentiful (but hard to dispose of) source of timber.]

 <i>n.</i> [(watersiders' ?ephemeral) -->[nf P] -->ca1951-52 Wellington Watersiders' (F.M. O'Brien), `During the 1951 wharf lockout <i>scab</i> became a banned term, and scabs were at first called <i>bananas</i> (they were `yellow'), then <i>Sandy McNabs</i>.]

### band cutter
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a harvesting gang member who feeds the threshing mill.

>  <b>CARR</b> The mills carry their own forkers, band-cutter, weigher and bagman (one man), and sewer and stacker. <i>(1913,Country Work,13</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> band cutter: the man who feeds the threshing mill. He cuts each sheaf band as he does so. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,4</i>)

### banded
 
 <i>adj.</i> As a distinguishing epithet in the names of birds and fish (`having a band of different coloured feathers or scales; or having characteristic band rather than blocks of colours'): bellowsfish, blenny, dotterel, galaxias, kokopu, moki, parrotfish, perch, rail, pigfish, rockcod, seaperch.

### banded kokopu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Whitebait are the juveniles of our migratory galaxiid fishes. These scaleless fish species include the inanga (around much of the country), banded kokopu (especially around Auckland) and koaro (especially in streams draining bush-covered and mountain areas. <i>(Apr 2005,National Waterways Project,18/1</i>)

>  <b>McDOWALL</b> Much less common is the banded kokopu (<i>Galaxias fasciatus</i>) sometimes known as Maori trout or native trout... Some whitebaiters refer to it as "golden bait". <i>IBid.</i> 49 Banded kokopu are also aggressive migrants, climbing high falls and penetrating substantial distances inland, reaching localities that seem inaccessible. <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,13</i>)

>  Now they are once again an undisturbed habitat for such creatures as the banded kokopu. This native fish hides by day under overhanging bush, emerging at night to lie in wait for prey, typically the larvae of aquatic insects, or land invertebrates, such as caterpillars and spiders, that fall into the water. <i>(Nov 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 60?50</i>)

>  Banded kokopu (<i>G. fasciatus</i>) and koaro (<i>G. brevipinnis</i>) constitute the bulk of remainder [sic], with very few giant kokopu and shortjaw kokopu being caught. <i>(2006,National Geographic Magazine,#77, Jan - Feb 2006, p31</i>)

>  The banded kokopu is another whitebait species reaching 26 cm. Its habitat is a pool in a small bush-covered stream which may be a considerable [73] altitude and distance from the sea. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,72</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> The juveniles of five species of galaxias make up the whitebait catch: inanga (<i>Galaxias maculatus</i>), koaro (<i>G. brevipinnis</i>), banded kokopu (<i>G. fasciatus</i>), giant kokopu (<i>G. argenteus</i>) and short-jawed kokopu (<i>G. postvectis</i>). <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,187</i>)

>  The banded kokopu is another whitebait species reaching 26 cm. Its habitat is a pool in a small bush-covered stream which may be a considerable [73] altitude and distance from the sea. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,72</i>)

>  <i>Banded kokopu Galaxias fasciatus </i>Widely distributed in forest streams and swamps. Small amber-coloured whitebait. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,48</i>)

>  On the West Coast, the season runs from September 1 till November 14 to improve the minor late-running species' chance of reaching coastal wetlands for giant kokopu, or forest-covered streams for short-jawed and banded kokopu, and koaro. <i>(Nov 11, 2003,Dominion Post,A18</i>)

>  [caption] <b>Under water:</b> Native fish found in the region's [Wellington] rivers and streams are, clockwise from top left, inanga, redfin bully, torrent fish, banded kokopu, giant kokopu and kaoro. <i>(May 7, 2005,Natural History of Canterbury,A21</i>)

>  Inanga are the most common species, but whitebaiters may also capture the young of giant kokopu, koaro and banded kokopu. "Shortjaw and giant kokopu are described as threatened species. ..." <i>(Aug 17, 2005,Whakatane Beacon</i>)

### banded rail
 
 <i>n.</i> katatai, moho; occas. New Zealand banded rail; a now scarce ground-dwelling swamp bird, similar to but smaller than the weka, with long toes, short tail and black and white striped underside. [also landrail / land rail, striped rail, etc]

>  The native land birds at that time on the island were as far as I can recollect: Saddleback (creadion carunculatus), .. banded rail (rallus philippensis), a few, white-eye (zosteropo coerulescens). <i>(Jun 11, 1913,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> [=striped rail of 1873 edn] RALLUS PHILIPPENSIS. (BANDED RAIL.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Patatai, Popotai, Mohotai, Moho-patatai, Moho-pereru, and Puohotata; `Land-Rail' of the colonists. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/95</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Pepe ... Rallus philippensis assimilis, banded<./i> or <i>land rail. </i> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,277</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The Banded Rail is also a rare bird with us, and manuka and fern growth of drier situations <i>(1927,Birds of New Zealand,77</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Katatai ... Rallus philippensis assimilis; banded rail. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,103</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BANDED RAIL <i>Rallus philippensis</i> Other names: Landrail, Striped Rail, Mohopereru, Mioweka. (The old sealers' name for the Macquarie Island race was Wigeon!) <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,104</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> <i>Rallus philippensis assimilis</i>, New Zealand banded rail, is the only species of this genus left in the country. It frequents swampy places throughout, and is not easily seen. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,268</i>)

>  131.a. <i>Rallus philippensis assimilis<i> Gray, 1843 <i>Banded rail (Moho-pereru)</i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,41,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Katatai n. Rallus philippensis assimilis, banded rail. </i> <i>(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,103</i>)

### bandicoot
 
 <i>v.</i> [AND 1896 potatoes] To steal potatoes by removing the tubers without disarranging the haulms; to rabbit. [ca1900 Havelock W.H.B. Orsman; ca 1940 HWO]

>  Jane Godfrey, My father used to tell (c 19..) us to bandicoot about a bit when he wanted us to dig out bits of earth (say around a post) with our hands, sort of pulling the earth towards the side of the hole, softening it, etc. <i>(1985,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: AC1898; nf W3]

>  Another was also proud of the growth of his potatoes but highly amazed and full of anger when upon digging them up he found virtually nothing underneath. He was unaware that some bright sparks had on one night bandicooted his spuds. Bandicooting as it was called, was done by scratching away the dirt from the spuds, taking them from underneath the tops and replacing the mulch and everything looked normal. <i>(Jan 2004,Onslow Historian,No. 34(1/2) : 6</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> No wonder they raided the farmer's orchards, pinched his eggs and bandycooted his potatoes and vegetables. <i>(1976,Water Joey,11</i>) [NOTES: 1977 edition]

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>adj.</i> bandy-legged

<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> be knocked bandy to worst completely in a fight or argument. [ca1941 St Patrick's College, Silverstream boxing instructor Keep your guard up or he'll knock you bandy. Also Taranaki 1940-ish (DEH)]

### bang
 
 <i>v.</i> add to

 <i>v.</i> to expound violently [a back-formation from bible-banger, -banging.

>  <b>BAXTER</b> `I don't believe in gambling.' ... `Leave the boy alone,' said a grey-headed rollsman. `It's not hois fault. His old man's always banging the Bible.' `Banging the bishop more likely,' Gandhi muttered. <i>(1985,Horse,21</i>) [NOTES: c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Mills of God) 21]



 
 <i>v.</i> fart [Known from childhood, Taranaki, 1930s]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bang</i> an intensive, suggesting everything, as in phr. `the whole bang lot'; mostly NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,12</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> of a woman reputedly free with sexual favours.

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bangs like a dunny door, she, (in the wind)</i> a sexually enthusiastic female. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,13</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BAXTER</b> `I don't believe in gambling.' ... `Leave the boy alone,' said a grey-headed rollsman. `It's not his fault. His old man's always banging the Bible.' `Banging the bishop more likely,' Gandhi muttered. <i>(1985,Horse,21</i>) [NOTES: c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Mills of God) 21]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> `I don't believe in gambling.' ... `Leave the boy alone,' said a grey-headed rollsman. `It's not his fault. His old man's always banging the Bible.' `Banging the bishop more likely,' Gandhi muttered. <i>(1985,Horse,21</i>) [NOTES: c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Mills of God) 21]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> `I don't believe in gambling.' ... `Leave the boy alone,' said a grey-headed rollsman. `It's not his fault. His old man's always banging the Bible.' `Banging the bishop more likely,' Gandhi muttered. <i>(1985,Horse,21</i>) [NOTES: c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Mills of God) 21]

### bang cows
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  I think any boy that works hard on a farm should get a couple of holidays a year if he is "banging cows." <i>(Aug 28, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,29</i>)

### bang on
 
 <i>v.</i> to harass with words

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bang on you</i> to nag; eg `The cops always bang on you, eh?' <i>[Alison Gray] </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,13</i>)

### bang slap
 
 <i>phr.</i> in collocation, as a noun or av, an intensifying elaboration of bang: e.g. I ran bang-slap into it.

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> `... You see, with a thing like a marquee, ... you want to put it somewhere where it'll give you a bang slap in the eye, if you follow me.' Laura's upbringing made her wonder whether it was quite respectful of a workman to talk to her of bang slaps in the eye. <i>(1922,Stories,488,Alpers 1984 ed.</i>)

### banger
 
 <i>n.</i> anything very large in proportion to the rest of its kind.; something extraordinary or excellent of its kind; a beaut, humdinger

>  Spare me it's [sc. the stink's] a banger,/ 'Tis the nox'odoratus/ Nasty Ngahauranga [sic, freezing works]. <i>(Sep 2, 1905,NZ Truth,1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a(n explosive) firework, [OED2 sb1 1. that which bangs: cit. Opie 1959]; a (large) firecracker, cf. double-banger, single-banger (cracker).

>  For the past two weeks I have been startled ... by `bangers' and `jumping jacks' let off by Wellington youngsters. <i>(Oct 21, 1972,Sports Post,9/5</i>)

>  The 80 stores would not be selling the small units of crackers or bangers, said Mr Liddle, .. <i>(Oct 31, 1972,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  For Guy Fawkes we used to have a few pennyworth of small `bangers' and, if we were lucky, some bigger `silver powder' crackers ... <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>) [NOTES: Sinclair `At the Point']

>  Only people with a permit to provide a public display of fireworks will be able to purchase `bangers. `Bangers' relate to any fireworks which rely principally on percussion for their main effect and basically include all crackers. <i>(Dec 31, 1989,Dominion,6</i>)

>  Some years ago big "bangers" were prohibited because the Government liked the future citizens to be as complete as possible. <i>(Nov 3, 1937,King Country Chronicle,6</i>)

### banjo
 
 <i>n.</i> a shovel [f. the shape]; also attrib banjo shovel. [>ca1950s p.c. F.M. O'Brien Invercargill, <i>A banjo</i>: A shovel.]

>  The 200 men who started work on the Te Anau-to-Milford road in 1929 were armed with tools ancient Egyptians would be flattered to see still in use: wheelbarrows, picks, and round shovels the locals call "banjos." <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/96</i>)

>  He performed the herculean(?) [sic] task of turning the first sod of the Waihi-Tauranga railway, with a specially sand-papered and varnished `banjo' of rather less than the orthodox No. 5 size. <i>(Mar 22, 1912,Maoriland Worker,54/9/2</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> The use of the pick and `banjo' (as the long-handled shovel is termed) is very useful accomplishment. <i>(1913,Country Work,20</i>)

>  It's a long time since you handled a `banjo'. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,105</i>)

>  A banjo: A shovel. <i>(1950,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1950s p.c. F.M. O'Brien Invercargill,]

>  .. a part of the West Coast's heritage as a digger's pan or banjo shovel. <i>(Jul 4, 1973,Dominion,5</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> When Jimmy finally gave the mustering game away he took on a job with the local county council _ on the pick and shovel. ... Jimmy claimed that [is] old dog was so humiliated at seeing his boss using a `banjo' that he lay down and died. <i>(1973,Big Country,205</i>) [NOTES: 1977 ed.]

>  <b>HUNT</b> And they'd go down the mine, ... scrape the coal down and load it into the skips by hand with great big `banjo' shovels. Then there were the old `pit-spiders', steel hooks you'd jab into the mine wall and hang your lamp on. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,133</i>)

>  Not for me the noisy proline/ but the quiet of the auger and fang-spanner - / long tom and jim crow,/ strumming on the old banjo./ ... [fn] long tom: long blade used for placing measured amount of gravel under sleepers jim crow: large horseshoe-shaped tool, with screw, used for bending rails banjo: shovel <i>(Mar 1, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>) [NOTES: David George `Euchre Weather']

>  <b>HOLLAND</b> He [NCO of Detention Camp, Field Punishment Centre] immediately ordered me to `grab a banjo' and go over and help fill sandbags. <i>(1919,Armageddon or Calvary,65</i>)

>  <b>BATTYE & EAKIN</b> banjo shovels special coal shovels with a large round blade. <i>Ibid.</i> 36 ... the gold miners enter ... carrying ... shovels. Their shovels are different from the `banjo' shovels of the coal-miners. <i>(1980,Shadow of the Valley,72</i>) [NOTES: Glossary]

>  Despite all the advances in mining technology that have happened since men hewed the coal seams in these hills with picks and the broad, rounded shovels known as "banjos" a centruty ago, the traditional eight-hour shifts have been kept: day shirt (7-3), night shift (3-11) and dog shift (11-7). <i>(Jul 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 68/50</i>)

>  See me in a dirty shirt, torn slacks (with red down the sides like a postman), sandbags for puttees and hobnail boots, without a wash or a shave for five days, swinging a pick or a long-handled 'banjo'. <i>(Feb 22, 1916,Hawera Star,4</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Ever use a banjo before?... I mean a shovel, chum. <i>(1971,Our Open Country,107</i>)

>  <i>banjo ... NZ & Austral. sl.</i> shovel. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,53</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; cf. 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 38]]

>  Our first contract was the same old gorie where we won our fame - viz., swinging the old banjo. <i>(Aug 16, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,V/50 : 33</i>)

>  We were still wielding the old "Banjo" in good style. <i>(Aug 30, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,V/51 : 56</i>)

>  We ultimately despatched it [a snake] with a banjo (shovel). <i>(Sep 15, 1915,Otago Daily Times,8</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> The job took a lot out of a man, that hard slog at the face with a pick, the laborious shovelling with the wide-mouthed banjo. <i>Ibid.</i>. 128 You filled the tub [with coal], shoving the big banjo into the soft heap at your feet ... <i>Ibid.</i> (Glossary) 242 banjo coal shovel <i>(1984,Blackball 08,114</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used mainly in S.I.] a shovel.

>  <b>CARR</b> The use of the pick and 'banjo' (as the long-handled shovel is termed) is a very useful accomplishment. <i>(1913,Country Work,20</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He [a roadman] was Rex Shaw, one of the old brigade who still used a shovel (the old banjo) and his job was to maintain a four-mile stretch of road with his shovel, pick and barrow. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,212</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> A lifetime labouring, fixing, mending, wheelbarrowing, digging, swinging the old banjo, the bucket, the broom. <i>(1976,Swagger Country,9</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The Works equipment was simple: banjo shovels and plenty of them, and a few wheelbarrows. <i>(1971,Our Open Country,133</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> They'd scrape the coal down and load it.. with great "banjo" shovels. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,133</i>) [NOTES: check page]

>  <b>BATTYE</b> banjo shovels special coal shovels with a large round blade <i>(1980,Shadow of the Valley,Glossary</i>)

>  <b>GLEN</b> An improvement on the banjo shovel is a cultivator. <i>(1980,Bush in Our Yard,101</i>)

>  <b>BATTYE</b> banjo shovels <i>special coal shovels with a large round blade</i> <i>(1980,Shadow of the Valley</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  [Maori Pioneer Battalion] We are still wielding the old Banjo in good style. <i>(Aug 30, 1918,NZEF Chronicles,57</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>banjo ... n. (Slang) </i>a shoulder of mutton, also a frying pan. -->(also thus in one of the Collins Contemps) <i>banjo ... n. (Sl.)</i> shoulder of mutton; frying pan; a shovel, hence <i>on the banjo</i>, to be working on the roads. <i>(1962,Australian Universal Dictionary,1266</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i>

### banjo shark
 
 <i>n.</i> [not NZ specific; one dictionary ref]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] shark, banjo 307 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### bank
 
 <i>n.</i> embankment (as distinct from the specially constructed grandstand) of many NZ sports (esp. rugby union) fields, often associated with rowdy spectators (See [cf. Aust. the hill](../A#aust.-the-hill))

>  <b>SLATTER</b> See the game from the bank and then replay it in the bar. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,90</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Rugby Football [sic cap] was a game to be observed from the bank, ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,115</i>)

>  <b>HOLCROFT</b> [sporting], The only protesters [at Lancaster Park, 1938] were on the Bank, old-timers who never missed a match and who felt entitled to chide players ... <i>(1986,Sea of Words,38</i>)

>  [sc. of a football game : on the bank], (`On the `Bank'') [title] On the Bank! 'Struth, don't it 'urt,/ .../ The team is filin' thro' the gate - just 'ear the shout;/ <i>(Mar 10, 1921,Quick March,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> to be very safe; to be completely trustworthy or correct.

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `How's the leg, Rusty?' asked Frank ... `Right as a bank. No trouble at all.' <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,301</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUNT</b> Leaving Wellington [c1892], the trains were usually overloaded so that a `bank' or additional engine, which shoved behind the guard's van, accompanied the train as far as `Mount Misery', just north of Khandallah. <i>(1951,Confessions,58</i>)

### bank and bank
 
 <i>phr.</i> of a river, in flood; cf. banker.

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Sometimes, perhaps half a dozen times in a year, the river is what is called bank and bank; that is to say, one mass of water from one side to the other. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,vi/79</i>) [NOTES: 1860 in 1863 Butler First Year vi 79]

>  I heard the Rakaia was bank and bank so I went to Harman's to see O.Brien. <i>(Jan 30, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,120,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <i>bank and bank</i>. A river runs bank and bank when all the streams join into one; i.e., when in high flood (Butler A First Year in Canterbury Settlement], p. 73). Hence a banker. The expressions were probably introduced by the shagroons. [Morris Austral English] gives a banker as meaning a river full to the top of its banks. New Zealand rivers, of course, do not get as full as that. <i>(Sep 9, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

### bank and ditch
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [fence](../F#fence))

### bank claim
 
 <i>n.</i> [also bank diggings (DNZE, but no cit.)]

>  <b>PYKE</b> The Dunstan gold workings were of two kinds - technically known as `beach claims' and `bank claims'. The latter were on the gravelly river banks where miners sluiced away bodily for ... the golden grains therein deposited. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,BkI iv 17,1889 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KITTO</b> If you are working a bank claim it is important that you keep the hole or paddock wide. ... This can only be done by taking out the corners every time you move ahead, and ... to do this it is necessary to keep the stern of the dredge out towards the centre of the paddock ... <i>(1900,Practical Dredgeman's Manual,18</i>)

### bank high
 
 <i>phr.</i> (See [cf. bank and bank](../B#bank-and-bank))

>  We are in a state of doubt about the Racia ... there is every chance of the river being bank high and not passable for a week, the first fresh is always the highest. <i>(Sep 26, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,60,1950 ed.</i>)

### Bank of New Zealand
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I take a good deal more notice of the Bank of New Zealand than I do of the other banking institutions. It is for the reasons that it is our own national establishment, and every man, woman, and child, is interested in its welfare and affected by its management. Virtually, it belongs to the public; the shareholders, nominally the proprietors, are merely trustees. My view may be extreme on this point but I believe the day is not far distant when the people of New Zealand will take possession of that bank in their own name, and tax all the others out of the country - a protection policy that would pay well. <i>(Jun 25, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,22</i>)

### bankage
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Unfortunately where we don't have naturally strong enough bankage we currently need to intervene... We have a responsibility to ensure these areas are managed well to make sure banks and bankage protection works will do their job during a flood. <i>(Apr 11, 1996,Contact (Wgtn),4</i>)

### Bankdown
 
 <i>n.</i> a NZ strain of hill-country Romney lamb bred by Peter Smith of Hawkes Bay

>  He sells more than 200 Bankdam rams a year, including some to Paul Bowen's Agridome Show in Rotorua and Japan, where they parade daily as examples of top-quality New Zealand Romneys... The Bankdam Romney provided a strong base, and he added East Friesian for milk, fertility and growth, Poll Dorset for carcass structure and also for milk, fertility and growth, and for toughness he put in the Cheviot.... <i>(Jan 29, 2004,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

### banked
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stockfeed or pasture] saved.

>  It is important to start planning how to use that banked feed. <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,Dec/Jan 60</i>)

### banker
 
 <i>n.</i> One who sits on the bank

>  <b>NICHOLLS</b> If I ever become a Saturday afternoon "banker", I should insist loudly, too, that there be as little as possible of the "bovine mutton-headedness".. and heaving and struggling in the game. <i>(1928,With the All Blacks in Springbokland,34</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a braided river containing enough water during a flood for streambeds to join and be filled with water ''bank-to-bank''.

>  <b>in KEEGAN (1983)</b> The Kaik creek was still a banker. <i>(1945,First Generation, The,77</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> Mr Couper found the Tukituki running a banker and rising fast. <i>(1967,Hawke's Bay Stations Scrapbook II,9</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Banker: In other countries a banker is a river threatening to overflow its banks. But in New Zealand, many of whose rivers have several streams meandering about the same bed, these rivers become bankers, or bank-and-bank, when the spate is sufficient to cause all the streams to unite. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,3</i>)

>  <b>ELLMERS</b> The small Mutera creek was now a banker. <i>(1993,Schoolhouse Under the Sky,30</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> A few weedy, wool-blind hoggets held up proceedings and one, just to be awkward, staggered out onto a rock jutting out into the river which was running a banker. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,97</i>)

>  <b>BRITTAIN</b> It is rare to see a banker - a 2km-wide stretch of turbulent brown water. [run a banker] <i>(2000,Lake Coleridge,55</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> A small platform fenced on three sides, on which concrete or mortar is mixed [1989 Charles Ransom notes: Banker - Everywhere else this is a mason's or sculptor's bench for dressing stone etc. but not with the use described here.]

>  <i>Banker</i>- A small platform fenced on three sides, on which concrete or mortar is mixed. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,12</i>)

 <i>n.</i> Coprosma Banksii [(named for Joseph Banks) spelling in citation sic?]

>  <i>Coprosma Banksii</i> Bank's coprosma <i>(1908,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: (And) Cockayne Botanical Survey Tongariro National Park]

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> PRION BANKSII. {BANKS'S DOVE PETREL.} <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,311,1888 2nd edn 2/211 ed.</i>)

### Banks' hard fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Blechnum Banksii</i> [(named for Joseph Banks) spelling in citation sic?]

>  <i>Blechnum Banksii</i> Bank's hard fern <i>(1909,Out in the Open</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>Pterostylis Banksii</i>

### bannock
 
 <i>n.</i> like a drop scone/pikelet

>  <i>BANNOCKS</i> a pound ... flour ... salt ... cream of tartar ... buttermilk ... the frying pan is the correct way of cooking these scones. <i>(1913,Australasian Cookery Book, The,41</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MILLER</b> <i>BARLEY MEAL BANNOCKS</i> ... Barley Meal ... Soda ... Flour ... Cream of Tartar ... Dripping ... Milk ... cut into triangles ... bake on an evenly heated girdle. <i>(1917,Economic Technical Cookery Book,267</i>)

### bantie
 
 <i>n.</i> [DARE banty widespread, esp. east of Mississipi river 1890; nf OED2.] A bantam, esp. as a pet.

>  <b>HYDE</b> Within, a mellow voice says reproachfully, `Hey, Banty! Banty!' The four little bantam hens ... quarrel bitterly for a place on Starkie's shoulders. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,13</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> `You don't want to sleep with the old banties, Flo. You'll get things in your head, ... But Flo was too young to understand. ... `Banty . . . Banty . . .' Starkie gave her her bantams back, and she snuggled down. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,318</i>)

>  Things look up when Allan Curnow ... explains exactly what happened that night the banties got shaken down out of the macrocarpas; ... <i>(Jun 1986,Landfall,158/142</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The special brown part-bantie picking with deft little darts the wheat in my cupped hand. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,120</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> He'd come in in his Sunday best, shoebox of jam jars under one arm, paper bag of banty eggs under the other... <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,103</i>)

### Bantu blood
 
 <i>phr.</i> Used for wine made by companies with South African links, during the apartheid years. One never bought or drank Bantu blood, of course.

### baptise
 
 <i>n.</i> applied to pre-Christian system [?omit].

>  <b>YATE</b> At five days old, but more frequently at eight, according to their ancient customs, the children of the New Zealanders are baptized; at which ceremony ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),82</i>)

### bar
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> The seepage races were working at capacity with the run-off pooling forces with the creek and building into the white cascade that fell into the head of the claim. Both men were working like beavers barring, shooting, redirecting, hosing, guiding and stoning-out. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,118,1963 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> ?forming a shallow; ?built up on the down side of a rock bar) or formed by river currents mid-stream behind a rock bar, or at bends

>  <b>EARP</b> ... a favourable indication was noticed at a bar in the creek, and a quantity of the gravel and the earth removed. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Earp NZ in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 109]

>  About twelve or fifteen miles below the junction ... we first obtained payable prospects. We tried a number of small bars that we thought would pay about an ounce a-day by working with a cradle. <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> They work on the bars of the river,/ And in many a crevice, I'm told,/ With knives they can pick out the nuggets, <i>(1864,Songs of the War,5</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> The river ... became wider and deeper, so that we were compelled to seek for fording places, which we learnt ... to choose at the bars or beaches above the falls, where the current was not so strong ... and the footing was therefore far better. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,30</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> They had found a `bar', such as satisfied George's critical discernment. They then went to work to turn the creek above the bar, and by nightfall the water was running in their new channel. <i>(1874,George Washington Pratt,47</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> Further up the river they observed on the eastern bank a series of long rocky bars projecting into the stream, about a quarter of a mile below the junction of the Kawarau, and immediately beneath the site of the present Cromwell brewery. <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,75,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MOFFAT</b> ... as the bar [in the river] commenced to dry in patches [as the river dropped], our hopes were high. The prospects were splendid. The wash was about six inches thick, lying on a false bottom, consisting of a thin layer of rusty cement, and under that thin white gravel [at Gabriel's Gully]. <i>(1896,Adventures by Sea and Land,58,1979 ed.</i>)

>  ... the contained gold ... has been concentrated in <i>leads</i> and <i>bars ... </i> <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,1030</i>)

>  The best [gold] was found on the beaches and shallow bars of the stream ... <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/12</i>)

>  <b>NOLAN</b> <i>Bar</i>: a ridge of rock crossing or nearly crossing a stream. Also, in North America, a gravel bank or a protruding beach. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,111</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> ... a favourable indication was noticed at a bar in the creek, and a quantity of gravel and earth removed, ... <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),258</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Oct 23 Report of the Sub-Committee on Recent Gold Discovery in the Coromandel District in 1853 Earp NZ 258]

>  ... a hole was dug ..., and a quantity of the soil washed, but without any visible satisfactory result ... [A] favourable indication was noticed at a bar in the creek, and a quantity of the gravel and earth removed, a panful was taken from about two feet below the surface and washed, it produced a number of scales of gold. <i>(Nov 9, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/26/165</i>) [NOTES: (Report of Sub-Committee on Recent Gold Discovery in the Coromandel District)]

>  Kapanga River Diggings,/ Coromandel harbour,/ 4th November, 1852. ... the annexed tabular report of the work at this Gold-field since my arrival. ... wherever I and the Messrs. Ring have `prospected' on the Kapanga ..., we have found gold, and that the greatest number of flakes in the prospecting dish, appeared upon washing some earth yesterday at a bar about half a mile lower down the stream ...; and below all the present diggings. <i>(Dec 10, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/30/183</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy's Report)]

>  Wednesday, Nov. 3./ How employed/ Down to bar at junction of path and stream, ... setting `Tom', &c. Digging and washing./ Remarks/ The earth passed through the `Tom' was top stuff, incidental to opening a new bar. <i>(Dec 10, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/30/187</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy's Tabular Journal)]

>  The largest cargo of timber yet taken over the Greymouth bar was shipped last week. <i>(Nov 4, 1924,Otago Witness,30</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [[??as in 1a, or is b. merely a normally extended use of noun bar].]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> The wash everywhere resting on water-worn bars and ledges of greenstone. <i>(1875,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),171</i>)

>  (j) That on a favourable bottom gold will lodge on the down side of a bar of rock running across the bed of a stream. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,5(NS):48</i>) [NOTES: (Park, Cromwell)]



 
 <i>n.</i> crowbar

>  ... she came out to watch him sinking a post-hole, and to explain the uses of a `bar' in clayey soil. <i>(1987,Happy Endings,105</i>) [NOTES: 1902 Lancaster Our Lady of the Plains in 1987 Happy Endings 105]



 
 <i>v.</i> usu. imp. (in two-up) cancel the spin! (of the coins): esp. the call bar 'em: also barred!

>  Even the metallic ring of the coins could be heard, followed by the suppressed sighs of `heads' bettors as `tails' were cried while `two ones,' `barred,' `heads a dollar,' `tails' ditto, `pass the bat,' and other equally quaint and even classic chants, were wafted on the midnight breezes. <i>(Jan 20, 1912,NZ Truth,343/7/3</i>)

>  `Bar!' shouted somebody. <i>(Mar 1912,Magpie, The</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> As the pennies flew into the air a man in the circle called `Bar 'em'. The man who barred them did not like the way they were spun. Any man has the right to bar 'em so long as he speaks while they are in the air. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,162</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to carry a passenger on the bar of a bicycle, also called to dink or double bank (mainly children's). [ca1935 (Havelock children) ca1954 July 31 (R.Mason: heard in Christchurch), `He used to bar me home to Burwood before we were married.']



 
 <i>n.</i> to dislike thoroughly [ca1940 Havelock I wouldn't have a bar of her]

>  <i>bar ... 21. won't have a bar of. Austral. & N.Z. inf.</i> cannot tolerate; dislike. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,84</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 39],]

>  `You're wrong,' I said. `It's going to be a scorcher of a day and we're set to do a record shear.' `Not a hope,' he said. `not [sic. l.c.] with these new handpieces. ... The shearers over at Fairview won't have a bar of them.' <i>(Dec 1986,Marist Messenger,33</i>) [NOTES: (Joy Cowley)]

>  And he [Prime Minister Kirk] was shouting back, `I won't have a bar of that, get out of here Patrick Millen', and all that sort of thing. <i>(Sep 27, 1987,Dominion Sunday Times,11</i>)

>  It's the old eternal triangle - the brash young newcomer out to make music with the older man's wife - but he's not having a bar of it. <i>(Jun 7, 1989,Evening Post,18</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [This has also been sent to hard: ?separate entry hard or cons here?]

>  In the Thames district the distance between the numerous parallel veins which traverse the goldfield seldom exceeds 200 yards, and in almost every instance the veins are separated from each other by a narrow belt of hard unaltered andesite. These hard bands, or `bars' as the miners term them, possess the same general strike and dip as the veins ... <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/22</i>)

>  The country rock separating two veins approximately parallel with each other has, in certain instances, been found to grade laterally from the vein-wall, where it assumes a highly propylitic facies, to a median rib of dark and only slightly altered rock. These ribs or dyke-like masses are the `hard bars' of the miners. In some cases, however, these `hard bars' are probably due to dykes. <i>Ibid.</i> 111The `hard bars' of mining terminology are in most cases merely remnants of less-altered rock, but occasionally as in the case of the `bar' mentioned in the Golden Pah mine, they owe their existence to intrusives. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,4(NS):101</i>) [NOTES: (Fraser and Adams, Coromandel)]

>  Further, the `hard' bars most probably interrupted the shoot, more of less horizontally. <i>(1948,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,42(NS):39</i>) [NOTES: (Gage, Reefton)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> as different as a bar of soap from a bar of chocolate; very different [ca1951 R. Mason: Wellington]

### bar up
 
 <i>v.</i> ? lift one's game

>  Duggan.. just enjoyed playing behind a pack who "barred up" for the occasion and being cheered on by the crowd will probably go to sleep tonight singing that dreadful Mooloo song. <i>(Oct 4, 1998,Sunday Star Times,B3</i>)

### bar-bound
 
 <i>adj.</i> grounded on, held up or confined to port by a river-mouth bar.

>  Still in Ch.Ch. looking for a ship. Mrs Briscoe of the Islands is here and very anxious to get down. The Cleopatra is the one expected to go down but she is bar bound in the Grey and may stay so for a month. <i>(Aug 1, 1876,Chudleigh Diary,250,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> Hearing at the latter port that the first boat had arrived at Hokitika [1864], but was bar-bound, we decided to travel overland, going via ... Otira. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,146</i>)

>  <b>WATT</b> ... ships which used it, were not infrequently bar-bound or weather-bound for lengthy periods <i>(1971,Centenary of Invercargill,82</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> pleasurably caught or held up in a hotel bar [ca1966 telegram `Barbound at Tirimoana' (licensed guest-house in the Grove Arm)]

### bar-tailed godwit
 
 <i>n.</i>

### bar-the-door
 
 <i>v.</i> children's game

### bar-the-gate
 
 <i>v.</i> childrens' game [ca1900 (1932) G. Turner Diary]

>  <b>TURNER</b> Among the [children's] games that I remember [from Matamau school near Dannevirke in the 1930s] are two varieties of hide and seek, called <i>All Home and Fox and Hounds in Den,</i> and<i> Hawk and Pigeon</i>, a game sometimes known as <i>Bar the Gate. </i> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,159</i>)

### baracoota
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [barracouta](../B#barracouta))

>  Probably it is fair to say that the spiritual home of our barracouta is in the south. I have seen hectare after hectare of flashing surface schools of them off Dunedin. <i>(Jun 2, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B7</i>)

### barb
 
 <i>n.</i> a barbed wire strand of fence.

>  <b>PERRY</b> On most properties an all-round utility fence ... will consist of six wires and a barb, with straining posts every 10 chains, four posts to the chain, and standards every two yards. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,15</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) barbed wire.

>  <b>DONEY</b> It is cruel to put the barb at the top. If a young horse or beast should get a fright and jump at the fence it will be nearly killed. <i>(1891,Essays on Bush-Farming,17</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Took battens off fence and lowered the barb. <i>(Sep 1, 1937,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>ELLISON</b> We used to tease her after that by riding our horses close to the fence ... 'Keep away from the barb' she would scream, 'Keep away from the barb.' <i>(1956,Whirinaki Valley, The,32</i>)

>  I strung a couple of extra snarly 3in barbs atop the new 8-wire boundary line and stayed the barbs up fairly liberally with totara battens. <i>(Aug 1972,NZ Farmer,10</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... numerous 'break' fences (short lengths of two or three barbs spanning gullies or running from existing fences up steep faces and into bluffs) were put in at strategic spots. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,199</i>)

>  <b>ADDENBROOKE</b> My good pedigree bull went and damaged himself trying to mate with one of the cows through a post and wire fence that had pig barb for the top wire. <i>(1992,Home from the Hill,143</i>)

### barb wire
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) wire with barbs used in boundary fences and as a top wire on stock fences. [Elsewhere barbed wire is the common usage.]

>  The trials made of the barb wire fences in various parts of the district all go to prove the value of the new commodity ... <i>(Jan 15, 1881,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

>  The Dunedin Barb Wire Company show barb wire locally made. The barbs are locked together so that they cannot come loose. <i>(Dec 2, 1887,Otago Witness,1</i>)

>  This well presented design for a jenny to handle either plain or barb wire on one-man fencing jobs comes from Wayne McDrury, Ladbrooks, Canterbury. <i>(Feb 23, 1978,NZ Farmer,73</i>)

>  Murray, Roberts & Co. Wool Merchants and Station Agents. Patent barb wire (Washburn brand). <i>(Jun 25, 1881,Evening Post,2</i>)

### barback
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Barback/Glassies 1-2 nights per week. easy $$ for the most important job in the bar. <i>(Jun 8, 2005,Dominion Post,E16</i>)

### barbador
 
 <i>n.</i> corruption of "bar the door"

>  The main [variation to bullrush] is the classic 'bar the door' (which for some phonetically evolved into 'barbador') where the difference is that the runners line up at both ends of the field. <i>(Nov 5, 1999,NZ Education Review,15</i>)

### barbecue
 
 <i>n.</i> [Jim Cameron writes: that <i>barbeque</i> is a very common variant spelling, mentioned in Fowler 3ed but not in any dictionary he has seen, including OED]

### barbed wire
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The women [sc Cairo prostitutes, WW1] of the old area were examined, and those infected were immediately placed in a concentration camp, known as the `barbed wire', behind which she remained until she received a clean [49] bill of health. <i>(1936,Recollections of an Amateur Soldier,48</i>)

### barbed wire soldier
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PUGSLEY</b> Those inflicted with venereal disease were treated as criminals.(?) A large venereal disease hospital was established near the Australian lines and run by the Australians for both Australians and New Zealanders. It was surrounded by barbed wire and guards to prevent men breaking out. Men unfortunate enough to be so caught earned the sobriquet `barbed wire' soldiers: `and it was rumoured that there were sufficient officers and men to form a whole battalion from colonel down - not omitting the padre.' <i>(1984,Gallipoli: the New Zealand Story,76</i>) [NOTES: [c1914]]

### Barber
 
 <i>n.</i> the prevailing Greymouth wind; a cold, cutting wind [ADD the vapour rising from the water on a frosty day: 1895: Can., NB, NS, Nfdland: DARE A winter storm in which frozen ice crystals are driven by fierce winds. 1832, 1889 Farmer (Cn.), 1889 St Laurence, Nova Scotia New England coast, 1982 strong wind that carries precipitation which freezes beard etc: DNfE 1. Cold mist or vapour arising from the sea on a frosty day; frost-smoke 1836 2. A sharp, stinging wind 1879: OED2 1c transf. Applied colloq. to a bitterly cold wind which seems to `cut' the face. Chiefly Can and NZ. Also = frost-smoke (cf Frost n 7c 1830 Can); 1933 NZ cf J Angus Gloss of Shetland Dialect: Barber, a haze which rises from the surface of the water with very keen freezing.] Often constr. with def. art., a keen, cutting wind (often with mist or fog). NZ orig. a West Coast, SI use, but by the 1950s fairly gen. in other parts of the SI, but not heard or recorded in the North: popularly derived from its being, e.g., `close cutting', `as keen as a razor', [ca1910 Havelock, W.H.B.Orsman, Usually refers to a keen or cutting, cold northerly, often blowing off the frost or off the water.]

>  <b>BARR</b> They have a prevailing wind at Greymouth called "The Barber" which sweeps down the Otira Gorge and smites the inhabitants hip and thigh... a keen biting wind which cuts like a sabre-thrust, nips like an ant, and stings like a whip-lash.`What do you think of `The Barber'?' in Greymouth is synonymous with `What do you think of our harbour?' in Sydney ... <i>(1908,With the British Rugby team,75</i>)

>  <b>94</b> The wind coming off the river was so sharp it was called The Barber, and it could shave off your hangover in brisk, terrible seconds. <i>(Mar 20, 2004,BLANK ENTRY</i>)

>  There is a wind in Westland.. so much colder than freezing point that they nickname it "The Barber". <i>(Jul 28, 1938,NZ Observer,8</i>)

>  The wind you get in Greymouth, the one they call the Barber, which slices along the Grey River and is so sharp that it can cure any hangover known to man. <i>(Jul 10, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  A very bitter cold wind which blows down the gorge of the Mawhera and afflicts the river front, railway station, and adjacent streets of Greymouth. The miners and drovers used the word in the 'sixties, and it is still in use. (C.[1890-1910]) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>PUGH-WILLIAMS</b> A very cold, slow-moving wind that comes down to Greymouth from the inland of a winter evening and then recedes the following day. It brings foggy conditions and is most unpleasant. I am unaware of its origin but locals inform me that it is nowhere as bad as some 20 years ago, their theory being that so much of the bush is cut out with the result that rain-saturated foliage is not exposed for fog-generating. <i>(1951,Letter</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> This day was clear. There was a frost on the grass in the morning, and a `barber' - the keen wind and night mist blowing down from the Grey Valley - was pushing across Blakestown ... and disappearing over the sea. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,393</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> There are several local names for winds, the <i>doctor</i> in Perth, ... the <i>barber</i> in Greymouth, N Z, a very cuttng wind, the Canterbury <i>nor'wester</i> resembling the European <i>Fo[diereis]hn</i> wind, ... and the<i> three-day sou'wester </i> in New Zealand, a form of the <i>southerly buster</i>, a name used in both Australia and New Zealand, probably more often in the shortened form a [sic] <i>southerly. </i> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,163</i>)

>  <i>Barber, the n.</i> Greymouth, N.Z. term for a bitter, cold wind. <i>(1969,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>CRUMPTON</b> A low ceiling of cloud engulfed the summits, and fog could be seen swirling down the ranges. `The barber is coming down and he'll shave you. A change coming - better camp in the bush tonight.' <i>(1979,Spencer: the Gold Seeker</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> Frosts and persistent fog are common in winter, and on fine, still nights, the air cooled in these inland valleys drains downs the Grey and Buller Rivers producing a keen and cutting morning wind near the coastal towns known locally as `The Barber'. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,197</i>)

>  Barber (the) bitter cold wind which blows through Greymouth <i>(1984,Blackball 08,242</i>)

>  <i>barber ... NZ</i> cutting cold wind. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,54</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.],]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Barber, the</i> the cutting Greymouth wind coming off the Mawhera River. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,13</i>)

>  <b>ELLIOT</b> There [West Coast, Grey Gorge] in winter raged an icy blast known as `the barber', for it was as keen as any razor. <i>(1937,Firth of Wellington,22</i>)

>  Walking the ice-coated planks of that bridge in the supercooled fog that is still one of New Zealand's few named weather phenomena was not one of the attractions of going to Taylorville School. That fog is called the Barber - because it cuts. <i>(Feb 13, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

 <i>v.</i> to peel

### barber's pole
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a serious internal parasite disease of sheep that attacks the stomach lining and draws blood, causing anaemia and sudden death.

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> The blame was laid on the Barber's Pole worm. A distinctive vein, in a red spiral like a barber's pole, gave the nasty little bloodsucking killer its name .. for the first time, the deadly little Barber's Pole worm had met his match. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,31</i>)

>  My first thought would be barber's pole worm (haemonchus) ... <i>(2002,Lifestyle Farmer,April/May 44</i>)

>  Justin Hurst .. warned farmers there was likely to be an outbreak of blood-eating "barber's pole", or haemonchus worms ... <i>(Mar 3, 2005,Hawke's Bay Today,22</i>)

 <i>n.</i> any of several bright red fishes of the genus <i>Anthias</i> (Berycidae): a fish ... of Madeira and the Mediterranean] [check any NZ application??]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 1. <i>Serranus lepidopterus</i>. - Butterfly Barber-fish. ... (Perca lepidoptera, J.R. Forster) <i>(1842,Travels in NZ,2/206</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Richardson's Fish) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/206]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293], barberfish, butterfly [TS] 1310 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### barberry
 
 <i>n.</i> [noun & attr.]

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Then three more heifers calved on the same day, and one of them put me through a thin place in the barberry hedge, just because I went over to admire her calf. <i>(1936,Follow the Call,20</i>)

>  One by one the riders headed towards a clipped fence in a barberry hedge. <i>(May 23, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B11</i>)

### barbie
 
 <i>n.</i> barbecue [AND 1976] A barbecue; a meal cooked on (or at) a barbecue, cf We're having a barbecue next Sunday and you're welcome.

>  Otherwise, we do barbies, which give us an entirely different reason for drinking: to stave off food poisoning. <i>(Jan 17, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  <i>Watch the barbie!</i> [headline] Paper and kindling wood is still the best way to light a barbecue, says a Wellington safety officer. <i>(Dec 12, 1986,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>barbie</i> barbecue. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,13</i>)

>  One feature of New Zealand speech that is often commented on by visitors is our inordinate fondness for abbreviations which end in `ie' or `y'. ... <i>Ibid.</i> 11 ... it's good to have a barbie on the back lawn. <i>(Jan 1989,Pacific Way,10</i>) [NOTES: Sharp New Zildish]

>  Quite a while ago, when outdoor cooking first took hold in New Zealand, he and his family came to our place for a barbie. <i>(Oct 27, 1999,Evening Post,20</i>)

>  We tend to.. carry on into the New Year pretending doggedly that summer has come while struggling in a howling gale to get the barbie lit. <i>(Feb 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

### Barcoo
 
 <i>n.</i> occas. previously used in NZ with some of the senses of boohai.

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Another memory that lingered on the trip, was that from Napier to Gisborne, to inspect a property known as `Te Rapa' and nearby was a `pubbery' (certainly not a hotel, for anybody would wonder why the Licensed Victuallers Association ever granted a licence for meals or liquor to be dispensed), and was typically designated `The Trap'. Truly, this pubbery was just short of shanty, typical of the `Outer Barcoo', but where Maories, their Wahines, or broken down `Remittancers' could drown their sorrows, wander into the `arms of Bacchus' or chase pink elephants. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,157</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Barcoo challenge</i>.-(1) to scrape the points of the shears on the floor or wall; or (2) to throw the belly over another shearer's head, indicating a challenge for the day's tally. <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The,13</i>)

Cody, <i>Man of Two Worlds</i>, Wellington, 1953, pp.122-123. <i>(Dec 1967,Political Science,76</i>) [NOTES: (P.S. O'Connor)]

### bare-back
 
 <i>n.</i> newly-shorn sheep

### bare-bellied
 
 <i>adj.</i> Sc. of a sheep, having no wool on underside of belly (and inside of legs).

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The [merino] ewes have many of them at shearing time no wool on the legs or under the belly, and hence are called `bare-bellies'. `Bare-bellied' ewes ... are quickly shorn. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,109</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] without wool on underbelly and legs.

>  Once the bare-bellied sheep is bred, he predicts big shearing savings <i>(Nov 29, 2002,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

### bare-point
 
 <i>n.</i> a sheep with the wool scraped off the points q.v.

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> When the word is given to start, the shearers go into the `catching-pen' and feel over the sheep, select one, carry it out on to the shearing-floor, and commence shearing. Naturally, the easiest-shorn sheep -- `bare-bellies' and `bare points' -- are selected first. It is amusing to watch the tactics of some of the shearers to dodge the Kapara (Cobbler) the last sheep in the pen, which, presumably, is the worst shearing sheep of the lot. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,143,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> In the old days, there were a lot of bush burns, with the result that bare bellies and bare points were much more frequent than today. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,9</i>)

### bare-pointed
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>MILLS</b> The Bowens are no `Sunday' shearers. Rams ... bare-pointed sheep, ... [105] these hold no terrors for these class shearers ... <i>(1960,Sheep-O,104</i>)

### barebelly
 
 <i>n.</i> a sheep that has lost all or most of the wool from its underside, a sheep with defective wool-growth caused by a break in the fibre structure, hence shedding on belly and legs; a sheep with (much of) its belly-wool scraped off or shed.

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> When the word is given to start, the shearers go into the `catching-pen' and feel over the sheep, select one, carry it out on to the shearing-floor, and commence shearing. Naturally, the easiest-shorn sheep -- `bare-bellies' and `bare points' -- are selected first. It is amusing to watch the tactics of some of the shearers to dodge the Kapara (Cobbler) the last sheep in the pen, which, presumably, is the worst shearing sheep of the lot. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,143,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The [merino] ewes have many of them at shearing time no wool on the legs or under the belly, and hence are called `bare-bellies'. Of course these `bare-bellied' ewes ... are quickly shorn. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,109</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Naturally, the easiest shorn sheep - `bare-bellies' and `bare-points' - are selected first [by shearers]. <i>(1870,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,143,1935 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1870s Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 143]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> A considerable proportion of our flock appeared on the shearing-board with bellies, sometimes with sides too, bare of wool, - `Pare perries' - bare bellies - joyfully the shearers hailed them in the catching-pens. <i>(1926,Tutira,157</i>)

>  <i>Barebelly</i>.-Sheep (generally a ewe) with no wool on the belly. <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BAKER</b> Sheep without wool on their bellies or inner portions of their back legs are <i>bare bellies. </i> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,39</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Barebelly</i>: A sheep which has lost the wool off its belly, i.e., torn off in matagouri or scrub. A sheep which has lost most of its wool is known as a `rosella.' <i>(1949,High Country Days,193,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>Barebelly</i>. Sheep (generally a ewe) with no wool on the belly. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,355</i>)

>  In the old days, there were a lot of bush burns, with the result that bare bellies and bare points were much more frequent than today. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,9</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> BARE-BELLY. A sheep with all the wool scraped or dropped off its belly. <i>(1956,Wool Away!,155</i>)

>  <i>barebellies n.</i> shearers term for sheep with light or no belly wool. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <i>Bare-belly</i>: Sheep with all the wool scraped or shed from its belly. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,52</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Barebelly</i>. Sheep without wool on the belly. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)

>  <i>bare belly</i>: A sheep with defective wool growth caused by a break in the fibre structure. This causes the wool to fall off the belly and legs and is more common in ewes. These sheep are sometimes called `Rosellas'. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/21</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep with no underbelly and leg wool.

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The ewes have many of them at shearing time no wool on the legs or under the belly, and hence are called 'bare-bellies.' <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,109</i>)

>  .. a big percentage of them carried very light fleeces, partly on account of the poor class of sheep and partly on account of the country being covered with fern and scrub. Plenty of barebellies in those days. <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,79</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Bare-belly, a sheep with all the wool scraped or dropped off its belly. <i>(1956,Wool Away!,155</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> ... I had a barebelly on the board slewed round so that the bare patch couldn't be noticed, and had started operations. [bare-belly] <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,257</i>)

### bareland
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) land without a dwelling.

>  Exceptionally well located bareland, 4 ha. <i>(Dec 2, 2000,Press, The,62</i>)

>  Bareland - runoff situated approximately 18 km south of Waipukurau.. <i>(Aug 18, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,26</i>)

>  Bareland - quality lifestyle block, bareland subdivided into two paddocks. [bare land] <i>(Jan 31, 2002,Fencepost.com,Propertybrokers Manawatu</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land] without a dwelling.

>  Attractive bareland block close to Rangiora. <i>(Nov 25, 2000,Press, The,61</i>)

>  A wonderful bareland block of 9 acres on Ireland road. <i>(Jan 31, 2002,Fencepost.com,Propertybrokers Manawatu</i>)

>  7 Hectares run-off. A well-located bareland block currently used as a support run-off. <i>(Jan 25, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,42</i>)

>  Paddy desperately needs bareland blocks between 1 and 10 acres. <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,38</i>)

### bargain-binner
 
 <i>n.</i> an item found in a bargain bin

>  He plays a tenacious covbers band called the Sugar Beats doing "Good Vibrations", and versions of other 60s anthems come from kitschy bargain-binners such as the David McCallum Orchestra. <i>(Apr 21, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>)

### barge board
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SALMOND</b> barge board a board covering the exposed edge of a gable roof - very often decorated <i>(1986,Glossary of Building Terminology,231</i>)

### bark
 
 <i>v.</i> signature call of Mongrel Mob

>  "As the mutts were approaching us they were saying 'c'mon niggers' and barking". <i>(Jul 20, 2002,Weekend Herald,A5</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> a term used in mustering sheep: `bark your dogs when you get to the top'.

>  <i>Bark.</i> Make dogs bark, e.g., `Bark your dogs well when you get on to the top'. (C.[1890-1910]) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> They ... stand and bark their dogs and give a long `Yahoooo' or a high yodelling hoot ... <i>(1951,High Country,23</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Barking my dogs, I started off in the dark. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,32</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Faintly he heard Mac on the top above him yelling and barking his dogs. <i>(1959,Tall Tussock,22</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> put bark on a hut or house

>  [Dec 21] the Men employed Putting Bark on the Capt's House and other necessary things. [Dec 22] The men employed in barking the Capt's House and carrying clay. <i>(Dec 21, 1837,Piraki Log,63</i>)

### bark biting
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the territorial biting of tree bark by possums.

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Droppings, pad runs and bark biting as well as the virtual stripping of favoured food trees is easily observed. <i>(1984,Possum Hunting in New Zealand,13</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Some trees are heavily clawed by possums and bark biting is common. Forest Service researchers believe that bark biting is mainly for the purpose of identifying territory, and only rarely for food. <i>(1984,Possum Hunting in New Zealand,15</i>)

### bark hut
 
 <i>v.</i> in early NZ, often from totara bark cf bark v2..

>  <b>WELD</b> Such accomplishments as painting and music, far from being out of place in a bark or wooden hut, are invaluable there. <i>(1851,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,12</i>)

### Bark Up
 
 <i>n.</i> a competition to find the dog with the most powerful bark

>  A new event - the Bark Up - will be held on Wednesday night to find the dog with the most powerful bark. <i>(Apr 27, 2004,Midweek,18</i>)

### bark up
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>McLEOD</b> <i>Bark up</i>: The musterer often stands on a point of vantage and makes his dogs bark to start sheep moving or to slow his position. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,253</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McLEOD</b> A good `bark up' soon had them [sheep] travelling out again, ... <i>(1959,Tall Tussock,118</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [mustering] to signal a musterer's presence by instructing dogs to bark or to command one's dogs to bark to make a mob move at a particular stage in a muster.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> It has not been unknown for musterers to do this [ie send a Nelson huntaway down] deliberately when a man persistently refuses to bark up and let his whereabouts be known. <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,144</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> We each barked up to show we had seen each other and moved on round the hill. <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,226</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> bark up: to make or command your dog(s) to bark. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,5</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> When the muster gets going again, the other musterers bark their dogs up (that is, make them all bark loudly, as a signal.) <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,85</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> They'll [Merinos] move away if you bark up, and they'll keep going all day, whereas Perendales will stand and watch you. You go on to a station where the owner believes you shouldn't use a lot of hunter voice and consequently when you bark up you get a great effect. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,94</i>)

### bark-up
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a situation where a musterer stands at a vantage point and instructs his/her dogs to bark, to reveal presence and movement of sheep and/or to show his position to other musterers.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> A good bark up soon had them travelling out again. <i>(1959,Tall Tussock,118</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> .. comes an echoing down from the shingle tops the first 'bark-up' of the day. <i>(1997,Different Worlds,11</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Around the mountain the gang goes, giving bark-ups at strategic points to keep sheep moving and to maintain contact and position. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,45</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> I will confess I didn't start a bark-up to let him know we were in the clear and moving on .. I stepped out of Michael's Land Rover and found myself struggling, whanging the dogs to keep them under control, wincing at the sound of a bark-up. [bark up] <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,47</i>)

### barker
 
 <i>n.</i> pied stilt

>  <b>FALLA at al.</b> PIED STILT <i>Himantopus leucocephalus</i> Other names: Stilt, Barker, Poaka, White-headed Stilt, Daddy Long-legs, Torea (Rotorua), Pip (Kaipara). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,147</i>)

>  "You won't see any birds," he told me, "barring the seagulls and 'barkers'." "Barkers" was his name for stilts, and they were no friends of his, for they would startle his game before he could get close enough for a shot. <i>(1970,NZ School Journal,3(3) : 28</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  BARKER 1. Workman who peels logs. 2. Machine that peels logs. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a huntaway dog with an effective bark.

>  Since I always seem to be talking about the barkers, I had better talk about Norman Boynton's Ben. <i>(Aug 24, 1972,NZ Farmer,85</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> Some huntaways are continuous barkers and can cause unnecessary dogging and bunching of sheep. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,39</i>)

### barker's egg
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog turd

>  <b>MCGILL</b> barker's egg: Dog turd. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,13</i>)

>  PETER Glossary 2 Barkers' eggs: dog turds [barker's egg] <i>(2000,Peter Cape Sings (record sleeve)</i>)

### barking
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of huntaway sheepdogs] with noise.

>  <b>MACGREGOR</b> The shepherds saw the wisdom of mating their best barking sires with bitches that could make a noise ... <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,38</i>)

>  <b>MACGREGOR</b> While the silent heading dog holds the sheep instinctively, the instinct of the barking huntaway is to drive and force them forward. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,40</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> You need a barking dog to yard sheep and you can't shear them wet ... <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,108</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> speaking [in Mongrel Mob slang]

>  Talking is 'barking', to be called a dog is a term of respect ... Barking is used by the Mongrel Mob to mean talking. It's also very common to hear Mongrel Mobs and associates bark, making barking noises like a dog. <i>(Sep 9, 2004,Press, The,A5</i>)

### barking-up
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering] the barking of dogs at heel to move sheep along.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Barking-up: During the course of his mustering work the musterer frequently gets his team to bark while at heel ( i.e. alongside him) the object being to frighten sheep along without actually running a dog. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,185</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cit. 1966](../C#cit.-1966))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Barking-up:</i> During the course of his mustering work the musterer frequently gets his team to bark while at heel (i.e. alongside him) the object being to frighten sheep along without actually running a dog. This is known as `barking-up'. On the free-running sheep this obviates a lot of work. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,185</i>)
