 <i>v.</i> (See [grass](../G#grass),[barley grass](../B#barley-grass))

>  On the bare ridges and hillsides, other pests have followed the grub and taken root, such as the invasive barley grass and thistles. <i>(Jan 24, 2003,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

### barmaid
 
 <i>n.</i> (euphemistic) bastard

>  [Caption] Come on Thenny You pretty 'ittle Lovely, Weeny Dive Bombing Barmaid. <i>(Aug 24, 1942,NZEF Times,II/61 : 4</i>)

### barmaid at Taihape, the
 
 <i>n.</i> a rumour

>  <b>MILLER</b> Dame rumour, known in our company as `the barmaid at Taihape', declares that we are shortly to be reviewed. <i>(1939,Camps, Tramps and Trenches</i>) [NOTES: 1917 Apr 21 in 1939 Miller Camps, Tramps and Trenches 60]

### barmaid's blush
 
 <i>n.</i> [AND 1912; P8] Rum and raspberry. [ca1945 Wellington]

### barn
 
 <i>n.</i> [Old English bere barley + aern house.] [W3 1 d. (trolley buses)carbarn; nf OED2.] In combinations such as bus-barn, tram-barn, a building housing public transport vehicles, usu. tram-, bus-barn, shed or roofed depot for housing tramcars or buses. a. ?????



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Distinctively styled barnhome with four double bedrooms... a three bay half-round barn and calf rearing shed, own dam and newly planted grass on easy contour. <i>(Apr 21, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,22</i>)

### Barnes dance
 
 <i>n.</i> Pedestrians scurrying across a street diagonally as soon as the indicator says `Go' or `Cross': at crossings where all traffic halts: Aust. and N.Z. since ca. 1950. The former was named after Commissioner Barnes of New York City - the inventor of the buzz crossing: origin of the latter, obvious. A pedestrian crossing which stops all vehicular traffic and allows pedestrians to cross the roadway in any direction, [1988 p.c. J.E.P. Thomson Heard in Dunedin for a `pedestrian crossing', thought by some to be a Dunedin coinage after a Barnes in the Dunedin traffic department.]

>  <b>SUMMERS</b> [sc. traffic light], The taxi crawled along Princes Street [Dunedin], stopping at every Barnes' Dance traffic light, turned west at the Exchange and up Rattray and McClaggan Streets. <i>(1964,Smoke and the Fire,22</i>)

### barney
 
 <i>v.</i> to argue, dispute.

>  He saw the prisoner and prosecutor `barneying' in the middle of some strangers. <i>(Jan 13, 1880,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> I can't barney with you all day. <i>(1913,Sodger Sandy's Bairn,76</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> c. A noisy dispute or altercation. Also Austral. and N.Z.; a rowdy argument; a quarrel; a fight [1951 15 F 8 Wellington H.S. 30B BARNEY [fighting] -->1951 16+ F 32 St Mary's, Wgton 30B BARNEY [F6] -->1984 17 M E112Pakuranga Coll. 30B BARNEY]

>  Mason and Saunders had a barney in a cafe in Hamilton East in 1993 and Mason left the band. <i>(Sep 1, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> [chapter title] A BARNEY. [about a fight and row with claim-jumping `rowdies'] <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/v/19</i>) [NOTES: (1889,1974)]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> A barney first commenced, in one of their Celestial revels. <i>(1864,Colonial Songster,86</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> I had a barney with Ryan about your share of the account. <i>(Apr 24, 1895,Mr Explorer Douglas,191,1957 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1895 Apr 24 Douglas in Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas (1957) 191]

>  And the yarns of strikes and barneys will be told till all is blue,/ <i>(Oct 15, 1896,Otago Witness</i>) [NOTES: 1896 Oct 15 Otago Witness in 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties 131]

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> The yarns of strikes and barneys will be told till all is blue. to Harper) <i>(1897,Station Ballads,53</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> We are scarcely less adept than the Australians in describing anything objectionable. ... An argument or a fight is a <i>barney. </i> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,62</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> [an argument about price], ... I was enjoying my first deal in cattle, and after about half-an-hour's barney I left old Sandy and still without my calves, in spite of his several advances up to [pounds]3 each. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,32</i>)

>  [argument, discussion], We had a bit more barney then she took it for three and six. <i>(1943,NZ New Writing,1/9</i>) [NOTES: Sargeson `Episode']

>  <b>SARGESON</b> barney: palaver, conversation implying insincerity and an end to be gained; argument. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,297</i>)

>  <b>3</b> <i>barney n</i>. argument, fight. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins)</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> `Heard Keith Hounsell and Mrs White having a bit of a barney so I looked out. <i>(1978,Murder in Fancydress,131</i>)

>  <b>LAWRY</b> `There was a bit of a barney down in Werry's this afternoon.' ... `And I got into a fight'. <i>(1980,Good Luck & Lavender,52</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> There was a bit of a barney between the McRaes when [50] they got back. <i>(1980,Whisky Book,49</i>)

>  <b>RAE</b> [found in English thriller set in Scotland ?Northern dial] It was breakfast time before Gregor Yule discovered what the brawl had been about. ... [242] the row had only put a cap [on his tiredness]. He spared a few minutes to placate the Harcourt bitch, assured himself that the barney had had nothing to do with what had gone on in the basement an hour before. ... The blow-up had been looming ... <i>(1972,Shooting Gallery, The,241</i>)

>  <i>barney Inf., chiefly Austral. & N.Z. ~n. 1.</i> a noisy fight or argument. <i>~vb. (intr.) 2.</i> to argue or quarrel. [C19: <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,86</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 55; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 39],]

>  They sometimes take a perverse delight in the barneys they have when they're out doing their duty. <i>(Apr 7, 1983,Dominion,7</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> barney noisy argument <i>(1984,Blackball 08,242</i>)

>  Some of the slang words that are marked <i>and Austral</i> are at least <i>UK</i> as well, such as <i>biff, barney (Coronation Street</i> 14.3.86 and much earlier too), <i>chuddy, jube</i> and to <i>barrack. </i> <i>(Apr 26, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>) [NOTES: (Forrest Scott),]

### barnhome
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  {Advt] Distinctively styled barnhome with four double bedrooms... <i>(Apr 21, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,22</i>)

### barnyard nugget
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hen's egg.

>  barnyard nuggets : eggs <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,2</i>)

### Baroona
 
 <i>n.</i> a brand of beer

>  Baroona is a funny name for a beer... it is associated with seasickness in the minds of many who squatted in its scrawny, diesel-filled guts, while it pitched and rolled its way across the Waitemata in search of Waiheke Island. <i>Baroona</i> was that ferry, now it is the name of Waiheke Island's one and only beer. There was a time when the residents of some Hauraki Gulf slands referred to the need to regurgitate after a bout of over-indulgence as "gotta have a baroona". <i>(Oct 19, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

### barra
 
 <i>n.</i> a commercial name for barracouta [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>BARRACOUTA </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Thyrsites atun.</i> (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Sea Pike; Couta; Snoek; Barra Fillets. (c) Other common names: B/couta; Bait. (d) Principal localities where taken: Gisborne, Motueka, Nelson, Timaru, Port Chalmers. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,11</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a barracouta, a type of loaf [ORIGIN: abbreviation, c1987 p.c. I.J. Godfrey Some dairy owners call any `uncut' (unsplit) ordinary loaf a <i>barra. </i>]

### barrack
 
 <i>v.</i> [1. To chaff or jeer at, esp. a sporting team. 2. to support or cheer: `which team do you barrack for?'. barracker, noun. The action of the verb is directed to support of the object: prob. not a separate etymon: ??combine with v1. as 2.]

>  ... the fellow, who must have been thoroughly ashamed of himself, and who had been barracked by his toffy friends who had seen his name in the Christchurch papers ... <i>(Sep 16, 1905,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  These blokes barracking him and you condemning liquor <i>(1909,Philip Loveluck,88</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> No sooner had he left the store than the diggers caught sight of his bell-topper and began [31] greeting him with cries of `Joe, Joe.' The township was soon ringing with the cry. The minister was puzzled to understand why he was being barracked so freely and asked Thomas the reason. <i>(1937,Thomas,30</i>)

>  It is tiresome and ungrateful to continue barracking the Academy, and so little relish have we for the task that we invited a conservative critic to take our place in the hope that he would have some pleasant things to say for a change. He was too busy. <i>(Jun 1945,Arts in NZ, The</i>) [NOTES: (A.Hamilton Mitchell),]

>  <b>DARE</b> ... they would enjoy a few minutes rest while barracking us slower ones ... <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,185</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to chuck off; tease; sneer

>  <b>SMYTH</b> `You cut along first, Ben,' he barracked. <i>(1928,Jean of the Tussock Country,88</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Noisy chaff, cheek (usu.good-humoured) cf baragin

>  ... and spite of the fact that our papers tried to rouse public disgust thereupon, there was very little barrack against it. <i>(Nov 7, 1895,Otago Witness,37</i>)

>  Oh, there's music in the barrack of the gay sparks in the pit. <i>(Apr 29, 1897,Otago Witness,39/5</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> There's the `barrack' at the table and the clever things are said <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,33</i>) [NOTES: [1890's] McKee Wright `Shearing' in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 33]

>  <b>FANNING</b> Barrack is not so highly specialised in New Zealand as in America, where the raucous `rooter' has his well-practised role.<i>Ibid.</i> 71 Barrack can be balm to a player, and barrack can be beer, heady beer, forty X beer, fighting beer (half tobacco juice). The barrackers set the music for the game. <i>(1910,Players and Slayers,69</i>)

>  By this time my bandmates entered into the spirit of the thing and backed him up with good natured `barrack' such as `Give it a go, Bill', `Have a shot', `Be a sport' ... <i>(1914,History of North Otago,68,1978 ed.</i>)

>  Sa-eeda Ahmed, Akers, arrak,/ Bints, Bomonti, backsheech, barrak;/ Centipedes and Cleopatra,/ Dust and desert, djinns and Fatima;/ `Eggs-cook, ana miskeen,'/ Fever, flies, and felaheen;/ Gehenam, Galabeyah, gods,/ Harems, hooshters, heat and hods;/ `Igri wallah, imshi Y'allah.'/ Jackos, jackals, `gala gala';/ Khamseen, kan kan, tala hena,/ Lice and locusts, love and Lena;/ Mirage, Mahomet, mosquistoe,/ Niggers, T.A.B. and C2;/ Olives oranges, askarie./ Peanuts, pyramids, and gharry;/ `Queens' and `Quacks' and `k'weis kateer,'/ Ruins, rum, and Gyppo beer;/ Scorpion, sand, sun, sin, and Scarab,/ Tibbin, taube, and tricky Arab;/ Whizz-bangs, wadis, `yin-al-deenak,'/ Yids, and yesterday, yeh meenak;/ Kestrels, kingfishers, and kites,/ Torrid days and sleepless nights;/ Mosques and minarets - maleesh;/ `O'ariglak,' `feluce - mafeesh' - / Bookra, badein, ack-ack-emma,/ The soldier's farewell - ma-es salemma; <i>(Jul 10, 1919,Quick March,33</i>) [NOTES: [is this barrack? or an Egyptian word],]

>  Football Humour ... Barrack is not so highly specialised in New Zealand as in America, where the raucous rooter has his well-practised role. <i>(Jun 10, 1919,Quick March,81</i>)

>  The other four, full of noisy barrack, were playing pitch and toss with a set of old horse shoes. <i>(1949,High Country Days,46</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to support noisily, to applaud (one's own team).1890 b. transf. argue for a cause 1897 (barrack for drunks).] [Where the action of the verb is directed against the object.] [AND prob. Br. dial. cf barrack to brag etc n Ire EDD. Unlikely any connection w. borak]

>  The team a person barracks for reflects their attitudes, ambition, the school they went to, their mates, the beer the drink. One father in the neighbourhood is in psychological meltdown because his five-year-old has stopped supporting the team he's barracked for since birth. <i>(May 23, 1998,Daily News,A6</i>)

>  <b>McMURRAN</b> Thousands of people are watching the games, and shouting themselves hoarse or `barracking', as it is called, in support of good play. <i>(1904,From New York to NZ,136</i>)

>  Some girls barrack quite seriously [at football matches]. <i>(Jun 10, 1919,Quick March,82</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> 'Struth it would be great all right to ... stand on the sideline barracking his head off and after the game go to a pub and get well oiled, Gil thought. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,171,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <i>barrack... v.</i> in Australia , loudly jeer, banter or criticise in chorus the players of a game; support, cheer; in .N.Z. to encourage, not jeer at. [Prob. from <i>Kiddie</i> language, <i>barrakin</i>, confusion, gibberish; Fr.<i> baraquinier</i>, to jabber]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <i> barrack 2 vb. Brit. & Austral. inf. 1.</i> to criticize loudly or shout against (a team, speaker, etc.); jeer. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,86</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 55; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 40],]

>  <i>Bar&#769;.rack</i> (b&abreve.r&#769;.rak), <i>v. i</i>. [Said to be fr. aboriginal <i>borak</i> banter, badinage; but cf. Prov. Eng. <i>barrack</i>brag, <i>barracker</i> a braggart. See BARRACK, v. t., BORAK, <i>n. 1</i>. To cheer, shout, or make a din, by way of partisan encouragement or derision, especially at a football game.<i> </i>2. To give partisan support in any way; as, to <i>barrack</i> for the school. [Colloq.] <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2017</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to argue, to row, to barney

>  <b>GRACE</b> Come in, come in ... and stop barracking like two old washerwomen. <i>(1914,Tale of Timbertown</i>)

>  "We are now at the situation where the Opposition doesn't like an answer, and is therefore going to barrack and yell because, like badly behaved boys, they cannot take the answer they are given." <i>(Apr 19, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> to abuse or noisily harass the opposition (team). [ca1920 (?) -->(This use may be a development of v1, or may be derived, as a contrary, from v2)]

>  <i>Bar&#769;.rack, v. t.</i> To deride; to decry by popular clamor. <i>Colloq.</i>] <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2017</i>)

>  To hoot or jeer at the players in a cricket or football match; also to `barrack for', to cheer for your own side. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),32</i>)

>  <i>barrack... v</i>. in Australia , loudly jeer, banter or criticise in chorus the players of a game; support, cheer; in N.Z. to encourage, not jeer at. [Prob. from <i>Kiddie </i>language, <i>barrakin</i> confusion, gibberish; Fr. <i>baraguinier</i>, to jabber]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> The others began barracking his impotence. <i>(1984,Day Hemingway Died, The,40</i>)

### barrack for
 
 <i>v.</i> to stick up for (a person, idea, team, etc.) (See [cf. US root for](../U#us-root-for))

>  After John Bull's barracking for the Japs ... <i>(Sep 2, 1905,NZ Truth,11/1/7</i>)

>  The Wellington `E[vening] P[ost]' is busy barracking for J.J. Michell. <i>(Sep 16, 1905,NZ Truth,13/5/3</i>)

>  <b>FINDLAY</b> The maids began to openly barrack for the cook. <i>(1908,Humbugs and Homilies,254</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> And if he meets the stony glare of any city snob,/ He'll barrack for the country town, I'm game to bet a bob./ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,58</i>)

>  <b>REES</b> Biddy always barracks for me as a rider. <i>(1928,Wild, Wild Heart,26</i>)

>  I'll not say which side I'll be barracking for. `No, fair go, said Bob `It's just general knowledge.' `I mean it,' I said. <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,9</i>) [NOTES: 1944 Gaskell in 1977 NZ Listener Short Stories 9]

>  ~ To hoot or jeer at the players in a cricket or football match; also to `barrack for', to cheer for your own side. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),32</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I don't care which side wins. There was a time when I would barrack for a team, but that was before I went on my trips overseas. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,75</i>)

>  For many years, hospital boards here have been barracking for a new system of funding. <i>(Dec 10, 1980,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 2YA Morning Report 7.40a.m.,]

### barracker
 
 <i>n.</i> [AND merely `one who barracks' 1889 (in a football club); M 1893; AC 1892; W3.] (Enthusiastic) Supporter; (and in a weakened sense) (vociferous) spectator (esp at sports gatherings, esp. rugby union).

>  The parties for, and anti, have given in their meetings plenty of fun, and barrackers for both sides have enjoyed themselves heartily. <i>(Apr 5, 1894,Otago Witness,37</i>) [NOTES: Morris]

>  <b>EYTON</b> The Dunedin pet of the gods, or rather `barrackers'. <i>(1896,Rugby Football,12</i>)

>  ... when, after the lights were turned down, the usual noise began, one of the worst offenders was marked; the light was turned on again, and the boisterous barracker was `fired out' into the street ... <i>(Oct 9, 1897,NZ Graphic,49</i>) [NOTES: reprinting an item from the NZ Times]

>  <b>BROWN G P</b> The barrackers grew frantic. <i>(1898,Lay of Bantry Bay</i>) [NOTES: 1917 ed.]

>  <i>Bar&#769;.rack-er</i> (-&etilde.r),<i> n</i>. One who barracks; a noisy partisan. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2017</i>)

>  one who barracks (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,21</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> The soulless larrikins of your street-corners - the blackguard barrackers of your football fields; for these are they. <i>(1898,In the Shadow of the Bush,124</i>)

>  Since his performance against the Kaikorai, Wirepa has been raised to a very high place by the small boy element of barrackers. <i>(1899,Tui, Te Aute, The</i>) [NOTES: (quoting Otago University Review),]

>  <b>HULL</b> For when the barrackers were hoarde and when the [hockey] match was done,/ Oh! then the players fraternised; ... <i>(1907,College Songs,15</i>) [NOTES: quote from 1902]

>  <b>FANNING</b> They ... make the most jovial of `barrackers', ... with a cheer for work well done [on the football field] by friend or foe. <i>Ibid.</i> 69 The barracker is the peculiar produce of the football field. <i>Ibid.</i> 71 Barrack can be balm to a player, and barrack can be beer, heady beer, forty X beer, fighting beer (half tobacco juice). The barrackers set the music for the game. <i>(1910,Players and Slayers,52</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> [Wool buyers] make more noise than a crowd of football barrackers ... <i>(1913,Country Work,16</i>)

>  The dashing policy of the backs,/ The exclamations of the crowd,/ Admonitions, objurgations, interjections,/ The continuous roar of the barrackers,/ <i>(1913,College Rhymes,50</i>) [NOTES: [Canterbury] 1923 ed.]

>  <b>INGRAM</b> A great game, full of excitement. Several fights amongst the excited barrackers lent extra attraction. <i>(1917,Anzac Diary,79</i>) [NOTES: 1987 ed.]

>  Barrackers, Good and Bad. ... A barracker is the peculiar product of the football field. <i>(Jun 10, 1919,Quick March,81</i>) [NOTES: Football Humour]

>  <b>MULGAN</b> When the attack was pressed, the barrackers of each side roared encouragement together, and when a try was scored caps went into the air like a cloud of starlings. <i>Ibid.</i> 87 'Varsity barrackers gave up hope. <i>(Sep 1934,Spur of Morning,23</i>)

>  n. one who jeers [Or. doubtful, possibly Abo. borak. (q.v.). <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),610</i>)

>  After the battle players and barrackers adjourned to J. Johnston's lawn for afternoon tea ... <i>(Sep 28, 1921,Mataura Ensign</i>) [NOTES: in 1966 Barry In the Lee of the Hokonuis 320 [=supporter]]

>  Any good play.. was loudly applauded by the respective barrackers of each club. <i>(Jul 12, 1890,NZ Observer,11</i>)

>  Fitchett.. muffed the ball a good deal, thus bringing himself under the lash of the merciless "barracker". <i>(Jul 10, 1890,Otago Witness,28</i>)

### barracking
 
 <i>n.</i> noisy verbal support [1962 cit. below reflects barragin q.v. not barracking??]

>  <b>McKEE WRIGHT</b> While we coves all laughed and shouted, and the barracking began. <i>(1893,Station Ballads,35</i>) [NOTES: (`The Bloke that Ran across a Snag' Check year]

>  The only buyer who can avoid this barracking is he who is quick to see the class of gum before him. <i>(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,3/266</i>)

>  .. the butt of a good deal of barracking from the disappointed ones. <i>(Aug 19, 1905,NZ Truth,9/5/6</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> All the fight was taken out of the bully, and it was not many days before the barracking he had to undergo caused him to take himself to fresh fields. <i>(1937,Thomas,28</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> b. Banter, goodhumoured teasing. `He came in for a bit of barracking at first. The boys used to send him on all sorts of ridiculous errands and so on and tell him this was the way things were done.' <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],37</i>)

>  The jockey boys have taken to 'barracking'. <i>(Jul 26, 1890,NZ Observer,15</i>)

>  <b>FANNING</b> For this lack of skill in the barracking department one is rather grateful than ungrateful. <i>(1910,Players and Slayers,70</i>)

>  <b>FANNING</b> The difference ... is that in America `rooting' is a well-established art, and in New Zealand barracking is merely a haphazard business. <i>(1910,Players and Slayers,70</i>)

>  ... in New Zealand [sports] barracking is merely a haphazard business. Few people go to a match deliberately to yell ... For this lack of skill in the barracking department one is rather grateful then regretful. <i>(Jun 10, 1919,Quick March,81</i>)

>  <i> ~ n</i>. shouting and cheering in chorus with a view to encouraging or discouraging one of the participants in a game, such as cricket. - <i>barracker n.</i>- to barrack for v.i. to clamour for; to support or discourage noisily. [Abor. borack, chaff at another's expense] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,1</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> Vigorous vocal support during the match for one of the teams: In a contest , e.g. cricket or football, or to one of two opponents in a contest, of physical skill. `Barracking' also includes sternly-voiced rebukes to the opposing team if they do not play fair, but also some fair praise for them at the moment of a fine display of skill. .in 4;`Come on, the Magpies. Go,man, go!' or `Get off his back! Get off his back. The bastard. See that. Oh! Oh! All he needs is a pair of spurs!' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],37</i>)

### barracks
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McKAY</b> Barracks, as cadet training was called, took place early in his first term, from the nineteenth to the twenty-second of February. ... with khaki replacing the school uniform during barracks, and on the days for cadet training throughout the rest of the year Baxter stood out by appearing in civvies on the bus, at assembly, and during classes. <i>(1990,James K. Baxter,54</i>)

>  Barracks were held in the week following Easter, and culminated in a miniature battle on Mt Victoria for the infantry and machine-gun practice at Trentham. <i>(1935,St Patrick's College 1883-1935,117</i>)

>  <b>McDONALD</b> The second camp, which lasted four days and was held in April was more correctly a "barracks", the boys sleeping in the school buildings. <i>Ibid</i> 234 The Cadet Corps continued to carry out its programme of weekly parades and annual barracks. <i>(1958,History of Waitaki Boys High School,201</i>)

### barracks week
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Children marching into class to "the Invercargill March", Barracks Week at secondary schools, compulsory military training and the influence of the RSA were all considered normal and appropriate. <i>(Nov 13, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  <b>McDONALD</b> The cadet corps functioned usefully throughout the period, with barracks week, usually held in February, as its most important occasion. <i>(1958,History of Waitaki Boys High School,313</i>)

>  <b>SANGSTER</b> The "barracks" week the School under military administration and usually immediately after Easter - stuttered along from about 1917 with occasional gaps of three or four years until from 1930 (when cadets were "astonished" to fall under a military regime in the first week) it became a regular feature, though remarkably often curtailed by adverse weather. <i>(1985,Pathway to Establishment,164</i>)

### barraconda
 
 <i>n.</i> hake, southern kingfish

>  <i>Jordanidia solandri</i>: Hake (Wellington), kingfish (Otago), barraconda (Auckland and Napier). <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SOUTHERN KINGFISH ... <i>Other names: Jordanidia solandri</i>; kingfish, hake, barraconda; king barracouta, Tasmanian kingfish (Australia); tikati (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,280</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BARRACONDA: <i>Another name for Southern Kingfish, or Hake. </i> <i>Ibid.</i>201 [The Southern Kingfish is] Called Hake in southern fish shops but bears no resemblance to the traditional English Hake which is far less oily. Tasmanian kingfish, barraconda and `tikati' (Maori) are other names. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,30</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [[?erron. or local var.].]

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>BARRACONGA</i> -See Kingfish, Silver. The term Barraconga is applied chiefly in the Port Chalmers area, to the Silver Kingfish, <i>Jordanidia solandri.</i> <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,11</i>)

### barracoota
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [barracouta](../B#barracouta))

### barracouda
 
 <i>n.</i> in NZ a spg var. or erron. for <i>barracouta</i>

>  <b>BUCK</b> A specialized but rather crude hook ... was used for catching barracouda <i>(Manga[a]). </i> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,220</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293], barracouda 2011, 2028, 2034 [check genus and sp] <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### barracouta
 
 <i>n.</i> 1. <i>Thyrsites atun.</i> =manga [the <i>snoek, snook</i> of the Cape of Good Hope]. Freq. spg bar(r)acoota; also barac(o)uta, barrac(o)uda, benecoota, benecootoo, ?boracoota, ?paracuta . See also barra, coota, king barracouta: contrast barracuda. [AND Leionura atun 1770 Cook, 1835: transf use of w. Indian barracuda, barracouta.] 1. A Southern Hemisphere fish similar but unrelated to the barracuda, usu. with long needle-like teeth. Also called manga. 2. An oblong bread loaf with a raised crust baked in an open tin [f. Spanish barracuda.] [see also barra, coota, king barracouta barracunda is used infrequently and not mentioned in most works, suggest it must originally have been no more than a misspelling of BARRACOUDA.] (See [see also barra](../S#see-also-barra),[coota](../C#coota),[king barracouta](../K#king-barracouta))

>  The barracouta is one of the few fish New Zealander anglers will encounter with teeth large or sharp enough to cut through most monofilament lines with ease. <i>(Jul 8, 1993,Evening Post,30</i>)

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> All the coves of this bay teem with fish of various kinds, such as cuttle-fish, large breams, ... small grey breams, small and large barracootas, flying gurnards, horse-mackarel, dog-fish, soles, dabs, mullets, drums, scorpenas or rock-fish, cole-fish, the beautiful fish called chimera, and shags. <i>(Jan 15, 1770,Journal,114</i>) [NOTES: [In the Stout Collection copy, Victoria University Library, an early 19th C. or late 18th C. hand has added: `*Baracootas, a fish remarkably smooth about 7 or 8 foot long [dagger]]]

>  Beside these are the snapper, baracouta and other kinds common to New Zealand waters. <i>(Nov 23, 1842,Letters from New Plymouth,66</i>) [NOTES: 1968 ed.]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The `barracouta' is much esteemed, as is likewise the `snapper'. <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,49</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> There was a grand procession [in Dunedin] to which the fishermen persisted in being admitted ... represented by five men slightly inebriated in a pony truck decorated with boughs and `Baracouta' fish <i>(Jun 30, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>) [NOTES: ATLTS]

>  (Black Stewart Island) The Barracouta, <i>Thyrsites atun</i> - a fish well known in Dunedin - is very abundant ... <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> A very common fish ... is well known throughout the colonies as the Barracoota (<i>Thyrsites atun</i>), which name is no doubt borrowed from the Barracouda Pike of the tropical parts of the Atlantic. This fish is a favourite with the Maoris, who call it the Manga or Maka. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,109</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> In the early days of the Otago settlement, when the colonists depended solely upon the Maoris for their supply of fish, it was very extensively used ... Dried barracouda - or snook as it is called [at the Cape of Good Hope]. <i>(1886,Fishes,12</i>)

>  Of the edible [pelagic] fishes ... such as the frostfish, barracouta, horse-mackerel, kingfish, ... <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Gandhi grasped Charlie from behind, ... and strained to lift him from the floor ... `An eight-pound snooker, I'd say. Or else a barracouda.' [context: banter and foolery in a blacksmith's shop: ?play on snook = barracouta; snook(er) =baby] <i>(1962,Horse,13</i>) [NOTES: (1985) (Mills of God)]

>  <b>PAUL</b> barracoota 2028, 2034 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>) [NOTES: [index to common names 283-293],]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> And they'd come to live [in Otago] on trouble,/ Pleuro and barracouta./ <i>(1865,Otago Songster,9</i>)

>  Black cardinalfish, frostfish, ghost shark, ribaldo, rubyfish, sea perch, trumpeter, white warehou and yellow-eyed mullet have now been included... Other reductions to catch limits are to specific stocks of grey mullet, barracouta and oreo dory. <i>(Sep 12, 1998,Dominion,2</i>)

>  <b>SHAW</b> Besides cod, there are seals, trumpeter, moki, barracouta, flounders, skate and warihou, of some of which the most fastidious gastronomist would only be too happy to make their acquaintance for the first time. <i>(1858,Gallop to the Antipodes, A,135</i>)

>  I would often glance enviously out the coolstore door at the filleters, even at the women further down the line, whose sole task was to pluck the parasitical worms from filleted barracoutas. <i>(Sep 21, 1999,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  The barracouta is in nowise particular about the colour of the wood [sc. of the lure] if in a feeding mood. <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Otago Witness,17</i>)

>  If you're a saint, I'm a barracouta. <i>(1968,Landfall,22(1) : 29</i>)

>  I was told that every barracouta caught had one or more [worms] in them, that they appeared like attenuated tape-worms, and moved freely about after being taken from the body of the fish. <i>(Nov 18, 1899,NZ Times,3</i>)

>  The candidates,. anxious, probably to make a good final impression on the wharf-workers of the electorate.. shouted "the faith that was in them" to the utmost of their powers. <i>(Dec 6, 1899,NZ Times,5</i>)

>  (Queen Charlotte Sound), ... the natives now and then brought Hake, [ed. fn <i>Merluccius australis</i>.] Paracutas, [ed. fn <i>Thyrsites atun</i>.] a small sort of Mackarel, [ed. fn <i>Scomber japonicus</i>?.] ... <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/808</i>) [NOTES: Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967)]

>  We also got a sort of skait ... hake, paracutas, parrot-fish. <i>(1785,Cook's Third Voyage,38</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> ... the canoes came alongside, bringing us an abundant supply, consisting of various descriptions of fish; among which were snappers, bream, parrot-fish, benecootoos, and some excellent crayfish. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/14</i>)

>  <b>BOULTBEE</b> boracoota [sic] - mo.kka [manga] [check MS] <i>(1835,Journal,113</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) ALSO 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 286 baracoota, m&omacr.kka [manga]]

>  <b>BEST</b> Port Nicholson abounds with fish the principal are the Kawa Kawa, Snapper, Harbouker, Barracouta, ... <i>(1840,Journal,230</i>) [NOTES: 1966 ed.]

>  barrakuda. <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/32/86</i>) [NOTES: check for spg form]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> `The <i>Barracoota</i>, or <i>Manga</i> of the natives, is a fish also found in shoals on all parts of the coast. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,160</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> Caught a fine fish the barracouta and had it fried for supper ... <i>(Jan 25, 1853,Traveller under Concern,71</i>) [NOTES: 1973 ed.]

>  Presently we let out a line astern and caught some kahuwai and two baracuta. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,236</i>)

>  <b>SENIOR</b> Amongst the sea-fishes fit for food there are ... a number of periodical fishes ... and these include frost-fish. barracuda, horse-mackerel, king-fish, dory, warehou, mackerel, and garfish. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,288</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Manga</i>, n.<i> barracoota</i>; <i>thyrsites atun</i>; [ibid.1957/177 ... barracouta, a <i>fish</i> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,87</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Manga</i>, n.<i> barracoota</i>; <i>thyrsites atun</i>; [ibid.1957/177 ... barracouta, a <i>fish</i> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,87</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> (Euphrasen). BARRACOUTA; MANGA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,47</i>) [NOTES: 1971 ed.]

>  <i>Thyrsites atun</i> (Euphrasen). Barracouta. Manga. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/415</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Barracouta <i>Leionura atun dentatus</i> (Bloch and Schn.) (Syn. <i>Scomber lanceolatus</i> Cuv. and Val., <i>S. splendens</i> Richardson, and <i>S. dentex </i>Richardson) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,310</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham. ALSO 1956 Graham NZ Fishes (2nd edn) 310 BARRACOUTA (MANGA) <i>Leionura atun dentatus</i> (Bloch and Schneider)]

>  <b>PARROTT</b> Barracouta Thyrsites atun Couta <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,169</i>)

>  <i>barracouta</i> also <i>barracouda n</i>, pl <i>barracouta</i> or <i>barracouta</i>s [modif. of AmerSp <i>barracuda (Sphyraena barracuda)</i>] : a large marine food fish (<i>Thyrsites atun</i>) related to the escolar and common on the coasts of Australia, New Zealand, and southern Africa - called also snoek <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,178</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Barracouta .. maka, manga <i>Thyrsites atun</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> BARRACOUTA (a) Scientific name: <i>Thyrsites atun</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Sea Pike; Couta; Snoek; Barra Fillets. (c) Other common names: B/couta; Bait. (d) Principal localities where taken: Gisborne, Motueka, Nelson, Timaru, Port Chalmers. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,11</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> BARRACOUTA Thyrsites atun <i>(1982,Collins Guide,287</i>) [NOTES: 1984 ed.]

>  <b>HULME</b> Simon made the acquaintance of barracouta, ling, trumpeter, and rig, and red cod, kelp cod, and rock cod. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,251</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Barracouta Thyrsites atun</i> ... A fish of Southern Hemisphere temperate coastal waters, including South Africa (as snoek) ... - <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,120</i>)

>  instead of a load of Whale Bone they brought a Cargo of Barakuta. <i>(Nov 18, 1837,Piraki Log,60</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> A nominal one-and-a-half pound loaf, baked long and narrow in an open-topped tin to give a raised crust which fancifully resembles the (back of a) barracouta fish, and often indented in the middle to enable breaking into two `quarter' loaves; also barracouta loaf. [also couta also barracuda] (See [also couta](../A#also-couta))

>  [Advt] Sunland's Many Varieties. Brought to you each day by the Sunland Bread Man... Long Tin.. Concertina or Ring Loaf.. Vienna.. Barr[a]couta]. <i>(Dec 7, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,00</i>) [NOTES: Check page number]

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> She had a ... plate of butter ... on the table before her and a big loaf called a `barracouta' ... .[fn 162] `barracouta': a longish narrow loaf with a rounded raised crust, fancifully resembling the barracouta fish (<i>Thyrsites atun</i>). <i>(1916,Aloe, The</i>) [NOTES: 1982 O'Sullivan p. 121]

>  <b>BYCROFT Ltd</b> Single or straight Barracuda. <i>(1934,Bread Baking in NZ</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>barracouta</i> oblong loaf of bread with raised crust, resembling the fish with the fearsome bite; displaced by French loaf. b. barracouta loaf. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,13</i>)

>  Her lunch was always two, thick, white barracouta bread sandwiches, spread with unsalted butter and filled with dark chocolate. <i>(Dec 4, 2004,Dominion Post,E7</i>)

>  We even ate in these pools, with those like me who didn't make much from the penny diving having to run shopping errands for a fee to get "half a loaf of baracuda." <i>(Jun 16, 1992,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Patting the dough into tins for barracudas and tank loaves. <i>(1989,Kiwi Baby Boomers,18</i>)

### barracouta hook
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> At the end of a stick three or four feet long, which [Worser] held in his hand, there was fastened by some three feet of strong line, a piece of red wood four inches long, two broad, and one inch thick. Through the end of the wood a common nail was driven, and turned back so as to form a rude hook. On the approach of the shoal, Worser thrashed the water within his reach with the bit of wood and the end of his rod, whirling it round with great speed ... when one was hooked, a dextrous heave flung him on on the deck with centrifugal force...] <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/183</i>)

>  <b>HEBERLEY</b> This is a piece of red wood with a nail driven through it and bent round in the shape of a hook commonly called a Barracoota hook [and used by whalers and Maoris for catching barracouta]. <i>(1839,Autobiography,35</i>)

>  [commentary on sketches], green stone maree/ Long CLUB or Mire/ Baracoota hook <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,44,1986 ed.</i>)

>  The Moriori used wooden fishhooks but only for kahawai and manga (`barracouter' <i>Thyrsites atun</i>). The `barracouter' hook is a canoe-shaped bit of wood inlaid on the one side with a strip of bright shell of <i>Haliotis. </i> <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 360</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 360]

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> The Maori used [paua] as the lure of the kahawai or barracouta hooks <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,284</i>)

### barracouta loaf
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [barracouta](../B#barracouta))

>  barracouta loaf NZ long loaf of bread. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,55</i>) [NOTES: nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict]

>  <b>FRAME</b> All the cars huddled in the streets, look like barracuda loaves of bread without any crust, only crisp snow for tasting by the saw-toothed wind. <i>(1963,Snowman Snowman,15</i>)

>  <b>McLauchlan ed.</b> As Clarry remembered we were a big family - a twenty-five double barracouta loaves and fifteen pints a week family. <i>(2000,Morrieson's Motel,199</i>)

>  Barracouta loaves of bread will no longer be available in Nelson as from Monday ... The withdrawal of barracoutas will mean that no longer will unsliced quarter loaves be available. <i>(Sep 29, 1972,Marlborough Express,6/3</i>)

>  Shands Bakery has been a familiar sight and smell for travellers passing through Huntly since 1915. The smell of a freshly baked Barracuda loaf, sandwich, tank loaf or the taste of a doughnut, Sally Lunn or pie was enough for many to make a stop at Shands. <i>(Apr 2004,NZ Memories,NNo. 47/12</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> Alice was making water-cress sandwiches. She had a lump of butter on the table, a barracouta loaf, and the cresses tumbled in a white cloth. <i>(1920,Stories,250,(1984 Alpers) ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> She had a lump of butter on the table, a barracouta loaf ... <i>(1920,Collected Stories,48</i>) [NOTES: (Prelude)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> I grew up haunting the Matata bakery, offering to help spoon out the tins of barracuda loaves just to enjoy the smell, and a few mouthfuls of hot dough. <i>(1985,G'day Country,9</i>)

### barracouta season
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [check ms] The Baracoota season had now commenced and we had abundance of these fish ... [ed fn: The barracouta, mohka, season ran from November until April.] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,75,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: also 1827 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 192]

### barracoutian
 
 <i>n.</i> an old identity, phs spec. a Dunedin boarding-house keeper or storekeeper [slang]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> So much did the name [`old identity'] come to be a term of reproach among the Australian diggers that the barracouta, a rather coarse fish, very abundant at certain seasons, and which were consequently very cheap, and a great favourite with boarding-house keepers, was christened by them `the old identity'.] <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,25</i>)

>  <b>BARR</b> There are few of our primeval storekeepers now remaining, doing business: indeed, besides those of our late, kind hearted mayor, Mr Mercer - a <i>barracoutian</i> of the true stamp - and that of Mr Robertson, I know not if there be any others. <i>(1879,Old Identities, The,49</i>)

 <i>n.</i> stem, trunk of tree.

>  <b>R. GILBERD</b> `Stringers' or `drawers' are drawn out of the barrel. <i>(1950,Letter</i>)

### barrelling
 
 <i>n.</i> an organised beating

>  His book included an account of "barrelling", or organised beatings... The cadet said time and place of barrelling would be spread among the cadets, and the staff would disappear. A cadet of the early 1880s, Mike, describes a "lesser barrelling", which involved being tied to a bed, taken outside and leaned up against a wall. Shaving cream, toothpaste, nugget and even excrement were rubbed on the victim who was then hosed off. <i>(Oct 7, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>) [NOTES: 1980s not 1880s presumably (and he would be very hosed off I should imagine) TD]

>  Justice Morris says some of the 81 former cadets he interviewed told him of instances of physical and sexual abuse including: nuggetting, spooning, barrelling, beatings and kicking, verbal abuse, extortion, forced showering, cigarette burns, and beatings with brooms and rifles. <i>(Dec 2, 2005,NZ Herald,A5</i>)

>  Barrelling: Forcing a cadet to run the gauntlet of other cadets, who punch and hit him. <i>(Dec 2, 2005,NZ Herald,A5</i>)

### barrier
 
 <i>n.</i> a long narrow sandy island lying parallel to a shore and built up by the action of the waves, currents and wind. Also called an offshore bar. [nf OED2; W3 2 b Auckland, Manukau Harbour]

>  <b>MRS VINCENT</b> There was water wherever we looked, with long, streaky lines showing the `barriers', or swampy bits of plain or sandbanks. <i>(1885,40,000 Miles,156</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [ca1952 Wellington Tramways p.c. F.M. O'Brien a term taken from horse-racing and used colloquially by Wellington trammies mainly for the Lambton Quay but also occas. for the Coutenay Place despatch points.]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the moveable gate or other device at the starting line in a modern racetrack which is opened to signal the start of a race.

>  [Hdg] Outside barrier presents Coup Ray with challenge. <i>(Mar 9, 2001,Dominion,23</i>)

>  Higgins's patent barrier was used in one race at the Avondale meeting. <i>(Sep 27, 1913,NZ Observer,23</i>)

### Barrier pine
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Dacrydium kirkii</i>

>  This species [D. kirkii] was sufficiently plentiful on the Great Barrier Island to admit of its conversion [to timber] a few years ago, and the timber was placed in the Auckland market under the name of Barrier pine. <i>(1878,TrNZI,10/384</i>) [NOTES: Kirk]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Manoao,</i> n ... 2. <i>Dacrydium kirkii, Barrier pine. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,176</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> ... the family having been established in the days when the white man came alone into the native settlement and picked his wife from the bright-eyed kotiros of the hapu. [fn. Maidens of the tribe.] The founder of the family slept in the graveyard, beneath the manoa trees on the summit of the hill, and his grave vibrated ... to the tread of the ocean rollers on the bar. [ed. fn. Correctly, manoao, a podocarp tree also known as Barrier pine.] <i>(1905,Toll of the Bush,41</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Manoao, ... 2. Dacrydium kirkii, Barrier pine.</i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,176</i>)

### Barrier skink
 
 <i>n.</i> a rock-dwelling, sun-loving skink found in the Barrier Knob area of Fiordland National Park. [<i>Oligosoma</i> sp. 'Barrier' [tag name; formal nomenclature pending]]

>  The tag name 'Barrier skink' was given to these skinks. These 'Barrier skinks' were medium to large in size (reaching up to 81 mm SVL [snout to vent length], 179mm TL, and weighting up to 12.1 g.) they were mainly black dorsally and laterally, with yellowish, green, cream or brown flecks, and white ventrally, with occasional black speckles... <i>(Nov 2008,Internet - see Notes,11</i>) [NOTES: http://csl.doc.govt.nz/upload/documents/science-and-technical/drds304.pdf Alpine lizard research in Fiordland National Park: February - March 2007. Bell, T.P.; Patterson, G.; Jewell, T. 2008. DOC Research & Development Series 304 NZ Dept of Conservation, Wellington.]

>  Taxonomic resolution and their subsequent conservation for both the Barrier and mahogany skinks were given urgent priority by DOC Southland in May 2007... <i>(2009,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.landcareresearch.co.nz/research/wildlifeecol/herpetology/projects.asp]

### barrow
 
 <i>v.</i> to shear a sheep

>  <i>Barrow</i>.- To shear or partly shear a sheep for a shearer. `No barrowing allowed on the board' was at one time a rule which the Shearers' Union got into the award. Boys often finish or begin a sheep for a shearer, who, of course, is responsible for its being properly shorn. (C.) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <i>barrow 4 v.i. NZ & Austral</i>. complete the shearing of a sheep for a shearer. [orig. unkn.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,56</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.],]



 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1900 at least (also v) [check RA papers: ?also in Chudleigh and other pighunting sequences]]

>  sc. of wild pig : <i>barrowing</i> was going on in 1850 Being out of meat we sallied forth to the chase and caught 2 boars and 1 barrow near the `Eaikuku'. <i>(Jan 26, 1850,Torlesse Papers,128,1958 ed.</i>)

>  [=pig], While both barrows and boars, and sows by the scores/ Cut their sticks with the wind at their tail./ <i>(May 1852,Canterbury Rhymes,16</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> BARROW: A male pig which has been desexed. Musterers often catch young pigs and perform this operation. <i>(1949,High Country Days,1950,193 ed.</i>)

>  <i>barrow 3 n. NZ & Austral</i>. castrated male pig. [OE] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.],]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MASTERS</b> As far as capital for breaking in the land was concerned, Jack was in the same barrow as the rest of the bush settlers; he just didn't have any. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,152</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to assist a shearer with his tally by shearing part or all of a sheep.

>  Barrow - to shear or partly shear a sheep for a shearer. <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> barrow: to begin or finish a sheep for a shearer. The practice of bellying a sheep so that a shearer can time his catch is unfair to others, and if it is finished for him and is done poorly he has to bear the responsibility. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,5</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> He may, in order to time this catch, have previously chosen a rosella or perhaps someone had barrowed one for him. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,17</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Each of the other shearers had rousies 'barrowing' for them. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,44</i>)

### barrow-boy
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [also barrowman] a person who will shear a few sheep to give a shearer a rest.

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The following day, Jim (later Sir Eruera [Tirikatene]) became my `barrow-boy' and washed and dried my used gear each run for the privilege of shearing the first sheep. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,94</i>)

### barrowing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The wool rollers and fleece pickers who were paid by the week spent the smoko spell resting. Some of the more energetic tried their hand at shearing, a practice known as `barrowing'. Usually these sheep went to the learner['s tally] or to any of the older or slower shearers. <i>(1949,High Country Days,54</i>)

>  <i>barrowing:</i> A term to describe the action of a shed hand who finishes off a sheep for a shearer after the bell has gone for the end of a run. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/21</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### barrowman
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> There were always a number of `barrowmen' and `learners' ... [fn] Friends of the shearers who came onto the board for a chat and, while yarning, frequently shore a few sheep for their pals. <i>Ibid.</i> 129 There were always a certain number of `barrowmen' and `learners' on the board, ... [fn] Friends of the shearers, who came on to the board for a chat and, while yarning, frequently shore a few sheep for their pals. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,112,1954 ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [also barrowboy] a person who will shear a few sheep to give a shearer a rest.

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> There was always a certain number of 'barrowmen' and 'learners' on the board, and some of the old shearers had no conscience where the beginners were concerned. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,129</i>)

### Barry Crump
 
 <i>n.</i> author of "A Good Keen Man", a persona he later adopted and popularised, particularly in a series of Toyata advertisements with Lloyd Scott.

>  Years ago, in a callow, Barry Crump phase of youth, he accidentally shot a native duck for a campfire dinner. <i>(May 2000,North & South,116</i>)

### Barry light
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Then caver ingenuity came to the rescue in the form of Hamilton speleologist Barry Weir. Weir came up with a 100w helmet light set like a cherry in an aluminium throwaway pie dish which when charged will give 20-30 minutes' illumination. These 45c battery-powered devices even provided light when caving divers went underwater ... The device has been so successful it has been used subsequently. The inventor now has his name in lights. Crews in Dunedin refer to them as `Barry lights'. <i>(Jun 18, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

### Bartlett's rata
 
 <i>n.</i> [<i>Metrosideros bartlettii</i>]

>  There are ten native species of rata in New Zealand all in the genus Metrosideros. Four of these form trees (northern rata, southern rata, Bartlett's rata and Parkinson's rata) and the remaining six species are all lianes. <i>(Jan 21, 2010,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.nzpcn.org.nz/page.asp?flora_vascular_flowering_plants_dicots_rata]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> (Environment) a rare and and endangered white-flowering rata, <i> Meterosideros bartlettii</i>, found mainly in the north of the North Island.

>  Hundreds of people voted in the New Zealand Plant Conservation Network's annual quest for the country's favourite native plant .. Bartlett's rata got the vote because it was less "boring" than its relative the pohutukawa ... <i>(Nov 24, 2006,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.landcareresearch.co.nz/news/release.asp?Ne_OD=219]

>  The tree ratas include the widespread northern rata and southern rata and the rare North Cape Metrosideros bartletti, distinguished by its small white flowers. <i>(Mar 13, 2004,Dominion Post,E10</i>)

>  The critically threatened Bartlett's rata (<i>Metrosideros bartletti</i>) grows at the very tip of the North Island, with only 30 adult trees remaining of this species. <i>(Mar 2003,Open Space,No. 60/18</i>)

>  Bartlett's rata (Metrosideros bartletti) grow at the very tip of the North Island only 30 adult trees remain of this species [sic]. <i>(Feb 19, 2004,Wellington Contact,31</i>)

>  The rarest of New Zealand's 12 species of <i>Metrosideros</i> was discovered less than 20 years ago by Papakura teacher and botanist the late John Bartlett... Like the northern rata, <i>M. robusta</i>, Bartlett's rata usually starts life as an epiphyte perched in the branches of another tree or ponga... Although some of New Zealand's shrub ratas have white flowers, this species is the only tree to have them. <i>(Jul 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 47/12</i>)

>  Without dedicated effort and intrepid plant "hunters", such as those who make up the New Zealand Plant Conservation Network, we might never enjoy such strange and wonderful sights as .. the blaze of late-spring blaze [sic] of Barrett's [sic] rata Metrosideros bartlettii (nationally critical). <i>(Oct 23, 2004,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### base walloper
 
 <i>n.</i> an alteration of gen. military base-wallah (with pun on <i>base</i> `bum'), a soldier, esp. an officer, employed behind the lines at base; a member of Base Staff; base-walloper, -wallower, bludger, bum-buzzard.

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> The New Zealanders' preferred version of [base wallah]. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,36</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> He is a shrewd head, but I think he would give a man a fair go although he is a base-walloper. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `The working class can kiss my arse, I've got a bludger's job at last,' sang Smithy to the tune of the Red Flag ... `All the boys sing it whenever they pass Bludger's Hill. ... Makes the base-wallahs mad.' ... `Almost makes a man want to be a base walloper. ... A nice quiet life. Nothing to do except get up in the morning and get the shaving water, make the bed, polish some buttons to keep away from parade, queue for a feed, siesta, tea, a beer at the Naafi in the evening, a game of two-up and so to bed'. <i>(Sep 1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,147</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `I tell ya I know a bit about the Luger', I reply ... `You wouldn't get close enough to the sharp end to see one', he snaps. `Base walloper I bet. Bet you never even seen one'. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,182</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>base walloper </i>Kiwi version of a base wallah, or military pen pusher; `wallah', Hindi `wala', connected with, was habitually added on to functions to suggest functionary in Anglo-Indian army; here refined by pun on base as bum. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,13</i>)

 <i>adj.</i>

### bash
 
 <i>n.</i> a booze-session; a party; a spree, a drinking bout. [ca1945 e.g. Jim's wedding was a pretty good bash. (cp We had a pretty good bash at Jim's wedding.)]

>  [caption] HAVING A BALL: Judy Rossouw and Michelle Stephenson raffle a rugby ball signed by the Kiwi rugby league team at last week's bash. <i>(Feb 7, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,3</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> In New Zealand, the term <i>bash</i> for a party where drink is the chief attraction is heard (or was, some years ago among Wellington students) and <i>hophead</i> for a person who drinks regularly. The term <i>hophead</i> is (or was) used in convivial greetings, `Hello, hophead', the implication that the person addressed was a persistent drinker being equivalent to calling him a decent joker. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,132</i>)

>  There was little sign of panic and in one nearby building a group of elderly people continued their Christmas bash for several hours before police quietly removed them. <i>(Dec 8, 1998,Dominion,1</i>)

>  The resultant "bash" was a beauty. <i>(Dec 31, 1947,NZ Observer,16</i>)

>  What he spent on beer week-nights would total no more than what most jokers spent on their Saturday bashes. <i>(1948,Landfall,6/111</i>) [NOTES: [OED2 1948 LF II 111 chk ref and cit](Ballantyne)]

>  `Hell, he has had a bash,' said Cliff. `Spent the whole night on the tiles, eh? ...' <i>(1984,Some Other Country,98</i>) [NOTES: [spree],1950 Gaskell in 1984 Some Other Country 98]

>  An informal PARTY. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,11</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> They tell me Breck held his liquor pretty well. Had to be really tanked up to show any signs. That sounds like a bash. <i>(1984,Murder in Vain,164</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a wild uncontrolled blow by a shearer.

>  <b>BOWEN</b> <i>(1956,Wool Away!,155</i>)

 <i>n.</i> corporal punishment with the cane (pl.).  <i>v.</i> (See [cf. boulder bash](../B#boulder-bash),[bush bash](../B#bush-bash),[etc](../E#etc))

>  `Push', `bash', `crash' occur pretty frequently in describing progress in accounts of tramping trips, and tell a tale of scrub and tangled bush. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,30</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  We would stream bash or plod our way up the ridges. <i>Ibid.</i> 90 We dropped into the Arahura River valley and bashed over some of the biggest boulders I ever want to see. <i>(1978,Manawatu Tramping Club Jubilee,71</i>)

>  At the first bluff he sidled up the spur but he got off the discs and had to bash through the lawyers and windfalls. <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

### bash hat
 
 <i>n.</i> a protective helmet

>  We put on full wetsuits and booties, with freezing-workers' gumboots and bash hats fitted with lights... <i>(Oct 18, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

### bash up
 
 <i>v.</i> [1984 16 F E17 Pakuranga Coll. 19 BASH (BOTCH) UP, A to build, make (quickly or inexpertly); to knock up, together.]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Take P.O.W. for example. Everyone had bashed up a duff for himself at one stage or another. <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,140</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to beat [->1951 16+ M 26 Marlborough C. 30B BASH UP -->1951 16+ M 32 St Mary's, Wgton 30B BASH-UP -->1951 15 F 9 St Mary's, Wgton 30A BASH-UP -->1953 16-17 M A35 Thames DHS 30 BASH UP [M3] -->1984 14 F E136Wgton Girls C. 30 BASH UP]

>  `... he was in my class at school.' `Sure, Rupert. You remember. I used to bash you up. Give you the old Chinese burn. You used to enjoy it too.' <i>(1984,NZ Short Stories,96</i>) [NOTES: (Buried Treasure Old Bones)]



 
 <i>v.</i> to cane [1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 23 BASHED UP [school discipline]]

### bash, the
 
 <i>n.</i> a hiding or beating; (fig.) a hostile reception.

>  Kids getting "the bash' from Mum and Dad - these things were the norm for a significant number of people around me. <i>(Jun 24, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  ...a five-year-old talks of seeing blood on the walls and dragging his brother to safety as his mum "gets the bash"... <i>(Aug 29, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  Mr Harawira, who earlier told Prime Minister Helen Cklark and her ministers they had got the "bash" at Waitangi's Ti Marae, might be loud and in your face, but his mum is the power behind the show. <i>(May 1, 2004,Dominion Post,E2</i>)

>  The incident also sparked an emotional response from Associate Maori Affairs Minister John Tamihere, who said there were elements who had already indicated "that it's good for the prime minister to get the bash". <i>(May 6, 2004,Dominion,A3</i>)

>  He was given a chance. And blew it - this time giving his missus the bash and threatening to kill her. <i>(Aug 8, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C8</i>)

### bash-crash
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It's fine to have a fanatical bash-crash merchant in midfield but with all the talent New Zealand can field we need rather more than that in the middle. <i>(Oct 1, 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,2</i>)

### basher
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bash n. (shearing)](../B#bash-n.-(shearing)))

>  <b>BOWEN</b> BASHER. A shearer who uses the above type of blows. [BASH. A wild uncontrolled blow by a shearer.] <i>(1956,Wool Away!,155</i>)

### basic slag/slag
 
 <i>n.</i> a fertiliser [see slag]

>  Advt.] V.O.B. Basic Slag is cheaper this year. The value of Slag Top-Dressing need not be stressed. <i>(Dec 18, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,10</i>)

>  The relative merits of superphosphate and basic slag for pasture top-dressing are much discussed in certain districts, particularly North Taranaki and North Auckland. Many experienced farmers are firmly of the opinion that slagged pastures have better milk production and fattening capacities than ones treated with superphosphate. How much this may be due to a greater effect from slag, how much to some effect on the mineral content of the herbage, and how much due to management it is impossible to say. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,389</i>)

>  Mr E. M. Smith stated before the members of the Iron and Steel Institute, London, that the slag from the ironsand smelting was worth 15s per ton as a manure, and it was quite equal to the basic slag from the furnaces at Home. <i>(Aug 17, 1896,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  SCOTIA BASIC SLAG is absolutely the cheapest Phosphoric Acid available to the farmers. <i>(Jun 21, 1907,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  WE ARE NOW BOOKING ORDERS FOR BASIC SLAG, MADE IN ENGLAND, IN LOTS TO SUIT FARMERS TO BE DELIVERED ABOUT JUNE NEXT. <i>(Apr 25, 1913,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

>  It is not correct that import licenses for basic slag for the coming season have been reduced. <i>(Aug 18, 1949,Southern Cross (Wgton),2</i>)

### basic wage
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a national minimum wage set according to age and gender (now called minimum wage).

>  The Court of Arbitration... also found that an adult female worker should have a basic wage of GBP1 16s. <i>(1945,NZ Parliamentary Debates,272/463</i>)

>  The fixing of the basic wage by the Court of Arbitration at GBP3/16/- for men at 21 years old and GBP1/16/- for women has provoked much adverse comment. <i>(Nov 11, 1936,Tomorrow,3(1) : 2</i>)

>  The wage level will remain below what it ought to be, and this is particularly true of those on or near the basic wage. <i>(Mar 1951,Public Service Journal,9</i>)

>  The Labour Government.. decreed that a [farm] cadet be paid the basic wage. <i>(Feb 15, 1956,Weekly News,45</i>)

>  No good purpose would be served by attacking the basic wage. <i>(Apr 29, 1937,King Country Chronicle,5</i>)

>  Everywhere they met the same reply - that they were not learned in any trade and therefore not worth the basic wage. <i>(May 6, 1937,King Country Chronicle,5</i>)

### basil
 
 <i>n.</i> used to distinguish from <i>roan</i>, one tanned with sumach.] Tanned sheepskin used for the manufacture of light leather.

>  <b>GISBORNE</b> ... basils (tanned sheep) <i>(1888,Colony of NZ,299</i>)

>  <i>Basil</i>: Tanned sheepskin used for the manufacture of light leather. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,52</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Reaching the end of the walk, and things just going fair, he returned and again said to the tall Moree Myall, `You are still cutting too often - go easy,' but the light limbed Australian continued to strip the `Basil' from the unfortunate stud ewe he was shearing. Standing by, the Moree native engaged in taking off the trimmings around the udder, then deliberately swept the glistening steel of his razor shears across the teats of the stud ewe, and deliberately shore them off level with the belly line. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,49</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The weight of the irons is therefore borne by the waist. Leather `basils' are worn by the prisoners round the ankle, to keep the iron from chafing the skin. <i>(1875,Otago Guardian</i>) [NOTES: c1875 Otago Guardian in 1980 Tonkin Dunedin Gaol 27]

### basin
 
 <i>n.</i> see cits (in frequent and poss. spec. use in NZ).

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> This place forms a basin with low ranges and made hills. [ed fn] Presumably gravel hills - i.e. the remnants of high gravel terraces formed (made) by the river in the past. <i>(Mar 27, 1864,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Mar 27 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 404]

>  In 1868 my orders were to ... see that ... no hill or spur had more sheep on it than it could well carry. <i>(1907,TrNZI,39/73</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> ... an abrupt succession of ranges, gullies, and basins. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/179</i>)

>  <i>Basin</i>. Open valley near the top of a hill;all the very highest tops of a run are sometimes called `the basins'. (C.) <i>(Sep 9, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Basin</i>: a depression or basin in a hillside. Most creeks originate in a basin. <i>(1947,Wayleggo</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> If sheep there sometimes grew fat it would be as deer occasionally grow fat on the tops and basins above our beech forests; ... <i>(Mar 19, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,26,1961 ed.</i>)

>  From the top basin we sidled up tiresome scree, ... and at last emerged on the top of the divide. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,41</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ~: A depression or basin in a hillside. Creeks often originate in basins at the head of a gully. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,185</i>)

>  <b>PINNEY</b> Even though there was a thaw in early May, the snow returned and lay deep on the tops and halfway down the basins. <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,53</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [ high country pastoralism and mustering] an open depression or near-flat area close to the top of a hill or ridge.

>  <b>HAY</b> An abrupt succession of ranges, gullies and basins. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,179</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Above the bush line the country opened into great carpet-grass basins. <i>(1949,High Country Days,140</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The country above the lake, with its miles of bush, great carpet-grass basins and heavy river flats is ideal country for deer .. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,169</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> basin: open and nearly flat area near the top of a ridge or hill. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,5</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> Since that night there has never been a reporting of a kea'd sheep in Jack Hall's basin. [carpet-grass basin] <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,135</i>)

### basin check
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a form of surface irrigation used in orchards and on flat land.

>  There a number of methods [sic] of irrigating land, of which the following are the principal:- .. 6. Basin-check. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,25</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> Basin Check Irrigation. Rectangular basins are formed around individual trees or small groups of trees, and the water turned from a head ditch into each of these basins in turn. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,193</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> ... the border-dyke system or furrow system would be used in preference to basin checks, owing to difficulty in cultivation with the latter system. [basin-check] <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,195</i>)

### Basin, the
 
 <i>n.</i> the Basin Reserve, a municipal sports ground (esp. for Assoc. Football and Cricket) made from a reclaimed tidal shipping basin)

>  AAA. Reserves ... that on Thorndon Flat is occupied by an extensive native pah, which the natives have not alienated ... The second was intended for a basin, an idea suggested probably by the swampy character of the ground around it; but besides that, such a project is of very questionable advantage; ...] <i>(1842,GBPP,(No.569) 185</i>) [NOTES: [1841 Oct 20 F.Mathew to Hobson in GBPP 1842 (No.569) 185]

>  If the Wellington Cricket Association has any sort of a surplus, it should at once indulge in the luxury of a more up-to-date scoring board at the Basin. <i>(Apr 2, 1910,NZ Truth,249/6/2</i>)

>  [Advt], Cracker Day at the Cricket Will New Zealand sink at the Basin? Full coverage of the final day of the Rothman's International One Day Cricket Series closes in ... ?.br;Used in Hutt Valley - spread from the market garden of Bartos. <i>(Feb 20, 1982,Dominion,9</i>)

### basket
 
 <i>n.</i> an offering of food [See also "plate" and "basket social"]

>  The part that needs clearing up is "Gents 1s; ladies, 1s, and basket." <i>(Dec 21, 1920,Otago Witness,1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a fish bin

>  Vessels were often under catch restrictions: no more than four or five baskets (the term 'baskets' was still freely used despite wicker baskets having been replaced by plastic Nellie bins) of species such as trevally and dogfish. <i>(2004,Hooked,305</i>) [NOTES: [note: this term 'Nellie bin' is an error. The correct term is 'Nally' bin, which is a brand name. Source: Tony Walker, Director, Fishing Industry Services, Christchurch, agent for Nally bins.]]

### basket fungus
 
 <i>n.</i> an unpleasant-smelling fungus, <i>Ileodictyon cibarium</i>, that expands to release a latticed structure from a white sphere (also called devil's purse or stink egg, lattice or net fungus, paruwhatitiri, thunder-dirt). (See [paruwhatitiri](../P#paruwhatitiri),[net fungus](../N#net-fungus))

>  <b>CROWE</b> Basket fungus starts off growing rather like a puffball 3-4 cm wide, but later, when the ball bursts, a white basket-like shape breaks out... The Maori ate the thick gelatinous volva or outer shell when young and before it burst. <i>(1997,Field Guide to Native Edible Plants of NZ,123</i>)

>  <i>Clathrus cibarius</i>, THE `BIRD-CAGE FUNGUS'. ... This fungus, known variously as `basket fungus,'`lattice fungus,', `Devil's purse,' `<i>tutae whetu,' `tutae kehua</i>,' belongs to the Phallaceae, a family included in the Basidiomycetes. It is found commonly throughout New Zealand during the late autumn and early spring. [Distribution.- Australia; Chile; ... New Zealand] <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/247</i>) [NOTES: Cunningham]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Matakupenga ... 3. Clathrus cibarium</i>, net or basket fungus. = <i>pukurau.</i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,188</i>)

>  The Maori treated unexpanded plants of the basket fungus as a great luxury. <i>(Oct 1959,Tararua Tramper,10</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> ... the Phalloids to which belong such conspicuous fungi as the SCARLET STINKHORN (<i>Aseroe rubra</i>) and the BASKET FUNGUS (<i>Clathrus cibarius</i>). [40]The BASKET FUNGUS or LATTICE FUNGUS spreads its globular latticed network a conspicuous white, slimy structure with an unpleasant odour, that never fails to amaze its beholders. Its miraculous and sudden appearance caused the ancient Maori to term it `thunder dirt'. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,39,1961 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1961 ed.]

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>Clathrus cibarius</i>, basket fungus. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,12</i>)

>  [photo captions] (1) Honey mushroom (2) Red tobacco pouch fungus (3) Violet tobacco pouch fungi ... (5) Basket or lattice fungus. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,64</i>)

>  In the home garden, a fairly common fungus found in potting mix, compost and bark mulch is the Ileodictyon cibarius white basket fungus. It has an eerie, skeletal appearance and is fascinating to watch grow. <i>(May 18, 2007,Nelson Mail,B15</i>)

>  At one stage of its growth, the open-look basket-fungus, Clathrus cibarius, is edible. <i>(Jun 8, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,64</i>)

### basket social
 
 <i>n.</i> a social event where food is raffled or sold to raise money

>  A well attended basket social was held at the residence of Mr. and Mrs. P. Maclean, Kelvin Road, Waimumu on Friday evening, ... <i>(Apr 11, 1921,Mataura Ensign</i>) [NOTES: in 1966 Barry In the Lee of the Hokonuis 363]

>  Basket social: a1949 North Auckland. As I understood it a function for making money. People bring a `basket' (in the sense `Ladies, a basket') of food which is auctioned and often re-auctioned. <i>(Jun 7, 1952,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: R. Mason Letter]

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> (Joe Gasparich, Northland kauri country), The way in which the bush people - the bushmen and their wives - stuck to one another is shown by the way they rallied round in the case of a family that was in need. A common method of raising money was called a `basket social'. A hall would be used for the function, and the people of the district would be invited to bring along baskets. Now these baskets were made of any material at all at hand, and it was remarkable the ingenuity and workmanship shown in making them. ... In the basket its maker put a supper, the food cooked at home ... [151] Now on the night of the basket social, all the baskets would be handed in ... They would then be auctioned, and the purchaser of the basket would have the pleasure and the honour of having supper with the provider of the basket. ... Now I've seen them pay twelve ... pounds in those days for a basket, just to make sure that the girl of the boy's choice saw that he really meant business. ... Every penny of that would go to the family in need ... <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,150</i>)

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> Basket socials were always a stir [in Northland c1920s]. ... Basket socials were the nearest we got to bacchanalia - tipsy cake in large doses could have strangely affecting results. The baskets were put up for auction just before the supper waltz; highest bidder got basket and girl - the girl, sometimes it turned out, for life. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,146</i>)

>  <b>BOSWEL</b> And then the mill workers organized a big basket social for us, and with the money they arranged a grand picnic. <i>Ibid.</i> 153 [pre WW1, Northland bush], A basket social took the form of a dance, with an auction of supper baskets. Each girl would make a pretty basket and fill it with good things for supper. When supper-time came, the baskets were auctioned from the stage and keen was the rivalry and competition for some of the baskets, young blades emptying their pockets and often dipping into their bank accounts in trying to out-bid their rivals. <i>(1955,Dim Horizons,31</i>) [NOTES: Northland 1890's]

>  A basket social and dance.. for Maori soldiers.. turned out an unqualified success. <i>(Jul 18, 1918,Hawke's Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> A common method of raising money was called a 'basket social'... The people of the district would be invited to bring along baskets. Now these baskets were made of any material at all to hand... In the baskets, the maker put a supper... Nobody knew who was the owner of the basket. They would then be auctioned. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,150</i>)

 <i>n.</i> former name of netball [date of name change? a general English change?]
