### basketing night
 
 <i>n.</i> when racing pigeons are assembled at the starting point before a race

>  For this race, they made their bets on "basketing night", the previous Thursday, when they gathered to place race rings on the birds' legs and put them into boxes for Friday's truck journey to Levin. <i>(Jun 11, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

### basking shark
 
 <i>n.</i> reremai [not NZ specific] (See [reremai](../R#reremai))

>  The basking shark is very large, its length ranging from thirty to thirty-five feet, and it usually gives from five to seven barrels of oil, sometimes much more. <i>(Jan 4, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  The Sea-Serpent ..There are almost as many and diverse modes of explaining him away and turning him into a specimen of a known marine species - squid, ribbon-fish, whale, basking shark, etc., just as the gradually awaking senses of litle Alice turned the monstrous Red Queen of Looking-glass Land into a harmless black domestic kitten. <i>(Jan 26, 1905,NZ Tablet,2</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i>Selachus maximus </i>.. Basking shark. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,115</i>)

>  <i>Notice of the Occurrence of the Basking Shark </i>(Selache maxima, L.) <i> in New Zealand. </i> <i>(1891,TrNZI,23/126</i>)

>  The basking-shark, <i>Cetorhinus maximus,</i> is among the largest of living fishes. It is ... known from all oceans and all temperate zones. The name has been applied because of its peculiar habit of lying motionless in the sunshine on the surface. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/266</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Cetohinus maximus </i>(Gunner). BASKING-SHARK; REREMAI. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,8,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Cetorhinus maximus:</i> Basking shark. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,71</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> BASKING SHARK (<i>Halsydrus maccoyi </i>), Reremai of the Maoris, is our largest shark. ... The Reremai occurs both in New Zealand and Australia. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,62</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Basking Shark <i>Halsydrus maximus maccoyi </i> (Barrett) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,397</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 397]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Shark, basking .. .. reremai .. .. <i>Cetorhinus maximus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family CETORHINIDAE contains the enormous basking shark or reremai (<i>Cetorhinus </i>) ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,198</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Basking shark Cetorhinus maximus </i> The only species in its family (Cetorhinidae), occuring in many cool temperate oceans ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,27</i>)

>  [species name] basking shark .. [scientific name] <i>Cetorhinus maximus </i> .. [other name(s)] -- <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] shark, basking 164-73 ...] SHARKS, BASKING 164-73 ...basking shark 164-73, BASKING SHARKS 164-73 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  The great basking shark, or mhor, is always harpooned; it is found floating, or asleep near the surface of the water, and is then struck with a harpoon eight feet long. <i>(Jan 24, 1851,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  The shark which was recently found on the beach at Kapiti is now reported by Professor Kirk to be of the ceporhinus maximum species (basking shark). <i>(Jan 22, 1919,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

### bass
 
 <i>v.</i> to swindle [criminals']

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BASS (v) Swindle. British racecourse slang. Late 19th century. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,245</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [A transf. use of northern hemisphere bass, a seafish of the perch family.] 1. Simplex <i>Polyprion moeone</i> of the family Percichthyidae, =bass groper. See also black bass 2. below.

>  Bass. <i>Polyprion americanus ... </i> <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/269</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps]

>  The fishermen who go out to the 100-150-fathom water off [Otago] frequently catch ... a huge groper, which they call `bass groper', and which seems to be specifically distinct from the ordinary hapuka. They say it is common off Wellington, and is there known as `black bass'. [216] ... I ... made inquiries of local [Wellington] fish-merchants, and learned that ... from Kaikoura a large groper was occasionally received, and was called a `bass' ... <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/215</i>) [NOTES: Waite]

>  POLYPRION AMERICANUS (Bloch and Schneider). <i>Bass; Moeone</i>. Generally to be secured in deeper water than the groper. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts. <i>(Jun 1921,TrNZI,IV/114</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps : Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> Polyprion americanus (Bloch & Schn.). BASS; MOEONE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,32</i>) [NOTES: 1971 ed.]

>  <b>POWELL</b> Most of the very large [hapuku or groper] are from 100 fathoms or more and are a different species, the Bass, <i>P[olyprion] moeone</i>, distinguished by having a much larger head and a deeper body. <i>((9/0/1947),Native Animals of NZ,70</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Moeone ... Polyprion americanus</i>, bass; a fish. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,205</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> BASS Polyprion moeone (Phillipps) ... Maori name: MOEONE. <i>Ibid.</i> 169 Bass <i>Polyprion moeone</i> Black Bass; Bass Groper <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,66</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BASS ... <i>Other names: Polyprion moeone</i>; bass-groper; moeone, toti (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,226</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK (ed)</b> Bass, Bass-groper .. moeone, toti, <i>Polyprion moeone</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK (ed)</b> Bass, Bass-groper .. moeone, toti, <i>Polyprion moeone</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BASS [<i>Polyprion moeone</i>] ... Bass-groper and the Maori moeone are alternative names. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,52</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family SERRANIDAE. The bass (<i>Polyprion moeone</i> ...) and the groper (<i>P. oxygeneios</i> ...) are two hefty members. ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,219</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> GROPER (two species) (a) Scientific name: (i)<i> Polyprion oxygeneios</i> (ii) <i>Polyprion moeone</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) New Zealand Groper; Hapuku (ii) Bass. (c) Other common names: (i) Hapuka, Stone-eye, Blue-nose, Bonita (ii) Deepsea Groper. (d) Principal localities where taken: [Mainly easten coasts, Foveaux, and Chathams] ... [27 Groper and Bass] should not be confused with the Australian and tropical groupers which they resemble superficially. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,26</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BASS Polyprion moeone</i> ... The bass is similar in appearance, colour and size to the hapuku ... It may be distinguished ... by having a deeper body, a larger eye ... and a short blunt snout ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,204</i>) [NOTES: 1984 ed.]

>  <i>bass n. (pl. basses</i> or collect. <i>bass</i>) common perch; other spiny-finned fish of perch family; similar NZ fish. [OE] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,57</i>) [NOTES: cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 88; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 41]

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Bass Polyprion moeone</i> ... Sometimes (and correctly) called bass groper. ... The species is very similar to the wreckfish, <i>P. americanus</i> of the northern Atlantic and Mediterranean. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,83</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> [index to common names 283-293], bass 1288-1311, BASSES, SEA 1250-1316 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  Taane cites Nelson's Gathering as the event that probably opened a lot of people's eyes to the possibilities of big bass, beating sun and outdoor beauty. <i>(Dec 31, 2000,Sunday Star Times,D1</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> Bass Polyprion moeone Black Bass; Bass Groper <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,169</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293], bass, black 1288, 1292, 1299 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The fishermen who go out to the 100-150-fathom water off [Otago] frequently catch ... a huge groper, which they call `bass groper', and which seems to be specifically distinct from the ordinary hapuka. They say it is common off Wellington, and is there known as `black bass'. [216] ... I ... made inquiries of local [Wellington] fish-merchants, and learned that ... from Kaikoura a large groper was occasionally received, and was called a `bass' ... <i>(1913,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,45/215</i>) [NOTES: Waite]

>  <i>Polyprion americanus</i> Bloch and Schneider. This deep-water representative of the genus is now identified by Waite ... with the wreck-fish of the Atlantic. It is occasionally taken outside Otago Heads, and is known by the local fishermen as `bass groper' or `black bass'. <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/228</i>) [NOTES: Thomson, Otago)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARROTT</b> Until 1911 the New Zealand Bass was considered to be a deepwater form of the common Groper or Hapuka. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,67</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BINNEY</b> `Sea-bass; caught the bugger out at Awatuna Mouth on Sunday,' said Les. <i>(1985,Long Lives the King,52</i>)

### bass groper
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bass](../B#bass))

### basshead
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Taane puts it down primarily to "Aotearoa's growing family of bassheads" and our beautiful outdoor environment. <i>(Dec 31, 2000,Sunday Star Times,D1</i>)

### bastard
 
 <i>n.</i> fellow, chap, joker, or as a term of address for a friend, esp. old bastard [As an informal term of both kindly and unkindly ref. and address without suggestion of illegitimacy more associated with Aust. than NZ, though as common here since the late 19c. as in that larger place: see AND: 1. of persons 1892; 2. of anything considered disagreeable or unpleasant 1919.]

>  The Peninsula vocabulary was notorious. ... [16] Reminiscences of panders' slang were used with the quaintest inappositeness. But pre-eminently the word `bastard' passed into common use. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,15</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> You're an extravagant bastard, Tony. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,30 et passim</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Dan Freel's conversation was plentifully interlarded with oaths and obscenities that are - or would have been in the 80's - quite unprintable. At first it seemed an arresting, if not an interesting idiosyncracy, but one soon sickened of it. For instance he used the word bastard as the Americans use guy, and as we were then beginning to use bloke, to mean anyone about whom he was speaking ... <i>(1944,Moonshine,87</i>) [NOTES: [Context NZ-Irish community c1880's]]

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> When used by one friend to another, is a routine expression in wry good humour. .in 4;`Why doesn't one of you lazy bastards take a turn at boiling the billy, why does the hardest worker (i.e. the speaker) have to do the extra little jobs?' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],43</i>)

>  <i>bastard n</i>. fellow, term of affection, e.g. <i>he's a good old bastard. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> (A) It means what you think it does, but it can also be a term of ENDEARMENT and ADMIRATION. You are a right bastard, aren't you? (see: hard shot, false friends) (B) a common variety of coarse metal FILE. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,12</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> have a bastard on (with) somebody: to be angry with, or upset with, or angry towards, somebody [cf. to have a cob on ca1943 p.c. Donald A. Singe (Sep 1952)] (See [cf. to have a cob on](../T#to-have-a-cob-on))



 
 <i>adj.</i> as a distinguishing epithet in names of plants which resemble (but often cannot match qualities of) their namesakes; or occas. have displeasing or nasty (`bastardly') qualities (e.g. astard-grass): see birch; grass, bastard grass; pine, black; puriri, sandalwood; thistle, nodding; thistle, scotch [check that not deleted in editing]; totara.



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUNT</b> We'd make dumplings, too, fried up in the camp oven. Buggers afloat we called them [c1916] ... And what did we call the poached eggs? A pair of bastards on a raft, and we'd have tits on toast - belly pickled pork on toast. I still use those old names; everyone laughs at me. <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five,29</i>) [NOTES: (Bill Henderson)]



 
 <i>n.</i> blow like a bastard. [of weather] to blow strongly

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> When we got to Queen Charlotte it was pissing with rain and blowing like a bastard and I thought, ahhh, this is more like home. <i>Ibid.</i> 44 The next morning I heard the forecast: it was blowing like a bastard, south-west. <i>(1998,On the Next Tide,41</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [1984 16-17 M E116 Pakuranga Coll.3 BASTARD [F16 M13] -->1984 14 F E119Wgton Girls C. 3 BASTARD [something/one disliked F7]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> often as bastard of a, and fig. of a thing, event, incident, person

>  Two witnesses in the two-up cases at Wellington mentioned that `bastard' was a common expression among the habitue's of the `school'. The word is usually preceded by the great colonial adjective, which flourishes in the vocabulary of assorted sizes and degrees of luridity. <i>(Aug 24, 1907,NZ Truth,114/5/7</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> `He's a bastard'. Guy used the term not for its dictionary meaning, but because among New Zealanders, no term expressed greater contempt. <i>(1937,Civilian into Soldier,29</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> A Padre ... muttering `The bastards!' ... in accents that gave to that common Anzac expletive a new and searing potency. <i>Ibid.</i> 112 It's a bastard of a country. <i>(1944,Troop Target,27</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> I thought it was a bastard of a place. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,99</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> A man offensive to the extreme degree. .in 4;`You'd hit me, you bastard, would you?' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],42</i>)

>  <b>DUCKWORTH</b> [as an intensifier], It's raining like a bastard, and we didn't put any [milk] bottles out. I'll have to go to the dairy. <i>(1963,Barbarous Tongue, A,181</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> (See [cod](../C#cod),[red; dory; skate; trumpeter](../R#red;-dory;-skate;-trumpeter))



 
<b>3</b> <i>adj.</i>

>  ... a small, shallow excavation in trachyte; ... which is here full of silicious segregations of irregular outline ... of a quartz-like character and bluish-white colour (`bastard quartz', in miners' phrase). <i>(1875,AJHR,11/3/31</i>) [NOTES: Report on Goldfields,]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He's the real nigger in the woodpile ... Some of you jokers ought to get off the Head's back and have a go at the real bastard of the bush, the self-appointed headmaster. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,104</i>)

### bastard birch
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Weinmannia racemosa</i>, kamahi.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. the Nelson hill country was predominantly `bastard' birch and of comparatively low fertility ... <i>(1973,Big Country,10,1977 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The birch timber - commonly known as 'bastard' birch - rots in no time and we had continual breakdowns along the line. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,107</i>)

>  <i> bastard birch [=] kamahi </i>[s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,4</i>)

### bastard black pine
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> indigenous pine, the miro. [<i>Prumnopitys ferruginea</i>, previously <i>Podocarpus ferruginea</i>, aka miro, the wishing tree, Hinehopu's tree.]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Podocarpus ferruginea</i>, Don. Miro, Bastard Black-pine of Otago, ornamental and useful tree; attains a height of 40-60 feet, trunk 2-3 feet in diameter. A useful wood, but not so durable as the matai or true black-pine wood <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,123</i>)

>  Miro (bastard black pine) .. Podocarpus ferruginea .. Wood hard, elastic, strong, and grain prettily figured; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 408 Miro (bastard black pine) slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,12</i>)

### bastard dory
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Dory </i>... its close relative, is called <i>Bastard dory</i> in New Zealand, and also <i>Boar-fish</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,21</i>)

>  DoZ.ry (d&omacr.Z.r&ybreve.), n. The fish. [Add.] <i>Bastard dory </i>(Zoo.l.), The Australian and Tasmanian name for the New Zealand boarfish (<i>Cyttus Australis</i>). <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2021</i>)

### bastard red cod
 
 <i>n.</i> two spp. often confused: <i>Pseudophycis breviuscula</i> northern bastard red cod and <i>P. barbata </i>southern bastard red cod

>  <i>Pseudophycis breviusculus</i> Richardson. Bastard red cod. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/405</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> BASTARD RED COD <i>Physiculus (Pseudophycis) breviusculus</i> (Richardson) The vernacular name of this fish was first given to it by commercial fishmen who, when one was hooked and brought to the surface, would exclaim: ` - another bastard Red Cod!' meaning to say it was not a true Red Cod; the name stuck to this fish and was used by line fishermen of Otago. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,173</i>)

>  The bastard red cod (<i>Pseudophycis breviusculus</i>) is stouter, a deeper red in colour, and has a rounded tail fin. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/372</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>NORTHERN BASTARD RED COD Pseudophycis breviuscula (Physiculus breviusculus)</i> ... The bastard red cod is similar in shape to the red cod but is much smaller ... <i>Ibid.</i> 144 <i>SOUTHERN BASTARD RED COD</i> (Bearded rock cod) <i>Pseudophycis barbata</i> ... The southern bastard red cod, known as the bearded rock cod in Australia, is similar to the red cod but does not reach such a large size. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,143,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Bastard red cods</i> There are two species related and rather similar to the red cod ... they are not easily separated from each other. <i>Pseudophycis barbata</i> occurs round the South Island ... and <i>P. breviuscula </i>around the northern North Island. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,58</i>)

>  nf W3 <i>(1883,Royal Commission on the Fisheries of Tasmania,35</i>)

### bat
 
 <i>n.</i> pekapeka the native bat.

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 1. Vespertilio tuberculatus. G. Forster. ... [182 quotes Polack 1/304] <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/181</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> New Zealand possesses only ... the bat (<i>Pekapeka</i>, two species) and a small indigenous rat (<i>Kiore</i>). <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),160</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Pekapeka</i>, n. <i>1 bat. </i> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,132</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> In the morning I described the new Shag & a new lark, & George drew the Shag and a new <i>Bat</i>, which we got. [ed. fn <i>Vespertilio tuberculatus</i> of <i>Descr. Anim</i>. p. 62 ... <i>(May 18, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/283</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We had already discovered a small species of bats in the woods ... <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/222</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the kip

>  The big brown pan that held the `bat'/ Was trembling like a leaf. <i>(1917,NZEF Chronicles,137</i>)

>  <b>343/7/3</b> Even the metallic ring of the coins could be heard, followed by the suppressed sights of `heads' bettors as `tails' were cried while `two ones', `barred,' `heads a dollar', `tails' ditto, `pass the bat', and other equally quaint and even classic chants, were wafted on the midnight breezes. ... Much to `Truth's' deep regret, for it detests having to give the show away, lately, at Miramar, when some road-making or mountain-razing work has been going on, some of them have engaged in the simple yet sinful process of tossing two bright copper pennies, with a bit of wood, popularly known as `the bat' or `the kip' and, therefore, in the eye of the law are hardened criminals. <i>(Jan 20, 1912,NZ Truth</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a hundred-dollar note

>  There are terms for money: <i>bat</i> for a hundred-dollar note, a <i>brick</i> and <i>bluebird chips</i> for a ten-dollar note, <i>lettuce</i> for a twenty and <i>tomato</i> for a hundred-dollar note. <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  ... we have two species, which are familiarly known by the names of the short-eared [220] bat (<i>Chalinolobus tuberculatus</i>), and the long-eared bat (<i>Mystacina tuberculata</i>). <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/219-220</i>)

>  South Canterbury is one of the few places where the long-tailed bat or pekapeka still survives in a rural landscape. <i>Ibid.</i> 29 Long-tailed bats may use fragments close to larger tracts of forests during their nightly forays that can take thgem up to 20 kilometres from their roost sites. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,304/11</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  ... we have two species, which are familiarly known by the names of the short-eared [220] bat (<i>Chalinolobus tuberculatus</i>), and the long-eared bat (<i>Mystacina tuberculata</i>). <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/219</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  A colony of rare native bats has been discovered at Walton. <i>(Feb 12, 1970,Evening Post,12</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The native New Zealand bat is believed to be almost extinct. <i>(Mar 16, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,21</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] Marooned Tararua bats battle on. Take off your hats to the tenacious Tararua Forest Park bats, still battling on after being marooned in the area by glaciation and volcanic activity more than 90,000 years ago. <i>(Nov 2002,Footnotes,No. 18/3</i>)

### Bata Bullets
 
 <i>n.</i> (type of footwear)

>  Sometimes the details work to evoke deeper emotions, such as when the girl stows her Bata Bullets and singlets neatly away in a holiday cabin, while unspoken tensions wait. <i>(Mar 16, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

### batch
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>CADEY</b> He probably wouldn't have a wife, in which case he'd batch with me. <i>(1933,Broken Pattern,126</i>) [NOTES: See bach]



 
 <i>n.</i> [Collocation batch of scones (occas. of bread).]

>  <b>SCOTT</b> My wife, having as usual forgotten the date and remembered only at the eleventh hour, did what she calls "slapping up a batch of scones" half an hour before she was due to start. <i>(1944,Life with Barbara,53</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> Missus knocked him up a batch of scones last week. <i>(1922,Stories,475</i>) [NOTES: 1984 Alpers]

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> ... kids will come dropping in at all hours with basins of soup, and batches of scones and so on. <i>(1927,Me and Gus,60</i>) [NOTES: 1977 Gus Tomlins ed. Sturm]

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> I never went to the Watson's house but what I had a cake or a batch of scones, or something edible to take home with me. <i>(1924,Follow the Call,14</i>) [NOTES: 1975 ed.]

>  <b>SUMMERS</b> Anna, there's a batch of scones under that tea-towel. <i>(1975,Anna of Strathallan,34</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> It was my scones that won him back. By now I was becoming a very capable cook, and could produce a mouth-watering batch of scones in no time at all. <i>(1982,Myrtle and Sophia,43</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> I never went to visit the Watsons but what I had a cake, or a batch of scones, or something edible to take home with me. <i>(1936,Follow the Call,27</i>)

>  <b>MULGREW</b> That, too, was the day Victor went mad and baked his batch of scones. <i>(1974,Gentleman's Magellan,82</i>)

>  I made a batch of scones and put them in the oven just before I left. <i>(Oct 29, 1927,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,40</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> Missus knocked him up a batch of scones last week. <i>(1922,Stories,476,1984 Alpers ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> No, I won't, I'll bake a batch of scones instead. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,141</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Those boys who "batch" and grow fretful when their turn at the frying pan comes around can comfort themselves by remembering that they are qualifying themselves for a happy married life. <i>(Jun 28, 1913,NZ Observer,17</i>)

### batch up
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to take up residence, pitch a tent or find a place to sleep.

### Bathurst bur/Bathurst burr
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>bur n</i> .-Bathurst bur n. a spiny seed, troublesome in wool; the plant that produces it <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  <i>bur, Bathurst</i> [s] <i>Xanthium spinosum </i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,9</i>)

### Bathurst burr
 
 <i>n.</i> A noxious weed whose seeds, enclosed in hooked burrs, stick to wool; <i>Xanthium spinosum</i> and its fruit [AND 1853 f. the NSW town; W3. Cf biddy-bid(dy), biddy-burr, burr.]

>  `Biddybids detract very materially from the value of the wool ... They are not quite so bad as the Bathurst burr, but are certainly in the same category. <i>(Jan 23, 1869,Otago Witness,36</i>) [NOTES: Morris]

>  .. the `Bathurst Burr' (<i>Xanthium spinosum</i>) of the Australian colonists. Although this plant has been known in Auckland for the last fourteen or fifteen years, it has done little more than maintain its existence. <i>(1877,TrNZI,10/367</i>) [NOTES: Kirk]

>  [weed affecting wool], Spreading in NZ <i>(1899,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,10</i>) [NOTES: Griffiths Collection]

>  BATHURST BURR. - A shrubby annual from 9in. to 3ft. high; ... The seed is ... thickly studded with hooked prickles. Believed to be a native of Chili ... <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,242</i>)

>  Noxious Weeds. ... Californian or Canadian thistle ... Bathurst Burr ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,203</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>Xanthium spinosum</i>, ... Bathurst Burr. North Island: Not uncommon ... from North Cape to the Upper Waikato, rarer southwards to Wellington. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,1076</i>)

>  ~ A spiny Australian plant now naturalized in New Zealand. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),33</i>) [NOTES: 8th ed.]

>  ~ n. plant with long triple spines and burrs which ruin wool [Bathurst, a western town in N.S.W]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),610</i>)

>  <i>Bathurst burr ... n.</i> plant with long triple spines and burrs which ruin wool [Bathurst, a western town in N.S.W]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF [& CALDER]</b> BATHURST BURR (<i>Xanthium spinosum</i>) `is not uncommon in warm and rich soils from the North Cape to the Waikato, rarer southwards to Wellington,' but occurs only on waste lands and corners of cultivated fields. It is a shrubby annual from 9 inches to 3 ft. in height; ... This plant was introduced about 1890, and caused a considerable scare, owing to the fact that in Australia, and one may suppose particularly at Bathurst, it caused depreciation in the wool estimated at 3d. per pound. It has, however, never caused any trouble here, and is hardly recognised by sheep farmers, even where it is most common ... <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,171</i>) [NOTES: 1967 ed.]

>  <b>REES</b> `What are those things in the wool of the sheep?' `Bathurst burr ...' `What the children call biddy-biddy?' `Yes.' <i>(1928,Wild, Wild Heart</i>)

>  <i>bathurst bur [s] Xanthium spinosum</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,4</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> BATHURST BUR (<i>Xanthium spinosum</i>) ... Seeds contained in small burs ... This South American weed has spread widely throughout the world in wool, and has long been established in Australia, notably at Bathurst, N.S.W. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,13</i>)

>  Noxious weed hunt continues [heading] The noxious weed bathurst bur, considered extremely dangerous to pasture could have been growing on a Richmond farm for two years before its discovery last week. <i>(Feb 16, 1982,Dominion,4</i>)

>  The following plants should be reported to the Greater Wellington regional Council for eradication: African feather grass, Bathurst bur, blue passion flower, climbing spindleberry, eelgrass, Manchurian wild rice, Madeira (or mignonette) vine, moth plant, perennial nettle, saffron thistle, smilax, sweet pea shrub and woolly nightshade. <i>(Feb 14, 2004,Dominion Post,E10</i>)

>  Californian Stinkweed, Digger's Weed (Navarettia (Gilia) squarrosa) . . . It is sometimes erroneously called "Bathurst burr." <i>(1895,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,III/121</i>)

>  Bathurst Burr (<i>Xanthium spinosium</i>).This objectionable weed is still reported as spreading in the Gisborne and some parts of the Auckland districts. <i>(1896,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,IV/127</i>)

>  Californian Stinkweed, Diggers' Weed <i>(Navarettia (Gilia) squarrosa) . Ibid.</i> 2 It is sometimes erroneously called "Bathurst burr." <i>(1895,Leaflets for Farmers,No. 22/1</i>)

>  Bathurst Burr (<i>Xanthium spinosum</i>) . . . Believed to be a native of Chile. <i>(1894,Leaflets for Farmers,No. 2/2</i>)

>  Recently reference was made to the existence of the Bathurst burr in Auckland. <i>(May 3, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a burred weed, Xanthium spinosum, particularly troublesome in sheep country [also used erroneously for Californian stinkweed, digger's weed (Navarettia squarrosa)].

>  Recently reference was made to the existence of the Bathurst burr in Auckland. <i>(May 3, 1879,Weekly News,8</i>)

>  We do not think the Bathurst Burr will ever become a serious nuisance in our climate ... <i>(Jan 1893,Northern New Zealand Settler,3</i>)

>  After a lively debate .. it was finally agreed to recommend .. and that a second schedule be framed to include Bathurst burr, broom, giant Burdock, gorse, hakea and ragweed ... <i>(Aug 1900,NZ Farmer,320</i>)

>  <b>BRETT</b> Bathurst burr.- A shrubby annual from 9in. to 3 ft. high ... <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,242</i>)

>  The Bathurst burr has got a footing in Northland. <i>(Jun 7, 1951,NZ Farmer,41</i>)

### bats
 
 <i>n.</i> short for dingbats, poss. infl. by bats = eccentric: bats in the belfry (cf dingdong bell)

>  <b>LORD</b> We say a man is .../ ... `on a bender,' `in the bats' if [check cit] <i>(1921,Ballads of Bung 'Stunology',1976 ed.</i>)

### batt
 
 <i>n.</i> A felted mass of fur, or of hair and wool in hat-making; often spelt batt.] A thick rectangular piece of matted fibre, usually intended for insulation. Usually used in the plural.

>  batt n. N.Z. & Austral. a slab-shaped piece of insulating material used in house-building. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,90</i>) [NOTES: nf. 1986 NZPOD; COM 41]

>  <b>MACRAE</b> `They had bales of batts lying around at one stage,' Chris said. <i>(1988,Awful Childhoods,154</i>)

>  &#65279;[Advt] Our fuel bills were monsters until we got the Batts@. Batts in the attic cut fuel bills 40%. <i>(Apr 3, 1969,Evening Post,16</i>) [NOTES: &#65279; NB Not yet Pink Batts].]

>  [Advt] Batts noticeably reduce heating costs and pay for themselves in a few winters. <i>(Jun 28, 1973,Evening Post,14</i>)

### batten
 
 <i>v.</i> ?Is this a stake and batten fence?

>  <b>HAY</b> After the stakes were set up we had to batten them together. We bought ... battens - rough outside boards split up, and the like - for next to nothing, at the Wairoa sawmills ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/191</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> When all the posts were in and we had strained-up the wires, we started battening. I would hold my axe against the backs of the battens and Dan would drive staples into the tough honeysuckle ... <i>(1959,Stone, The,51</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> put battens on a fence

>  There's plenty to watch as they're all at different stages of their fence - battening, gate-hanging, wire-straining, one straggler still digging postholes. <i>(Jul 21, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a narrow wooden support for fence-wire

>  Posts which run out in the splitting and are short can be split into battens ... <i>(Jul 2, 1923,NZ Farmer,927</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> In general sense of a lathe-like piece of timber, not necessarily used for fencing (for board and batten vertically timbered construction, for example); a light strip of wood for fastening or joining items such as fence wires. 2. A thin strip of wood to separate stacked timber. [cf. fillet [OED2 sb1 1. and 2. a scantling; a strip of wood 1658, 1673.]] (See [cf. fillet](../F#fillet))

>  Biorns and Henry in the Bush cutting battens and fencing the shed. [Feb 9] Biorns putting battens on the shed. <i>(Feb 6, 1844,Piraki Log,148,ed. Anson 1911 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAM BEETHAM</b> ... split some battens for the sheep pen. <i>(Oct 21, 1858,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Oct 21 William Beetham Diary in 1967 Rickard Strangers in the Wilderness 99]

>  W.B.O. and J.F. sawing battens. <i>(Nov 11, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  putting battens on leanto. <i>(Mar 1861,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] to add battens to a post and wire fence.

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> Manu and I were battening a fence some distance off when we noticed fifty or so ewes eagerly feeding on the fresh crop. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,124</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] a narrow, slatted floorboard in woolshed catching and holding pens.

>  <b>DONEY</b> Let the pen where the sheep are kept before shearing have a floor made of battens a little apart. <i>(1891,Essays on Bush-Farming,19</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> The floor of the catching pen is of battens. There is some difference of opinion on the direction in which the battens should run ... <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,352</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Batten and wire fence, at least 3 ft. 9 in. high, of substantial material, firmly erected, with one or more wires, not lighter than No. 8 in steel, or black or galvanised iron; battens either driven securely into the ground and securely stapled, or intertwined in wires in an upright position, and not more than 3 in. apart. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,161 (2.)</i>) [NOTES: 1895 Fencing Act in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 161 (2.)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> A dropper, orig. and usu. of wood, but also more recently steel, wire, chains or plastic strip, used to keep the wires of a fence at equal distances apart. [[?Spec. use of OED2 sb1 2. or 3. Naut.]]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> I ought to have mentioned that the first thing to do is to put in the `straining-posts,' and then run a wire ... to act as a line to erect posts by. ... Between the posts the wires are stapled to `droppers,' consisting of about three by one battens. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,68</i>) [NOTES: 1935 ed. [cf DROPPER]]

>  <b>CARR</b> In the North Island the bulk [of fences] is constructed with wooden posts and battens. In the South ... flat iron standards with from five to seven wires run through them are used. <i>(1913,Country Work,19</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The tops of the timbers were sawn off straight, and sawn battens, 6 inches broad and 3 inches thick, were laid along the top and fastened to the stockade with 7-inch spike nails. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/169</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Sam used to hook his horse on to big trees when he was logging up, and then hammer the horse over the head with a batten when it couldn't pull <i>(1924,Follow the Call,7</i>) [NOTES: 1975 ed.]

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Then he ran to a seven-wire fence and tore a batten off it with a single jerk. <i>(1924,Me and Gus,14</i>) [NOTES: 1977 Gus Tomlins ed Sturm]

>  ... fences of standards and droppers [are useful] where suitable timber is unobtainable ... Steel posts or standards [are] ... quick to erect, since they are driven into the ground and the steel droppers can be rapidly clipped in position. ... steel posts can be used with wooden battens. <i>(Jul 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,10</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> .. the fence consisted of four posts [246] to the chain, with seven plain galvanised No. 8 gauge wires supporting three or four battens between each post. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,245</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,126</i>) [NOTES: see strainer-post]

>  <b>WILSON</b> They were scientific, too, these fences. ... six posts to the chain, six battens between each, strainers double footed and stayed; eight tightly strung wires, the top barbed. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,177</i>)

>  <b>Catherine Duffie</b> `You put battens on a fence up here [in Wanganui], but droppers in the south. <i>(Jul 21, 1978,Letter</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> All new silver pine posts and white pine battens an good English number eight wire. <i>(1978,Elver, The,45</i>)

>  Five battens between each post and always put the staples in at an angle. <i>(Mar 21, 1981,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

>  <i>(1979,Heinemann NZ Dictionary</i>)

>  <i>batten</i>: Used to keep the wires on a fence at equal distances apart. It also gives the fence strength. Can be made of wood, steel, wire, chains or plastic strip. Same as dropper. the load. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,27</i>)

>  batten ... NZ upright member in fence not fixed in the ground, used to keep wires an equal distance apart (batten staples ... $56.90 a case). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,57</i>) [NOTES: cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 90]

>  The typical new ... farm fence is a glittering grid of gleaming and galvanised No 8 wire, with battens and strainers ranked like troops at a passing-out parade. <i>(Jul 23, 1988,Dominion,8</i>)

>  When all the wires are strained tightly the battens or droppers, which are split to about two inches square, are stapled at spaces varying from two to three feet. <i>(1965,Open Country,55</i>)

>  [Hdg] Fence battens recycled... All the furniture is crafted from hand-selected, native split fencing battens... The battens, mostly made of totara and heart rimu, have withstood the test of time, enduring blistering summers and cold and wet winters, not to mention stubborn livestock. <i>(Nov 25, 1997,Evening Post,Living Style Section 2</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> the shearing floor of a shearing shed

>  <b>ACTON-ADAMS in McCASKILL</b> Allow no men in the shed after working hours, no pressing of scoured wool by candle-light, and no smoking on the battens <i>(1969,Molesworth,87</i>) [NOTES: Original 1901]

### batten stapler
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an implement used in fence-battening.

>  Mr Morecraft from South Auckland bought a batten stapler so his grand daughters could do the battening on their farm and save the cost of getting in a professional. <i>(2001,Farming Direct Annual Catalogue,20</i>)

### batten-cutting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  That's how he got down this way.. trapping possums, then into deer hunting.. and later into batten-cutting, logging and, 27 years ago, eel fishing. <i>(Sep 19, 1998,Dominion,17</i>)

### battening
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the laying of a grated floor in a woolshed.

>  DD battening woolshed. <i>(Jan 7, 1856,St Leonard's Station Diary</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the adding of battens to a fence.

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> They were fenced off with only the battening left to do on the Monday. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,112</i>)

>  We worked on the battening task , reckoning to staple until five when we would take the ewes off. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,124</i>)

>  There's plenty to watch as they're all at different stages of their fence - battening, gate-hanging, wire-straining, one straggler still digging post-holes. <i>(Jul 21, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  Then it was a matter of setting to to make the Taranaki gates as we discussed. Have completed three gates and dug in nine tie-downs, but still more will be needed ... Did some more battening. <i>(Jun 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,32</i>)

>  <b>CANDY</b> We are nearly half a mile from the house and I have only an hour's battening to finish the new fence. <i>(1988,Every Living thing,99</i>)

### battening up
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the placing of battens on a newly constructed post and wire fence.

>  The "battening up" was done next day. <i>(Mar 21, 1981,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The `battening up' was done next day. At 6.30am I heard the sound of a hammer. Ross was already there. <i>(Mar 21, 1981,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

### batter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mary Collins is a hard-faced, Christchurch batter, who serves as a sort of human football for the local Harvey Duffs, who shoot her between the gaol ports [?pun on goal posts] of the booby hatch on the slightest provocation. <i>(Mar 13, 1909,NZ Truth,195/1/7</i>) [NOTES: Is this a misprint or mistranscription or var. of <i>battler</i>?]

### batter about
 
 <i>v.</i> ?to knock around

>  I had, to use the colonial term, "battered about" the colonies for somewhere about ten years. <i>(May 3, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,20</i>)

### battery
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAY</b> The next objects of interest are the quartz-crushing batteries. ... The smallest has four stampers, and the largest sixty-two. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/279</i>)

>  Kurunui Battery ... of six stampers, and one stamper specimen battery with Berdans at the end of tables to grind the tailings. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,93</i>)

>  The most important work in mining for precious metals is quartz-reefing; here the system differs little from underground mining in general. ... the ore ... when brought to the surface, is committed to `batteries' or crushing-mills, where the stone is stamped or ground into powder. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,369</i>) [NOTES: E. Tregear: Labour in NZ]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> BATTERY: Quartz crushing mill. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,185</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains]

>  The mill on the Hauraki claim consists of a fifteen-stamper battery, Wilfley, vanner, berdans, and accessories. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No. 4(ns) : 112</i>) [NOTES: Coromandel]

>  The crushing machinery in all these batteries consisted of four to ten heads of light stamps, and the gold-saving plant of amalgamated copper plates, with or without the addition of a small berdan. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.6 (NS): 26</i>) [NOTES: Mikonui]

>  <b>BLYTH (ed.)</b> Battery: Any number, more than one, of stamp mills would constitute a battery. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary)]

>  The battery consisted of ten head of stamps, of a very old type, driven by a traction engine. <i>(1935,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,16 : 111</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> BATTERY: set of stampers for crushing auriferous quartz or cement. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,526</i>) [NOTES: 1962 ed.]

>  <b>NOLAN</b> Battery: specifically, the stamping machinery used to crush ore, but often applied to the extraction plant in general. <i>(1962,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,111</i>) [NOTES: gloss]

>  <b>HUNT</b> Then sometimes of an evening we'd all meet up at the boarding house, right near the gold battery. <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five,29</i>) [NOTES: Bill Henderson]

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> This [immorality] was not true of olden days - not until the Maori had noticed how the skin-bleached newcomer, the lawless pakeha bay-whaler, conducted his festivities with limitless rum, . <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,36</i>)

>  Little now remains - part of old sluicing and machinery from the mines and batteries further up the Waitawheta Stream and on the junction of stream and river the massive concrete and iron foundations of the Woodstock Battery. <i>(Jun 2002,NZ Memories,No. 36/17</i>)

>  A 10-stamp quartz battery was set up together with an inclined tramway linking it with a wharf. <i>(Feb 28, 2004,Dominion Post,E14</i>)

>  Battery : The name given to the treatment plant. (Derived from a battery of stampers). <i>(Sep 1999,Ohinemuri Regional History Journal,43 : 14</i>)

>  The remains of an old stamping battery and a railway lie beneath the bush canopy. <i>(Jan 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  But, with the mining company gone and a noisy battery closed down, the only customers were the few miners who remained, the miners turned farmers who took up land in the valley and the original settlers. <i>(Apr 2004,NZ Memories,No. 47/53</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> usu. modern term ?batter

>  <b>FOSTON</b> The sides of the cutting had a sloping battery <i>(1921,At The Front,65</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sheepwashing] the action and/or equipment of warm water rinsing.

>  The clip from the Castle Rock Station which was battery-washed, had been readily purchased. <i>(Dec 12, 1863,Weekly News,15</i>)

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> Afterwards went to sheep wash at Longridge Stream - there is a good stream of water for washing and battery and suitable hut for washers, engine to heat water and all complete. <i>(Jan 19, 1880,Simpson Diary of Tours of Southland Estates</i>)

>  <b>WILSON & FRASER</b> First the sheep went through two or more dips, then they put the battery on them - that was warm water out of a hose. <i>(1936,Book of the Pioneers, The,31</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The stamping battery was sited against the hill... It was powered by water and drove two six-foot pelton wheels. <i>(Apr 2004,NZ Memories,47/52</i>)

### battery box
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also box](../A#also-box))

>  The battery has three stampers in each box, double cams, quickslver placed in battery boxes, grating perpendicular, with round holes. The copper-plate tables are about 12ft. long by 5ft. wide, and raised about 14in. above the blanket boards; ... [The blankets] appear to be ordinary grey blankets of a very inferior description. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,93</i>)

### battery hand
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I want to say, though, that none but the battery hands [out of the Waihi miners] signed it, and some of them came to me afterwards and told me that they had been hoodwinked into doing so. <i>(Jul 7, 1911,Maoriland Worker,18/13/2</i>)

### battik
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [patiki](../P#patiki))

### battle
 
 <i>v.</i> make pregnant

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>battle</i> make pregnant, merely make love. Vince Boyle of Winton was researching a history of Mossburn when he came across a fellow recalling his wagoning days in 1914 in Northern Southland, and spoke of how he `battled a woman'. He was going to marry her, but a few days before the wedding she had a dentist remove her teeth and sent him the bill. They did not get married. Farmer and Henley describe a battler as one given up to debauchery, which leaves an outside chance that the wagoner merely seduced and failed to fertilise. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,12</i>)

### Battle of Manners Street
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  In a battle of a different kind - in Wellington on April 3, 1943 - the "Battle of Manners St" erupted when US servicemen refused to let Maori servicemen into the Wellington Allied Services Club. In New Zealand's worst riot, 1000 American and Kiwi troops and several hundred civilians fought for four hours. At least two Americans were killed. <i>(Dec 12, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C6</i>)

>  There were other Sources of conflict, the best known being the "battle of Manners St", which started when some southern Americans argued with Maori, which led to an all-out street brawl. <i>(Jul 20, 2002,Dominion Post,F18</i>)

>  The Battle of Manners Street, inflated by folklore and <i>An Encyclopedia of New Zealand</i> is properly reduced, citing an official report in National Archives... <i>(Dec 18, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),103</i>)

### Battle of Molesworth Street
 
 <i>phr.</i> a confrontation between police and anti-Springbok-tour protesters 29 July 1981

>  The first sign of the tougher police attitude came days later in Molesworth St, Wellington... The Battle of Molesworth St, as it came to be known, lasted only moments. But it had a traumatic effect on a country accustomed to thinking of the police as an essentially benign law enforcement agency. <i>(Jul 14, 2001,Evening Post,20</i>)

### Battle of the Bridge
 
 <i>phr.</i> a rugby or other sporting contest between North Harbour and Auckland

>  Every time we play them it's like the Battle of the Bridge, when Auckland play North Harbour, or Otago play Canterbury, we're neighbours so it's all on. <i>(Aug 17, 2001,Dominion,24</i>)

### battle-axe
 
 <i>n.</i> ?mere

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... they are short but almost shaped like the N.Z. battle Axes. <i>(Mar 15, 1774,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,3/470,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EDGAR</b> Their Weapons consists of Spears darts and battle Axes & Patoo Patoos - the Patoo Patoo's is an instrument made of bone of large Fish or Stone -it is in shape of a battldore [cf WADDY 1]& about 16 or 18 Inches long, <i>(Feb 12, 1777,Log</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb 12 Edgar's Log in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/225]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... we heard a loud hallooing on ... an island, which on this occasion obtained the name of Indian Island; and standing on the shore we perceived one of the natives ... He stood with a club or battle-axe in his hand, ... and behind him ... two women, each of them having a long spear. ... held a long speech ... swing round his club, on which he leaned at other times. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/137</i>)

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> [applied to Maori weapon] <i>(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),27</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  ... on presenting the conquerors with a greenstone battle axe (the <i>mere pounamu</i>), they were again allowed to be called a tribe ... <i>(1873,TrNZI,5/26</i>)

### battle-bowler
 
 <i>n.</i> tin hat

>  <b>MARTYN</b> I had left my `battle-bowler', otherwise my tin hat, behind. <i>(1943,Tripoli and Beyond,23</i>)

### battler
 
 <i>n.</i> One who perseveres or battles on against odds; a toiler. [[AND has battle 1895 in various senses: and battler 1. one who battles 1897 a battler for grievances 2. an unemployed person 1898 (b. who lives by his wits 1946) 3. a racecourse frequenter 1895 4. prostitute 1898 : OEDS 2. Aust swagman b. Aust and NZ: in various other senses and shades of meaning of a person struggling against odds; AC1896; nf W3.]]

>  <b>SHARPE</b> On top of the load another battler was wading around ... <i>(1962,Country Occasions,88</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The <i>battler</i> seems [in Australia] to have been the poorest itinerant. In this sense, the word is not current in New Zealand where <i>battler</i> is used for a person who puts up a hard fight against adverse conditions without giving in, and is thus a general terms of praise. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,144</i>)

>  A battler all his life ... but now he's going broke. ... It would be a tragedy if, after a lifetime of effort, he is thrown on the scrapheap. <i>(Sep 19, 1972,NZ Truth,8/6</i>)

>  <b>DAVIES</b> You were always a battler, Dad ... But don't crack too hardy. <i>(1975,Outback,104</i>)

>  battler n. NZ & Austral. person who struggles against the odds (New Zealanders love a battler, an underdog fighting against adverse circumstances). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,58</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 90],]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>battler</i> conscientous worker approved of by peers; reflects antipodean recognition of life's strugglers, the word also meaning a swagman; a petty punter living on winnings; a prostitute independent of ponce or brothel; eg `Bob has no flair, but he's a battler.' ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,13</i>)

>  `Now the old battlers of Kainga will be able to own their own flush loo like every other New Zealander,' he said <i>(Sep 4, 1986,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> A <i>toiler</i> or <i>battler</i> is a hard, conscientious worker - both are used with a shade of condescension ; <i>battler</i> is also found in Glasgow slang, but there it has the signification of a gangster who fights with his fists. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,88</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> In the old days ... he was willing to wait for the farmer's wool cheque before he was paid. In most cases he was paid in the end, but he made it possible for many a battler to carry on. <i>(1946,There's Gold in Them Hills,15</i>)

>  <b>SHARP</b> ... each one cut and carried by that peculiar old battler, Dave Gun. <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,94</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... here again I saw that remarkable old battler Alick Curtis still running a dog. ... At well over eighty years of age, he must surely be the grand old man of the dog trial game. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,159</i>)

>  <b>McCLENAGHAN</b> `[The people] have to be real battlers, otherwise they wouldn't take this country on.' <i>(1976,Travelling Man,23</i>)

>  AFTER decades of sterotyping ourselves as wee battlers, a nation who can fashion anything out of number eight wire and as generally tough old boots..it was depressing to watch Lisa Chappell whinging and moaning her way around Malaysia on <i>Intrepid Journeys</i> <i>(Nov 23, 2005,Capital Times,10,23-29 November 2005 ed.</i>)

>  Mary Collins...battler <i>(Mar 13, 1909,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  The grim constable stated, further, that whether Lady Malkin was married or not, she was one of the most persistent battlers in Upper Willis and Ghuznee-streets, and both women were known to the police as Magdalenes of an unrepentant sort. <i>(Jan 18, 1908,NZ Truth,6</i>)

### batwing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HARPER</b> Our camp has not yet been described, and ... it is the simplest and best form of shelter for a party of two, ... It is an invention of Douglas's, and we call it a `batwing'. In the ordinary course of camp life, survey parties can have their loads packed on horseback, and carry tent and fly, with a second smaller fly to pitch at the end of the tent to shelter the fire. We ... [51] could not afford to take such a weighty camp. We therefore pitch an ordinary 6 ft. by 8 ft. canvas tent, on a ridge pole, with an 8 ft. by 10 ft. fly six inches above it; and cut the tent in half along the ridge, and taking away one half, leave the other half standing. This is just large enough to allow two men to lie `heads and tails'. [The side left open, and one side of fly raised 4 ft. in middle, and under this the fire burns so have shelter in wet weather.] <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,50</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,39</i>) [NOTES: >18??Douglas in 1957 pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 39]

### baubles of office
 
 <i>phr.</i> [(various wordings) from a Winston Peters quote]

>  The Golden Bauble Award. Winston Peters wins this one from United Future leader Peter Dunne by a country mile, not only because he was cynical enough to grab the baubles of ministerial office with both hands even after telling his supporters he had no interest in the said perks, but because of his brashness in claiming it was all a media beat-up. <i>(Dec 19, 2005,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Before Winston said he "would not accept the baubles of office", the word referred to showy trinkets or trifles. Now it has been redefined to mean large powerful positions much greater than either warranted or deserved and the trinkets now refers to junkets of the first class travelling variety. <i>(Jan 22, 2006,Sunday Star Times,A11</i>)

>  [Rod Donald]'ll be up there, with his sunflower braces on over his angel's nightie, roaring with laughter at Winston's baubles, along with the rest of us. <i>(Nov 19, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  [Caption] I have not been seduced by the baubles of office ... As minister of Foreign Affairs outside of Government I have all the baubles but no office. <i>(Nov 22, 2005,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  If attending the All Blacks' grand-slam winning match last weekend wasn't a bauble of office, what is? <i>(Nov 30, 2005,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Is it for enjoying the baubles of office when he wasn't even elected in Tauranga, or is it simply because he's sold his soul to the Prime Minister Helen Clark to win a ministerial salary? <i>(Nov 29, 2005,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Peters knew he was on trial, and defendants shouldn't grin. A smile might suggest he was enjoying the baubles of office. <i>(Oct 25, 2005,Sunday Star Times,1</i>)

>  The next election is still a long way off, but the latest 3 News poll suggests NZ First is being punished for leader Winston Peters' going back on his "baubles of office" promise. <i>(Nov 7, 2005,Dominion Post,7</i>)

### bay
 
 <i>n.</i> Constr usu. the bay occas. cap. used locally to refer to the best known `bays' in various districts, those with widest currency including:

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> ... for once do not regret having procrastinated, as a vessel is leaving the Bay so soon after our arrival, ... <i>(Dec 3, 1835,Robert Maunsell,30,1938 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Till after the late war-expedition of the great body of the Bay-of-Islanders to Tauranga, ... And when those ... resolved upon an attack, and left the Bay for that purpose, ... all hope seemed to be utterly destroyed. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),204</i>)

>  <b>LANG</b> The Bay <i>(1839,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>)

>  <b>MATHEW</b> We ... prepared for the troublesome business of the day, which was the same nature as that at the Bay - meeting and negotiating with the [51] Chiefs [over Treaty of Waitangi]. <i>(Feb 16, 1840,Journal,50</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Feb 16 F.Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 50]

>  <b>SARAH MATHEW</b> ... Captain Mollison ... commenced with asking her name, where from, where to, and we found to our great satisfaction that the Bay we saw her working out of was the `Bay of Islands'. <i>(Mar 16, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar 16 Sarah Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 92 [Bay of Islands]]

>  I have most carefully examined every part of it, and although the back-land is rugged and precipitous there is a much larger portion of it level than I have yet seen in the Bay, and fully sufficient to afford space for a very pretty and convenient town. <i>(1842,GBPP,(No.569) 145</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar 23 F.Mathew to Hobson in GBPP 1842 (No.569) 145]

>  <b>BEST</b> The Bay abounds with fish and ... I saw the Gurnard, Mullet, a kind of Perch, a fish resembling White Bait, Eels, Snapper and a species of Sole. I have also caught a small Shark or Dog-fish. <i>(May 1840,Journal,222,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CHAPMAN</b> The Bay [of Islands] was for many years the resort of excaped convicts and runaway sailors; ... <i>(1843,NZ Portfolio,126</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> European Children in the Bay for the most part are brought up in ignorance, dirty, filthy and ragged. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,92,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FAIRBURN</b> `the Bay' [in quotes, with capital `B'] <i>(1930,Maharatanga,2</i>) [NOTES: DATE PURELY A GUESS]

>  [Dr Montgomery and C.W. Richmond] had a long korero about New Zealand and the Bay, where the Doctor had been quartered for 5 years. <i>(Aug 20, 1855,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/176</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond),]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Capn. Dean ... mentioned that the Brig at Kororarika was in a state of mutiny ... Told him that he was in the Bay we should not interfere. <i>(Aug 20, 1831,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,189,1961 ed.</i>)

>  There are 40 Europeans and their families residing at the Bay, independent of the Mission, and of the British Resident and his family. <i>(Aug 9, 1834,NZ Independent,30,1979 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [=Bay of Islands],]



 
 <i>n.</i> mate, friend [Glennis Moriarty (j.moriarty@xtra.co.nz; ph. (06)8626-368) says probably from "boy" and used regularly by East Coast students when talking to each other, as in "Come here, bay".]

### bay villa
 
 <i>n.</i> see cit. 1983

>  It refers specifically to the villa type with a bay window on the front facade (and, in larger versions, on a side facade too). It is a term one hears often (in fact I devoted a section to the `bay villa' in an architectural thesis I recently completed). The `bay' refers to the bay window (naturally enough); and also, some say, to San Francisco Bay. It was (and is) a house form common in that city; and it may well have spread to New Zealand from there. <i>(1983,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: C.B.Orsman M.Arch]

>  <b>SINCLAIR & HARREX</b> By the later decades of the [19th] century the most popular house was the villa or 'bay villa' - a house of simple design usually with one or more gables thrown [check] out, one or more bay windows, and a veranda. <i>(1978,Looking Back,118</i>)

### bay whaler
 
 <i>n.</i> a person engaged in bay whaling (specifically from the Bay of Islands 1882 below) [AND 1867]

>  Mostly the bay whalers operated from shore stations, which saved the cost of a ship. <i>(1971,New Zealand's Heritage,1/427</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> This [immorality] was not true of olden days - not until the Maori had noticed how the skin-bleached newcomer, the lawless pakeha bay-whaler, conducted his festivities with limitless rum, ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,36</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> The curse.. of the island then was what Mr. Hunt calls "the Bay whalers," a set of men from the Bay of Islands. <i>(1882,Chatham Islands, The,25</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McNAB</b> The <i>Lucy Ann,</i> as a bay whaler instead of a trader, began to seek for fresh places for the pursuit of whales and sailed on 1st June [1835] for Port Cooper. <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days</i>)

### bay whaling
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf. shore whaling]

>  <b>CLENDON</b> The season also would be far advanced for Bay Whaling ... <i>(May 6, 1837,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1837 May 6 J.R. Clendon to Rhodes in 1954 Rhodes Whaling Journal 102]

>  <b>EARP</b> The Wellington settlers ... were ardent followers of the whale fishery, carried on chiefly on the coasts of the northern and middle island by means of boats - a method technically called `bay-whaling', ... <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),95</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> There are eight kinds of whales, so bay-whaling is carried on round the coast. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/230</i>)

>  By 1820 sealing had been replaced by bay-whaling, and with the setting-up of shore-whaling stations came the first attempts [in Otago] ... to cultivate small patches of ground ... <i>(Apr 1953,NZ Geographic,18</i>) [NOTES: (Kibblewhite)]

>  <b>STRAUBEL</b> The pursuit in coastal waters of black whales was known as `bay whaling',and the procedure followed was for the ships to lie at anchor in a convenient harbour or bay, sending out their boats to cruise in nearby waters for `fish'. <i>Ibid.</i> xviii [dating on NZ coast from about 1829 at Cloudy Bay, and 1835 in Banks Peninsula] <i>(1954,Rhodes Whaling Journal,xvi</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> A more positive step to permanent settlement came with the establishment of `bay whaling' stations at points along the coastline of both islands. <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,5</i>)

>  Bay whaling in New Zealand was based on the regular migrations of the right whales as they came into sheltered waters to calve. <i>(1971,New Zealand's Heritage,1/427</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> The old bay whaling parties have long since ceased to trouble the Islands; <i>(1866,Chatham Islands,60</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> An old-time bay whaling station consisted ... of at least two boats, with their crew of six men each. The headsman, or mate, four ordinary oarsmen, and the harpooner, or `boat-steerer,' who pulled the bow-oar, and drove in the harpoon, and who, when the whale had been struck and `fastened on,' went aft and steered the boat, while the mate took his place, and by repeated lance-thrusts killed it. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,75</i>)

### bayonet grass
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Aciphylla</i> spp., an old name for speargrass or taramea, contrast <i>A. squarrosa</i> a little later `soft leaved Spear grass', =spear-grass cf bayonet-plant. [[Could this have any priority over or connection with NZ Spaniard: the dating would suggest otherwise: phs from same metaphorical conception:]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The Spaniard (spear-grass or bayonet-grass) `piked us intil the bane' ... <i>(May 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,5/72</i>)

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> ... the giant Aciphylla Colensoi, the Spaniard or Bayonet-grass. <i>(1862,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,25</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> The larger species [of <i>Aciphylla</i> ] are familiar to the settlers as `Spear-grass', or `Bayonette-grass', `Wild' or `Bloody-Spaniard', [<i>bloody </i>=?] in allusion to their very rigid, strong, poniard-like, sharp-pointed leaves ... Hence a `Spear-grass Scrub' on the hill ranges is frequently most formidable ... to men and horses, though it forms one of the favourite hunting grounds of the Wild Pig. In the Matukituki Valley, `Bayonette-grass' forms an undergrowth in a dense scrub of prickly thorn ... <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,49</i>)

>  The plains are covered with grasses, the roots of which are gathered into tufts or tussocks, intermixed with the Bayonet grass or `Wild Spaniard' <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/5</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  The remarkable genus <i>Aciphylla</i> comprises the `spear-grass' and `bayonet-grass' of the colonists, so called from their rigid pungent leaves, which occasionally inflict uncomfortable wounds on incautious travellers. <i>(1871,TrNZI,4/255</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>HAAST</b> They formed with the gigantic <i>Aciphylla Colensoi</i>, the Spaniard or Bayonet grass, ... which ... most severely punished man and horse. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,25</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i>Danthonia pungens</i> Bayonet-grass. <i>(1899,Students' Flora,207</i>) [NOTES: [check this]]

>  <i>(1909,AJHR,C-12</i>) [NOTES: (And) Botanical Survey Stewart Island]

>  <i>taramea</i>, a plant (Aciphylla squarrosa and other sp.), spear-grass, bayonet-grass, spaniard: a hill and mountain plant from whose spiny blades the Maori by heat and torsion extracted a valued scent. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/158</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]
