### be cobbers with
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be close friends with

>  <b>MANTELL</b> `Nobody! You're cobbers with Wilkins, aren't you? Blaney?' <i>(1978,Murder in Fancydress,85</i>)

### be good value
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Good value. Worth having: Australian and New Zealand coll.: since <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1064</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> `Wavell's pretty good value'. <i>(1943,Troop Target</i>) [NOTES: ca. 1920.]

### be in
 
 <i>phr.</i> to join in; to get started; to be active in; take part in, or one's fair share of, whatever is going on; get stuck in. (Often an exclam. be in! as an invitation, or in expressions `You gotta be in (to win); you gotta be quick', that is, take your share or somebody else will get it.) [(often imper.)]

>  Temptation was placed in their way and they decided, to use a colloquialism, to `be in' [on a two-up school]. <i>(Nov 10, 1941,Evening Post,6/3</i>) [NOTES: Nov. 10 or Nov. 16?]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> We got Maggie to come and be in as well [sc. on a party]. <i>(1946,That Summer,85</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> We got Maggie to come and be in as well [sc. on a party]. <i>(1947,Big Game,32</i>)

>  `Come in and get your share' (usually of food). I have heard it frequently among trampers. e.g. `Be in now or you may miss out.' <i>(1953,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: R. Mason]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> One for Herbie... Be in, it's ya birthday Herbie. And the man in the chair pushes through the mob with hands decked with glasses ... <i>(1969,Gun in My Hand, A,90</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> One for Herbie... Be in, it's ya birthday Herbie. And the man in the chair pushes through the mob with hands decked with glasses ... <i>(1969,Gun in My Hand, A,90</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> `The old girl just brought in the famous tray,' the girl said. `Come on, you better be in or the rest of them'll clean it up.' <i>(1960,Maori Girl,73</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> quoting Sargeson That Summer I was one of the first to be in. sc get stuck in <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,138</i>)

>  <b>HOOPER</b> `You coming along?' [19] `Yeah, I'll come,' I said casually. `Might as well be in.' <i>(1981,Goat Paddock,18</i>)



 
 <i>phr.</i> (as imperative) take one's full share of a round of drinks

>  Drinking on the West Coast has an etiquette of its own. It condones none of the `bob-in' touch of a city bar when a man will have the two drinks he wants and then leave. `Be in or be out', you will be told, is the motto on the Coast. <i>(1948,NZ Geographer,166</i>) [NOTES: Pascoe `Coal from Buller']

### be in it
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be in the race (lit. or fig.).

>  Wi Waka was expected to run well in the Hack Hurdles, but he gave a miserable display and was never in it. <i>(Jul 14, 1906,NZ Truth,56/2/4</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To join, to participate in. To be among a number of persons, or creatures. `O.K. I'll be in. What day are we to go?' `Sam was in it, he was in it up to the neck. But he was lucky, his part was behind the scenes.' <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],532,1974 ed.</i>)

### be in like a robber's dog
 
 <i>phr.</i> to take up something enthusiastically, greedily [ca 1973 Dave Inglis]

>  If there was something like a twilight tournament on a weekday evening he'd be "in like a robber's dog". <i>(Nov 25, 2000,Evening Post,31</i>)

### be in on
 
 <i>phr.</i> be in on (something, on a course of action) [P8 to have a share in ca 1919.] to have a part, or share, in.

>  [Advt.] Dig down for that half-crown right now. Be in on the last $5000 Art Union without fail. <i>(Sep 15, 1926,Aussie (NZ Section),XV</i>)

### be in on a win
 
 <i>phr.</i> be well off, comfortably placed

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> He thinks he might be in on a win if he had an intelligent wife. But you only get him all balled up. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,150,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Play the Fife Lowly)]

### be in to win
 
 <i>phr.</i> in various expressions: be in to win, be in like shags to a dead fish (1958 West Coast), be in like rats (ca1940), be in like maggots on a dead sheep [nf W3]. [ca1949 R. Mason, `They'll be in like ...' heard in Westland]

>  <b>McGILL</b> be in encouragement to participate, often in phr. `be in to win'; eg `C'mon Clive, go for the bull, be in to win.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,14</i>)

### be on a fuss
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be made a fuss of, be given special treatment

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> After I got home with all that stuff, I found I was still on a fuss there, too. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,26</i>)

>  A resident of the Salvation Army's Norman Kirk Home in Upper Hutt, Mr McCarthy was on a fuss today as staff and other residents celebrated his birthday. <i>(Oct 8, 1980,Evening Post,4</i>)

### be on the crosscut
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be angry, at odds, crossgrained (with a person).

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> Me an' your mate ... had a bit of a barney some days ago. I thought I'd just step round and bury the hatchet. ... Yes, `tain't the square thing to be on the cross cut at Xmas time. ... So I'm on the buryin' lay - that's my lay tonight. <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,69</i>)

### be one's muttons
 
 <i>phr.</i> be what one likes; be one's strong point

>  Milk, however, is small Charlie's muttons <i>(Feb 1940,National Education,16</i>) [NOTES: (Leggett)]

>  <b>BAKER</b> When we speak of something being<i> our muttons</i> or <i>a person's muttons</i> we mean that it is regarded with particular favour, that we like it especially well. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,54</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Muttons</i>. 3. `When we speak of something being <i>our muttons</i> or a <i>person's muttons</i> we mean that we regard it with particular favour, that we like it especially well,' B., 1941: New Zealand and Australian: C. 20. Ex the excellence of New Zealand mutton. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1115</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>that's our muttons</i> just what we like, with reference to the excellence of our mutton; eg `Look at our rowing eight go. That's our muttons!' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,112</i>)

### be put off
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to leave a farm against one's will, or to be made bankrupt.

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> It [facial eczema] was the gravest danger any of us faced and could put us off our farms. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,52</i>)

### be rough on
 
 <i>phr.</i> [ca1951 Don't be too rough on that wire brush. It's got to last a week. ca1941 Don't be too rough on the eggs. We've only got six left. I didn't mind him stopping but he's pretty rough on tucker.]

>  <b>BARKER</b> It is only natural that you should be glad to go, yet it is very rough upon us. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,137</i>)

>  The Rev. Dr. Thackeray, `The Clerical Publican,' of Hoddesdon, Harts, is rough on the total abstainer crowd. <i>(Jul 14, 1906,NZ Truth,56/1/7</i>)

>  <b>192/4/5</b> Mr.Justice Cooper, on certain offenders whose crimes were of a desperate nature was, to use a current but expressive term, `rough.' Long terms of imprisonment were ordered, and his Honor [sic] uttered a threat of trying what effect the lash would have. <i>(Feb 20, 1909,NZ Truth</i>)

>  The boss was a bit too rough on him <i>(1913,Sodger Sandy's Bairn,11</i>)

### be-swagged
 
 <i>adj.</i> Loaded up with swag

>  <b>COWAN</b> Wirihana ... glances round to see that all hands are be-swagged and ready, then quick march ... <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,18</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> That column of four hundred men ... short-kilted like Highlanders, heavily beswagged, had toiled up from the Ruatoki Plain ... <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,175</i>)

### be/get hooked
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be caught (by esp. a girl); to become engaged

>  `I'm hooked,' responded Marty with resignation, and smiled at Myrtle, who did not appear to resent this description of her engagement to Mr. Wheeler. <i>(Nov 10, 1915,Triad, The,68</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> to get hooked, to find a girl friend. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,92</i>)

### beach
 
 <i>n.</i> The seashore as a place for an outing.. -->1. [W3] In NZ not only the word for `the shore of the sea' (OED2 sb 3 citations connote a littoral and a literal meaning), but also the usual word for British seaside, the beach as a place of enjoyment or recreation (seaside has an unidiomatic, British association even in children's stories), cf beach cottage: in early days beach had commercial connotations: cf The Beach, beach trader. [Jim Cameron writes: The importance of the beach as a NZ icon suggests that there might be some distinctive NZE compounds. OED has <i>*beach bag, *beach ball, *beach buggy, *beach bum, beach flea, *beachfront, beach grass, beach plum, beach rock, *beachwear</i>. Macquarie 1981 has also <i>beach break</i> (a surfing term), <i>beach hut, beach inspector, *beach mat, *beach robe, beachworm</i> <i>*surf beach</i> - in NZPOD - and <i>*ocean beach</i>. As well I have seen <i>beach music, *beach picnic</i> and <i>beach volleyball</i> recently in overseas publications. [*Known to me as in NZ use or former use also]. <i>Beachfront</i> is tagged in OED as chiefly US, and all citations refer to places outside the UK. It has long been used in NZ. NZ citations?] (See [cf. beach cottage](../B#beach-cottage))

>  Note, In speaking of Mercury Bay I had forgot to mention that the Mangrove trees found there produce a resinous substance very much like rosin, something of this kind I am told is found in both the East and West Indias. We found it first in small lumps upon the Sea beach, but afterwards found it sticking to the Mangrove trees and by that means found out from whence it had come. <i>(Nov 16, 1769,Cook's Journals,I/204</i>) [NOTES: 1955 ed.]

>  <b>BELL</b> If the fishing is to be carried on by a shore party, the try pots and huts are erected on the beach ... The boats are sent out at daylight every morning, and when ... they kill a fish it is towed ashore and flinched and boiled upon the beach. <i>(Nov 1831,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1831 Nov Bell Letter in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days]

>  <b>BOULTBEE</b> The distance we had to go was about 20 miles, part of the way led through woods, and part along Sandy beaches. <i>(1835,Journal,72</i>) [NOTES: 1986 ed.]

>  landed 100 Cask and shooks on the beach - <i>(Feb 20, 1836,Piraki Log,28</i>)

>  My brother Peter was on the beach waiting for me. <i>(Feb 19, 1842,Letters from New Plymouth,7</i>) [NOTES: in 1843 Letters from New Plymouth (1968)]

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> Following this towards the coast ...; then descended to the shore, returning along the beach. <i>(Apr 19, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: in 1898 Hocken Contributions 208 [distinct from shore]]

>  Another day he would rush them all off ... down to the beach for a picnic and a swim. <i>(1934,Winds of Heaven,181</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> The Beach. Why wasn't everyone going to the Beach? ... they set off down the fir-bordered road that led to the sound of the sea ... <i>(1984,Some Other Country,102</i>) [NOTES: 1951 Frame `Swans' in 1984 Some Other Country]

>  <b>MUIR</b> `Where do you want to go, Em?' `Not to the beach. All those awful people.' and donkeys on the beach. <i>(1960,Word for Word,84</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Disgruntled Pommies going back to dear old blighty where there is some life and donkeys on the beach. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,22</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> I would ... be handed over to some family friend for a visit to the zoo ..., the pantomime, or in summertime the beach; ... <i>(1965,Memoirs of a Peon,16</i>)

>  <b>GRUNDY</b> The lagoon was rich with flounders, or flatties as the [Chatham] islanders call them, while the beach offered pauas and sea-eggs. <i>(1968,Who'd Marry a Doctor?,51</i>)

>  In our latest readers' poll.. the perennial answer to the perennial question of what is best about living in New Zealand is "the beach" (specifically its accessibility and lack of crowding) and, of course, the nice weather. <i>(Dec 9, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> In among the trees under the bridge Kathy spread a beach lunch of mutton and mint sandwiches, tomatoes and apple shortbread, milk and Thermos tea. <i>(1960,Tangahano,24</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Perhaps you sweat less when you are old but more probably it is because she keeps well away from the coal ranges and heated flat irons of beach life when the temperature is in the nineties, as it has been for weeks. <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,25</i>)

>  They are large, these flies, sticky-footed beach ones which cling. <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,25</i>)

>  We talk about the real beach weather we have been having, we tell each other that it is perfect for swimming but that the nights are almost too warm. <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,26</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> We stopped at Hampden.. walking higgledy-piggledy carrying kits and rugs down to the picnic ground by the beach with its beach 'dunny', the dark stained wooden seat split down the middle, the concrete floor puddled muddy and salt-smelling. <i>Ibid.</i> 51 Along the path from the lean-to, the 'dunny' beside the cypress tree and soon known as 'the cypress', was covered with the small fragrant roses known to us as 'dunny roses', and was without a door, while the dunny seat, long and wide like a beach dunny, was set over a deep hole half-filled with old 'kiki' (our word for faeces) in varying shades of brown, topped with faded pieces of newspaper, the <i>Oamaru Mail</i> and the <i>Otago Daily Times</i>. <i>(1986,Angel at My Table, An,14</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. beach claim](../B#beach-claim))

>  Wilson's Beach [Wakamarina, Marlborough] <i>(1856,Evening Post,1/7/2</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> We camped on the sandy beach of the river ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,12</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... a creek flowed through the valley, winding over a small beach of shingle. <i>Ibid.</i> 30 The river ... became wider and deeper, so that we were compelled to seek for fording places, which we learnt ... to choose at the bars or beaches above the falls, where the current was not so strong ... and the footing was therefore far better. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,24</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> As a result, the adjacent ground was left untouched; so that the Co. were enabled to work claim after claim of a really rich beach. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,I/xiv/47,1889,1974 ed.</i>)

>  The best [gold] was found on the beaches and shallow bars of the stream ... <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/12</i>)

>  <b>DOBSON</b> A number of gold-diggers were working in the neighbourhood, some on the beaches and some on the adjoining terraces. <i>(1930,Reminiscences,141</i>)

>  All `beaches' obliterated and boulder-hopping a necessity. <i>(1933,NZ Alpine Journal,5/20/233</i>) [NOTES: 1932 Diary in 1933 NZ Alpine Journal 5/20/233]

>  <b>NOLAN</b> <i>Beach:</i> flattish gravel deposit at a riverside. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,111</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> as The Beach. An obs. name for what became Lambton Quay, Wellington, cf the early 1840 Wellington use of beachcomber @n.

>  [The beach (Wellington)], A person calling himself Captain Richards, of the schooner Cheerful, has beguiled and smuggled from the [Wellington] beach, four to five young females to carry with him to the Chatham Islands, to locate them ... amongst the reckless set who form the members of a whaling gang ... <i>(Mar 6, 1841,NZ Gazette,3</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Mar 6 NZ Gazette and Spectator (Wellington) 3 quoted in 1982 Richards Whaling and Sealing in the Chatham Islands 22]

>  <b>GODLEY</b> What is called `the Beach'; that is the long [Wellington] street (about a mile and a half) if street it can be called, that has houses only on one side and runs along by the sea. <i>(Aug 27, 1850,Letters,94,1951 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1850 Aug 27 Godley Letters (1951) 94]

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> All the classes of men ... congregated [c1840s] on Thorndon and Te Aro flats, and along what we knew for years as `the Beach', but is now known as Lambton Quay ... <i>(1910,Reminiscences,5</i>)

>  <b>COAD</b> Truth to tell, he had been a nuisance to his relatives and they had shipped [42] him to New Zealand with orders to make his fortune. This he evidently thought could be done by strolling about the beach [at Wellington] with his hands in his pockets, grumbling. <i>(1931,Such Is Life,41</i>)

>  The anniversary of the landing of the first settlers was celebrated from the very first year, usually with aquatic sports off the Queen's wharf, which was much nearer to `The Beach,' as Lambton quay was then called, than it is today. Climbing the greasy pole was always one of the events on Anniversary Day ... [l.c. in above sic] <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,104,1988 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SARAH MATHEW</b> [not shore etc - from ship - first experience of Kororarika], The situation of this place [Bay of Islands] is very beautiful at the head of a fine cove, ... Immediately on the beach stands an extensive well-built store, with wharfs and such buildings as boat houses, etc., ... <i>(Mar 19, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar 19 Sarah Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 94]

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> [(?hist) Kororareka], Since Hobson had landed on the Beach this morning, reading England's Proclamation of Annexation in the little wooden church ..., the Beach's principal business had been to get drunk. <i>(1938,Promenade,22</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> `The Beach', as Kororareka was called, was not that fabled `Beach' which the explorers had sought: the dissolute men known as `the beachcombers' found no gold nuggets; they made a living by preying on seamen in one way or another. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,45</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> I believe there is plenty of gold on this river, but I also believe that there must be pumps to work the ground, as they are all heavy beaches. <i>(Apr 19, 1864,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Apr 19 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 407]

### beach carnival
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORDYCE & MACLEHN</b> In 1933 the progressive association organised Titahi Bay's first beach carnival, at Easter. Yacht races, speed boat races, treaure hunts, children's races, sideshows, a merry-go-round, golf, swimming, tennis and dancing kept visitors entertained.<i>Ibid.</i> 106 [Caption] The Plunket Beach Carnival was opened by the Minister of Health, Jack Marshall. <i>(2000,Bay, The : A History of Community at Titahi Bay,70</i>)

### beach chair
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> There is a small reshuffling of behinds on beach chairs at Harry's contribution. <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,63</i>)

### beach claim
 
 <i>n.</i> [see wet claim, dredging claim, etc.]

>  He bought a beach claim there, and worked it with some success until a storm and flood carried away his plant. <i>(Oct 2004,NZ Memories,No. 50/8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining] (See [contrast bank claim](../C#contrast-bank-claim))

>  Arrow Township, 10th July. - ... Huts, tents, wheels, sluice-boxes - everything on the beach and river claims have been swept off by the torrent, ... <i>(Jul 18, 1863,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> The Dunstan gold workings were of two kinds - technically known as `beach claims' and `bank claims'. The latter were on the gravelly river banks which the miners sluiced away bodily for the golden grains ... The former were on the sandy margins of the river, and were often extended by means of `wing-dams' and other ingenious devices far into the rushing waters. In these claims the gold was found lodged in `pockets' or fissures formed by the slate bars ... <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,Bk I iv 17</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> Collet's quicksilver plates rapidly became common on the beach-claims although they did not completely supplant the simpler cradle and sluice-box. <i>Ibid.</i> 244 It was a matter of nice judgement. The digger recognized four classes of payable ground: `tucker ground' kept him in food with nothing to spare; a `wages claim' gave him a pound or two above living expenses; a `riser' was a rich claim; and a `piler' or `homeward bounder' the richest of all. Probably most of the `pilers' were beach-claims, where the leads were shallow and rich, the outlay small and provisions cheap because of easy access. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,170,1962 ed.</i>)

### beach custodian
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORDYCE & MACLEHN</b> Charlie Hasse helped Adam Wright as a beach custodian each season between Labour Weekend and Easter. They directed traffic, collected rubbish and glass bottles, maintained and cleaned public facilities, and were responsible for general beach maintenance. <i>(2000,Bay, The : A History of Community at Titahi Bay,69</i>)

### beach diggings
 
 <i>n.</i> [West Coast; cf. beach claim.]

>  <b>MUELLER</b> The greatest drawback with beach diggins is, they are so quickly washed out. Unless they find gold in the Ranges, and have deep sinking, there is very little prospect of the diggers now at the Coast remaining long. <i>(Jan 2, 1866,My Dear Bannie,100,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> From the `Fire-mill' ... and other beach diggings in the neighbourhood [of Okarito] tons of gold were won; and a little is still obtained by industrious beach-miners. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,90</i>)

### beach front
 
 <i>n.</i> [Jim Cameron writes: OED says <i>chiefly US</i> and all citations refer to places outside the UK. It has long been used in NZ.]

>  <b>FORDYCE & MACLEHN</b> Others associated with beachfront places at this time included Charlie Hayward and the two auctioneers from Wellington - Clifford Dunbar Sloane and Eric Johnston, keen fishermen out most weekends on their boats. <i>(2000,Bay, The : A History of Community at Titahi Bay,67</i>)

### beach gold
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> `Beach gold!' I once heard an old-timer remark with heavy scorn, `it's like feathers. ...' <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,38,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> The sinking was shallow, the ground dry, and the gold coarser than usual for beach-gold. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,219,1962 ed.</i>)

### beach horse
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEAVE</b> ... on one occasion, a team of beach horses, especially fine animals that served the surf boats [on the South Canterbury coast], were stuck in the creek at Severn Street ... <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,83</i>) [NOTES: 1980 W.Gard Reminiscences of 1860s in Neave The Land of Munros 83]

### beach house
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We originally travelled to the Bay for weekends while the McCormick clan built what was to be a family beach house. <i>(Jun 24, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

### beach lead
 
 <i>n.</i> [mining]

>  <b>DOBSON</b> Very rich gold-bearing ground was found at Charleston, and in the terraces on the adjoining hills, old beach leads were worked up to 500 feet above sea level, ... <i>(1930,Reminiscences,154</i>)

### beach potato
 
 <i>n.</i> a beach version of a couch potato

>  Even for a beach potato like myself, Don Grady certainly makes the prospect of Queen Charlotte Sound excessively inviting. <i>(Jan 5, 1997,Sunday Star Times,D4</i>)

### beach ranger
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) a beachside robber [(obs) -->[nf OED2, W3: ?f. an alteration of bush-ranger--> (AND 1806): poss. too early (Earle was in NZ in 1827) to suggest influence of beachcomber (OED2 1840, NZ 1845).]]

>  <b>EARLE</b> But there is another class of Europeans here [in the Bay of Islands], who are both useless and dangerous ... These men are called `Beach Rangers;' most of whom have deserted from, or have been turned out of whalers for crimes, for which, had they been taken home and tried, they would have been hanged; some few among them ... had deserted from their ships, and were then leading a mean and miserable life amongst the natives. <i>(1832,Narrative of a Nine Months Residence in NZ,82,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> An even earlier form,<i> beach ranger</i>, apparently formed on the Australian <i>bushranger</i> ... is recorded by A. Earle ... in 1827. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,14</i>)

### beach resort
 
 <i>n.</i> [in OED only in two definitions of headwords]

>  <b>FORDYCE & MACLEHN</b> The benefits of the Wellington-Manawatu Railway line were already being felt and the recent subdivision at the beach resort of Plimmerton was a topic of local conversation. <i>(2000,Bay, The : A History of Community at Titahi Bay,50</i>)

### beach umbrella
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The families turn the beach into a feudal society and mark out the fiefdoms with beach umbrellas. <i>(Feb 26, 1977,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

### beach workings
 
 <i>n.</i> [mining]

>  <b>MAY</b> The beach-workings, which began so promisingly on the Watsons' South Beach claim, extended in spectacular fashion during the last three months of the year. <i>Ibid.</i> 186 The beach-workings of south Westland, stretched along sixty miles of coastline with the diggers perched on the sand between bush and high-water, were in sharp contrast to the Ross gold-field <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,170,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> When the river is `up' the beach workings are covered and the occupants cease from their labours. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,I/viii/30,1889,1974 ed.</i>)

### beach-bird
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Bluff <i>oyster-catcher, avocet,</i>/ And tripping beach-birds, seldom met/ Elsewhere - ... <i>(1833,Ranolf and Amohia,2/63</i>)

### beach-miner
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> From the `Fire-mill' ... and other beach diggings in the neighbourhood [of Okarito] tons of gold were won; and a little is still obtained by industrious beach-miners. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 90</i>)

### beach-trader
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Although at the outset the Maori got rid of his coins ... by purchasing `anything and everything' from beach-traders, he later found that his visible riches were an embarrassment. <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,37</i>)

### beachcomb
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>CRUMP</b> I remember when I was beachcombing up in the Bay of Islands ... What, just living off the beach, Son? ... Yeah. Bit of fishing and mussel-picking, sheep-stealing and garden-raiding when a man felt energetic or hungry. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,61</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> [West Coast mining], For a considerable time [in 1860s] I was coasting and beach combing until Wakamarina rush took place. <i>(1914,Digger Stories,45</i>)

### beachcomber
 
 <i>n.</i> A prospector or miner who works black-sand beaches for gold, a new goldfields formation from beach n2. (cf beach claim), and comb @v and comber @n, (cf beacher); and perhaps associated with the older beachcomber 1. above. (See [blacksanding](../B#blacksanding))

>  <b>BATEMAN</b> The small quantity of gold under the boulders, which lie on the sandy beach [on the West Coast, South Island] is generally supposed by gold prospectors, otherwise termed `beach-combers', to be due to the lifting action of the surf. <i>(1881,Colonists,36</i>)

>  The beaches ... have now been worked for many years, but the yields are not so good. ... still, the `beach-combers' make fair wages. <i>(1887,Handbook NZ Mines,217</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(Though by 1846 (Auckland), and 1849 (Canterbury) the word seemed derog. used throughout the colony, the first recorded use above has perhaps some reference to or colour from Wellington's main waterfront street of the 1840s, `The Beach'.)]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Idle, drunken vagabond ... he wanders about without any fixed object, cannot get employed by the whaler or anyone else, as it is out of his power to do a day's work; and he is universally known as the `beach-comber'. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/338</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Even the corrupt beachcombers and whalers of Kapiti would go out of their way to say a good word ... for Mr. Hadfield. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,2/4</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> ... a few half-drunken loafers (beach-combers, as they are called here [in Auckland]) hanging about the numerous pot-houses. <i>(Jul 29, 1846,Sketches in NZ,4,1849 ed.</i>)

>  Funds are running out and the evil consequences of employing beach-combers, escaped convicts and sailors [in pre-settlement Canterbury] are becoming every day more apparent. <i>(Dec 9, 1849,Torlesse Papers,117,1958 ed.</i>)

>  ... Lyttelton .... by all accounts is the receptacle of loose beachcombers and escaped lags and convicts, who have been employed in preference to honest and able men from the settlements. <i>(Feb 17, 1850,Torlesse Papers,131,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> It was impossible that a community [Kororareka in 1838] composed of sailors ..., convicts, traders, beach-combers, sawyers, and New Zealanders could live together ... <i>Ibid.</i> 297The Pakeha Maori must not be confounded with the idlers and beachcombers who loitered about Kororareka. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,285</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> It is a series of three islands with a climate varying from temperate to subtropical, a land of industry with no place for the beachcomber; ... <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,10,1949 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUICK</b> During the remainder of the day [February 1840] a strong effort was made by the beachcombers and whisky sellers of Kororareka to spread dissension among the chiefs ... <i>(1936,Treaty of Waitangi,149</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealanders have coined or adapted many expressions to meet local requirements, as illustrated by the following: ... <i>beachcomber</i>: first applied to deserting seamen and escaped convicts from Australia. Recorded before 1840 <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),117</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Several sealers lived permanently on the Chathams because there was no law there as the group only nominally belonged to England and was not yet a part of New Zealand. It was the home of escaped convicts, ne'er-do-wells and beachcombers from Australia chiefly, but those with the worst reputation came from Callao in Peru. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,101</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> `The Beach', as Kororareka was called, was not that fabled `Beach' which the explorers had sought: the dissolute men known as `the beachcombers' found no gold nuggets; they made a living by preying on seamen in one way or another. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,45</i>)

>  <i>beachcomber n.</i> man, often an outcast, making a precarious living on beach or remote place [N.Z. origin, first recorded in 1844. An earlier form was <i>beachranger</i>]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POWER</b> ... a few half-drunken loafers (beach-combers, as they are called here [in Auckland]) hanging about the numerous pot-houses. <i>Ibid.</i> 1849 Dec 9 (1958 ed. ) 117 Funds are running out and the evil consequences of employing beach-combers, escaped convicts and sailors [in pre-settlement Canterbury] are becoming every day more apparent. <i>Ibid.</i> 1850 Feb 17, 131 ... Lyttelton .... by all accounts is the receptacle of loose beachcombers and escaped lags and convicts, who have been employed in preference to honest and able men from the settlements. <i>(Jul 29, 1846,Sketches in NZ,4</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> It was impossible that a community [Kororareka in 1838] composed of sailors ..., convicts, traders, beach-combers, sawyers, and New Zealanders could live together ... <i>Ibid.</i> 297 The Pakeha Maori must not be confounded with the idlers and beachcombers who loitered about Kororareka. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,I/285</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> It is a series of three islands with a climate varying from temperate to subtropical, a land of industry with no place for the beachcomber; ... <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,10</i>) [NOTES: 1949 ed.]

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealanders have coined or adapted many expressions to meet local requirements, as illustrated by the following: ... beachcomber: first applied to deserting seamen and escaped convicts from Australia. Recorded before 1840 <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),117</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Several sealers lived permanently on the Chathams because there was no law there as the group only nominally belonged to England and was not yet a part of New Zealand. It was the home of escaped convicts, ne'er-do-wells and beachcombers from Australia chiefly, but those with the worst reputation came from Callao in Peru. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,101</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> `The Beach', as Kororareka was called, was not that fabled `Beach' which the explorers had sought: the dissolute men known as `the beachcombers' found no gold nuggets; they made a living by preying on seamen in one way or another. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,45</i>)

>  beachcomber n. man, often an outcast, making a precarious living on beach or remote place [N.Z. origin, first recorded in 1844. An earlier form was beachranger]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>BUICK</b> During the remainder of the day [February 1840] a strong effort was made by the beachcombers and whisky sellers of Kororareka to spread dissension among the chiefs ... <i>(1936,Treaty of Waitangi,149</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The Pakeha Maori must not be confounded with the idlers and beach-combers who loitered about Kororareka. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,I/297</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Most of the beachcombers are there for this treat, plus what looks to be most of the inhabitants of the pa. <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,104</i>) [NOTES: Jim Cameron writes : <i>Beachcomber</i> may have a wider meaning than is given in DNZE sense 2 (a person who searches beaches for interesting or valuable objects) and be applied to a person who simply frequents or lives at the beach.]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> A person who searches beaches for interesting or valuable objects

>  A strong smell seems to be considered a necessary attribute of ambergris by the average beach-comber. <i>(Sep 30, 1972,Marlborough Express,3/3</i>)

### beachcombing
 
 <i>n.</i> used to connote the irresponsibility associated with beach-combers n1..

>  While putting up a printing office [in Port Nicholson] I picked up two wanderers, who I engaged to saw ... I adopted them because they were shaved and clean. ... They appeared to me to contrast to great advantage [?with] the regular `beach comber'. I write the phrase large because I think it is [???new] to you. It is appropriate to the class. Its meaning I assume is evident. The `beach combing' class is large. The fellows are daring, dirty, requiring much excitement, accustomed to work on shore and on board, generally about the beach, and take what they find without any very strong sense of doing that which is improper. <i>(May 1840,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1840 May 15 S. Revans to H. Chapman [TS] Letters 42]

>  Except among the old whalers and the `beachcombing population' marriages with native women seldom take place. <i>(1853,New Zealand and Its Six Colonies</i>)

>  ... such political beachcombing vituperation as has been indulged in [by the <i>Petone Chronicle</i>]. <i>(Dec 21, 1912,Hutt Valley Independent,3</i>) [NOTES: [The reference here may be partly and snidely to the fact that Petone is a beachfront town.]]



 
 <i>n.</i> [Orsman database: (?ppl a) [nf OED, W3, P] A transferred use, derog. used to connote the irresponsibility associated with beach-combers n1..]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> I remember when I was beachcombing up in the Bay of Islands ... What, just living off the beach, Son? ... Yeah. Bit of fishing and mussel-picking, sheep-stealing and garden-raiding when a man felt energetic or hungry. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,61</i>)

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> Generally the greater portion of the sand is removed, but in favourable spots, sheltered either by a slight indentation in the coast-line, or by some large pieces of drifted timber, its heavier particles remain; these consist [529] of black iron-sand (both magnetic and titaniferous oxide of iron) associated with small garnets and with gold. These black layers are often one or two inches thick, and repose upon coarse quartzose sands. As soon as a storm has subsided, the `beachers' or `surfers' as they are called, examine the coastline near their houses. When they come upon one of the rich spots, the fine particles of gold being often visible to the naked eye, they at once remove the black layer of sand out of the reach of the tide, and wash it when convenient. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,528</i>) [NOTES: Quote from 1868]

>  While putting up a printing office [in Port Nicholson] I picked up two wanderers, who I engaged to saw ... I adopted them because they were shaved and clean. ... They appeared to me to contrast to great advantage [?with] the regular `beach comber'. I write the phrase large because I think it is [???new] to you. It is appropriate to the class. Its meaning I assume is evident. The `beach combing' class is large. The fellows are daring, dirty, requiring much excitement, accustomed to work on shore and on board, generally about the beach, and take what they find without any very strong sense of doing that which is improper. <i>(May 15, 1840,Letters,42</i>) [NOTES: 1840 May 15 S. Revans to H. Chapman [TS] Letters 42]

>  ... such political beachcombing vituperation as has been indulged in [by the Petone Chronicle]. -->[The reference here may be partly and snidely to the fact that Petone is a beachfront town.] <i>(Dec 21, 1912,Hutt Valley Independent,3</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> He was ... a rolling stone who moved about the country ... beachcombing with Maoris in Northland ... <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,110</i>) [NOTES: 1986 ed. Paua Gatherers]

>  It's too cold for beach-combing down our way. <i>(1947,Big Game,106</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Idle, drunken vagabond ... he wanders about without any fixed object, cannot get employed by the whaler or anyone else, as it is out of his power to do a day's work; and he is universally known as the `beach-comber'. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/4 Even the corrupt beachcombers and whalers of Kapiti would go out of their way to say a good word ... for Mr. Hadfield. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,I/338-339</i>)

>  Except among the old whalers, and the 'beachcombing population," marriages with native women seldom took place. <i>(1853,New Zealand and Its Six Colonies,58</i>)

>  <b>HASMAN-JUDD</b> So when he drops off I go for a wander along the beach, just beachcombing really. <i>(2000,Woman Who Loved Plants & Other Stories, The,117</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HARPER</b> A few diggers live here [Gillespies Beach, West Coast], working for gold on the beach ... This `beach combing' is sometimes profitable, as a great deal of `surfacing', or black gold-bearing sand, is now and then deposited after a storm, ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,83</i>)

>  [At Hokitika] the gold of the black-sand deposits is very fine, and ... the recovery of a high percentage presents some difficulty. The various methods of recovery known as `beach-combing', have already been described. <i>(1906,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 1 (N.S.) 91</i>) [NOTES: (Bell)]

>  ... while along the beach north of the river `beach-combing' is sometimes resorted to after heavy weather, to extract the precious metal from the leads of `black sand' with which [gold] is associated. <i>(1910,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 1 (NS) 37</i>) [NOTES: (Webb), [Westport District]]

>  <b>PFAFF</b> [West Coast mining], For a considerable time [in 1860s] I was coasting and beach combing until Wakamarina rush took place. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,45</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> When they come upon one of the rich spots, the fine particles of gold being often visible to the naked eye, they at once remove the black layer of sand out of reach of the tide, and wash it when convenient.* ... [fn] This method of working the beaches was also called `haymaking' or `beachcombing'. These terms were not applied to beach-workings above high-water mark. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,222,1962 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1868 Haast in 1967 May West Coast Gold Rushes (1962) 222]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> [1959 citation below belongs with beachcomb verb q.v. (TD)]

>  <b>GASKELL</b> It's too cold for beach-combing down our way. <i>(1947,Big Game,106</i>) [NOTES: [nf W3] (A transferred and weakened use, perhaps a coinage, formed rather from beach-comber n1 than from a verb.) ?Lazing on the beach.]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> He was ... a rolling stone who moved about the country ... beachcombing with Maoris in Northland ... <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,110</i>) [NOTES: (Paua Gatherers)]

### beachcrosser
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The first clue comes in a chapter headed " Beachcrossers 1769-1839": "Unlike Tasman, James Cook actually crossed New Zealand beaches, literally and metaphorically." <i>(May 6, 2005,NZ Herald,p18</i>)

>  There are few Belich-type catchphrases (though "beachcrossers" may enter the lexicon)... <i>(Jul 2, 2005,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

### beacher
 
 <i>n.</i> A prospector or miner who works the blacksand beaches for gold; beach-comber 3. cf surfacer, surfacing. [West Coast goldmining]

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> As soon as a storm has subsided, the `beachers' or `surfacers,' as they are called, examine the coast-line near their houses. When they come upon one of the rich spots, the fine particles of gold being often visible to the naked eye, they at once remove the black layer of sand out of the reach of the tide, and wash it when convenient. <i>Ibid.</i> 364-365 In the descriptive part of this publication I have already alluded to the black-sand beachers, who watch the coast principally ... where small indentations favour the preservation of magnetic ... iron-sands <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,159</i>)

>  <b>ELDER</b> On this ... beach he found ... a number of miners who worked ... amid the black-iron [check] sands to be found on the beaches. In the early days of the gold-rush many of these `beachers' or `surfacers' had prospected the whole southern part [of the West Coast]. <i>(1930,Goldseekers and Bushrangers in NZ,155</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> BEACHER: a miner working a sea-beach claim. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,526</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> As soon as a stream has subsided, the "beachers" or "surfacers," as they are called, examine the coast-line near their houses. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,159</i>)

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> Generally the greater portion of the sand is removed, but in favourable spots, sheltered either by a slight indentation in the coast-line, or by some large pieces of drifted timber, its heavier particles remain; these consist [529] of black iron-sand (both magnetic and titaniferous oxide of iron) associated with small garnets and with gold. These black layers are often one or two inches thick, and repose upon coarse quartzose sands. As soon as a storm has subsided, the `beachers' or `surfers' as they are called, examine the coastline near their houses. When they come upon one of the rich spots, the fine particles of gold being often visible to the naked eye, they at once remove the black layer of sand out of the reach of the tide, and wash it when convenient. <i>(1868,Julius Von Haast,528</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> ?An old shore-whaler (?or f. naut. on the beach; cf beached. [Otago whalers']

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> At each place which I have visited, I have been assured that the Maories are dying rapidly - `perishing like rotten sheep,' these people often say; thus at Kurreroo or Molineux, where there are now half-a-dozen natives, they say that formerly, and at no remote period, about two generations previous to the present, there were above a thousand; and in many other places within the last sixteen years, which is about the extent of the experience of the older beachers, there has been, in many places, a corresponding diminution of numbers. If this is an unexaggerated statement, it is a very distressing one to reflect on. Since Dr. -- visited this settlement, one of the residents, Mr. --, an educated man, has opened a school for half-bred or any other children, and the progress which the children have already made is very encouraging. <i>(May 22, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 May 22 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 223]

### beachie
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> Sure that's it. All the beachies at Northend are talking about it. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,27</i>) [NOTES: (Doss-House)]

### beachmaster
 
 <i>n.</i> [shore whaling, hist]

>  <b>GROVER</b> The chief-headsman, or beachmaster, as he was often known, did not keep order [among a shore party in the 1830s] ...by knocking down any man who argued with him. <i>(1982,Cork of War,53</i>)

### beachscape
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORDYCE & MACLEHN</b> [Hdg] Beachscape and boat changes <i>(2000,Bay, The : A History of Community at Titahi Bay,98</i>)

### beachside
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> For almost two weeks in the January of my fourteenth summer my sister and I stayed with our Aunt Elly and Uncle Roy in a beachside cabin. <i>(1990,Bad Music,14</i>)

>  <b>FORDYCE & MACLEHN</b> Beach side development of Tireti Rd was a mix of weekend baches and a few more substantial places.<i>Ibid.</i> Her regular clinics were run from an old bach behind the northern boatsheds where some of her patients' beachside injuries included jellyfish stings, cuts and lacerations caused by fish hooks, box thorn prickles and the barbed wire which had been placed around the beach during the war. <i>(2000,Bay, The : A History of Community at Titahi Bay,66</i>)

### Beachy Bill
 
 <i>n.</i> [WW1 Gallipoli]

>  <b>WAITE</b> The most deadly gun (or a battery of them) fired from the Olive Groves away inland from Gaba Tepe. As this gun enfiladed the beach, it became widely known as `Beachy Bill'. <i>(1919,New Zealanders at Gallipoli,186</i>)

### Beacon
 
 <i>n.</i> (Geology) geology of Antarctic classification [usually with article: 'the type Beacon'; also Beacon (Supergroup)]

>  The "Beacon", probably the best known name in Antarctic geology, is now being defined; limits are being set, and its whole nature is becoming known. [5] This highlights the necessity for workers in areas other than the Trans-Antarctic Mountains to endeavour to see the type Beacon, and conversely those workers familiar with the type Beacon should try to see what is regarded as the Beacon in other areas, particularly the Antarctic Peninsula. <i>(Dec 1970,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,29/4, 5</i>)

>  Miss R. Askin: Palaeobotanical Studies of Beacon Supergroup, Antarctica. <i>(May 1971,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,30/28</i>)

### beacon school
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ...already parents report one of the reasons they are desperate to get into so-called beacon schools is that, with a shortage of quality teachers, it's the big popular schools that are less likely to have problems attracting well-qualified staff... When the child completes its education - paid for by the taxpayers of New Zealand who cannot get their own children into those beacon schools - the family will leave. <i>(Feb 16, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

### beagle boys
 
 <i>n.</i> police dog squad

>  We don't want confrontation - the moment it gets that way we have to use the beagle boys and the Maori wardens and that's where it gets ugly. <i>(Feb 8, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A13</i>)

### Beagle Boys
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mr Cosgrove was a key member of the infamous "Beagle Boys Brigade" that surrounded Mr Moore when he was ousted as Labour leader by Helen Clarke in 1993. <i>(Aug 23, 1999,Dominion,2</i>)

>  He praised the "Beagle boys" - a group of young men who'd helped organise Labour's 1993 election campaign. <i>(Aug 25, 1999,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  The campaign team earned the name "Beagle Boys" for their black eyes, caused by working 18 to 20 hours a day trying to get Labour into government. <i>(Apr 12, 2005,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

### Beagleholean
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>HOARE</b> Lest we slip, however, irrevocably, into the Beagleholean slough of Forster despond - that imaginary Tolkienish world of Teutonic druids and fantasies in which the New Zealand editor of Cook delighted for his anti-Forster excursions - we do well to recall ... that not all of Forster's relationships were ... disastrous. <i>(1982,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,1/75</i>)

### beak
 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1940 Wanganui Collegiate. N.C.C. Small, boys' cry (from beak =`nose')]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the [slang] expressions listed by children in two schools are: `Up the shoot, going eyes out, bullswool, go jump in the lake, ... shivery dick, shiver me timbers, ... you're telling I, got it on the brain, like smoke in a wheelbarrow' (Kaitaia), `Sheila, crow, bud, frig it, grub, holy moses, blast it, mole, balls to you, shut your face, ... nerts to you, go bite your back, have a roll, fluff off, scram, ants in your pants, rag bag, ... you're bats, dronk, drongo, dill, dip, dippy, ya big lug, greetings gruesome, who dented your beak. (South Wellington). 2. (ejaculation) [nf W3] Mind your own business! Get your nose out of my business! <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/677</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a constable; a trooper.

>  [The runaway] saw me ride up to the house and thought I was a beak. <i>(Jul 20, 1861,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,64,1914 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a prefect. [1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 24C BEAKS = PREFECTS]

### beaked whale
 
 <i>n.</i> [southern beaked whale, NZ beaked whale, Arnoux's beaked whale, et al]

>  A rare Gray's beaked whale that died in a stranding at Pencarrow Head was pregnant... It is only the 15th known stranding of the species in the Wellington region since 1874. <i>(May 27, 2004,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  The rarest "find" ever recorded in New Zealand is the beaked whale, Tasmacetus shepherdi, which was washed ashore near Hawera in 1933. <i>(Jan 27, 1938,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Beaked Whales Not Uncommon. <i>(Apr 29, 1942,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  "The animal is one of the so-called beaked whales, and is known as the strap-toothed whale, on account of the two peculiar teeth which grow from the lower jaw and curve over the upper jaw, so that the mouth is almost prevented from opening. .." <i>(Mar 29, 1912,Colonist (Nelson),6</i>)

>  Beaked Whales Not Uncommon. <i>(Apr 29, 1942,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  The New Zealand beaked whale (<i>Mesoplodon hectori</i>) is known only from a skull in the British Museum and a skeleton in the United States National Museum, Washington, but of the latter no description has been published. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/136</i>) [NOTES: (Oliver)]

>  The southern beaked whale <i>(Mesoplodon grayi)</i> is distinguished by its pair of triangular flat teeth being situated just in front of the hinder end of the symphysis of the mandible. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/135</i>) [NOTES: Oliver]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [NZ beaked whale]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> A garfish cf half-beak.

### beako
 
 <i>n.</i> nose

>  Michael Cullen said "I'm going to stick my beako in here". <i>(Dec 1, 2000,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Sean Plunket on "Nine to Noon"]

### beam
 
 <i>n.</i> [FW =?, modern chain]

>  <b>CARR</b> ... there is only one job that the most fastidious could refuse, and that is `picking pie'. Tuis [?=This] consist [sic] in pulling the wool off the trimmings that have been taken off by the `wool pullers' at the beam. These trimmings are ... allowed to rot until the wool comes away easily. [Pie pickers] live and eat by themselves. <i>(1913,Country Work,19</i>)

### beamer
 
 <i>n.</i> [Cf OED2 beaming 2. (Tanning) 1885 `"Beaming", or unhairing, derives its name from an inclined convex wooden form called a "beam", on which the hide is spread during the operation.': also beam v. 4.; cf EDD beam sb1 5. a fleshing-beam.]

>  <b>HOWARD</b> Incoming skins are sorted to arbitrary standards - wigs (old males), clap-matches (females), bulls, yearlings, grey or silver pups (under one year), black pups. If the cargo is intended for the European or American market, the skins are removed from the animal with an inch of blubber adhering, and in the final simple treatment are washed, heavily slated, and then folded or `booked' into rectangular shape for packing. Skins destined for China, however, are treated with more preliminary care. They are taken as cleanly as possible, and after careful washing are sent to the `beamer' to be thoroughly scraped free of the last remaining particles of flesh and blubber. <i>(1940,Rakiura,40</i>)

### bean
 
 <i>n.</i> money, a coin [ca1950 You got the beans, eh?]

>  A relic of the past was discovered in a ploughed field at Taumarere, one day last week. It was in the shape of a George III. penny, dated 1797, ... If that `bean' could speak, what wouldn't it say on the land policy of New Zealand's early days. <i>(Oct 7, 1911,NZ Truth,328/1/5</i>)

>  Beans, bread, buckaroos, cash, coin, dough, loot, green stuff, lucre, mazuma, moolah, want some? <i>(Dec 2005,Tearaway,48</i>) [NOTES: ASB ad.]



 
 <i>n.</i> Be beaten up

>  <b>NEWTON</b> BEANS (n) e.g., get one's - . Be beaten up. British army slang quoted by Kipling in 1890. [this from Partridge] <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,244</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> to give a thrashing to, to beat up

>  One guy said if I touched his car, he'd give me my beans,' Hunt said. <i>(Apr 7, 1983,Dominion,7</i>)

### bean-tosser
 
 <i>n.</i> the penis

>  <b>COLIN GORDON</b> When jokers cut themselves there is a nurse [employed by the Works] to do up cuts, etc.; so others try to make out they have cut their cocks, and the terms [associated with] this are to cut your <i>dong-wollop, kidney-puncher, bean-tosser, hawk-sword, family maker. </i> <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Feilding Freezing Works terms]

### bearded cod
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Euclichthys polynemus</i>, fam Euclichthyidae, a small deepwater cod.

>  <b>AYLING</b> 150 BEARDED COD (Eucla cod) <i>Euclichthys polynemus</i> ... The bearded cod is a small deepwater species with a large head and a long tapering body ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,1984 ed.</i>)

### bearded mousetail
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [mousetail](../M#mousetail))

>  <b>FEATON</b> MYOSURUA ARISTATUS (<i>Benth</i>.) The Bearded Mousetail. ... This minute plant is a native of the Northern Island; ... <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,5</i>) [NOTES: And)]

### beardie
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  LOST at Pelorus Bridge, Brown Beardy dog, last seen at Bridge. Would anyone knowing the whereabouts please ring 670 Spring Creek collect. <i>(Oct 21, 1972,Marlborough Express,11/7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a bearded collie, usually used as a huntaway; [also beardy]

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Hammond's got ther best dogs. Beardies. Run all day; gutsy and clever with it. I never seen better holding dogs than his. <i>(1979,Supper Waltz Wilson,89</i>)

>  <i>Beardie</i>. A variety of sheep dog with very long hair, something like a Skye terrier's. (C.) <i>(Sep 9, 1933,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> For his breed - that is for a cross between bull terrier, blue merle, beardie, ... - Flynn was a tractable dog. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,22</i>)

>  Out on the flanks might be ... two young beardies whose main job was heading, and shepherding the stragglers would be Charlie's strong-eyes, Bean and Belle. <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,126,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton `Saving the Breed')]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> <i>Beardy</i>: Shaggy, with hair drooping over the eyes and almost sweeping the ground. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,253</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> Tat was delighted to be free. He frisked around the yard and made friends with the old beardie tied under [34] the dray. <i>(1970,Tat,33</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Beardie</i>: A woolly curly-coated dog. Broadly speaking we have three types of sheepdog - beardie, long or rough-coated and short or smooth-coated. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,168</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... he didn't even bawl at Franz, his yellow Beardie pup, ... <i>(1973,NZ Jack,85</i>)

>  Beardie :A type of dog - very hairy. Usually used as a huntaway. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,24</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> `Don't mind Taipo', she said as she ushered me in. I froze. ...`What the devil is it?' She chuckled. `A Beardie'. `Does it bite?' <i>(1985,G'day Country,122</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> `Don't mind Taipo', she said as she ushered me in. I froze. ...`What the devil is it?' She chuckled. `A Beardie'. `Does it bite?' <i>(1985,G'day Country,122</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BEARDIE: <i>another name for Red Cod </i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,28</i>)

>  <i> beardie n. NZ & Austral.</i> (also <i>beardy</i>) long-haired variety of sheep-dog. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,60</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.],]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a tall (?shaggy-dog) story [ca1951 Dilworth School (Auckland), McWatt's, Letter: `From the name of a boy, White, who was once in the school, and whose nickname was `Beardie' ... `Beardie' told evidently tall yarns and now it means a yarn, although the boy and the reason for the name are almost forgotten.']

### bearer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SALMOND</b> bearer the lowest member in the house frame, sitting directly on the foundations <i>(1986,Glossary of Building Terminology,231</i>)

### bearing
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) everted internal organs in a sheep or cow; a prolapsed vagina

>  Another reason for the losses was a rise in bearings - prolapsed vaginas - in ewes that were carrying more than one lamb. As the ewe died so did the lambs. "Bearings are a mystery to us, we don't know why they vary from farm to farm and from year to year, but whatever it is that sets those up, they're set up this year over quite a big area".... <i>(Nov 15, 2004,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

>  Things that stood out were checking ewes prior to lambing, assisting when cast, dealing with 'bearing problems' and at times acting as midwife. <i>(Jun 2001,NZ Memories,No. 30/43</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an everted vagina or uterus of a ewe [either just prior to or just after lambing; occ. a ewe with a bearing.]

>  .. there have been serious losses among ewes owing to bearing trouble. <i>(Jul 1886,NZ Farmer,36</i>)

>  Among the older ewes in particular, from now, on, there is likely to be a certain amount of trouble through the protrusion of bearings. <i>(Jul 2, 1923,NZ Farmer,939</i>)

>  The bearing is seen protruding. If seen early, the bearing can be successfully returned. <i>(Jun 21, 1926,NZ Journal of Agriculture,376</i>)

>  The incidence of 'bearing' trouble is considered often to be connected with the type of feed. <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,111</i>)

>  It is certainly the short, blocky ewe with the wide pelvis which is the most liable to put out her bearing. <i>(Jun 15, 1939,NZ Journal of Agriculture,470</i>)

>  They [the new breed ewes] will also be able to be fed well without the bug bear of bearings or getting cast. <i>(Sep 14, 2000,Wanganui Chronicle,12</i>)

>  Nearly all breeding sheep flocks in New Zealand are affected by vaginal prolapse or bearings, at a cost of between $30 million and $200 million. [bearing trouble] <i>(Apr 26, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  A high incidence of bearings (prolapsed uterus) was common, while abortion problems randomly affected properties. <i>(Nov 25, 2005,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

### bearing ewe
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ewe with a prolapse as a result of lambing [a condition often occuring on flat to easy country where ewes close to lamb become lazy and won't get up to urinate. ]

>  The number of bearing ewes salvaged from death on any farm is very low. <i>(Jul 22, 1971,NZ Farmer,13</i>)

### beastie
 
 <i>n.</i> colostrum [ca1940 Havelock: used of the first milk from a calved house-cow]

>  <b>MRS WILLIAMS</b> For the first week the cow's milk, called `beastie', should be reserved entirely for the calf. <i>(1882,Facts Relating to NZ,36</i>)

### beat
 
 <i>n.</i> The stretch of country over which a musterer operates. [ca1953 Dec R. Mason, `Walking beat' used by Les Roberts, Bluff Station, Clarence.]

>  <b>MOORE</b> On one occasion I had the top beat and had to wait for the men below me. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,18</i>)

>  <i>beat ... NZ</i> area traversed by musterer of sheep (<i>a lambing beat</i>). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.],]

>  <b>TINNE</b> It is customary to invite all the neighbours to your `muster,' as they can then pick out their missing sheep which have strayed from the adjoining runs. As they complete each flock, it is turned over to a shepherd, who would drive it off with the aid of his dogs to a beat, possibly ten or twenty miles distant ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,38</i>)

>  <b>CAIRD</b> When a considerable stretch of country had to be mustered each man got his separate beat. <i>(1874,NZ Sheepfarming,20</i>)

>  That awful Wilson did not muster his beat at all, fiend of a fellow. <i>(Oct 25, 1906,Chudleigh Diary,436,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Beat.</i>-A musterer's <i>b</i>. is the ground off which he has to clear the sheep each day; often top <i>b</i>., bottom <i>b</i>., middle<i> b</i>., etc., also hard and easy <i>bs</i>. The word is also applied to the ground on which a rabbit poisoner has to lay his poison. <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss]

>  <b>BURDON</b> Each man has his beat, for which he is responsible. <i>(1938,High Country,107</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The men were out on their beat by daylight, sometimes well before. The beats were about half a mile apart, or more if the country was open; on broken ground we had to keep pretty close together. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,122,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The following [1924] muster I was entrusted with a `beat' on the hill... <i>Ibid.</i> glossary, Beat: the strip of country each man musters. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,23</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>Beat</i>. A musterer's<i> beat</i> is the ground off which he has to clear the sheep each day, often <i>top beat, bottom beat, middle beat</i>, etc., also <i>hard</i> and <i>easy beats</i>. The word is also applied to the ground on which a rabbit poisoner has to lay his poison. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,356</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Boy was certainly the fastest sheep dog I have ever seen. He put in a splendid hunt, which would have been a bad head if the sheep had not been so fast, and hunted them all on to the next man's beat. <i>Ibid.</i> 18 I was two beats from the top, and there was shingle all the way. It slid continuously, and all the time I had to bear up hill, to keep a level track. However, we got off `the hill' quite early, put the sheep across Cat Creek at Tarndale, and raced for the homestead. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,13</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Before dawn next morning, the musterers, as per custom, in order to be on their beats before sun up, started into action. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,18</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>beat</i>: The strip or area of country each musterer has to cover or muster. Six men, say, will each have own beat to muster when `doing' a block of country. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,185</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> Mr Parkins and Stan were to leave a 3 a.m. for the range, the former working his two dogs and the latter his faithful Pat. `Stan, you know your beat,' announced Mr Parkins ... <i>(1968,Turn of the Tide, The,63</i>)

>  <i>beat n.</i> land patrolled by a shepherd. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area of land allocated to a rabbiter, possum trapper or deer culler, to kill such pests.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> beat: (ii) area of ground allotted to a rabbiter for poisoning. [see also bottom beat, double beat, high beat, lambing beat, low beat, middle beat, mustering beat, outside beat, second beat, shepherd's beat, top beat and walking beat.] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,6</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Simplex <i>beat</i> in sense <i>to hit, to thrash</i> [OED 971] is mainly in secondary school use esp. for corporal punishment, with associated beat (up) etc., and as n pl beats (n), and beating(s). [(strokes). a. beats. -->1984 16-17 F E14 Pakuranga Coll. 23 BEATS [F3M3] -->1984 18 F E60 Pakuranga Coll. 23 BEATS OR A LASH, MAN ! OR THRASH b. beatings. -->1984 16-17 F E36 Pakuranga Coll. 23 BEATINGS [F2M5] -->1984 14 F E119 Wgton Girls C. 23 BEATINGS]



 
 <i>v.</i> ??to (start) walk(ing); ??to search thoroughly

>  <b>SCOTT</b> When you didn't turn up with the grub, we turned out and beat the track, finding the tucker and your camping place. <i>(1900,Colonial Turf,207</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> beat it (on the hoof).

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> ... and in New Zealand among criminals for `to run away' (Nelson Baylis, letter of 1932). <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,28,1961 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to walk, run or go (away).

>  <i>beat the feet:</i> walk <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>)

>  <i>Beat the feet</i>: run, especially on some errand <i>(1981,Auckland Savings Bank</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection),]



 
 <i>v.</i> (prison) To run away, escape [check: prob. beat the fleet v&sup4.]. [[check: prob. beat the fleet v&sup4.].]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BEAT THE FEET (v) Run away, escape. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,244</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> Running from the screws, contrast if correct, beat the feet Big Huey.

>  Everyone who is anyone in the underworld ... when needing to sell a radio, hide out some place if `beating the fleet' or just wanting a few jugs. <i>(Sep 19, 1973,Salient,10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a drover's round or section patrolling a resting cattle-herd

>  All hands walked centry [sic] [over the cattle] all night in this order. ... 40 yards [on] was another fire. Here ended my beat. Draper's began, and went to another fire and to the sea. <i>(Apr 28, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,35,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> 1836 a course habitually traversed by anyone



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mustering or shepherding area.

>  <b>CAIRD</b> When a considerable stretch of country had to be mustered each man got his separate beat. <i>(1874,Notes on Sheepfarming in NZ,20</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Boy.. hunted them all on the next man's beat. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,13</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> In those days, when men were glad of jobs, shearers used to write asking for a 'pen', just as the musterers did to ask for a 'beat' on the station of their choice. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,84</i>)

>  beat: (i) that area which a musterer is detailed off to clear. An easy beat is one of the easy walking, usually the bottom or top beat, though the latter may entail a long initial climb. A hard beat is usually one of the middle beats and very often includes bluffs and also much need to run dogs on the blind. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,6</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> There was no chance of getting mustering because many men in their fifties were still mustering and you had to wait for someone to die before a beat became available. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,38</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> On higher beats there may be no water for thirsty dogs, who dehydrate and die quickly, so prudent musterers carry an emergency bottle of water. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,45</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DAVIES</b> ... the Boss was adamant that the two boys couldn't miss their lambing beat. <i>(1975,Outback,75</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> the ground on which a rabbit poisoner has to lay his poison [beat in Acland Glossary 1934 cf. possum-trapper's block] (See [beat in Acland Glossary 1934](../B#beat-in-acland-glossary-1934))



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> the usual haunt

>  <b>AYSON</b> They got a good muster of cattle, took them to the yards, put them through, kept those that were to be tamed in the paddock, and let the rest out in a mob, each to find its way to its own beat. <i>(1937,Thomas,86</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> I don't think she was ever yarded, though many attempts were made to get her in; finally she had to be shot on her beat in the bush at the Upper Hook. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,135,1954 ed.</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> Position `station'. [ca1953 Dec p.c. R. Mason, `I was on a standing beat' Russell Smith, Manager, Remuera.']



 
<b>6</b> <i>n.</i> a swagger's transit or set round

>  <b>BELL</b> He has crossed the Straits, then. Times must be bad among the stations when Davie [Dunlop the Sundowner] has come so far out of his beat. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,11</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> Well, I took to the track. I came up north [from Southland] and swagged with a character that was well known on the track at that time [the mid 1920s]. ... They were fellows that were on the track permanently, and they all had their beats. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,68</i>) [NOTES: (Bob Edwards, bushman)]

 <i>v.</i> to win an acquittal (See [cf. beat the rap](../B#beat-the-rap))

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BEAT IT (v) Win an acquittal <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,244</i>)

 <i>v.</i> To cut or strip off outer bark and wood cf beating-off wedge. [kauri timbermen] (See [also  backing-up wedge](../A#also--backing-up-wedge))

 <i>n.</i> an often self-conscious pron. of 'beauty' (noun and adj.); ?orig. Aust. and often thought of as an Australianism. [1960 17C M B8 Wanganui Girls C.I 18 BEAUTY PRONOUNCED BJUDEE) -->1984 16 M E81 Pakuranga Coll. 29 BEAUTY (BUDY)]

>  [cartoon: after-match function] He's a little beaudy in the ruck - I'll say that for him ... <i>(Sep 27, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

### Beaumaris shark
 
 <i>n.</i> [UK term for Porbeagle shark]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293], Beaumaris shark 158 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> PORBEAGLE SHARK <i>Lamna nasus</i>: A swife-swimming shark similar in appearance to the mako, but not rated highly by game fishermen ... Also known as Beaumaris-shark and Mackerel-shark. Prominent in New Zealand, particularly in Cook Strait. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,219</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] shark, Beaumaris 158 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### beaut
 
 <i>adj.</i> superior, excellent or admirable; corker, grouse, neat (in predicative use difficult to distinguish from the noun). [ca1935 Havelock ca1960s G.J.Griffiths notes: Aust, but also fully used in NZ; originally merely a slang contraction of beauty or beautiful but now virtually a usage in its own right; but only oral ca1950 2ZB Wellington in Aunt Jenny's `True-life Stories', Jimmy Blake continually uses the aj: e.g. `A beaut new heat-controlled iron is the first prize' to imply a childlike enthusiasm or innocence.]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> What a beaut! What a beaut try! <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,81</i>)

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> Gee, it must be a beaut! But he bet the one his father was going down to finish would be beauter. <i>(1960,Tangahano,25</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> A beaut pair of gossips. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,8</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> Look, Hori, at the lovely tomatoes ... Aren't they beaut, and only 8d a pound. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,4</i>)

>  <b>HARVEY</b> Under our house was a beaut basement where Dad put all his tools and things and there was a big pussy willow ... with a green seat underneath made out of white pine. <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,1</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> She's the makings of a real beaut pig dog. <i>(1965,Stand in the Rain,136</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Australian importations include ... <i>beaut</i> and <i>super</i>, terms of approval, ... <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

>  <i>beaut. ... adj</i>. excellent, splendid [Adopted from U.S. slang]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> PERA. Makes a beaut stole! <i>(1969,Awatea,23,1978 ed.</i>)

>  Helen Shrowder, an Australian teacher, ... who has taken a job at Dunedin Children's Library, said yesterday: `It is beaut working with children in a library setting, and it is going to help me a lot when I get involved in teaching again. <i>(Apr 24, 1972,Otago Daily Times,5</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection),]

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> `Aw, that's a kid's dress. ... There's a beaut style in your Woman's magazine.' <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,29</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>beaut</i>:; BEAUTIFUL in the sense of `that was a beautiful dive into the swimming bath ', or she's a beaut horse or sheila, etc. ... Note that unfamiliar words in New Zealand are likely to be contractions offamiliar ones, as beaut for beautiful, or speedo for speedometer, or park for parking place (see: park). <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,12</i>)

>  <i>beaut Sl., chiefly Austral. & N.Z. ~n. 1. </i>an outstanding person or thing. <i>~adj., interj. 2</i>. good or excellent. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,93</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 60; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 43],]

>  Once when she couldn't have me Dad let me go to the flicks by myself. He gave me some extra money, so I went to the milkbar and bought a gobstopper and some other lollies. I stuffed my face, and felt crook all the way home. He bought new footy boots for my eldest brother too, and an Art Union ticket for Mum. He must have had a beaut time up the RSA that day, I reckon. <i>(1988,University Entrance Board Bursaries Exam,6</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> Wouldn't it be beaut if they abolished distance? <i>(1988,Carpathians, The,66</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a beautiful person, a beauty; an outstanding person or thing. [-->1984 17 M E97 Pakuranga Coll. 8 BEAUT -->1984 14 F E120Wgton Girls C. 4 BEAUT [F2] -->1984 16-17 M E90 Pakuranga Coll. 4 BEAUT (BUET) [M2]]

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> Well I guess she aint a bute. [rhyming with cheroot] <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,79</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [-->1960 17C F B8 Wanganui Girls C. 18 BEAUT [exclam. of joy, etc.] -->1960 17C F B10 Napier Girls H.S. 18 BEAUT]

>  <b>MASON</b> GILHOOLY, <i>rapturous</i>: You beaut! You bloody beaut! I could kiss the face off yer! <i>(1969,Awatea,34,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <i>you beaut</i> <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Something or someone of superior, excellent or admirable quality (often used with wee or little to express endearment; equivalent to, and to some extent replacing, earlier bottler, corker, or bonzer, bosker; whopper etc.), and often passing into an adjectival use. [AND 1898. -->AND Recorded earliest in US: n. 1898, aj also as exclam 1918; OED2 n passing into adj. Also (now rare) bute: Chiefly US, Aust, and NZ slang 1866 (US). -->1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 8 BEAUT [something good] -->1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 4 BEAUT [M3][somethin/-one liked] -->1952 May 6 VUC First Year English Class (6 times), I would include among New Zealand English words: beaut -->1953 14-17 M A6 Thames DHS 4 BEAUT [M12] [something liked] -->1953 14-15 M A12 Thames DHS 8 BEAUT [M5] [something good] -->1960 16C F B1 Nelson Girls C. 8 BEAUT -->1960 16C F B3 Marton D.H.S. 8 BEAUT -->1960 17C M B6 St Bede's, Chch 8 BEAUT]

>  Okahu's flagship is the Kaz range and the latest 1998 Kaz shiraz and cabernet are beauts. <i>(Dec 9, 2000,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  You're a beaut," I said. "Wouldn't it rotate you!" <i>(Jan 24, 1944,NZEF Times,III/135 : 11</i>)

>  Isn't it a beaut? <i>(Aug 23, 1943,NZEF Times,III/113 : 6</i>)

>  Everything in Bulgaria was beaut and hummer. <i>(Sep 18, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/169 : 4</i>)

>  She's a beaut, Dig. <i>(Sep 25, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/170 : 10</i>)

>  A certain Kiwi of his acquaintance put what may justly be called "a real beaut" over "George." <i>(Dec 23, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/183 : 14</i>)

>  Lord! a smash/ From someone's boot/ Caught him a `bute'/ Now he's a mash <i>(Apr 7, 1906,NZ Truth,42/1/7/</i>)

>  Eleven tries were notched and only two were converted, one by Rush, however, being a `beaut'. <i>(Jul 11, 1908,NZ Truth,160/3/2</i>)

>  `A curse of a Fritz .../ Guv me a prick .../ A beaut of a Blighty, be Gosh!' <i>(Jul 10, 1919,Quick March,17</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> I'm reading a beaut now, Drag Harlan. <i>(1950,Some Other Country,80</i>) [NOTES: 1984 ed.]

>  I'm reading a beaut. It's a corker. <i>(1950,Landfall,13/20</i>) [NOTES: (Gaskell) [Children]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> What a beaut! What a beaut try! <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,81</i>)

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> `I don't want a man to help, Missus. I want my dad to say I bought a beaut.' <i>(1960,Tangahano,148</i>)

>  ~ n.(slang) anything excellent. superfine, especially in phrases, its a beaut, what a beaut <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

 <i>adv.</i> [ca1935 Look, I can ride beaut.]

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 8 BEAUT [somewthing good] -->1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 4 BEAUT [M3][somethin/-one liked] ->1953 14-17 M A6 Thames DHS 4 BEAUT [M12] [something liked] -->1953 14-15 M A12 Thames DHS 8 BEAUT [M5] [something good] -->1960 16C F B1 Nelson Girls C. 8 BEAUT -->1960 16C F B3 Marton D.H.S. 8 BEAUT -->1960 17C M B6 St Bede's, Chch 8 BEAUT ---->1984 17 M E97 Pakuranga Coll. 8 BEAUT -->1984 14 F E120Wgton Girls C. 4 BEAUT [F2] -->1984 16-17 M E90 Pakuranga Coll. 4 BEAUT (BUET) [M2]]

>  <b>SMYTH</b> We didn't quite get the hang of things first go off, but now I reckon it will be a `beaut' [sc. record]. <i>(1929,Girl from Mason Creek, The,161</i>)

>  <i>Solo</i>: She was cute. <i>All</i>: You betcha! Gee! <i>Solo</i>: What a beaut! <i>(1937,Auckland Capping Book,24</i>)

>  I'm reading a beaut. It's a corker. <i>(1950,Landfall,13/20</i>) [NOTES: (Gaskell)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> What a beaut! What a beaut try! <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,81</i>)

>  <b>WALLIS</b> `I have to hand it to you, Shona,' Ron had said. `It's a beaut.' <i>(1963,Point of Origin,129</i>)

>  <i> ~ n.(slang)</i> anything excellent. superfine, especially in phrases, its a beaut, what a beaut <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

>  <i>beaut Sl., chiefly Austral. & N.Z. ~n. 1.</i> an outstanding person or thing. <i>~adj., interj. 2.</i> good or excellent. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,93</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 60; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 43],]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (?SI); often, esp. in Otago-Southland, the collocation wee beaut is used idiomatically as a familiar term of admiration, encouragement, etc. where gen. NZ usage might prefer little beaut, see wee aj. [->ca1951 Dunedin, It's a wee beaut (of a kitten).]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HARVEY</b> I let out a real beaut [bad word] at the table one night and Dad grabbed me by the collar and took me into the bathroom. <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,6 [n]</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> A wild party, one of those real beauts where they poured beer inside the piano, to mellow the tone ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,175</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> `I'll go up North to Auckland ... save up money and go to England, where the poms are. ... My grandad was one of them and he was a real beaut. ... He was a boozer and loved the trots, went every Saturday.' <i>(1897,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,54,1988 ed.</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> Ironical use from prec. sense, often in senses of `character', dag; hard case. [ca1949 He's a regular beaut - been on the piss solid for a week.]

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> Pommie officers are beauts ... Hasn't anyone told them that we are fighting for democracy. <i>(1944,Troop Target,26</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Missus Wagner's a bit of a beaut, isn't she. She's a hard case all right. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,159</i>)

### beautiful white devil
 
 <i>n.</i> greater bindweed.

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> GREATER CONVOLVULUS [BINDWEED] (<i>Calystegia sepium</i>) has the same popular names as the last species, [i.e. convolvulus] and in addition, beautiful white devil, and its native name of pohue. It is much like convolvulus [bindweed], but is larger in all its parts. The leaves are 3 to 5 inches in length and the flower 2 inches across, more usually white than pink. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,137,1967 ed.</i>)
