 <i>n.</i> something excellent, admirable, or to one's liking [cf. beaut n. 1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 4 BEAUTY [something/-one liked] ] -->1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 4 BEAUTY [M4] -->1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 8 BEAUTY [something good] -->1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 8 BEAUTY [M3] -->1953 14-16 M A5 Thames DHS 4 BEAUTY [M7] -->1953 14-16 M A2 Thames DHS 8 BEAUTY [M5] -->1960 18C M B11 Nelson Boys C. 8 BEAUTY -->1960 16C F B2 Hawera Tech, C. 8 BEAUTY -->1984 14 F E121Wgton Girls C. 4 BEAUTY [F2] -->1984 16-17 M E82 Pakuranga Coll. 8 BEAUTY [M4]] (See [cf. beaut n](../B#beaut-n))

>  Only a reversion to the steady rhythmic pull will restore his sense of well-being and cause his lips to form.. the words: "You little beauty." <i>(Sep 11, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/168 : 5</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> Our own dogs. .. turned out to be `beauties'. <i>(1897,How I Stole 10,000 Sheep,11</i>)

>  His try was a real `beauty'. He took a pass cleverly, and dashed over in an instant. <i>(Sep 28, 1907,NZ Truth,119/3/4</i>)

 <i>n.</i> in phrase a beauty of a (thing): a very good or great

>  A long time ago I made a beauty of a kite. <i>(1978,Listener Short Stories,2/65</i>) [NOTES: 1963 Cowley in 1978 Listener Short Stories Vol 2 65]

>  `All right, you hori hooer! Send us one [frozen carcase] down for a try. ... If I get one of those big hooers up my arse, you'll get one of my size nine kadoodies up yours! Oh, I'll root you a bewdy with me size nine kadoody.' <i>(1979,Islands,27/461</i>) [NOTES: McGee (Out in the Cold)]

 <i>n.</i> familiar, patronising (sometimes ironical) use in the expression (often exclamation) (you) little/wee beauty [ca1935 You little beauty! ca1950 (Dunedin), You wee beauty! Isn't she a little beauty.]

>  <b>HYDE</b> [of a wound] `Jakeloo, Starkie; she's a little beauty, clean through my arm!' <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,174</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> (See [cf. beaut(y) n](../B#beaut(y)-n))

Yeah, they're beauty, eh? although here, of course, the meaning of `beauty' is considerably less aesthetic than the Pakeha concept and has little connection with physical beauty or attractiveness. <i>(1965,Speech and Idiom of Maori Children,94,1963 ed.</i>)

### bed
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also river-bed](../A#also-river-bed))

>  Strictly speaking, the term `bed' is applicable only to that part of the valley actually covered by the flowing water. But the cass, like most New Zealand rivers, is continually liable to flood ... In this paper the term `river-bed', following the colloquial usage, is used to include that part of the valley liable to be covered with water in times of severe floods. This `river-bed' is bounded on either side by a broad expanse of flat land, chiefly tussock-covered, termed colloquially `river-flat', but in this paper `terrace'. <i>(1917,TrNZI,49/4</i>)

Normally a bedlog 1.2m (4 ft) long is sufficient for corner and strainer posts. <i>(1996,Gallagher Power Fence Manual,19</i>)

### bedrock
 
 <i>n.</i> the solid rock base underlying alluvial or other formations (See [also bottom](../A#also-bottom))

>  In the meantime Mr. C. Ring found it necessary to abandon the first digging, owing to the obstruction offered to his reaching bed rock by the presence of large Quartz Boulders, ... <i>(Nov 9, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/26/165</i>) [NOTES: (Report of Sub-Committee on Recent Gold Discovery in the Coromandel District)]

>  That the troughs and lead be made by the diggers: each party supplying an additional length, ... [184] The Californian diggers on the stream [at Kapanga] agree in the belief that the bed rock could be worked in such a manner to great advantage. <i>(Dec 10, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/30/183-184</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy's Report from Coromandel)]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> ... and the nearer to the bed rock the excavations were carried, the more abundant was found to be the yield of gold. <i>(1853,Auckland,92</i>)

>  <b>WEKEY</b> The mere prevalence of blocks of `cement', as it is technically termed, ... forms no sure indication of the presence of a lead, but if the `cement' be found strewn over a limited tract, round which the <i>bed-rock</i> crops out ... it may then be taken as a guide in prospecting. <i>(1862,Otago As It Is,21</i>)

>  BED-ROCK.- The mass of rock in which the veins or lodes occur. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,53</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... we managed ... to prospect most of the creeks ..., though with no success as regarded any discovery of `the colour'. I am myself of opinion that the bed-rock on the eastern side of the dividing range is at so much greater depth [28] ..., as to render it unlikely that it would ever pay for the labour of sinking for a prospect, but time will shew. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,27</i>)

>  ... then the river gradually cut a channel through the flat, as well as many feet down into what we unscientific men call `bed-rock.' <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/132</i>) [NOTES: (Booth)]

>  <b>BLYTH</b> <i>Bed Rock</i>: The rock beneath alluvial deposits. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> <i>Bedrock or bottom</i>: Rock, sandstone, or clay on which the best gold deposits are found. See also <i>False Bottom</i>. <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,200</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> After digging to a depth of four to five feet through boulders and shingle, the `bed-rock' is struck. <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),97</i>)

### beech
 
 <i>n.</i> an important group of Southern Hemisphere timber trees forming extensive forests especially in southern and mountainous regions of New Zealand. Also called nothofagus and popularly but incorrectly, birch; often found with prefixed epithets denoting appearance of tree (black, silver), quality of timber (hard, red), habitat (mountain); the preferred name for spp. of <i>Nothofagus</i> (to 187?, <i>Fagus</i>), the southern beech, and for spp. of other genera resembling the southern beech (see false beech 2 below); contrast the popular name birch q.v. now obs., and for the NI esp., the Maori generic taw(h)ai; and (erroneously) kamahi in the SI. [See esp. the discussion by J.C. Andersen <i>Popular Names of New Zealand Plants</i> (TrNZI vol. 56, 57), and T. Kirk <i>On New Zealand Beeches</i> (TrNZI 17/298ff.).]

>  <b>KIRK</b> With the view of obviating the confusion caused by the misuse of the common names hitherto applied to the New Zealand beeches, I would suggest the adoption of new names based on the obvious characteristics of their foliage:- For <i>Fagus fusca</i>, tooth-leaved beech; for <i>Fagus solandri,</i> entire-leaved beech; and for <i>Fagus menziesii,</i> round-leaved beech. <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,17</i>)

>  <i>Beech</i> (b&emacr.ch), <i>n. [Add.] ... The ordinary</i> native beech <i>of commerce in New South Wales is Gmelina Leichhardtii.</i> The New Zealand beeches are <i>Fagus Solandri, F. Blairii, F. Menziesii, F. fusca, F. Cliffortioides, </i>and <i>F. apiculata.</i> The word <i>beech</i>, however, is rarely used in New Zealand, the trees being called <i>birch</i> by settlers and bushmen, and <i>tawai</i> by the Maoris. See under BIRCH,<i>n</i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)

>  <i>Birch</i>. Various species of Nothofagus. There are various kinds (white, red, black etc.) of trees in New Zealand called birches. They are all really beeches and the newspapers and forestry people are trying to get them so called, but up country they are still always called birches. Birch (creeks, hill, etc.) is one of the commonest components of local names in the South Island. ([1898 Morris Austral-English], and an unpublished letter to Hunter Brown from Dr Hodkinson, August 9, 1854.) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> English names of animals and plants are sometimes mistakenly applied. The native beech, <i>Nothofagus</i>, has been called <i>birch</i> since the early days and is still often so called by bushmen; ... <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Beech .. tawai, tawhai <i>Nothofagus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Five species of <i>Nothofagus</i> were formerly recognised in New Zealand, but more recently two of these have been combined as varieties of one species. <i>Nothofagus menziesii</i>: silver beech (the bark is white and silvery particularly in young trees). <i>Nothofagus fusca:</i> red beech (the timber is reddish in colour). <i>Nothofagus truncata:</i> hard beech (the wood has a high silica content making it hard). <i>Nothofagus solandri</i> var. <i>solandri</i>: black beech (the bark is very dark partly due to the growth of a sooty mould). <i>Nothofagus solandri </i>var. <i>cliffortioides</i>: mountain beech (grows mainly at higher altitudes than other beeches). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,121</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> A noble forest of beech trees (tawai). <i>(1845,Journal,3/166</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> High beech or Tawai trees. <i>(1845,Journal,I</i>) [NOTES: (Taupo)]

>  Fagus (Beech Trees) <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/47</i>)

>  <b>BAYLY</b> The next biggest [NZ tree] is a tree whose wood resembles beach tree in England. These bear a flower somewhat resembling the Horse Chestnut flower and bears a nut shaped like a fig but much smaller, with large long leaves. <i>(Apr 12, 1773,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1773 April 12 Bayly's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/206]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Near Hikurangi] we passed through a noble forest of beech trees (Tawai) some were 8 feet in diameter and of great height <i>(Nov 10, 1845,Journal,3/166</i>)

>  [?Mountain B], What gave still greater interest ... was the presence of large green alpine parrots (<i>Nestor notabilis</i>), the Kea of the Natives, which visited constantly the small groves of beech trees near our camp. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,215</i>) [NOTES: 1862 Von Haast in 1948 Von Haast Julius Von Haast 215]

>  In ascending Mount Alta ... the first 2000 feet ... is covered with Beech (<i>Fagus fusca</i>) <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/29</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  This morning there are signs of recent rain but the track itself is surprisingly dry, the South Island beech black with honeydew that will draw wasps like flies round a dung heap as summer ends. <i>(Mar 10, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C14</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (use by early amateur and professional botanists, and careful (? esp. Brit.) writers, and since WW2 by forestry and other `technical' writers, esp. for State forests: the timber and wood is now generally beech.).

>  <i>Beeches </i>... Red Birch of Otago ... , <i>Beech</i> etc, ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/32</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago),]

>  Beech, or Black Birch [Fagus fusca] <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/90</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk Botany Thames)]

### beech burr
 
 <i>n.</i> [(Griffiths Collection has following from 62 of H.J. Anderson) He went over to his hut and came [sic: something missing: check] burr somewhat larger than a soccer ball. A burr is a form of external growth found on beech trees. It is generally attached to the trunk of the tree by a very small connecting branch and can usually be removed from the fallen tree quite easily. In appearance it is something like a pumpkin, covered over with round knobs of bark about as big as marbles.]]

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> ... Nick Ward the head sawyer, called me over and said, `I've got a beech burr for you. It'll make a lovely clock for your bedroom.' <i>(1975,Milford Road,62 ed.</i>)

### beech bush
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bush](../B#bush))

### beech fever
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Beech fever</i>. An Otago term which refers to a sickness which affects some people in beech country. It may be the same as<i> birch itch</i>, suffered by people allergic to beech pollen <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,31</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

### beech forest
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Beech Forests. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/323</i>) [NOTES: (Travers)]

>  <b>HAAST</b> Five miles from the junction of the Havelock and the Clyde the beech forest (<i>Fagus Solandri</i>) which still ... grew upon the steep mountain sides, disappeared ... <i>Ibid.</i> 151 This newly-formed gorge of the Northern Poulter is very wild and romantic, fine beech forest growing in every suitable spot; ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,5</i>)

>  Fire eats further into [State] beech forest. <i>(Mar 9, 1953,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  But, higher than the beech forests, there is a Flora of great interest and beauty. <i>Ibid.</i> 14 [He has been speaking of Fagus, and of colonists' `Black-birch'] The city of Nelson is now almost entirely dependent for its supply of firewood upon the beech forests ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/10</i>) [NOTES: (Monro)]

>  Our first excursion was through ... beech forest in which we noted ... the gorgeous crimson mistletoe, and up ... to a height of 4,100 feet to the first of the Edelweiss. <i>(1869,NZ Journal of Science,1/202</i>)

### beech fungus
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [fungus](../F#fungus))

>  <b>DAWSON</b> ...silver beech is the only species attacked by a fungus, <i>Cytaria</i>, whose orangey, golf-ball-like fruiting bodies may be abundant on the branches of some trees; ... <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,123</i>)

### beech mistletoe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Trilepetala (Tripetala?)adamsii</i> - unseen since 1954 - is presumed extinct. The three beech mistletoes and <i>Tupeia antarctica</i> are all in gradual decline nationally. <i>(Jul 2005,Open Space,No 64</i>)

>  Beech mistletoes are our most showy and attractive mistletoe species, flowering between December and January, but they are no longer common in most parts of New Zealand. Their strongholds are near the Southern Alps. <i>(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 59/88</i>)

### beech potato
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The most counspicuous of these [pouch fungi which develop underground] is the violet-tinted BEECH POTATO (<i>Hysterangium scerodermum</i>), a tuberous-looking fungus resembling a small Derwent potato. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,37,1929 ed.</i>)

### beech strawberry
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [strawberry fungus](../S#strawberry-fungus))

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Finally, there are those curious, yellow, spherical `fruit' of the so-called beech strawberry (<i>Cyttaria gunnii</i>), a fungus which attacks the silver beech. These strawberry-like fruits form clusters on the twigs at the end of the branches, their honey-combed surface making recognition easy and certain. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,43,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> Fungus icicles - <i>Hericium coralloides</i> ... Beech strawberry -<i>Cyttaria gunnii</i>... Lawyer's wig ... Huperei - <i>Gastrodia cunninghamii</i> Hakeke - Jew's ear fungus - <i>Auriculuria judea</i> <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,85</i>)

### Beehive
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Such, in fact, is the devotion to duty of the nation's MPs that at least a quarter of them turn up for a wine tasting, hosted by Air New Zealand and the Wine Institute, in the Beehive banqueting hall on Wednesday evening. <i>(Jun 22, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  Mr Bolger has refused comment on very high (excessive?) Beehive staff salaries. <i>(Feb 26, 1995,Sunday Star Times,C4</i>)

### Beehive, the
 
 <i>n.</i> (in Paihia)

>  <b>Mrs Williams</b> Within an enclosure of paling stood our raupo hut, which had, except in shape, the appearance of a beehive. [15] On Sunday, Mr. Williams opened another raupo hut for a chapel. <i>(Sep 15, 1823,Journal</i>) [NOTES: in 1940 Tales of Pioneer Women (1988) 14, 15]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Beehive, the Williams Family's First House at Paihia From a sketch by Henry Williams soon after its completion in September 1823 <i>Ibid.</i> 90 My brother and myself engaged ... this day ... plastering and repairing some of the cells of the Beehive, <i>(1823,Early Journals 1816-1820, The,96</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  That figure is denied by a Beehive aide. But the Beehive source admits `there is a possibility it [the tax take] is down'. It is possible, he said, that companies have found unexpected loopholes in the tax code. <i>(Mar 28, 1986,National Business Review,10</i>)

>  The Beehive's well-oiled publicity machine appears to have run away with itself, if Prime Minister David Lange is to be believed. <i>(Jul 26, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),A9</i>)

>  But all the jawboning from the top floors of the Beehive and No 2 The Terrace will not convince business that the government is on the right track unless it leads from the from the front by taking and axe to its own budget. <i>(Nov 30, 2005,NZ Herald,C2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> The circular domed building housing Cabinet, some parliamentarians, and parliamentary refreshment and entertainment facilities; the government (informal) [1981 Feb Avondale College (Auckland) Slang Words in Use (Goldie Brown Collection), the Beehive: Parliament]

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> ..I had an hour with [the Prime Minister] in his office in the Beehive ... <i>(1981,Straight from the Shoulder,94</i>)

>  <b>HARRISON</b> ... some of them went for a ... walk along Willis Street and Lambton Quay and round the great circular Beehive Building of Parliament. <i>(1976,Broken October,44</i>)

>  beehive ... 2. (Capital, informal) ... the dome-roofed building which houses Parliament [See Parliamentary Offices] <i>(1979,Heinemann NZ Dictionary,90</i>)

>  beehive... 4. The Beehive. the dome-shaped building which houses Parliament in Wellington, <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,95</i>) [NOTES: nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 43]

>  <b>OVENDEN</b> In the middle of them all ... the Beehive Building, with its multitudinous combs, the centre of the hum of government activity, ... <i>(1984,Ratatui,13</i>)

>  The great Cambridge scholar, F.R. Leavis, wrote many years ago that the health of a culture can be judged by its sense of absolutes. Where does that leave us as a nation or as individuals (within the Beehive or outside it?). We need the symbol of the woman crushing the head of the serpent as a reminder that between good and evil there can be no compromise. <i>(Dec 1986,Marist Messenger,7</i>) [NOTES: Frank McKay]

>  `No Aunty,' said Tom. `I don't know Wellington. I thought you fellas would no all that!' `Aue!' ... `Hey, does anyone know how to get to Government House?' `You mean the Beehive, Aunty?' <i>(1988,Short Stories from NZ,133</i>)

>  <i>the Beehive:</i> Parliament <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>)

>  <i>(1982,NZ Almanac,54</i>)

>  Porirua's newest "beehive" may not be full of politicians or even bees, but it is attracting enough attention to rival that surrounding Wellington's famous parliamentary monument. <i>(Jun 1, 2000,Contact (Wgtn),1</i>)

>  Ever since ministers moved into the Beehive 16 years ago, they have complained about it. <i>(Feb 19, 1995,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  Legend has it the Beehive started life as a playful sketch on a Bellamy's napkin, as an eminent visitor sought to amuse then Prime Minister Sir Keith Holyoake. <i>(Feb 19, 1995,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  Cue gasps of horror and fainting spells in the Beehive. <i>(Aug 5, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

This species is the most serious insect pest in New Zealand. The larvae feed on roots of grasses. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/871</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

The player won who could flick a beetle up first. <i>(1961,History of Children's Play,128</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

### beg-pardon
 
 <i>n.</i> an apology usu. in collocation no beg-pardons without apology; with no quarter given.

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> There's no askin' pardons about the bush; it's just life and death. <i>(1905,Toll of the Bush,399</i>) [NOTES: [cf no asking pardons]]

>  There are no beg pardons about Susan, who gave a gasp of scornful incredulity when Marshall accused her of blanky. dirty, filthy and ragged. <i>(Aug 27, 1910,NZ Truth,270/6/1</i>)

>  As soon as the last load is brought in, without word or a beg-pardon to any one, she [a horse] trundles herself, her cart, and her daughter round to the stable. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,145</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Rivalry for top tallies [of truck-loads] was keen ..., but there were no `beg pardons' in the cut and thrust of tally building. <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,118,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> However, this time `no beg pardons'. An irate equestrienne took stock whip that fell across the quarters of one of the horses, and the shoulders of one bailiff. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,11</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Tony, after a maternally-directed beg pardon ... <i>(1965,Memoirs of a Peon,187</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Use yourselves. No beg pardons. Start playing like a First Fifteen for a change. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,209</i>)

>  She could take you in slices like bacon/ and no beg pardons. <i>(1972,Islands,1/17</i>) [NOTES: 1972 O'Sullivan `Butcher's Dozen' in Islands 1/17]

>  <b>HOWITT</b> We used to hit each other really hard. There were no beg pardons. <i>(1975,NZ Rugby Greats,61</i>)

### beggar's ticks
 
 <i>n.</i> a weed resembling a dahlia, whose seeds stick to wool, also called cobbler's pegs.

>  <i>beggars' ticks</i> [s] <i>Bidens frondosa</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,4</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Annual with general likeness to the garden dahlia ... introduced from Europe ... a weed whose seeds fasten so readily to wool and clothing. ... <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,15</i>)

### beggars on coals
 
 <i>phr.</i> damper (See [cf. buggers afloat](../B#buggers-afloat))

>  <b>MONEY3</b> These and other employments, such as baking my dampers, or "beggars on coals," exploring the creeks that came tumbling down the wooded precipices on every side, snaring the wood-hens, mending and washing my clothes, kept my time fully occupied. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,31</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Beggar on the coals</i>. A small damper: New Zealand and Australian: ca. 1850-1900. B., 1941. Also <i>bugger</i>... Morris <i>Devil on the coals</i> `quick-baked small damper' Aust rural coll 1860: SE 1900 <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,987</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> And tell you another thing we made, dumpling things. Doughboys, that's what they're called, but we called them buggers afloat. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,89</i>)

### begonia
 
 <i>n.</i> [Extended use of begonia.] Elatostema rugosum; parataniwha

>  <i>[Elatostema rugosum] </i> <i>(1917,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>) [NOTES: (And.)]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Another herb with attractive foliage is the PARATANIWHA or so-called NATIVE BEGONIA <i>(Elastostema rugosum),</i> though it is in no way related to the begonia, ... . <i>(1961,Flora NZ,246,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>dun</b> <i>Elatostema rugosum </i> <i>(1925,Duncan & Davies' NZ Native Trees</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

### begonia fern
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>ELATOSTEMA RUGOSUM, parataniwha.</i> The botanically improbable name `begonia fern' is heard in the Far North. Like a begonia, the plant has thick rhizomes and juicy stems; ... Like a fern, it grows in great drooping cascades on damp banks ... <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,82</i>)

### behind
 
 <i>adv.</i> further away from settlement or the coast than the point of reference.

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> [We] learnt that the party were pushing back to occupy a piece of new country behind us, and proposed to proceed on the following morning. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,145</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adv.</i>

>  <i>get in behind</i>: behave <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>adv.</i>

>  Certain parts of the building have become 'out of bounds', and any attempt to contravene this will surely prompt a sharp 'geddin behind' and see a small terrier race out to restore order. <i>(Feb 27, 1995,Salient,8</i>)

### behind the bike sheds
 
 <i>phr.</i> used of something a bit forbidden, e.g. smoking or sex

>  Prime Minister Helen Clark said she believed National Party bosses had "got him behind the bike sheds and roughed him up" last night after his reported comments that he might leave Parliament early. <i>(May 1, 2002,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  If they do [read the business sections] it's only to sneak a look at the value of the stocks and shares they've acquired, like naughty children doing something behind the bike sheds. <i>(May 4, 2002,Dominion,14</i>)

>  <b>39</b> Cuban sexuality has an openness and decency not found "behind the bike sheds" or in every second advertisement in Western culture. <i>(Jan 5, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  They might be fine kids in their home environment, but send them off to school where there are drugs behind the bikesheds and it's out of your control. <i>(Jan 25, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)

### behind the sheep
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> [shepherding or boundary keeping] being close to sheep in order to protect them against wild dog, pig or a sudden storm.

>  <b>NELSON</b> behind the sheep: keeping close to sheep to protect them from likely hazards. <i>(1999,Eugene Nelson Glossary,1</i>)

### Beige Brigade
 
 <i>n.</i> a band of New Zealand cricket team supporters, similar to the British 'Barmy Army, distinguished by their outdated beige cricket uniforms

>  At the end of each series Richardson, 33 the slowest runner in the New Zealand team, races a player nominated by the opposition... The Beige Brigade (touring New Zealand team supporters) fixed me up with a beige lycra sprinter's bodysuit... Now the Beige Brigade are going to get a suit embroidered with race results. Losses will be listed as unofficial dead heats." <i>(Jul 31, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  The Beige Brigade, which calls itself a group dedicated to keeping cricket fun, says it will run a "Pash for Cash" competition during upcoming West Indies tour. <i>(Jan 12, 2006,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

### Belgian biscuit
 
 <i>n.</i> white icing; Belgian biscuits =German biscuits [Jim writes : DNZE gives use as "during and after World War I", with no citation after 1926.It remains current <i>sub..nom. belgian cremes</i>, an everyday item on supermarket shelves, often (?usually) called Belgian biscuits. I also recall it as an alternative to <i>spice biscuits</i> in cakeshops.]

>  <b>FUTTER</b> <i>BELGIAN BISCUITS </i>... Flour ... Brown Sugar ... Butter ... Treacle ... Egg ... Baking Powder ... Put together with jam while hot. Ice ... <i>(1926,Home Cookery for NZ,209</i>)

### Belgian cake
 
 <i>n.</i> (recipe)

>  <b>BASHAM</b> <i>BELGIAN CAKE </i>... Beat butter and sugar, add eggs ... flour ... baking powder. To a small half of the mixture, add ... cornflour. Spread in tin, then ... jam, then remainder of mixture ... Bake ... <i>(1939,Aunt Daisy's Cookery Book,143</i>)

### Belgian roll
 
 <i>n.</i> Belgian, Belgium sausage; formerly German sausage.

>  <b>GRUNDY</b> A set of scales appeared from somewhere and a brisk trade was started in sausages, saveloys, belgian roll, ham and chicken, and black pudding. <i>(1968,Who'd Marry a Doctor?,138</i>)

### Belgian sausage
 
 <i>n.</i> [See also "Belgium"]

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> On other days of the week, she did her best with curried sausages, Belgian sausage, tinned fish, mince. <i>(1995,Mum,103</i>)

>  1914 Sep 17 Hawke's Bay Tribune in 1984 Boyd City of the Plains 210 <i>(Sep 17, 1914,Hawke's Bay Herald Tribune</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> And in all the butcher-shops, pink-rinded German sausage, with the delicious little triangles of bacon fat, had become `Belgian sausage'. Belgium is brave little Belgium, one of our [WW1] Allies. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,65,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> In those days most of the bulls ended their days as pig food, whereas nowadays a large number of them become involved in the luscious Belgian sausage. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,142,1954 ed.</i>)

>  There was first the Belgian sausage sandwich on my plate. <i>(1984,Some Other Country,237</i>) [NOTES: 1982 Marshall in 1984 Some Other Country 237]

>  My mother regarded belgian sausage as a last resort, but I loved it because it was soft and moist. <i>(Nov 30, 1999,Evening Post,23</i>) [NOTES: Check whether "Belgian" or "belgian"]

>  We've got a packet of Ryvita between us, and a scrap of some South Island thing called Belgian sausage. <i>(Jul 1998,NZ Geographic,No. 39/114</i>)

>  Half a pound of Belgian sausage for a pale little office girl who "rooms" by herself. <i>(Apr 28, 1928,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,39</i>)

>  [Advt] Whether it's pork sausages, Belgians, saveloys.. or bacon, and you mention "Morepork", you've said it. <i>(Aug 12, 1916,Hawke's Bay Herald,4</i>)

### Belgium
 
 <i>n.</i>

### Belgium sausage
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of Belgian sausage (See [also German sausage](../A#also-german-sausage),[luncheon sausage](../L#luncheon-sausage))

>  <b>CHRISTIE</b> Vera also cooked the meals, which consisted monotonously of porridge for breakfast, Belgium (luncheon) sausage with rice and curry sauce ... <i>(1987,Candles and Canvas,93</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  Large quantities of Belgium slag.. are on order. <i>(Mar 23, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### bell sheep
 
 <i>n.</i>

### Bell's brick
 
 <i>n.</i> a brick-shaped loaf [ca1940 R. Mason Bell's Bricks: Canterbury (and visiting) trampers (Bell's Bakery in Christchurch baked brick-shaped wholemeal loaves favoured by trampers because they kept better than ordinary loaves)] (See [also brickie](../A#also-brickie))

>  A loaf of Bell's Brick sliding into Bill's back ... <i>(1948,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,4/18/290</i>)

>  <i>Bell's bricks,</i> the brick-shaped, wholemeal loaves of bread produced by Bell's bakery in Christchurch, were not unknown further afield, but no doubt are being forgotten since the firm closed down a few years ago. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,26</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a station cook.

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> ?or Wahlenbergia [Campanula usu. = bluebell] ?here a Calystegia

>  <b>POLACK</b> The clematis, campanula trachelium, or bell flower, whose hues, white, red, and yellow, with the convolvulus, or bind-weed, and immumerable liands, [106] hung around in pleasing disorder. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/134 The Campanula or bell flower of various hues, pink, yellow, and white was observable, climbing around the stalk of every shrub of sufficient strength to bear its light weight. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/105</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>
