### bell-wether
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a tame wether that leads others to their doom at freezing works or a leader of a mob of sheep, furnished with a collar and bell that sheep will follow. (See [Judas sheep](../J#judas-sheep))

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> Even in New Zealand's early farming days the flock followed the shepherd who led a bell-wether. This was usually a tame sheep wearing a collar with a bell .. In later years the bell-wether was the fore-runner of the Judas sheep in the freezing works. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,2</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Bell-wether. To this day tame wethers are used to lead others to their doom in the freezing works. They carry no bell but still retain a broad leather collar. This will protect them, and would effectively blunt the knife of a sticker who might absent mindedly seek to slit the wrong throat. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,9</i>)

>  <b>MCLAUCHLAN</b> In pioneering days, dogs were used more to contain sheep in the absence of fences than to drove them. The shepherd often led his flock with a bell-wether. <i>(1981,Farming of NZ,155</i>)

### bellahonie
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> In a few tagging games the players joined hands with the He when they were caught and helped him catch the other players. These games were known as <i>The Bellahonie </i>(E-21) (Wellington, 1910), Kick Post One (Hampden, Otago, 1880), and Ballyhooley (Wellington, 1920). More popular were those games in which the players helped one another to fight the He, and of these the most widespread was the game known as <i>Kick the Tin</i> (e-3), Kick the Boot, Kick the Block, or Homiacky. In this game the He was put on guard of a jam tin placed inside a small circle marked on the ground. [Other players hid: those He could see he named and tapped the tin three times and `captured': other hiders waited till they had chance to kick tin out of circle: then all were free again.] <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,80</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> There are games recorded in this period for which there is no record before 1900 though they may well have existed under other names. <i>West Coast</i>: Dunk and Davey, Spang Weazling; <i>Wellington</i>: Who Goes Round My Stone Wall, Beg o' My Neighbor, Bellahonie, Run a Mile, On and Off the Bridge, Here Come Two Nuns, Colours, Isaac and Rebecca. Scrag, Diabolo, Bumble Puppy, Homai, Fives; <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,231</i>)

### bellamy
 
 <i>n.</i> a cake

>  Eve's Pantry has just rolled out a new $300,000 retail store in the Auckland suburb of Henderson, where old-fashioned goodies such as nougat rolls, lamingtons and bellamys are in popular demand. <i>(May 6, 2004,Sunday Times (NZ),D4</i>)

### Bellamy's
 
 <i>n.</i> the catering establishment within Parliament; the term, taken over from the British House of Commons, for the dining and drinking establishment of the NZ House of Representatives.

>  <b>HOGG</b> ... those who talk to Dillon Bell,/ Or do not talk,but only vote,/ Or act in Bellamy's the sot. <i>(1875,Lays and Rhymes,228</i>) [NOTES: (The Moon)]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> ... there is a trap-door in the window at the side of my office, where liquor can be got from `Bellamy's,' the Parliamentary refreshment rooms. We can get a pint of Burton ale for sixpence and other liquor of the very best quality equally cheap. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,74,1935 ed.</i>)

>  Wellington. Interviewed the premier Sir John Hall ... Dined at Bellamys ... <i>(Jul 10, 1880,Chudleigh Diary,289,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BLAIR</b> A senator waxes eloquent over the benefits ... of native industries ...; but when he retires to Bellamy's, to refresh the inner man ..., he will not touch what he calls the `beastly colonial'. <i>(1884,Industries of NZ,59</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> There too is Bellamy's, which was forbidden territory to every petticoat whatsoever ... <i>(1934,Journalese,30</i>)

>  The dead who drink in Bellamy's/ Are glad when school kids clap/ ... <i>(1979,Collected Poems,266</i>) [NOTES: 1963 Baxter in 1979 Collected Poems 266 (An Ode to the Reigning Monarch)]

>  <b>GUSTAFSON</b> Although friendly with many journalists, with whom he would often have `a great `schooner' of beer' in the press bar in Bellamy's, Savage disliked many of the papers for which they worked. <i>(1986,Cradle to Grave,230</i>)

>  For many years, Bellamy's (named after its 230-year-old counterpart in the British House of Commons) provided the only premises in Wellington licensed to serve liquor late into the night, adding to what many perceived as a private-club atmosphere. <i>(May 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 63/64</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [c1876, and other Provincial chambers check Trollope)]

>  <b>TROLLOPE</b> ... the Provincial Assembly will be a real Parliament with a `Bellamy' - as the parliament refreshment rooms are called, in remembrance of the old days in the House of Commons at Home. <i>(1874,New Zealand (various authors/titles),59</i>)

>  I succeeded, also, in having the fine room above Bellamy's - the so-called coffee room, afterwards the Superintendent's Office - set apart for Museum purposes. <i>(1882,TrNZI,14/509</i>) [NOTES: (Haast),]

### bellbird
 
 <i>n.</i> a bird commonly found in the New Zealand bush, noted for its beautiful song; various NZ birds from their bell-like notes, also called korimako, makomako.



 
 <i>n.</i> an indigenous honey-eating songbird, Anthornis melanura , also known as korimako, mockymock and mockie. The bellbird is not, however, a mockingbird like the tui. [aka bell-bird, bell bird; mako, makomako, korimako, mock-a-mock, mocker, mockie, korimoko, =mokomoko]

>  Tuis work their way round the suburbs drawing nectar from seasonal flowers... Bellbirds have similar habits but are extinct in northern New Zealand. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,26</i>)

>  Anthornis melanura. Bell-bird. Korimoko. Abundant. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  (Black Stewart Island) The following is a list of the birds shot ... <i>Anthornis melanura</i>, bell bird or mocker; <i>Petroica albifrons</i>, wood robin; <i>Petroica macrocephala,</i> yellow-breasted tit; <i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>, pied fantail; <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>)

>  <i>Bell&#769;.bird&#769;</i>. (b&ebreve.l&b&etilde.rd&#769;.), <i>n. (Zoo.l) [Add.] (c)</i> The <i>Oreoi.ca glutturalis</i>of Western Australia. <i>(d)</i> The<i> Anthornis melanura</i> of New Zealand; - called also <i>korimako</i> and <i>makomako. </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)

>  This morn [at Queen Charlotte Sound] I was awakd [sic] by the singing of the birds ashore ... [456] their voices were certainly the most melodious wild musick I have ever heard, almost imitating small bells but with most tuneable silver sound imaginable to which maybe the distance was no small addition.] <i>(Jan 17, 1770,Banks Journal,1/455,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The melodious chimes of the bell-bird were especially distinct. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,23</i>)

>  <b>GODLEY</b> Across the path ... comes the bush, full of birds singing all day, tuis and bell-birds and New Zealand robins ... <i>(Sep 8, 1851,Letters from Canterbury, NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Sep 8 C. Godley ?Letters from Canterbury in 1947 Reed Story of Canterbury 100]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> As the dawn first peeped forth I was deafened by the sound of bell-birds. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of Otago,122</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Every now and then we stood ... to listen to the Bell-bird, a dingy little fellow nearly as large as a thrush with the plumage of a chaffinch, but with such a note! <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,93</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 13. ANTHORNIS MELANURA. Sparm. Bell-bird. Mocker. Korimoko. Moko-moko. <i>A. ruliceps.</i> PELZ. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,6</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ANTHORNIS MELANURA. (BELL-BIRD.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Mako, Makomako, Komako, Kokomako, Korimako, Kohimako, Kokorimako, Titimako, and Kopara. Of the above names, Korimako is most generally used by northern and Makomako by southern tribes. The Ngatiawas call this bird Rearea; and the natives of the Bay of Plenty distinguish the male and female as Kokorohimako and Titapu. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,91,[1888 2nd edn I/85] ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Every morning ... we were awakened by the songs of numerous birds ... amongst which the New Zealand Thrush (<i>Keropia crassirostris</i>), the Bell Bird (<i>Anthornis melanura</i>) ... could well be distinguished. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,38</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> And <i>honey</i>-bird and <i>mocking</i>-bird/ And he of clearest melody,/ The blossom-loving <i>bell</i>-bird <i>Ibid.</i> 2/338 ... the Bell-bird or Korimako ..... Anthornis Melanura. This most interesting song-bird seems in danger of extinction, owing, according to Dr. Buller, to the ravages of the Norway rat. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/96</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Bell-bird </i>... name given to several birds, from their note, like the tinkling of a bell. In Australia, a Honey-eater, <i>Myzantha melanophrys</i>, Gould (`Birds of Australia,' vol. iv. pl. 80), the `Australian Bell-Bird' (the same bird as <i>Myszantha flavirostris,</i> V. and H.), chiefly found in New South Wales; also <i>Oreoica gutturalis</i>, Gould (Vol. ii. pl. 81), the `Bell-bird' of Western Australia; and <i>Oreoica cristata</i>, Lewin. In New Zealand, <i>Anthornis melanura,</i> Sparrm., chief Maori names, <i>Korimako</i> in North, and<i> Makomako</i> in South. Buller gives ten Maori names. The settlers call it <i>moko</i> (q.v.). There is also a Bell-Bird in Brazil. <i>(1898,Austral English,26</i>)

>  12. <i>Anthornis melanura</i>, Sparrm. Bell bird, Korimako, Makomako, which last name settlers often render into mockymock. <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,277</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts),]

>  <b>BULLER</b> <i>Anthornis melanura</i> ... the Bell-bird - so called from the fanciful resemblance of one of its notes to the distant tolling of a bell. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/85</i>)

>  <b>PARKER</b> Bell-bird, Korimako, or Mako-mako, is still common in many parts of the South Island - e.g. in the neighbourhood of Dunedin ; but has disappeared from the North Island. <i>(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,119</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Korimako, ... bell-bird. <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,72</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Korimako in North ; Mako-mako in South. ... The settlers call it Moko. <i>(1898,Austral English</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... the sweet little golden tinkle, and chime of the korimako, the bell-bird, came across the creek by our camp-side. <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,17</i>)

>  [Yellow-breasted tits] are now only rare visitants in the winter, when the bell-bird (<i>Anthornis melanura</i>), or mokomoko, as it was called by the Otago Maoris (not makomako, as in the North), also visits us. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/277</i>) [NOTES: (Bathgate, Otago)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> The <i>tui</i> and the bell-bird make music here ...; and here too are to be seen wild ducks and that rare and beautiful bird the crested grebe. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 126</i>)

>  <i>Bellbird.</i> Mako-mako, or Moki-mok. (Anthornis melanura). The name is still sometimes used; but the bird is now generally called a `Mokky'. [1951 Glossary merely has after `melanura': The bird is sometimes still called a `mockie'.] ([1874 Lady Barker Station Life],p.93; [1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Sep 9, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>HOWARD</b> [Stewart Island birds include] the fern-bird (<i>Bowdleria fulva</i>), the bell-bird (<i>Anthornis melanura</i>), South Island Robin (<i>Miro Albifrons</i>)... <i>(1940,Rakiura,323</i>)

>  <i>bell bird</i>: singing forest bird <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  ~ A name for some Australian birds and one New Zealand bird from the bell-like note; in New Zealand also called mako-mako or `mocky-mock'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),37</i>)

>  <i>bellbird n</i> [so called fr. its bell-like note] : any of several birds whose notes are likened to the sound of a bell: as <i>b</i> : a honey eater (<i>Manorina melanophrys</i>) of Australia or a honey eater (<i>Anthornis melanura</i>) of New Zealand <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,200</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> bell-bird: a bird frequently the subject of verse by magazine poetesses in the early decades of the century. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,297</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BELLBIRD <i>Anthornis melanura Other names</i>: Korimako, Makomako, Mockie. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,218</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> Back come the multiple voices of the river, of night-owls and of bell-birds at dawn ... <i>(1966,Black Beech & Honeydew,287</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Bellbird .. korimako, makomako, etc. <i>Anthornis melanura </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i> ~ n</i>. name given to several birds from their note, like the tinking of a bell. The Maori name is Moko. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),611</i>)

>  <i>bell-bird ... n.</i> small, grey-green bird with bell-like note; Maori names are <i>Mako, makomako</i> and <i>koromako. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <i>265.b. Anthornis melanura melanura</i> (Sparrman, 1786) <i>Bellbird (Korimako, Makomako) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,69,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>bell-bird ... 2.</i> ... a New Zealand honeyeater. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,97</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 63; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 44],]

>  <i>Bellbird Anthornis melanura</i> (SPARRMAN, 1786) ...<i> Three Kings Bellbird A.m. obscura</i> FALLA, 1948. ... <i>Chatham Island Bellbird A.m. melanocephala</i> GRAY, 1843. ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Korimako, makomako, mockie. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,290</i>)

>  Bellbirds have been seen in the Porirua Scenic Reserve for the first time in many years. Wellington ornithologist Ian Armitage said he recently heard several bellbirds (korimako) in the reserve, the only time since he started tramping there 15 years ago. <i>(Oct 29, 2003,Northern Courier,2</i>)

>  A bid to bring back the bellbird's melodic song to gardens in Auckland and Hamilton starts on Sunday with the release of 200 sanctuary-bred birds at four sites. <i>(May 7, 2010,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

>  [Kookaakoo, macrons] is the bird to which the echoic name bell-bird was first applied. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55-144</i>) [NOTES: Anderson Maori Words]

>  The birds are not remarkably musical - the linnets are rather so; The Bell-Bird, is of a purple colour, with a white spot on the breast, shaped like a bell. The Saddle back, is a brown bird with a bright red spot on its back like a saddle, these two last species, are about the size of a thrush. There are green parroquets, large hawks, and several kinds of wild ducks, besides Emus, greenbirds and woodhens, which are birds of excellent flavour. [ed. fn: The linnet, brown creeper (Finschia novaeseelandiae>), was occasionally called brown linnet in the earlier days; ... `parroquets', probably the red-crowned parrakeet ...] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,104,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: also in 1828 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 222]

>  Now the sanctuary is doing the same with another songbird: the bellbird. The sanctuary is about to relocate a third lot of bellbird into the sanctuary following one unsuccessful and one successful release. <i>(Jun 5, 2003,Contact (Wgtn),7</i>)

>  Bird enthusiasts are in for a treat with a good chance of seeing .. tui, bellbirds and kaka in the regenerating forests ... <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 7</i>)

>  And developments at the Otari Native Botanic Garden in Wellington.. means [sic] that bellbirds, last seen in 1902, are again calling in the forest. <i>(Jun 24, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Anthornis melanura obscura. </i> [analogous use for native crow, and Three Kings bellbird - total 2 cits]

>  <i>265.a. Anthornis melanura obscura</i> Falla, 1948 <i>Three Kings Bellbird </i>Three Kings Islands. <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,69,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Bellbird Anthornis melanura</i> (SPARRMAN, 1786) ...<i> Three Kings Bellbird A.m. obscura</i> FALLA, 1948. ... <i>Chatham Island Bellbird A.m. melanocephala</i> GRAY, 1843. ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Korimako, makomako, mockie. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,290</i>)

>  Native crow was called by early pioneers `bell-bird' because it had a beautiful bell-like note. <i>(Oct 17, 1951,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 Oct 17 2YA Wellington Nature Session]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

### bellied
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of sheep) shorn of belly wool before the main shearing

>  (Wallace Shearing Glossary) <i>bellied sheep</i>: A sheep that has had the belly wool shorn off it prior to the main shearing. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/21</i>)

### bellies
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [belly](../B#belly))

### bellman
 
 <i>n.</i> an auctioneer's bellman

>  <b>BARLOW</b> People were now flocking in pretty fast, and the bellman showed symptoms of fatigue ... <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,61</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also bellringer](../A#also-bellringer))

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Amongst its [a capsized ferry's] passengers was one of the bellmen who plied their trade, and often made a hideous noise in the city streets. This man had been engaged to announce the different events on the day's programme [at Vauxhall Gardens, Dunedin, c1860s], and as he landed dripping on the Vauxhall jetty he exclaimed: `Oh! What'll I do? I've lost my bell.' `Wring your clothes,' said a bystander. <i>(1925,Random Recollections,16</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> To attract attention to the programme ..., it was customary to send a bellman round [Hokitika] with the familiar goldfields invitation to `Roll up; roll up.' <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,22</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> But New Zealand ... is ... a land of which some future bellman may some day sing - [etc]. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/401</i>)

>  Queen's birthday up here, so says the bellman [of the Mataura diggings]. <i>(May 24, 1863,Diary of an Unsuccessful Digger</i>) [NOTES: 1863 May 24 Diary of an Unsuccessful Digger in 1931 Aug 27 Star (Christchurch) in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 95]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> Bell-men pervade the thoroughfares and announce various entertainments, ... <i>(Mar 12, 1868,Town by Night,30,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> The services were published by the bellman [in Hokitika, 1865]. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,121</i>)

>  About this time [c1880s] the town's bellman was Jimmy Reid, who ... was in great demand by the auctioneering firms on sale days. ... As soon as Bob [a competitor] had finished ringing his bell, and started to make his announcements, Jimmy with a louder bell would drown his opponent's words ... <i>(1914,History of North Otago,99,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> Theatrical performances in Kumara were still in my time advertised by the bell-man. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,40</i>) [NOTES: c1880s in 1968 Seddon The Seddons 40]

### bellowsfish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Centriscops Humerosus </i>... Bellows Fish. <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/169</i>) [NOTES: Waite]

>  <i>Centriscops humerosus</i> Richardson. The bellows-fish was taken off Taiaroa Head. <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/230</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> Snipe fishes (or bellows fish), Pipe-fishes and sea horses, which have the front of the head produced into a snout with small jaws at the tip, are a group regarded as an offshoot from the Garfish. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,198</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 198]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Centriscops humerosus</i> (Richardson). BELLOWS-FISH. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,19,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Centriscops humerosus</i> (Richardson). Bellows-fish. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/403</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Bellows Fish <i>Centriscops humerosus</i> (Richardson) (Syn. <i>C. h. obliquus </i>Waite) ... Bellows Fish <i>Centriscops sinuosus</i> Regan <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,402</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 402]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> BELLOWS-FISH <i>Centriscops humerosus</i> (Richardson) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,140</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> . . .the bellows fish ... has scales too small to see clearly, though their roughness can be felt, and also has rows of large plate-like discs/ along its variously-coloured sides ... <i>Centriscops obliquus</i> ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,211</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BELLOWFISH [chk spg] Deep-bodied fish of the family <i>Macrorhamphosidae</i> which resemble related pipefish and seahorses, with their mouths at the end of a tube-like snout. ... Whiteley suggests that they get their name from the closeness with which they resemble the old-fashioned bellows for blowing fires. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,25</i>)

>  [species name] bellowsfish .. [scientific name] <i>Centriscops</i> spp. .. [other name(s)] Unspecified - see also BANDED BELLOWSFISH and CRESTED BELLOWSFISH <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293], bellowsfish 1104-17 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [?merely syn. <i>Centriscops</i>].

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Bellows Fish <i>Notopogon xenosoma</i> Regan ... Bellows Fish <i>Notopogon lilliei</i> Regan (Syn. <i>Centriscops cristatus </i>McCulloch) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,402</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 402]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Centriscops obliquus.</i>

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293], bellowsfish, banded 1107 ... banded bellowsfish 1107 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BANDED BELLOWSFISH Centriscops obliquus </i>... The banded bellowsfish is a deep-bodied species that attains a maximum size of about 25cm. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,192,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BANDED BELLOWFISH <i>Centriscops humerosus</i> : A deep-water inhabitant tasken in depths up to 450 fathoms ...Sometimes mis-named Spinefish (to which it is no relative)and Snipefish (English). <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,25</i>)

>  [species name] banded bellowsfish & <i>Centriscops obliquus</i> .. [other name(s)] -- <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Banded bellowsfish Centriscops obliquus</i> Distributed around the Southern Hemisphere ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,69</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  [species name] centriscops humerosus .. [scientific name] <i>Centriscops humerosus</i> .. [other name(s)] blue bellowsfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  [species name] crested bellowsfish .. [scientific name] <i>Notopogon lilliei</i> .. [other name(s)] elevated bellowsfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293], bellowsfish, striped 1107 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>CRESTED BELLOWSFISH Notopogon lilliei </i>... They are found south of Cook Strait in water between 100 and 600m deep ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,193,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] crested bellowsfish .. [scientific name] <i>Notopogon lilliei</i> .. [other name(s)] elevated bellowsfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Crested bellowsfish Notopogon lillei</i> South-eastern Australia as well as New Zealand, where it is mainly found in deep waters south of Cook Strait. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,69</i>)

### bellringer
 
 <i>n.</i> bellman

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> One of the last of the bellringers to parade the streets of Maoriland could often be heard [at Reefton c1900], ringing out his sad or glad, or indifferent tidings, .. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,45</i>)

### bells
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Next in order of importance [to Egg Cap] in the pitching games was <i>Bells </i>(K-23). This was based on the game of Pitch and Toss ..., but had a more innocuous character. The coins for this game, that is the `bells', were the round, flat tops of the cylindrical Bell [Company] Wax Vesta matchboxes. The head was the blue end with the red flowers in the middle. The `sugaries' at the other end of the box (the spot on which the match was struck) were not often used, although there is one report in which they were said to represent half the value of the bell. ... <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,170</i>)

### belltopper
 
 <i>n.</i> an old word for a black top hat, a (black, silk) top hat. [1904 McMurran, see graft n]

>  The life-nominees shall wear long-faced bell-toppers,/ <i>(1866,Canterbury Rhymes</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Sep in 1866 Canterbury Rhymes 30]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> His volunteer cap's on whenever I meet him,/ He wears it to save a belltopper no doubt;/ <i>(1862,Auckland Vocalist,5</i>)

>  <b>SKEY</b> Now this your stiff belltoppers won't do,/ One kick and their day is done;/ So the billycock soft my looks shall renew,/ And let off my innocent fun. <i>(1889,Pirate Chief and the Mummy's Complaint..., The,48</i>) [NOTES: (`On the Choice of a Hat'),]

>  <i>Bell&#769;.top&#769;.per</i> (b&ebreve.l&#769;.t&obreve.p&#769;.-p&etilde.r), <i>n.</i> A tall silk hat. <i>[Colloq.] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Bell-topper</i>. The ordinary Australian name for the tall silk-hat. <i>(1898,Austral English,28</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> <i>(1874,25 Years of Emigrant Life,79</i>)

>  <b>KELLY</b> <i>(1860,Life in Victoria,268 fn</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> And the firm of Mills Brothers/ Was there in a belltopper;/ <i>(1864,Invercargill Minstrel,6</i>) [NOTES: (The Caledonian Gathering),]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> One new chum had got an umbrella,/ And in a beltopper [sic] was rigged;/ <i>(1864,Songs of the War,6</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> `A very fine man in appearance, stature and spirit' he [Francis Hicks] disembarked wearing a bell-topper and swallow-tails in 1859. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,23</i>)

>  ... he was observed walking briskly to his 8am appointment with his work, dressed in the invariable belltopper frock coat, check trousers ... <i>(May 12, 1979,Evening Post,12/3</i>)

>  <i>bell-topper ... n</i>. Australian name for tall silk hat. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <i> ~ n. </i>Australian name for the tall silk hat. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),611</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> When on his rounds he rode an old horse and always wore a bell-topper hat. It was not unusual to come onto the doctor's horse and bell-topper on the side of the road or track, and the doctor lying down a band or somewhere nearby fast asleep. [pre-1910] <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,148</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bell-topper.</i> Current in New Zealand as early as 1853 (B., 1941). <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,987</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Father had brought out a belltopper with him [in 1840] ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,11</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> No sooner had he left the store than the diggers caught sight of his bell-topper and began [31] greeting him with cries of `Joe, Joe.' The township was soon ringing with the cry. The minister was puzzled to understand why he was being barracked so freely and asked Thomas the reason. <i>(1937,Thomas,30</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> Did you go to school in a belltopper hat? Mike says the boys do at Eton. He says it would be a bosker game kicking them round the yard. <i>(1934,Winds of Heaven,143</i>)

>  <i>bell n</i>.- bell-apple n. a large soft-skinned passion fruit.- bell-topper n. a silk hat. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,1</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> He had a good voice; he liked to sing in public, he liked to dance and attend pic-nics - to put on his `bell topper' and walk out of Church if he disapproved of anything said in the sermon - and he had a passion for inventing highly unpracticable things, like collapsible umbrellas or folding lamps. He had one saying with which he met all difficulties. `Depend upon it, it will all come right after the Maori war.' <i>(1916,Aloe, The,71,(1982 O'Sullivan) ed.</i>)

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> And when his `bell topper' he puts on, his looks there's no withstanding. <i>(1905,Poems & Sketches,149</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> BELL-TOPPER: High hard hat with wide flat crown. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,185</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 185]

>  Auckland swell. `I wonder you new chums wear those disgusting bell-toppers'. <i>(1868,Punch or the Auckland Charivari,4</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> ... no tall black hats (`bell-toppers,' a [a-acute] la coloniale) ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,57</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> An unfortunate publican was once weak enough to return from a commercial visit to Dunedin with a regular black hat - popularly known as a `bell-topper' - ... and his friends kindly kicked the offending hat into the river. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/ix/100,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  ... a bell-topper ... <i>(1877,Colonial Experiences,8</i>)

>  <i>Larrikinism</i> [title] ... I was coming up Rattray St. today, wearing a bell-topper, and found about thirty boys preparing to pelt all comers. ... Nobody supposes that the masters are unaware of this larrikinism; ... <i>(1878,Evening Star</i>) [NOTES: 1878 Evening Star (Dunedin) quoted in 1974 Wood Victorian New Zealanders 31]

>  <b>GREY</b> In the fall, my bell-topper and its wearer parted company. <i>(1879,His Island Home,23</i>)

>  <b>INNES</b> An individual was seen swimming [to] the ship, with his `belltopper' fixed firmly on his head. <i>(1879,Canterbury Sketches,19</i>) [NOTES: [reference 1851]]

>  [Of the mistaken idea new emigrant has of Colonial costume] The outlandish gear [sc wide-awake hat] is put aside, a `bell-topper' is bought, gloves are resumed, and a first illusion over. <i>(Aug 30, 1884,Taranaki Daily News</i>) [NOTES: 1884 Aug 30 `Daily News' in 1886 Clayden Handbook NZ 209]

>  <b>BARLOW</b> In the meantime the only `bell-topper' to be [209] found among the head-gear ... had been called into requisition ... <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,208</i>)

### belly
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the thick portion of a stockwhip, located on the middle of the lash. (See [belly-wool](../B#belly-wool))

>  Belly... The thick part of the lash of a stock whip ... <i>(Sep 9, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> .. The belly, or thickest part of the thong, was filled out with pieces of leather laid lengthwise ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,150</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> belly: (i)(n) thick part of a stockwhip, about the middle of the lash. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,6</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> It [the whip] has a taper and belly to make it lash easily ... <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,80</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [vehicles] to become stuck on rocks or high ground in a paddock or in a river crossing.

>  Watch out for big boulders 'cause even with 4 wheel drive you can get bellied. <i>(May 22, 1969,NZ Farmer,24</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> He told them how he'd bellied on a hummock within five minutes of starting and then realised he'd neglected to bring a wire rope and shovel. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,56</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to remove belly wool of sheep before shearing or before the winter.

>  He and his married man crutched and bellied the hoggets themselves in July ... <i>(Sep 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,08</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> When we had cut out the ewes he looked forward to crutching the smaller and lighter hoggets. They should have been much faster as we would not have to belly them. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,37</i>)

>  Then the way is clear for the elite mob to come in; when they are bellied and crutched, bladeshorn and put back on the hill. <i>(Dec 7, 2000,NZ Farmer,21</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also stockwhip](../A#also-stockwhip))

>  <i>Belly</i>. (1) The thick part of the lash of a stock whip, toward the middle. (C.) <i>(Sep 9, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
 <i>n.</i> the wool on or from a sheep's belly; short form of belly wool, shorn belly-wool.

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> The big stations may make classing a very simple business, ... and may even divide up the skirtings or pieces from the fleece into different classes, besides keeping the bellies, locks, and stained wool separate. <i>(1920,Australian & NZ Sheep Farming,144</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The skirtings of the neck and shoulder can be called first pieces, and the britch ends made into second pieces. The `bellies' can be placed in a pack hung on the shearing-board, and when two of these are filled they can be pressed. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,121</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> There are also baskets for the bellies and locks ... The `picker up' is the name given to the young man or boy whose duty it is to [75] carry the wool from the shearer to the skirting table ... <i>Ibid.</i> 88 The bellies that have been collected by the pickers-up are sorted into two classes: FIRST BELLIES ... BELLIES ... <i>(1927,Sheep & Wool Industry,74</i>)

>  Regarding the respective values of the clips, it is hardly necessary to state that fleeces from March-shorn sheep were not able to be rolled. From them only stained wool and bellies were sorted out ... A star bale sold at a crutching sale made 101/4d., while a fadge of the same wool pooled later made 121/4 pence ... <i>(1929,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,10/326</i>)

>  <i>Belly.</i> (1) The thick part of the lash of a stock whip, toward the middle. (C.) (2) the wool off a sheep's belly, pressed and sold as `bellies'.(C.) <i>(Sep 9, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  `Bellies' form the bulk of the skirtings and are seldom classed except for type or condition. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,710</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> I take the next [bale of wool] and weigh it. `Bellies and pieces,' they say. <i>(1940,All Part of the Game,2</i>) [NOTES: 1940 Gaskell `You Can't Go Three Days' in 1978 All Part of the Game 2]

>  <b>MARSH</b> It's not bellies in that pack, either. Bellies smell a bit but nothing to touch this. <i>(1945,Died in the Wool,16</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> I take the next [bale] and they weigh it. `Bellies and pieces,' they say. <i>(1947,Big Game,18</i>)

>  Frames hinged to a wall can be very useful to support a wool pack for bellies, necks, etc. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,311</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Bellies often receive less attention than they warrant, ... There is, however, a big difference in ewe and wether bellies, and they should not be mixed, ... <i>(1955,Wool Away!,94</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> They also swept up the bellies and pieces, and so kept the shearing board clean and tidy. A young Maori helped in the sheep-yards drafting out the strangers, as it is taboo to shear sheep belonging to another station. <i>(1964,Doctor in the Mountains,32,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> bellies: see <i>picking up fleeces. </i> <i>(1964,Collected Stories,297</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> `What's 2nd Pcs H?' `Grade 2, pieces - that's skirtings - hogget wool.' `And Bls E?' `That's belly wool. E grade bellies.' `Lox?' `Oh, that's floor sweepings.' `What about Lbs Wigs?' `Lamb top-knots - off the head.' <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,166</i>)

>  <b>BREMNER</b> <i>Bellies</i> see skirtings. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>)

>  <i>belly ... 7. Austral. & N.Z. a</i>. the wool from a sheep's belly. <i>b. (as modifier): belly wool. </i> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,97</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 63; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 44],]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Fleece</i>. The main part of the sheep's wool when shorn. It is picked up in one piece and carried to the wool table, where it is skirted and classed. The small bits and pieces that are cut off separately by the shearer are the locks, bellies and pieces. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)

>  An "improved Romney ram" was credited with 31 lb of wool, without the "belly". <i>(Jan 2, 1919,NZ Herald,4</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) belly wool removed from the underside of sheep. (See [belly-wool](../B#belly-wool))

>  .. last year my clip, including pieces, bellies and locks, averaged gross in Timaru ... <i>(1906,Farmers' Union Advocate,9</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Put up two temporary wool bins in the woolshed for the bellies and pieces, then started to plane boards to go round the bottom of the wooltable. <i>(Oct 14, 1914,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  belly - the wool off a sheep's belly, pressed and sold as 'bellies.' <i>(Sep 9, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> A bale of bulk ewe bellies can be depreciated a grade because of the inclusion of a few wethers' bellies from which urine stains have not been removed. <i>(1956,Wool Away!,94</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> Bellies are stored in two fadges. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,356</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> belly: (ii) (n) belly wool. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,6</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Pizzle stain: Unscourable urine staining in wether and ram bellies, and ewe crutchings. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,57</i>)

>  Bulky skirtings, second shear bellies and pieces and first crutching were unchanged on Nov 8. <i>(Nov 24, 2001,Manawatu Evening Standard,9</i>)

### belly board
 
 <i>n.</i> a small surfboard (See [flutterboard](../F#flutterboard))

>  Summer days were spent on flutterboards and hastily constructed wooden "belly-boards". <i>(Jun 17, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

### belly crutch
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a removal of ewe belly wool prior to lambing.

>  In the South Island when shearing full-wool ewes with winter combs plus lifters, a belly crutch is often done six to eight weeks beforehand. <i>(Jun 2002,In the Paddock,2</i>)

### belly crutching
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the removing of ewe belly wool prior to lambing.

>  It [shearing twice a year] also does away with the ewe belly crutching. <i>(Jul 2004,Country-Wide,37</i>)

### belly fadge
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a woolshed bag or bale containing belly wool.

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> As each shearer shore off the belly a fleeco would pick it up and put it in the belly fadge. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,25</i>)

### belly shear
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to crutch the belly wool from sheep.

>  <b>PEAT</b> We crutch and belly shear in late April or May ... <i>(1992,Falcon and the Lark, The,49</i>)

### belly wool
 
 <i>n.</i> wool from (or on) a sheep's belly; cf. bare-belly, belly [ca1951 still used as a technical word, though (? often, ? mainly) replaced idiomatically by shortened from `BELLY' n (pl)]

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> The belly wool and the pieces or locks are kept separate from the fleece when rolling it, ... <i>(1920,Australian & NZ Sheep Farming,148</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> ... the shearers wandered over to the yards and caught a few sheep and felt their belly wool. <i>(1959,Stone, The,42</i>) [NOTES: (The Stone)]

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> `What's 2nd Pcs H?' `Grade 2, pieces - that's skirtings - hogget wool.' `And Bls E?' `That's belly wool. E grade bellies.' `Lox?' `Oh, that's floor sweepings.' `What about Lbs Wigs?' `Lamb top-knots - off the head.' <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,166</i>)

>  Belly wool: Wool from the underside of a sheep. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,52</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> ... I was allowed to miss school to help in the shed by picking up the bellywool and removing the pizzle stains from the wether bellywool. I was also `tar-boy', which meant putting dabs of Stockholm tar on any cuts inflicted by the shearers. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,55</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Belly wool.</i> The wool shorn off the sheep's belly. It is often twiggy and stained, and is pressed separately and sold as belly wool, a lower grade of wool. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)

>  <i>belly, belly wool</i>: The wool shorn form the sheep's belly. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/21</i>)

>  <b>WELD</b> ... their mothers do not lose the belly wool, as they would do by lambing in the spring, when their wool is long, and the heat of the body renders it liable to peel off. <i>(1851,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,10</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) wool from a sheep's underside, often soiled.

>  <b>WELD</b> Their mothers do not lose the belly wool, as they would do by lambing in the spring ... <i>(1851,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,10</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> The belly wool and the pieces or locks are kept separate from the fleece when rolling it. <i>(1920,Australian & NZ Sheep Farming,148</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> I was allowed to miss school to help in the shed by picking up the bellywool. [bellywool; belly-wool] <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,55</i>)

### belly-buster
 
 <i>n.</i> a belly-flop(per). [1953 14 M A4 Thames DHS 16 BELLY BUSTER [have a fall] [M2]]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Juvenile delinquency. What about us? ... [10] ... Climbing over the tin fence into the town baths and heaving the stand planks into the water when we tired of bombs from the side and bellyfloppers from the board. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,9</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>belly-buster</i>: is not an excess of candy and ice cream It is the inexperienced swimmer's least favourite dive - a BELLY FLOP. (see <i>honey pot/bomb, duck dive</i>) <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,13</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>belly-buster</i> disastrous dive where stomach hits water; aka `bellyflop'. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,14</i>)

### belly-crutch
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to remove the long, soiled wool from the belly of a sheep.

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> John Thompson and Tony Prestage belly-crutching ewes. <i>(1993,Station Country I,94</i>)

### belly-flop(per)
 
 <i>n.</i> an awkward dive in which one lands on one's stomach on the water; in NZ as elsewhere mainly children's for (a) dive into water landing flat on the stomach. [recorded earliest in NZ]

>  <b>HYDE</b> `It hurt,' she added ... `So I didn't do any more worshipful belly-flops.' <i>(1937,Wednesday's Children,49</i>)

>  <b>HARVEY</b> He trotted down into the surf and did a beaut belly flop into the water that sent a big wave going the other way. What a lousy dive for a man going to the rescue, ... <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,67</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> How white his father looked ... poised for a bellyflopper. But he swam with powerful strokes ... <i>(1965,Power of Joy,63</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The rope lashed to the willow above Larsen's Hole that swung him in a glorious sprawling bellyflopper into the river. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,51</i>)

### belly-piece
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... the latter is always skirted and classed, whereas the black fleece was rolled up just as taken from the sheep's back, the belly-pieces being also left in. ... For example, white wool divides, when skirted by the roller at the wool-table, thus: Belly pieces, say, 6d. per pound; first pieces, 8d. per pound; ... fragments or locks, 4d. per pound. <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/196</i>)

>  ... the latter is always skirted and classed, whereas the black fleece was rolled up just as taken from the sheep's back, the belly-pieces being also left in. ... For example, white wool divides, when skirted by the roller at the wool-table, thus: Belly pieces, say, 6d. per pound; first pieces, 8d. per pound; ... fragments or locks, 4d. per pound. <i>(1900,TrNZI,33/196</i>)

### belly-rope
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The animal was at length brought with its head close up to the corner of the post [of the stockyard], the end of a small rope fastened to one of the rails ... was now passed rapidly round its body, and handed to the men outside to put round another rail and hold ... Almost at the same time that this `<i>belly-rope</i>', as it was called, was being fastened to keep the body of the animal upright, Harold would be putting a rope round one of the hind legs. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,190</i>)

### belly-scarf
 
 <i>v.</i> (See [contrast back-scarf](../C#contrast-back-scarf))

>  <b>KENWAY</b> He will `belly-scarf' and `back-scarf' the lot, that is to say, he will cut about a third through on both the lower and higher sides. <i>(1928,Pioneering in Poverty Bay,38</i>)

>  <b>KENWAY</b> selecting a certain group of trees he will `belly-scarf' and `back-scarf' the lot. <i>(1947,Quondam Quaker,53</i>)

### below in the gully
 
 <i>exclam.</i> a warning cry [(kauri bushmen's)]

>  The stereotyped cry `Below in the gully!' is sent forth, echoing from tree to tree. This is a timely warning for those working below to betake themselves a spot of safety from flying limbs .... <i>(1914,Auckland Weekly News</i>) [NOTES: [1914 Auckland Weekly News] quoted in 1953 Reed Story of Kauri 160]

### belt
 
 <i>n.</i> a strip of trees or bush (poss. elliptical for shelter-belt) [0 / 0 / 1950 Source Farm & Station Verse Author WOODHOUSE Vol / Page 47 Edition Quote You rattle thro' the belt and scrub and up the little hill. Notes 1899 `The Lash' [pseud.] in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 47]

>  <b>Swainson</b> These belts and clumps consist of a rich variety of wood; the graceful tree-fern, and the deep-green, glittering-leaved karaka, clustering, in unusual profusion, around the tall stems of the statelier forest trees. <i>(1853,Auckland,38</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> town belt, an urban reserve kept mainly in parkland.

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> It is to be hoped, that as they [the wooded Tinakore heights] are included in the Belt of land reserved all round the Town for public purposes, strict precautions may be taken to preserve the timber from depredation. <i>Ibid.</i> 175 ... necessary reserves for public purposes, including a broad Belt of land round the Town for the recreation of the inhabitants; ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,96</i>) [NOTES: [=Town Belt (he uses Town-belt 97)]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a prize (in phrases give one the belt, hold the belt) [Transf. use of OED2 sb1 1 b. (1872 boxing, hold the belt)]

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To name the biggest liar, or, the most constant talker. .in 4;`Old Dexter's talked us all down tonight. He hasn't stopped to draw breath. We unanimously agreed to give him the belt.' <i>Ibid.</i> 407 The ensign of a champion bestowed in name only. .in 4;`Harry can talk longer, and on more different topics than any of us, and after he had gone on non-stop most of the evening,we told him we'd give the belt for the greatest talker known among us all.' <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],54</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a public reserve of bush, plantation, or parkland in a city or town. a. ?Original boulevards planted on Christchurch town reserves (cf A.H. Wylie).

>  <b>HAAST</b> If the citizen [of Christchurch c1866] went out to the Belts, the boulevards of the City, he found ... the trees that had been planted in the East [Belt] had been neglected, ... the iron enclosures were rusted, the plantations infested with weeds, ... the footpaths covered with grass. ... The North Belt towards the East was a narrow foot-track ... <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,462</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> seems to be confined to cities: reserve seems used in smaller boroughs or country places.

>  <i>(P. heterophylla)</i> was more abundant on the Belt than it is now. <i>(1890,TrNZI,22/486</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso) [dateline lost; try this],]

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Their way lay along `the Belt' that broad band of Native bush which encircles Dunedin. <i>(1904,Tussock Land,61</i>)

>  The wax-eye (<i>Zosterops lateralis</i>), too, used to build in the bush on the Belt, and even in suburban gardens, and was decidedly more abundant than is the case now. ... <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/278</i>) [NOTES: (Bathgate, Otago)]

>  On the suburban side of the Belt all to the eye was uniform and conforming, the latest tar-seal serving the affluent middle-class and their housing : new cars breaking the speed limit ... <i>(1974,Islands,7/92</i>) [NOTES: 1974 Sargeson (Brixton)]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> On the suburban side of the Belt all to the eye was uniform and conforming, the latest tar-seal serving the prosperous middle-class and their housing; ... <i>(1976,Sunset Village,31</i>)

>  <b>BRASCH</b> We shot with bows and arrows; bows of cherry, arrows of bamboo or of hine hine (we pronounced it [in Dunedin] henny-henny), the whitey wood that grew in the Belt just outside the back gate ... <i>(1980,Indirections,26</i>) [NOTES: [?ca1918]]

>  <i>belt ... Town Belt</i> green area. <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,44</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD],]

>  [HOLD THE BELT, win the prize; (transf) take the cake], It is reported from across the Tasman that when a sheep was being shorn at a station near Penhurst, a starling's nest with one egg in it was found in the wool. To hold the belt, Zealandia will require to produce a duck that has hatched a sitting of eggs in a collection plate. <i>(Dec 24, 1910,NZ Truth,287/1/3</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the prize in a sharpshooting contest

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Mrs. Roland Reed was nearly as good with the rifle as was her husband. On two occasions she won the belt at the Pongaroa match for scoring the possible. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,149</i>)

### belt-up
 
 <i>n.</i> a fight, rough stuff

>  <b>SLATTER</b> A belt-up in the ruck, a bunch of five, fair enough - A paucity of good players today - Yeah, they're very pauce - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,162</i>)

### belt-vanner
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McARA</b> <i>Belt-vanners</i>: Machines consisting of a short but wide, inclined, moving, rubber belt on which the pulverised ore was washed by a counter-flow of water to separate the concentrates, washing away the lighter material and leaving the heavy sulphides to be discharged at the top end. ... <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),312</i>)

### beltman
 
 <i>n.</i> a member of a lifesaving team

>  But the cotton line became waterlogged, and the beltman could make no progress against the surf. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/38</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> He won the `double' - the surf-race championship and the individual beltman's title - ... in 1939 and 1940. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/329</i>)

### bench
 
 <i>v.</i> (usually as (well-)benched adjective) (of a hillside track or road) to cut out or form as a ledge. [bench n. also as benched out] (See [bench n](../B#bench-n))

>  <b>PASCOE</b> The treacherous Hokitika River is bridged by a wire and cage, and where the boulder beaches merge into gorges well-benched foot-tracks make delightful walking. <i>(1960,Over the Whitcombe Pass,14</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> benched out [of a track on the side of a hill] <i>(1961,Good Keen Man, A,21</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> Beyond Top Hut the track is benched for maybe 20 minutes before large poles take over to mark the easy, well-worn path across the tops. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,65</i>)

>  One of my correspondents, tramping with her husband, referred to the `benched-out' track they were following up the hillside, to be met by an uncomprehending stare.... She, on the other hand, knew of no other name for the kind of farm track that at one time was dug and is now more commonly bulldozed along and up a hillside. `Benched-out' roads, she assures me, climbed all over Banks Peninsula where she was brought up and it had not occurred to her that the phrase was in any way unusual. <i>(Apr 28, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>) [NOTES: [I.A. Gordon],]

>  At Smith Creek Shelter a well-benched track ascends gently to the head of Smith Creek... <i>(2008,NZ Geographic,94/30</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the buyers at a stock sale.

>  Competition was good from a relatively small bench which included some of the North Island's biggest finishers of cattle. <i>(Sep 18, 1997,NZ Farmer,46</i>)

>  Just over 700, in 13 lines were presented to an animated bench which wasted no time in bidding. <i>(Nov 18, 1999,NZ Farmer,25</i>)

>  A capacity yarding of 550 cattle sold to a good bench of buyers at Awakino on Tuesday. <i>(May 3, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,22</i>)

>  The sale attracted a top bench of buyers and many of the sheep went to Wairarapa. <i>(Dec 12, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,20</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a ledge, usually a sheep-track, along a hillside; chiefly used as an adjective, benched, benchy.

>  On inland northerly and westerly situations ... these breaks develop successively in definite steps or benches. ... This problem is very real in holding-paddocks and on hillsides that are frequently used as access routes. ... Near the crests of hills sheep aggravate the position by camping on these terracettes ... <i>(1946,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,27/159</i>)

>  <i>bench ... 6. N.Z.</i> a hollow formed by sheep on a hillside. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,98</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 45],]

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> In the early evening, White Patch happened to find himself on a wide grassy bench. <i>(1980,Stag,18</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> He thumbed back with faint double-click the hammer of his rifle, before warily continuing along the ever-narrowing bench. Soon it was no more than ten paces across. <i>(1984,Razorback,14</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> saw-bench

>  <b>BAINES</b> At the near side of this saw we had a movable bench or table of wood twenty inches wide by thirty feet long. The saw being in motion, we had certain gear by which this carriage could be `gigged' backwards or forwards or fed forwards. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,180</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] a terraced river flat, high country terrace or hillside sheep-track.

>  <b>BURDON</b> .. perched up on a tussock bench above the Godley. <i>(1993,Of Mountains, Men and Deer,42</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The most physically testing country for sheep and dog is a reasonably steep hill striped with benches running across it, all the way up. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,156</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Some of his 1,000-strong flock of Romney sheep foraged on a grassy bench. <i>(2001,Riding Country,15</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a kitchen counter or workbench

### bench loader
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> When the flax arrives at the mill it is weighed and credited to the cutter. The `bench loader' then takes two or three bundles and puts them in a barrel (heavy end down). He then takes the longest leaves and places them on a bench convenient to the `feeder'. ...[44] The `feeder' takes each blade separately and places the butt end into the `stripper' which takes the green outer covering off. ... The fibre as it leaves the stripper is caught by the `fly boy', who collects enough to make into a hank, which he places convenient for the `shaker'. The latter, with a motion like cracking a whip, gets rid of as much rubbish as possible, after which he places the hank handy for the `washer'. This man places the fibre in a vat, through which ... clean water is running. <i>(1913,Country Work,43</i>)

### bench rider
 
 <i>n.</i> a substitute in rugby (on the bench)

>  When he returned to the All Blacks again in 2002 it was as the bench rider behind Greg Somerville at tighthead and Dave Hewett on the loosehead side. <i>(Jun 18, 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,7</i>)

### benchie
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a rugby reserve (on the bench)

>  ...the likes of Maling or Paul Tito could also be used as benchies who can slot in at blindside or lock. <i>(May 19, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

### benching
 
 <i>n.</i> in sawmilling, the occupation of benchman.

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> ... he would go among the men from the mills ... asking if there were any jobs going. `Benching? - any benching? How about breaking-down?' <i>(1965,Power of Joy,45</i>)

### benchman
 
 <i>n.</i> person on the sawbench who feeds the log or flitch into the saw; occas. one who works on or operates a sawbench, cf tailer-out

>  <b>rust</b> Timber was being got ... along its immense stretch of foreshore, hundreds of bushmen, loggers, jackers, bullock-drivers, cross-cutters and rafters were employed besides stackers, saw doctors, benchmen and mill-hands in the different sawmills [at Maungakaramea]. <i>(1936,Early Whangarei,RUST,163 ed.</i>)

>  In Southland, at this date (May, 1894) benchmen are paid at the rate of 9s. per day ... <i>(1894,NZ Official Handbook,334</i>) [NOTES: -->1894 NZ Official Year-book 334 (T. Kirk),]

>  BENCHMAN Head-sawyer of a breast-bench. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <b>GIBBONS</b> He was one of the best benchmen on native timber. <i>(1988,Recollections (various authors/titles),39</i>)

### benchy
 
 <i>adj.</i> [?dateline missing in BL333a also: is this Crump GKM 21 or McLeod, My `yahoos' failed to get an answer and on some benchy outcrops of rock half way up the hill I could see a whole lot of sheep. [Canterbury station]]

>  Upon the benchy hillside/ Where hoggets love to lie,/ <i>(1944,NZ New Writing,3/56</i>) [NOTES: (McDonald)]

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> It was a slow process, getting those sheep down. The hillside was benchy and rough. But once they were started on the downward journey, the danger of a breakaway was past, and all that remained was hard punching to keep them together. <i>(1970,Tat,99</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land or hillsides] terraced with river terraces or sheep benches.

>  <b>MCNAUGHTON</b> The hillside was benchy and rough. <i>(1970,Tat,99</i>)

>  <b>JANES & ROWE</b> We set off in heavy fog to stalk the benchy country across the Trent towards Harper Pass. <i>(1994,Great New Zealand Hunting Stories,31</i>)

### bend
 
 <i>n.</i> the land semi-enclosed by a bend or curve of a river.

>  Should they prove auriferous, these flats, or bends, will afford room for thousands [of diggers check]. <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and ReSources,21</i>)

### bend over
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to shear.

>  <b>KERR</b> The account of the following conversation comes from Eric Boyle, who was bending over a ram on the board at Otamatapaio at the time. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,109</i>)

### bender
 
 <i>n.</i> A Catholic

>  <b>HILL;IARD</b> `... if they found out I'd joined the Catholic Church ...' Maisie Hobson must have heard. She called out ..., `How are your religious observances going, Sharon?' ... `Are you still with the benders? I bet the knees of your stockings don't last long with that crowd.' <i>(1974,Maori Woman,91</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a sixpence

>  I reckon I am earning my eight and a bender <i>(1846,NZ Journal,1/18</i>)

>  <b>P S ARDERN</b> 7 and a bender - not heard for many years <i>(Jun 1951,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> Sixpence [:] tanner, sprat, tizzy, bender, snapper <i>(1972,From Beads to Banknotes,151</i>)

>  For the shilling <i>bob</i> was the most common nickname in use, but also recorded is <i>deener</i>. This latter name was used more widely in New Zealand and Australia than in Britain. The sixpence attracted many slang terms -<i> tizzy, tanner, sprat, bender</i>, and a<i> kick. </i> <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a circus performer

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> He was a bender, and used to do a double trapeze with me. [fn] [bender] The india-rubber man. <i>(1879,Spangles and Sawdust,8</i>)

### benders
 
 <i>n.</i> corporal punishment [1953 14-17 M A10 Thames DHS 23 BENDERS [M16]]

### beneficial island
 
 <i>n.</i> any of various statutorily named islands in the south of NZ, the breeding places of the <i>muttonbird</i>, to which/certain Maori people (<i>beneficial owners</i>) have an inherited sole right to a take of muttonbirds.

>  <b>WILSON</b> There is a schedule to the agreement which sets out the areas reserved and ninth in the schedule is a list of the Titi Islands which have become known as the Beneficial Islands. <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,21</i>)

>  `Beneficial island' means any one of the following Titi Islands - ... <i>(1949,Land Act Regulations</i>) [NOTES: 1949 Land Act Regulations in 1979 Wilson Titi Heritage 174]

### beneficial owner
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Beneficial owner' means a Rakiura Maori who holds a succession order from the Maori Land Court entitling him to any beneficial interest in any beneficial island: <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,174</i>)

### beneficial right
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> Beneficial rights to each island were very jealously guarded, and any natives landing on an island to which they had no hereditary right, and taking birds, were very severely punished. <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,27</i>)

### beneficiary
 
 <i>n.</i> [A spec. use of <i>beneficiary</i> OED2 n. B. 3.] Welfare beneficiary etc. check.

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> James and Viv could both be defined as beneficiaries of the welfare state, you know... Without the welfare state they couldn't have stayed at high school and wouldn't have had a chance to find careers in the public service. <i>(1995,Mum,224</i>)

>  <i>beneficiary ... 4. N.Z.</i> a person who receives government assistance: <i>social security beneficiary. </i> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,99</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 64; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 45],]

>  Beneficiary admits breaking windows [heading] ... David John Shore, 38, invalid beneficiary, amitted intentionally damaging six windows at the property. <i>(Nov 25, 1988,Dominion,10</i>)

>  Social Welfare minister Michael Cullen spoke out yesterday against those who criticised beneficiaries as `undeserving' or `idle bludgers'. <i>(Nov 11, 1988,Dominion,2</i>)

>  The defendants are ...Cave, 23, writer, ...Kere, 31, scallop opener, ... Nathan, 47, welfare beneficiary. <i>(Apr 22, 1989,Dominion,6</i>)

### beneficiary island
 
 <i>n.</i> = beneficial island

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Herekopare and Woman's Island are the only two beneficiary muttonbird islands. All the rest are Crown lands. ... I know ... that the fierce nettle also grows on Herekopare. Other lovely ... plants are there too - the Stewart Island yellow senecio, teteaweka of course, ... Cook's health-giving scurvy grass, soft native tatahi grass and punui, the giant-leafed umbrella-like araliad. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,69</i>)

### benefit
 
 <i>n.</i> unemployment benefit, dole; any payment or assistance given by an institution, government, etc.: `unemployment benefits'.

>  There is a large population of Maori people on the benefit. <i>(Apr 10, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

### Benghazi burner
 
 <i>n.</i> a thermette; a portable makeshift device for heating containers of water within an open-ended jacket fired usu. by petrol (HNZD). [See also thermette]

>  He should have completed courses in the care and maintenance of stoves, oil-burning, wickless, Burners Benghasi, and heaters, Forli improved pattern. <i>(Jan 22, 1945,NZEF Times,IV/187 : 4</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... forward into battle our laden vehicle goes: ... A Benghazi burner - fireplace kerosene tin. Bully and biscuits<i> ad nauseam </i> <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,17</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Then driver and clerks set to and stowed boxes, maps, personal gear, packs, jerrycans, billies, benghazi boilers, stoves and all the motley that accumulates about a truck. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,306</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `Time to brew up', Alec said. He set a light to the Benghazi boiler he had made out of odds and ends of tin. <i>(1951,Sullen Bell,114</i>)

>  <b>ELLIOT& ADSHEAD</b> ... the Kiwis had to have their boilup. The Benghazi burner would quickly oblige with a brew. Its principle was simple in the extreme: ... one 4-gallon tin with holes pierced in the top. First sand was dropped in, next a little petrol, and finally a lighted match. <i>(1967,Cowshed to Dog Collar,90</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> <i>Benghazi burner</i>: A little known weapon of war. Left burning by the roadside it would either explode in the face of German troops or poison them with a rust coloured liquid. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,182</i>)

>  I could hear [my batman-runner] pottering about as he got the benghazi boiler going for my shaving-water. <i>(Jun 1973,Meanjin Quarterly,32(2)/133</i>) [NOTES: [D. Davin]]

>  <b>BURTON</b> An ingenious contraption was developed for brewing water for tea. Known as the Benghazi burner, it consisted of an upright cylindrical sleeve inside which a diesel or petrol fire was made. The jacket was filled with water which boiled with the rising heat. ... An adapted version of these burners is still popular on camping trips and picnics in New Zealand. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,32</i>)

>  <b>UREN</b> <i>(1945,Diamond Trails of Italy,90 fn</i>)

>  Stevens agreed with Orsman in most respects, except that he said Hart got his first patent for the Thermette on October 29. 1930, and the later one was an extension... Both Sources agreed that Kiwi soldiers nicknamed it the Benghazi boiler (after a coastal town in Libya), and that every desert unit had one as standard equipment <i>(Sep 18, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  <b>THE SARGE</b> From his bundle of gear, Herbert produced a small Benghazi. <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!,28</i>)

>  Some old soldiers rang in. They had used the same device during the desert campaign in World War II. But it hadn't been called a Thermette. They knew it as a Benghazi Boiler or chip heater. <i>(Jun 10, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  Present at thousands of New Zealand picnics from the '30s through the early '60s, before the thermos displaced its function, was the Thermette. A picnic kettle that guaranteed boiling water in a matter of minutes, the Thermette was the invention of New Zealander John Hart. The patent was issued in 1930 ... <i>(1993,At The Beach,32</i>)

### bent leg
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) osteodystrophy in sheep and cattle grazed on native pastures, particularly in Marlborough and Canterbury back country. (See [bowie](../B#bowie))

 <i>n.</i> a kind of absorbent clay formed by breakdown of volcanic ash, used especially as a filler.

### benzine
 
 <i>n.</i> petrol

>  "They have some beautiful roads down there." There was benzine in the words. <i>Ibid.</i> 88 Cars needed petrol - known as "benzine" - and regularly broke down, so as stables and blacksmiths went out of business, garages proliferated. <i>(May 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 63/85</i>)

>  A rumour has been current for several days among Napier business people, that benzine is shortly going up in price. <i>(Aug 30, 1916,Hawke's Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> Then she collected ... empty petrol tins for our farm allowance of benzine, empty kerosene tins for our lighting spirit. <i>(1944,Life with Barbara,38</i>)

>  While chasing the cattle he had seen some benzine stacked in the scrub. <i>(Jun 15, 1917,Wairarapa Daily Times,5</i>)

>  <b>MUNRO, D.J. & P.J.</b> The fuel they used was called "benzine", not "Petrol", and was all imported in wooden boxes holding two tins, each about 4 gallons, the same size as kerosene tins, the latter being imported also and in wide use for kerosene lamps, which those days were the principal means of illumination. <i>(1984,History of Port Chalmers Fishermen's Co-operative,7</i>)

### benzine box
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GIBBONS</b> A benzine box was a box which held two four-gallon tins. <i>(1988,Recollections (various authors/titles),17</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

### benzoline
 
 <i>n.</i> petrol, benzine

>  The motive power is gas, generated from benzoline (purified petroleum) <i>(Mar 1898,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: 1898 Mar Evening Post in 1971 MacLean and Joyce Veteran Years of NZ Motoring 9 (Griffiths Collection),]

### berdan
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i> a device for separating gold dust from crushed rock

>  The blanket tailings or blanketings, as they are otherwise called, consist mostly of iron pyrites and other sulphides, cobines with quartz, and contain a fair proportion of gold ...that had escaped over the silver tables. These tailings are treated in berdans with extra quicksilver and ground up. The berdans now in use at the Thames ... are generally five feet in diameter ... At one time a couple of rotating balls were considered sufficient for the amount of crushing required; now, the general practice is to have a loose ball as well as a stationary one attached to a chain, and it is called a drag ball; this drag does more work than a loose ball ... for the drag grinds the bowl as much as the tailings. <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/179</i>) [NOTES: (Goodall Thames Gold-fields)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> Gold was crushed at first in a `berdan', until they were able to provide [153] an engine of many horse-power. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,152</i>)

>  There are but two or three small Berdan machines on the spot [for crushing stone]. <i>(Oct 27, 1867,Trip to the Thames Gold-fields,28,1978 ed.</i>)

>  Kurunui Battery ... of six stampers, and one stamper specimen battery with Berdans at the end of tables to grind the tailings. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,93</i>)

>  <b>No.4 (NS) 112</b> The mill on the Hauraki claim consists of a fifteen-stamper battery, Wilfley, vanner, berdans, and accessories. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin</i>) [NOTES: [Coromandel]]

>  The crushing machinery in all these batteries consisted of four to ten heads of light stamps, and the gold-saving plant of amalgamated copper plates, with or without the addition of a small berdan. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.6 (NS) 26</i>) [NOTES: [Mikonui]]

>  <b>No.42 (NS) 39</b> In 1880 the Welcome built a 10-head mill with berdans ... <i>(1948,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin</i>) [NOTES: [Reefton] [1880] 1948 NZ Geol SB]

>  <b>BALE</b> In front of him was a massive, rust-covered iron dish, with a big iron block inside it. ... `That's a berdan,' replied Mr Alexander. ... `You place crushed ore inside the revolving bowl. ... The iron block stays still, grinding the ore into a slime. Water flows into the dish and spills over the edge carrying the lighter gold-free particles and slurry with it. Then mercury is added to absorb or amalgamate the gold and silver. It's usually used for testing the ore. <i>(1971,Maratoto Gold,54</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> <i>Berdan pan</i>: An annular-shaped cast-iron bowl or basin about four feet in diameter containing heavy weights which were revolved slowly to grind the ore, particularly concentrates, finely. Sometimes mercury was added to the berdans to form an amalgam with the finely-ground gold. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),312</i>)

>  If all that is claimed for this invention is borne out by actual working, the old berdan will at length have more than a rival. <i>(Nov 20, 1890,Otago Witness,13</i>)

>  Almost weekly something turns up which fully tends to prove that the Provincial Government.. should take immediate steps to appoint someone to ascertain for whom the berdan proprietors crunch so many small parcels of quartz. <i>(Apr 19, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,4</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> The soldiers of this war call this unsavoury portion of Cairo [viz The Wasser] the `Berker'. <i>Ibid.</i> 97 Doubtless any old soldier who fought in Egypt in the last war will remember the `Wasser', with the streets the same, the houses ...; but the soldiers of this war call this unsavoury portion of Cairo the `Berker'. <i>(1943,Passage to Tobruk,77</i>)

>  <b>TUCKER</b> I may say that during my stay in Cairo I did all the things that young soldiers do, visiting such places at [sic : ?as] the Burka, which is the red-light district of Cairo ... <i>(1978,Thoroughbreds Are My Life,23</i>)

>  Over the next few decades, Prices produced hundreds of stamper batteries for crushing rock,...berdans (devices for separating gold dust from crushed rock), steam engines [79] and boilers for the goldfields at Thames and later those at Te Aroha and Waihi. <i>(Apr 1997,NZ Geographic,No. 34/78</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> And a man would get very meskeen too, losing his kale on two-up at the Naafi and bints in the Berka. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,147</i>)

### berko
 
 <i>adj.</i> berserk, wild; go berko also go berker(s) (See [also burko](../A#also-burko))

>  <b>CRUMP</b> He's okay when he's sober but when he's got a few in he goes berko. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,163</i>)

>  One celebrated afternoon question time, she literally wagged her finger at National's shadow minister Wyatt Creech and went berko. <i>(Feb 26, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  Sales affect me in an odd way. I seem to go a trifle berko and rush round buying tomes on pot plants. <i>(Jun 18, 1963,Salient,2</i>) [NOTES: [El Crud, student commentator, writes]]

>  A 23-year-old housewife, ... who said she was under emotional strain because of family problems, was convicted of breaking property valued at [dollars]120, assaulting a police constable and using obscene language. ... The cause of her behaviour was probably a combination of the alcohol and the drugs, she said. `You went what is commonly called berko,' Mr Ross said when remanding the defendant for sentence ... <i>(Apr 4, 1972,Otago Daily Times</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>berko</i>, or <i>to go berko:</i> GO BERSERK, committing violence upon persons and/or property. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,14</i>)

>  <b>KATH COLERIDGE</b> Used by a washing machine serviceman about machines going berko (wrong or out of control) <i>(Nov 29, 1986,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>berko</i> berserk, though not really; to <i>go berko</i> is to be temporarily angry and out of control. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,14</i>)

>  Grey Panthers go berko in this very odd geriatric vigilante movie. <i>(Mar 25, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  Soome go very realistically berko (maybe it's the sight of the hair transplant). <i>(May 14, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

### best cow
 
 <i>phr.</i> the water tap

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a tap outside the cowshed used by unscrupulous dairy farmers to ''water down'' the milk.

>  He knew what the 'best cow' was. It was the tap outside the milking shed which the unscrupulous dairyman looked on as the cheapest milk. He has the story of a dairy farmer's wife, who during the visit of an inspector to the farm, in some distress advised her husband that the 'best cow' was choking. The farmer rushed outside to remove a potato which the children had stuffed up the tap outside the back door. <i>(Feb 11, 1977,Press, The,19</i>)

### bet
 
 <i>v.</i> past tense of beat [Gerard Reid (12 Maranui Ave, Pt Chevalier, Auckland, Ph. (09) 846 4376 Fx (09) 815 1636, gerard@eganreid.co.nz) heard this on a newsbroadcast on National Radio by Catherine Harris, 4/11/1999. Previously only heard informally; this is the first time he has noticed it being used formally.]

### bet half a dollar to a gooseberry
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  One sporting sub wanted to bet half a dollar to a gooseberry that the troop train would be snow-bound. <i>(May 2, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,II/17 : 110</i>)

### beta lamb
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an export grade of lamb.

>  Beta lambs are a case in point. At least 30,000 will be wanted next year for the Italian market. <i>(Jul 14, 1983,NZ Farmer,82</i>)

>  ... he points out the project got under way because they had been messed around so much with their beta lambs. <i>(Oct 15, 1998,NZ Farmer,6</i>)

### beta-beta
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [biddy-bid (piripiri)](../B#biddy-bid-(piripiri)))

### better an empty house
 
 <i>phr.</i> better an empty house than a bad tenant, or similar, said when farting in public. [ca1930s used by Jane Morison, Havelock.]

>  <b>BEATON</b> LOU farts. ... MA: Better an empty house than a bad tenant. <i>(1984,Outside In,27</i>)

### Better Britonism
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  So why has this book made such a meal of recolonisation and its alter ego, Better Britonism, returning to them again and again? <i>(Oct 28, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C5</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> better than something worse [(no cit. and not in Orsman) cf. SOED sharp stick, Wilkes burnt stick]

### better-conditioned
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sale stock] capable of being further finished.

>  Better-conditioned store lambs $35-$40. <i>(Aug 1, 1996,NZ Farmer,42</i>)

### better-wintered
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of farm stock] with more condition than expected following a winter season.

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> Sound teeth will mean a better-wintered sheep and more profit to the owner <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,13</i>)

### between the fences
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [mustering and droving] [of stock] on a road or lane.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They were now 'between the fences' and would travel by back roads where the feed would be good. <i>(1949,High Country Days,114</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (biscuit recipe)

 <i>n.</i> (informal) a beverage [ORIGIN: abbreviation, Google search Nov. 2006: 1 Austral. citation found.]

### bewdy
 
 <i>adj.</i>

 <i>n.</i> variant of beauty n.

 <i>phr.</i> ?beyond civilisation

 <i>phr.</i>

 <i>abbr.</i> Building Industry Authority

The BIA has also issued an edict requiring any new sanitary fitting.. to have fitted a valve that limits the temperature of the hot water coming out to 55 degrees C. <i>(Oct 2, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),39</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [1984 16 F E13 Pakuranga Coll. 24D BIBLE BLURBERS [names for clergy]]

 <i>n.</i>

### Bible-banger
 
 <i>n.</i> [1953 15-17 M A39 Thames DHS 24 BIBLE BANGER [names for clergy] [M8]] (See [cf. cow-banger; Bible-puncher](../C#cow-banger;-bible-puncher))

>  <b>HARCOURT</b> We don't want any damned Bible bangers around here! <i>(1942,Parson in Prison,35</i>)

>  <b>ADSETT</b> He was probably going somewhere quiet to read the Bible. Everyone said he was a Bible-banger and he would be worse today because it was Sunday. <i>(1963,Magpie Sings, A,45</i>)

 <i>adj.</i>

 <i>n.</i> variant of Bible-banger [->1953 16 M A32 Thames DHS 24 BIBLE BASHER [names for clergy] -->1984 16-17 F E16 Pakuranga Coll. 24D BIBLE BASHER [F14M23] -->1984 14 F E143Wgton Girls C. 24D BIBLE BASHERS [F2]]

>  In those days he could be seen at Albert Park `jumping Sundays' berating the Bible-bashers and ridiculing religion. <i>(Oct 8, 1972,Sunday Times (NZ),2/7</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> A very religious, often AGGRESSIVELY RELIGIOUS PERSON. -->This term is often to the door-to-door abvocates of religion ... - <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,14</i>)

 <i>n.</i> variant of Bible-banger (cf. cow-puncher)
