### Bible-thumper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> `Tommy was the only one in a dog-collar that I'd have called a man at all. When the war finished up, he could have gone back and been a Bible-thumper, same as the rest, ... <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,93</i>)

### Bic Runga while
 
 <i>phr.</i> a long while

>  [Anika Moa] "I'm not ready to make my new album. I've got heaps of new songs but it will be a while. It won't be a Bic Runga while, but it'll be a while." <i>(Jun 13, 2002,Evening Post,15</i>)

### bicultural
 
 <i>adj.</i> of or relating to the equal partnership of Maori and Pakeha, as based in the articles of the Treaty of Waitangi.

>  The [Te Arawa] tribes never ceased to struggle for a genuinely bicultural future. <i>(2009,Mana,86/71</i>)

### biculturalism
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Recently.. John Kennedy.. expressed opposition to biculturalism in favour of multiculturalism. <i>(Jan 10, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  For this reason, the Hawke Report is curiously out of phase with the officially recognised policy of biculturalism, in its failure to recognise the constitutional status of the Maori with certain rights guaranteed by the Treaty of Waitangi. <i>(Dec 10, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53</i>)

### bid
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a biddy [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> `Think what Ma-Mac would say if her worst enemy was elected to the Committee. She'd more likely revert, the silly old bid.' <i>(1981,Fish Factory,31</i>)

### biddy
 
 <i>v.</i> (See [biddy-bid v](../B#biddy-bid-v))



 
 <i>n.</i> biddy-bid. [1933 Acland [see cit. biddy-biddy]]

>  After tea we went further up the creek, some of us gathering ferns and the rest throwing bid-a-bids at each other. Those throwing "biddies" must have been enjoying themselves. <i>(Feb 22, 1905,Otago Witness,74</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> He was completely stuck over with 'biddies' - the hateful little hooked seeds of a species of acaena, that cling so persistently nothing but a scraping with a knife will dislodge them. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,109</i>)

### biddy-bid
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Acaena</i> spp. esp. <i>Acaena anserinifolia, </i>(often formerly <i>A. sanguisorbae </i> occas. <i>A</i>. novae-zelandiae), <i>and other spp., </i> a (mainly) creeping plant and its burr, a prostrate herb having a many-seeded fruit with hooked spines which catch in clothing, the wool of sheep, etc. [from Maori piripiri]; hutiwai, piripiri q.v. cf also related forms biddy-burr, bizzie, and the Aust. alteration<i> bidgee-widgee,</i> AND 1910. [[a1927] Ver.= Vernacular names heard by J.C. Andersen from sawyers, settlers, etc. (And) [biddy-bid]]

>  After tea we went further up the creek, some of us gathering ferns and the rest throwing bid-a-bids at each other. Those throwing "biddies" must have been enjoying themselves. <i>(Feb 22, 1905,Otago Witness,74</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> (And) [bid-a-bid] [not found] <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The</i>)

>  <b>MUELLER</b> <i>(1866,My Dear Bannie,110,1958 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [see cit. at 4. below]]

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> Hence the plant [<i>Acaena sanguisorbae</i>] is only too familiar, as the `Native Burr' to the sheep farmers alike of New Zealand, Australia, and Tasmania. ... To the Otago settler the plant is also known as the `Bidi-bidi,' or `Beta-beta;' to the North Island Maori as `Hutiwai,' `Piri-piri,' (Colenso), or `Piri-kahu,' (Colenso). <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,59</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> PIRI PIRI (<i>Aceara ... sanguisorbae</i>). This name is commonly corrupted into [called] bidi bid. In the North Island the plant is called hutiwai or utiwai and by mistake poroporo. It is a native species well known to all New Zealanders, and is a weed chiefly on land in process of being brought into occupation. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,99,1967 ed.</i>)

>  Then whiskers full of biddy-biddy. <i>(Nov 15, 1923,Aussie (NZ Section),29</i>)

>  Sharp spears of golden spaniard grew through beds of bidibid, and the screes were dotted with bedraggled remains of celmisias, which must have been a picture in spring. <i>(May 8, 2000,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  Many of our plants grow too well in California. Our bidibids are a noxious weed there... Bidibids have also run wild in the British midlands, where they escaped from wool-scouring plants. <i>(Nov 3, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  The hutiwai plant, referred to by Europeans as biddy-bid, was boiled to take care of kidney problems. <i>(Dec 26, 2008,Nelson Mail,A13</i>)

>  You will gather the heads of bidibidi.. in your socks. <i>(Jul 26, 1992,Dominion Sunday Times,21</i>)

>  Will you please let me know if there is any way of destroying utuwai, or bidabid as it is more commonly called? <i>(1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,VI/687 or 6(87)</i>)

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> That troublesome, burr-bearing plant called by the natives bidi-bidi. <i>(1870,Martin's Bay Settlement,12</i>)

>  Will you please let me know if there is any way of destroying utuwai, or bidabid as it is more commonly called? <i>(1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,687</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> Piri-piri (<i>Acaena sanguisorbae</i>) by the settlers has been converted or corrupted into biddy-biddy; a verb has been formed on it, which is in very constant use for a great part of the year at least. To biddy is to rid one of burrs, as `I'll just biddy my clothes before I come in'. <i>(1880,Out in the Open</i>) [NOTES: 1880 T.H. Potts `Out in the Open' NZ Country Journal 195 (Morris),]

>  <i>Acaena ascendens</i>, Bidibidi [Piripiri] [sic], in fruit. Found on hillsides [on Macquarie Island]. <i>(1883,TrNZI,15/488</i>)

>  <b>JACOBSON</b> The pioneers were reduced ... to using ... seeds of biddy-biddy for tea. <i>(1893,Tales of Akaroa,226</i>)

>  `Yes, biddybids detract very materially from the value of the wool, and the plant should not be allowed to seed where sheep are depastured. They are not quite so bad as the Bathurst burr, but they are certainly in the same category.' <i>(Jan 23, 1896,Tales of Akaroa,2/36</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <i>Bid&#769;.dy-bid&#769;.</i> (b&ibreve.d&#769;.d&ybreve.-b&ibreve.d&#769;.), <i>n</i>. [Maori <i>piripiri</i>.] [Written also <i>biddy-biddy.] (Bot.) </i>The black berry growing in clusters on the tree piripiri. It becomes a troublesome bur. <i>[Local, N. Z.]</i> See PIRIPIRI. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)

>  <b>CRADDOCK</b> Biddy-biddies ... <i>(1904,Sport in NZ,44</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> [bid-a-bid] <i>(1906,Introductory Classbook of Botany</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Acaena sanguisorbae [novae-zelandiae] (The [New Zealand] Bidi-bidi). </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,201,1940, p. 210 ed.</i>)

>  (biddy-bid) <i>(1909,Catalogue NZ Exhibition</i>) [NOTES: CNZ (Vernac) (Andersen)]

>  I would also like to know whether the `bidabid' has any value as a food for stock ... As grasses assert themselves, so the `biddy-biddy' or `piripiri' becomes less prominent. It has little or no value as food for stock. <i>(Jan 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,81</i>)

>  <b>HOWES</b> `Bidabids!' she cried. <i>(1912,Where Bell-Birds Chime,40</i>)

>  Slang words of Maori origin were once common, but most of them are dead and dying [and include] ... `cockabully', (kokopu) `bullabull,' a shrub (poroporo), `biddybiddy' (piripiri), ... <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <i>piripiri</i>, a scrambling plant-pest (Acaena Sanguisorbae), burr, biddy-bid (corruption of Maori name); also called hutiwai. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>biddybid</i>: corruption of piripiri, a burr. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Jack's dog was lying over near Bert, pulling with his teeth at the biddy-bid that had got [121] entangled in his hair, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 273 (gloss), biddy-bid: bidibidi (piripiri), a grassland plant the seed-heads of which attach themselves to clothing or the wool of sheep. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,120,1974 ed Rhodes) ed.</i>)

>  Bidi-bids (<i>Acaena</i>, 16 species) solve the distribution problem by hooking their spiny seeds onto any passing furry object, and there can be few better ways to get around than on a tramper's socks. <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/51</i>)

>  For digestive troubles there is virtue in an infusion of the piripiri or hutuwai, the stickfast plant popularly known as the "biddibid". <i>(Jun 1936,NZ Railways Magazine,29</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> [biddy-bid] <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>PERRY</b> It is a mistake to think that seed (hatiwai [sic] or bidi-bidi) in wool is crushed to dust in the dumping, ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,138</i>)

>  <i>Biddy-biddy, Biddy-bid, or Biddy</i>. - Various species of Acaena. A burr, very harmful to wool when seeding. The name is a corruption of the Maori word piri-piri. <i>(Sep 9, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>FRAME</b> Father shook the bidi-bids off the big red and grey rug and then he spread it out again in the grass. <i>(1951,Lagoon, The,19,1961 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (`Keel and Kool')]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>ACAENA ... sanguisorbae ... var. A. </i>... Burr, Biddi-biddi. PIRIPIRI ... <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,31</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Hutiwai,</i> n. <i>Acaena anserinifolia</i>, a plant, the bidibidi = <i>piripiri.</i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,72</i>)

>  ~ The name of a class of small New Zealand plants bearing a prickly seed case. Some also grow in Australia; <i>piri-piri</i> in Maori. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),39</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> So now it was all boggy in the lower bits and a mass of blackberry and bidi-bidi on the higher ground, ... <i>(1959,No Remittance,132</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Many native plants have Maori names more or less mutilated such as <i>matagowri, goai</i> (kowhai), and <i>biddy bid</i> (piri piri). <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> ~ piripiri, piriwhetau, <i>Acaena </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>~ n</i>. (corruption of Maori name piri piri) kind of burr <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

>  <i>biddy-biddy ... n.</i> kind of burr [Corruption of Maori <i>piri-piri</i>]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <i>bidibid, bidibidi, bidi bidi</i> [=]<i> piripiri</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,5</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> The clumps of tussock ... sheltered a scattered growth of English grasses, of dainty wild flowers ..., a delicate little blue-bell, almesia, gentian, and ... the bidibid which hooked itslef onto the sheep's wool to form great tangles. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,37</i>)

>  <i>biddy-bid</i>: small prickly seed <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  ,<i> biddy-biddy n.</i>, pl. -<i>biddies. 1</i>. a low-growing rosaceous plant of New Zealand, having prickly burs. 2. the burs of this plant. ~Also called: <i>bidgee-widgee</i>. [< Maori <i>piripiri</i>] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,103</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 68; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 47]]

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Acaena</i> BIDIBIDS Piripiri Most of the 15 or so New Zealand species produce globular burrs with the annoying ability to stick fast to clothing and hair. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,178</i>)

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> It was December and most of the sheep were unshorn, ... and the bidi bidi was beginning to stick to the wool. <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,75</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The farm itself was very poor and covered with hutiwai (biddy bids), so the sheep were fairly slow cutting. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,82</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> SHEEP'S BURR (<i>Acaena ovina</i>), called also Australian burr, false bidi bid, and very loosely piri piri or bidi bidi, is not uncommon in fields and waste places in certain localities of both islands, but is commoner in the north. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,99,1967 ed.</i>)

>  I had nothing but a plaster of biddy-bids. <i>(Apr 8, 1924,Otago Witness,72</i>)

>  The bidi-bidi season is approaching and if shearing is delayed until the wool becomes seedy, its value is expected to drop. <i>(Feb 9, 1942,NZEF Times,I/33 : 4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [[phs belongs at sheep's burr or australian burr].]



 
 <i>v.</i> to extract biddy-bids from (wool, clothing, etc.) (See [biddy-bid n](../B#biddy-bid-n))

>  <b>POTTS</b> Piri-piri (<i>acaena sanguisorbe</i>) [Morris, sanguisorbe sic] by the settlers has been converted or corrupted into <i>biddy-biddy</i>; a verb has been formed on it, which is in very constant use for a great [Morris, good: check] part of the year at least. To biddy is to rid one of burrs, as `I'll just biddy my clothes before I come in'. <i>(1880,Out in the Open</i>) [NOTES: 1880 T.H. Potts `Out in the Open' NZ Country Journal 12/195 (1898 Morris, 29),]

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> In the early days a verb `to biddy-bid' was in use, a man having to biddy-bid or biddy his clothes before entering the house. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,380</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Acaena sanguisorbae.</i>

>  <i>(1915,AJHR,C-6</i>) [NOTES: (And). Cockayne: Plant Covering Kennedy's Bush (Canterbury)]



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Acaena novae-zelandiae.</i>

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> [red-flowered b-b], By some [British] Florists, <i>A[caena] Novae Zelandiae</i> is reckoned one of the most important and beautiful introductions yet made from New Zealand. [check] <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,59</i>)

>  <i>(1915,AJHR,C-6</i>) [NOTES: (And) 1915 Cockayne Notes on the Plant Covering of Kennedy's Bush ... Canterbury]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>REES</b> <i>(1928,Wild Will Enderby</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> For other genera of burrs, loosely for any burr, esp. among children and sheep farmers: eg. for burdock, Bathurst burr.

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> SHEEP'S BURR (<i>Acaena ovina</i>), called also Australian burr, false bidi bid, and very loosely piri piri or bidi bid, is not uncommon in fields and waste places in certain localities of both islands, but is commoner in the north. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,99,1967 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> horehound.

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> HOREHOUND (<i>Marrubium vulgare</i>), is actually called piri piri and bidi bid in some localities. It is common in waste places and sheep camps in both islands. [The dried flower head acts as a burr and sticks to wool.] <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,151,1967, p. 161 ed.</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> [stick like biddy bids, phrase]

>  <b>LANGTON</b> They stick like mud, or bid-a-bids. <i>(1889,Mark Anderson,115</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> But for all their air of imminent irredeemable disaster, stick like bidi-bids they did to their slips and shingle slides. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,92</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> [as tea-like infusion]

>  There were ... wild pigs in the surrounding bush; fuchsia berries could be gathered for tarts ... For tea, a concoction of bidi-bidi was used, and I have heard it described by one of these early pioneer women as horrible stuff too!] <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,30,1988 ed.</i>)

>  Despite these difficulties their shanty has been recorded in Puhoi history as the scene of many long dances and a hospitable place to stop for a cup of bidibidi tea, said the secretary of the family reunion, Mrs Judith Williams. <i>(1986,NZ Herald,1</i>)

>  <b>MUELLER</b> The last of our tea and sugar we shall have tomorrow ..., and after that `bidy-bidy' tea, or `Manukau' tea, without sugar. <i>(Jan 9, 1866,My Dear Bannie,110,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> Like the <i>Leptosperma,</i> in the early days of colonisation, the plant [<i>Acaena </i>] was much used, in bush journeys, to yield by infusion a substitute for<i> tea. </i> <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,59</i>)

### biddy-burr
 
 <i>n.</i> biddy-bid

>  <b>BAUME</b> I've got biddy-burrs on my stockings and crawlies over my feet. <i>(1933,Half Caste,221</i>)

### bidi-bidi
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [alteration of piripiri] a burred weed of the Acaena spp. that flaws fleecewool and that was used by colonists to augment tea leaves. (See [also biddy-bid](../A#also-biddy-bid))

>  I would be greatly obliged if you would let me know through your Journal if there is any known method of eradicating 'bidabid.' <i>(Jan 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,81</i>)

>  ... we do not think the quantity [of seedy wool] was much greater than that handled in the 1916-17 season, except in lambs, which later in the season were heavily infested with bidi-bidi. <i>(1919,Wool in NZ,40</i>)

>  <b>SILK</b> We could not afford to buy tea and used, as most of the settlers had to use, bidi-bidi tea. <i>(1923,History of Puhoi,46</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> odd lambs from No 8 and No 6 covered with biddi-biddi. <i>(Jan 10, 1929,Alexander Mowat Diaries,630</i>)

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> When tea was running low they eked it out by mixing it with dried bidi-bidi; and if no whaler appeared in time with new stocks, they used bidi-bidi alone or the young shoots of manuka. <i>(1940,Women of NZ, The,102</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Another lacked crimp - too much bidibid or pumice. <i>(1975,Tussock Fever,161</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> The bidi-bidi loved the steep, poor coastal hills more than the grass did ... [bidibid; bidabid] <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,171</i>)

### Big Bay skink
 
 <i>n.</i> A dark brown skink resident in North Fiordland and South Westland. [<i>Oligosoma "Big Bay"</i>]

>  Results from genetic work show all Big Bay skinks collected belong to a new species within the <i>Oligosoma nigriplantare polychroma</i> complex . . . The exotic weed <i>Ulex europaeus</i> (gorse) encroaching on to stable cobble fields is likely to be the most immediate threat to Big Bay skinks, and gorse control should continue in order to keep cobble habitat clear. <i>(1999,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.doc.govt.nz/upload/documents/science-and-technical/casn228.pdf]

>  The two skinks known from the Area [sic] are the Open Bay Island skink and the 'Big Bay Skink', the latter being locally common in cobble strands in northern Fiordland and at Barn Bay, and possibly the skin[k] species that is relatively abundant in the rocky areas on the Cascade Plateau ... A brown striped skink of unknown affinity has been found in sphagnum swamp at Okuru. <i>(Nov 2002,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.linz.govt.nz/docs/crownproperty/high-country-leases/leaselist/cascade-con-res.pdf The material in this survey was copied in 2002; exact date of writing is unclear.]

>  <b>Chapple, Ritchie, Daugherty</b> There was no support for the Big Bay skink (BBS1, BBS2, BBS17) being genetically distinct from O. inconspicuum (Fig. 3). The level of genetic divergence between the two subspecies of O. nigriplantare is 7.6%. <i>(Mar 2009,Molecular Phylogenetics and Evolution,52/478</i>)

### Big Day Out
 
 <i>n.</i> a festival celebrating things gay, lesbian and transgender. [a play on Big Day Out]

 <i>n.</i> a pop music event, held annually in summer in Auckland (also in some Australian cities) (See [BDO](../B#bdo))

>  You could do worse than stand at the exit gate of the Big Day Out next Friday night selling "I Survived The White Stripes" merchandise. <i>(Jan 13, 2002,Sunday Star Times,D4</i>)

>  The Big Day Out is one of those events that has assumed mythical status in the minds of devotees, who have never quite got over the giants of festivals past... <i>(Jan 11, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  Just before midday, the streets around the Big Day Out were littered with boozing and stoning teenagers. <i>(Feb 3, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  "So, there's a whole generation now that are going to take the stuff they hear at the Big Day Out with them through into their thirties and forties." <i>(Feb 23, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

>  Meeting rock stars is easy. Just drive out to Piha Beach on the eve of the Big Day Out, set a drift-net and wait. <i>(Feb 2, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  "The whole idea is that it's Auckland's Big Day Out, " Hood affirms. "It's basically for the local bands, and the international groups come in to give it a higher profile and broaden the audience base." <i>(Jan 29, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

### big house
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] a homestead or property-owner's home on a farm or station.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The 'big house' itself, built of stone and of impressive design, is set in attractive grounds and is probably the finest station homestead in the province. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,201</i>)

>  I was often taken up to the 'big house' to play with the girls. <i>(Apr 23, 1959,Hawke's Bay Herald Tribune,6</i>)

>  <b>MILLS</b> ..all the shepherds, including George Simpson, were invited one Sunday to afternoon tea. Guests at the 'Big House!' This was something totally unexpected and without precedent. <i>(1966,Huntaway,186</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> The farm employees were given a holiday, and they too had lunch at the 'Big House.' <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,96</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Big House: the owner's house, which is not always the biggest on the station. The whare or hut may easily cover twice the area. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,7</i>)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> At first the term 'big house' was used to denote the owner's residence on a station and the homestead referred to his residence if it was sited some distance from the other farm buildings. <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,22</i>)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> It was the custom for station staff to have their own quarters, sometimes located a considerable distance from the homestead or 'big house.' <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,176</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> A passage [from the men's quarters] leads to the `big house' as the homestead is generally called. <i>(1926,Butcher Shop, The,15</i>)

>  <i>Big house.</i> The owner's residence on a station. The men called it big house even though their own huts were larger. (C.) <i>(1933,Press, The,16</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary (ca1890-1910)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> When he came across to live here his first residence was a cottage ... He left there to reside in the `big house' at Matannic, ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,29</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Big house</i>. The runholder's residence. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)

### big lag

To <i>knock your lag out</i> is to serve your time in prison. <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 1</i>)

### big laughing man
 
 <i>n.</i> the mushroom <i>Gymnopilus spectabilis</i> [citation as copied has 'gym' not 'man' ??]

>  [I] concluded that what I'd found was the species <i>Gymnopilus spectabilis</i>, commonly known as big laughing gym. Eating this mushroom gives whole new meaning to the expression, " I nearly died laughing". That is literally what can happen. The victim goes into fits of uncontrollable laughter (perhaps because of the mushroom's psilocybin content), but also experiences nausea, abdominal pain, convulsions or death. <i>(May 31, 2003,Dominion Post,F6</i>)

### big OE
 
 <i>n.</i> long-term overseas experience or working holiday. [ORIGIN: abbreviation] (See [OE](../O#oe))

>  The big OE must be one of our most enduring cliches, provoking images of grimly carousing, hazy patriotic sentiment and sweat-annotated <i>Lonely Planets</i>. <i>(Sep 2, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

>  She had worked for ASB Bank in Auckland before leaving New Zealand for her "big OE". <i>(Aug 20, 1998,Dominion,5</i>)

>  Embarking on the big OE can be a frightening prospect but there's plenty of groups willing to offer help. <i>(Jun 10, 1996,Evening Post,20</i>)

>  One is this very New Zealand thing of going out in our 20s, like to war, or OE, and seeing these extraordinary places, but actually ending up finding out more about ourselves. <i>(Oct 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  Now the average Kiwi going on his or her OE is older and, arguably, smarter. Far from funding their OE working in a London pub, they're more likely to work in an office... Auckland's Jess Startup, 24, who went overseas in 1998, believes the pub-working OE is a thing of the past for most New Zealanders... For example, going to teach English in Korea or Japan is a lucrative way of having a Big OE. <i>(Aug 23, 2003,Dominion Post,F1</i>)

>  Candice and Brian Reading came about their perfect retreat a different way. Brian is a Londoner and met Candice at a wine-bar on her big OE. <i>(Mar 1, 2006,NZ Herald,Summer Escape, p H2</i>)

>  The big OE is a Kiwi rite of passage, but some people do find planning an international career move daunting. <i>(May 5, 2012,Dominion Post,G1</i>)

### big one
 
 <i>n.</i> a big note, $100 bill

>  According to the Old Bill, three tea leafs went into the Willis Street shop on Tuesday about lunchtime, and while one kept the manager busy, the other two lifted four big ones from the till. ... Three people robbed Arthur Daley's Lockup of $400 on Tuesday. <i>(Nov 15, 1986,Dominion</i>)

### big one, the
 
 <i>phr.</i> (informal) the statistically predicted forthcoming NZ earthquake of great magnitude [probably borrowed from California but now deep in the NZ psyche]

>  [Hdg] Kelburn viaduct strengthened for the big one. <i>(Jun 19, 2002,Capital Times,3</i>)

>  This is a study that manifests the worst fears of those who inhabit the shaky isles: what happens when the Big One strikes? <i>(Sep 9, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  [Hdg] Be honest : are you ready for the big one? Young people, trendy apartment dwellers and recently-arrived immigrants - you're all in big trouble if the "bog one" hits Wellington. <i>(Apr 2002,Absolutely Positively Wellington,2</i>)

>  Here on the faultline, I'm waiting for the Big One. <i>(Nov 4, 2001,NZ Catholic,6</i>)

>  When the big one hits, Auckland's first task will be to gather vital medical supplies from around the country, as well as overseas, and transport them to Wellington. <i>(Jul 17, 2002,Capital Times,6</i>)

>  The new [puppets] aren't as good as the previous lot, and there's always the worry that you'll end up with Helen Clark or Nandor Tanczos on your head if the big one strikes. <i>(May 12, 2001,Evening Post,31</i>)

>  When the big one hits, will you be ready? <i>(Sep 4, 2002,Capital Times,4</i>)

>  A 2008 report co-authored by Victoria University senior lecturer Geoff Thomas highlights a major problem wiht the safety of homes should "the big one" strike. <i>(Sep 8, 2011,Dominion Post,1</i>)

>  Can we one day hope to be given warning of the Big One before it jackhammers Wellington into the bay? <i>(Mar 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  Unnervingly labelled "active" on the geological maps, it's being watched closely as the prime suspect for the next "big one". <i>(Jan 2, 2003,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> `This is an earthquake.' ... In Napier, the gravedigger ... leaving out [of a newly dug grave] to avoid burial in The Big One. <i>(1980,New Zealanders, The (Henderson)</i>)

>  <i>Magnitude Eight Plus</i> is a comprehensive account of "the big one" that last hit New Zealand... In the next "big one", which could be tomorrow, much of central New Zealand may be zapped. <i>(Sep 2, 2000,Dominion,26</i>)

>  If the big one ever hits a major New Zealand city, then we'll want to be sure of two things - that our loved ones are safe and that we can rebuild our homes. <i>(Sep 9, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

>  [Hdg] Kelburn viaduct strengthened for the big one. <i>(Jul 19, 2002,Capital Times,3</i>)

### Big Oxford
 
 <i>n.</i> the OED

>  A complete dictionary (such as the big Oxford) would of course record these among all other uses. <i>(1982,PSA Journal,4/3</i>)

>  <b>86</b> `There are few universities that have less faults', a usage quoted by Big Oxford ... <i>(Sep 17, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>) [NOTES: [I.A. Gordon]]

### big pay
 
 <i>n.</i> [mining]

>  <b>McARA</b> <i>Big Pay</i>: The four-weekly contract pay-day when the underground co-operative contractors received payment for their contract work over and above the wages `sub pay' which was paid as an advance a fortnight earlier. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),312</i>)

### big picture
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) the main or feature film in a cinema programme as distinct from the newsreels and other shorts [ca1935 Havelock]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> She was a bit like the girl in the big picture. <i>(1948,That Summer,58</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> We went to every film, watching through the news, the cartoon, the Pete Smith Novelties, the James Fitzpatrick travel talks, the serial, and, after halftime or interval, the `big picture'. <i>(1982,To the Is-Land,108,1984 ed.</i>)

### Big Red
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> Phar Lap

>  Like most big gelding stayers, Phar Lap was slow to develop... Affectionately known as 'Big Red' he was acclaimed the greatest racehorse in Australian turf history. <i>(Jun 2004,NZ Memories,No. 48/14</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a Wellington city bus

>  From 1949 till 1964, the year the capital's last tram ran, council-owned Wellington City Transport built up a fleet of 199 [trolley] buses. Until the mid-1950s they were painted silver and called "silver ghosts" because they were so quiet. Then they were painted red like the trams they replaced and became fondly known as the Big Reds. <i>(Dec 22, 2004,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

>  These days the former advertising executive (remember the National Party's dancing Cossacks and 'are you man enough to drive a big red?') spin doctor, and professional board member.. is concentrating exclusively on being a writer. <i>(Oct 20, 1999,Capital Times,14</i>)

>  "O Big Red, I am gathered here together in the presence of weather and the absence of car. Look down, I beseech thee, on this thy wet servant. O Big Red, where are you?" <i>(Jun 7, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  What's there is the Cook islands equivalent of the whacking with the damp one-section Big Red bus ticket. <i>(Apr 24, 1992,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  ... men ... sat peacefully waiting for their bus while the big reds came and went in clouds of blue exhaust. <i>(Jul 1984,Islands,33/10</i>) [NOTES: (Wedde `Fast History')]

>  Wellington's Big Reds are to go the way of telephone boxes - their red colour will be phased out. <i>(Jan 27, 1993,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Is it the intention of the company still to call the fleet Big Reds? <i>(Mar 17, 1993,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  [Hdg] Citizens Find their Feet to Beat the Big Red Bus Stoppage. <i>(Jun 29, 1973,Evening Post,1</i>)

### big ring
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> In the nineteenth century the ring game seems to have been the most popular marble game. (In more recent years marble games with holes have had the greater role.) There was Big Ring, in which a circle a yard or more in diameter was drawn on the ground; there was the mullibar variation, in which an outsize taw was catapulted amongst the marbles in this circle. There were Little Ring, Liney, Follows, Follow the Taw, Holey, and then, on a less important scale, Butcher's Board, in which the marbles were bowled through the holes in a board, Eye-Drop, Knock-Backs, Leggings-Out (or Leggin's-Out), Span-'Em, and Backits. The last was more usually a girls' game. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

### big shed
 
 <i>n.</i> premises for discount marketing [attrib. use below]

>  ...the sales are another blurring of the retail calendar and a further sign of the "Walmartisation" of the $3 billion-a-year department store sector - where "big shed" stores on the edge of the town buy in bulk and slash prices. <i>(Jul 15, 2004,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

### Big Smoke
 
 <i>n.</i> (esp. from a rural perspective) any big town or city [perhaps introduced by soldiers of WW1, it was originally attested only of Dunedin city, but apparently in Otago rural can mean the nearest town (1951 Ray Coombes); in Aust. (see Baker 1942), Sydney or Melbourne, and common in Aust. radio plays among (low or) outback characters]

>  Among those living out of town who visited the big "smoke".. were All Black Mick Lomas, "Circus" and Morgan Haywards. <i>(Sep 17, 1947,NZ Observer,27</i>)

>  It's clear that the folk from the big smoke have different ideas about driving than we do. <i>(Oct 10, 1999,NZ Catholic,8</i>)

>  It may be possible to spread the herd thinly over say 70 per cent of the farm while you clear off to the "big smoke". <i>(Jan 1972,NZ Dairy Exporter,65</i>)

>  I left a radio/tape recorder running.. to find out what this signal from the big smoke was all about. <i>(Jan 7, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  <b>South Bank Show, Kiri Te Kanawa</b> I love sitting out on the edge of the - we call it "The Point" - and I thought, my God, it's God's country, wild, and the peacefulness of this glorious place. All these memories come flooding back to you. And suddenly, you have to leave it to go to the Big Smoke - and it's horrible. <i>(1992,TV1</i>)

>  Wellington is to share with Auckland the distinction of being the "big smoke" of the colony. <i>(Feb 13, 1904,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  <b>McGill</b> She'd sell out if there was a buyer and be off back to the big smoke. <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,6</i>)

>  Kelly and her mate Lisa hoon around in a big bogan car and, when they encounter two boys from the big smoke, they take them on a chilling ride into the depths of the New Zealand heartland. <i>(Sep 21, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),23</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> `Well Sonny! What do you think of the big smoke ...?' <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,190</i>) [NOTES: (Not for a Seagull)]

>  ... as a consequence of which the gang breaks up and the narrator slopes off back to the big smoke. <i>(1973,NZ Short Stories,147,GRANT ed.</i>)

>  <b>BINNEY</b> Elegance, irony, discerning taste were not Honest - you only got away with that in the Big Smoke. <i>(1985,Long Lives the King,24</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> When we were kids, Whakatane was the nearest Big Smoke, and we thought we had arrived when the bus drove through the Rock. ... Right now it looked like a genial Hicksville with a joker in a Davy Crockett possum-skin hat, ... <i>(1985,G'day Country,66</i>)

>  It was touch and go, but we bought the business, and moved down [from the suburb of Karori] into the big smoke in the middle of Lambton Quay. <i>(May 6, 1986,Karori and Western Suburbs News,6</i>)

>  Beaten 72-0 after being starved of possession and original ideas for most of the game, they were content with their rare trip to the big smoke. <i>(Aug 27, 1987,Dominion,28</i>)

>  Zan, who has since returned to Shannon to open 'Cafe Extreme,' was a bored fifteen year-old back then and left as soon as she could for the big smoke (Wellington). <i>(Sep 2000,Salient,No. 19 : 14</i>)

>  That these institutions have become exceedingly popular.. is evidenced by the addresses given by applicants for leave whose sole "reason" for visiting the "Big Smoke" is for the purpose of renewing family ties. <i>(Oct 16, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/4 : 87</i>)

>  To read their articles [sc. the London dailies] one would imagine we were quite the simplest things that ever hit the "Big Smoke". <i>(Jan 30, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,III/34-35 : 29</i>)

>  After years working in the big smoke on the trading desk, he and wife Shannon decided to strike out on their own. <i>(Nov 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,E6</i>)

>  [Hdg] He's Heading Back to the Big Smoke. <i>(Jun 24, 1949,Southern Cross (Wgton),7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> orig. London, and used in England by NZEF soldiers of WWI, and in WW2 (and by Australians); now any large town or city

>  Tory papers in the `Big Smoke' <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth</i>)

>  My first day in `the Big Smoke' ... <i>(Apr 24, 1917,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1917 Apr 24 in 1939 Miller Camps, Tracks and Trenches 61]

>  <b>INGRAM</b> Arrived back from our three days `draft leave' which I spent in `the Big Smoke', London. <i>(Jul 6, 1917,Anzac Diary,34,1987 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> My knowledge of the Big Smoke ceased at Aldgate Pump. <i>(1922,Happy Wanderer,18</i>)

>  <b>HAYDEN</b> Most of us made tracks for London or the `Big Smoke', as we affectionately termed it. <i>(1941,NZ Soldiers in England,10</i>)

### big smokers
 
 <i>n.</i> (people in) the city (the Big Smoke q.v.)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> After watching for a polite moment the car heading back towards the big smokers, leaving Carlton's little puff behind. <i>(1979,Skin Deep</i>)

### big snow
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a heavy snowfall causing substantial stock loss.

>  <b>WILSON & FRASER</b> The winter of 1878 was very severe and is still spoken of as 'the year of the big snow.' <i>(1936,Book of the Pioneers, The,77</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> In 1873, Barnhill with Captain Brown, purchased Blackmount station but the big snow of 1878 ruined him ... <i>(1952,History of Northern Southland,56</i>)

>  At least two good things came out of the big snow. The number of slinky skins from dead lambs went up from 700 to 20,000. <i>(Sep 13, 1973,NZ Farmer,26</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The winter of 1895 saw one of the most devastating 'big snows' causing heavy stock losses for high-country stations ... <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,31</i>)

>  .. it collapsed under the weight of the 'big snow' of 1878. <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,74</i>)

>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> The legendary 'Big Snow' of 13-14 July 1945 was an experience the land girl team would not forget. <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,92</i>)

### big spit
 
 <i>n.</i> (the big spit) a vomit, a spew (See [cf. technicolour yawn](../T#technicolour-yawn))

>  <b>V O'SULLIVAN</b> A Maori says: `I'm going outside for the big spit'. <i>(1960,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>big spit, the</i> vomit, often in phr. `to go for the big spit'. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,14</i>)

### Big Stoush
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical, informal) the First World War

>  The fact that he was 'winged'in the Big Stoush does not detract from his success as a lawyer or as a very popular member of the Wellington Law Society, of which he was recently president for a term. <i>(Mar 25, 1926,NZ Truth,6</i>)

### big wet
 
 <i>n.</i> a long rainy spell [more Australian than NZ]

>  [headline]<b>Big wet set to continue <i>(Apr 30, 2006,Sunday Star Times,A2</i>)

### big-eye cardinal fish
 
 <i>n.</i> [also big-eyed cardinal fish]

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BIG-EYE CARDINALFISH Epigonus lenimen </i>... The big-eye cardinalfish is an elongate deepwater species ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,211,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] epigonus telescopus .. [scientific name] <i>Epigonus telescopus </i>.. [other name(s)] bigeye cardinal fish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> cardinalfish, bigeyed 1332 ... bigeyed cardinalfish <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography,293</i>)

### big-note
 
 <i>v.</i> to act the big man; to try to impress, or make a great impression (as with continually using big bank notes), ?mainly advertising. [ca1979 Wellington bar, `He seems to be shouting a lot of top shelf tonight.' `He's only big-noting.']

>  Before then our only overseas presence was a handful of non-achievers who had served in previous National cabinets staffing our embassies, supplemented by Muldoon's regular forays big-noting around the international financiers (who, anyway, have never figured large in New Zealand's inward visitor statistics). <i>(Sep 11, 1987,National Business Review,10</i>)

>  Most of his suspensions involved third-formers bringing cannabis to school to "big-note" among their peers. <i>(Jun 27, 1988,Dominion,13</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>big-note</i> to boast or exaggerate; eg `Barry's big-noting again about how many he's scored over the years.' c. 1935. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,14</i>)

### big-noter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  You need guts to go up to some big-noter businessmen and ask for [pounds]20,000 or even [pounds]60,000 for the theatre. <i>(Dec 1986,Metro,142</i>)

### big-scale fish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [species name] melamphaeid .. [scientific name] <i>Melamphaeid </i>.. [other name(s)] big-scale fish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### big-spined boarfish
 
 <i>n.</i> = yellow boarfish

>  [species name] yellow boarfish .. [scientific name] <i>Pentaceros decacanthus</i> .. [other name(s)] big spined boarfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### bigeye
 
 <i>adj.</i> as a distinguishing epithet in names of fish, see tuna, cardinal fish.

>  <b>WILSON</b> Some of the biggies [sc. fishing flies] used after dark are the Muddler-Minnow, White Moth, Grey Sedge and Red-and-Black Palmer. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,89</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  Thousands of big eye swarm among the kelp, almost touching as they move away. <i>(Nov 1, 2003,Dominion Post,E9</i>)

>  Other fish, such as big eye and slender roughy also live in trhe dark areas, emerging at night to feed on drifting plankton. <i>(May 24, 2003,Dominion Post,F8</i>)

>  <b>DOAK</b> Bigeye <i>Pempheris adspersa</i> (Aust: bullseye) <i>(1972,Fishes,33</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BIG EYE Pempheris adspersa </i>... The big eye is a small deep bodied fish .... Most of the head is taken up by the huge eyes ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,227,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Bigeye Pempheris adspersa</i> Family Pempheridae (bullseyes). Also occurs in southern Australia. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,98</i>)

>  Once my sight adjusts, the owners of the eyes become visible: they're the aptly named bigeyes, little velvety brown, teardrop-shaped fish with saucers on their faces. <i>(Jul 3, 2005,Sunday Star Times,Escape/8</i>)

### bigeye tuna
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  My local Mt Eden fishmonger also had big-eye tuna, but she suggested bluefin for these recipes. <i>(Oct 17, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),43</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BIGEYE TUNA Thunnus obesus </i>... The bigeye tuna is a large deep-bodied species, sporting a huge head and, as its name suggests, a big eye. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,292,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Thunnus obesus </i>.. [other name(s)] . <i>(Jul 23, 1982,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Bigeye tuna Thunnus obesus </i>Widespread in topical and subtropical seas. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,129</i>)

### bigger-block-of-cheese
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Theirs are the only scales I believe, since Mum and I calibrated them, using a full bag of sugar, a number of cans of vegetables and a bigger-block-of-cheese. <i>(Dec 26, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

### biggy-bung
 
 <i>n.</i> an early Ma.-Eng. (?pidgin) nickname for an artilleryman: a Moaricisation of <i>big bang</i>, with an extra vocalic syllable added to<i> big</i> and the E. [`ash'] approximated to Maori [a] appearing to English ears as [the sound of but].

>  <b>POWER</b> ... an artillery officer is Biggy-bung; one wearing spectacles is Four-eyes or No-eyes ... <i>(1848,Sketches in NZ,159</i>) [NOTES: [Maoris' nicknames for the whites in the party]]

### bignonia
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Rhabdothamnus Solandri</i> [a tropical genus with. trumpet-shaped flowers]

>  (And) <i>(1917,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>)

### bike park
 
 <i>n.</i> a park with special facilities for cyclists

>  A team of enthusiasts has painstakingly dug out a 500-metre gentle uphill track that will give beginners and families access to what is becoming one of New Zealand's most popular bike parks. About 35,000 cyclists have visited the Makara Peak mountain bike park on the edge of Karori this year. <i>(Dec 6, 2000,Dominion,13</i>)

### bike-happy
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of farm dogs] excited when around a farmbike, with difficulty in concentrating on their work.

>  Bike happy - condition of some dogs who, whenever you start a bike, won't shut up and spend most of the time in the paddock yodelling at the two-wheeler and not the sheep. <i>(Aug 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,10</i>)

### bike-rack
 
 <i>n.</i> cf bicycle-shed oin OED Suppl, bike (2)

### bike-spanner
 
 <i>n.</i> a special small spanner for adjusting push-bicycle nuts

>  I don't kill things with a bike-spanner. <i>(Dec 1903,Huia, The,11</i>)

### bikeathon
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Long summers spent in New Zealand getting the cash together to set off in search of his mountainbiking fortune. There were the cheese rolls, sold at a local race, the bikeathon in the Octagon where people guessed how far he would go in an hour (all the time sitting in one spot), more raffles than he would care to remember... <i>(Aug 19, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B14</i>)

### bikie
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a member of a motorcycle gang. (See [(contrast British biker)](../(#(contrast-british-biker)))

>  <b>MARRINER</b> Lefty had contrived to elevate himself to first-name terms with the bikies... <i>(2001,Stonedogs,220</i>)

>  During 1976 bikie gangs were again in the news. <i>(Nov 25, 1978,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> It was a snake and his leg was pinned under his bike. ... We ... got his bike off him. That was the last time that snake gave a Queen Street bikie a ticket ... <i>(1971,Bullshit and Jellybeans,36</i>)

>  [a (youthful) motor-bike devotee, particularly one who belongs to the cult and whose behaviour may be anti-social], ... no-one could pretend that commerce has any more right than `bikies' to run noisy vehicles' <i>(Jan 25, 1972,Otago Daily Times,4 Editorial</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  He called me a `dirty bikie bastard' ... <i>(Jan 26, 1972,Otago Daily Times,7</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  Special police mobile task forces similar to one set up to deal with New Plymouth's `bikie' invasion last week-end, may be widely used. The police officer-in-charge of the Wellington division, Superintendent E.J. Trappitt, said yesterday that the concept of task forces was being developed to meet the mobility of `bikies'. He said that the movement of `bikies' was a recent development. <i>(Jun 7, 1972,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Two children suffered injuries at Waihi Beach on Saturday morning when a speeding bikie swept up a Kon Tiki line which dragged them into the tide. The children both suffered burns from the nylon line. The motor-cyclist, reported to be a member of a gang, was riding illegally ... <i>(Oct 25, 1972,Press, The,2/4</i>)

>  <b>JUSTIN</b> We were ordered to our cells, ... and it was found that an ex-bikie was missing. ... The bikie was an overfat ... and universally hated prisoner. <i>(1973,Prisoner,80</i>) [NOTES: [also EX-BIKIE]]

>  <b>BELL</b> Well he knew it not just Maori, cause they all left Hawaiki,/ And he knew he must warn other tribes and wished he was a bikie./ But of course there wasn't any roads, only little tracks,/ Just wide enough for little sleds and Maori wild dog packs. <i>(1974,Ballads of a Racegoer,9</i>)

>  <b>CLEARY</b> ... but what can one old woman do with night coming down and nobody to protect her from these larrikins. ... In two minutes each of these bikies has a heavy hand on his neck. ... <i>(1975,Pocketful of Years,22</i>)

>  <i>(Jan 4, 1976,Evening Post,1/6</i>) [NOTES: see Kung Fu Star 2]

>  There are a number of MOTOR-CYCLE gangs in New Zealand whose young MEMBERS model themselves largely on the `Hells Angels' although the all Maori `Mongrel Mob' might not like that description. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,14</i>)

>  <i>bikies:</i> motorbike gang <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection),]

>  <i>bikie n. Austral. sl.</i> one who is dedicated to riding a motorcycle, often in a gang. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,104</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 68],]

>  <b>DUCKWORTH</b> Punks, bikies, trainee civil servants, inner-city fringe people, clad in Opportunity Shop garments. <i>(1984,Disorderly Conduct,109</i>)

>  <b>REILLY</b> He was little better than a bikie, the only difference being he wasn't Maori and he didn't belong to a gang as such. <i>(1986,Deputy Head,90</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bikie</i> member of motorbike gang. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,14</i>)

>  Where else are they so big on hypocorisms? ... They're those dinky little words we make up, like these: kiddies (children), cardies (cardigans), boatie (person who sails boats), yachtie (person who sails yachts), drinkies (drinks), bikies (people who get together to ride motorbikes), wrinklies (old people), rellies (relations), wellies (Wellingtons, as in gumboots), wallies (variation on wankers), littlies (children). <i>(Sep 1988,North & South,41</i>)

>  <b>5/272</b> When they got back to the farm Chris had arrived. He heard them ... and saw, bullocking in through the gate, two 650s, red, and orange, on one a Maori ... riding out of any bikie saga ... <i>(1973,Islands</i>) [NOTES: Gifkins (Winter Riders)]

>  <b>Grace</b> Some like the mural. Others think it modern and hideous and say that ancestress Ngarua has been made to look like a gang member or a bikie. <i>(2001,Dogside Story,141</i>)

### bikie gang
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MANTELL</b> `Kind of totem thing. Bikie gangs in Auckland - all the rage. Sort of paramilitary thing.' <i>(1980,Murder and Chips,69</i>)

### bikoi
 
 <i>n.</i> a protest ride by motorcyclists [modelled on hikoi (compare carkoi); also spelt bikeoi on non-newspaper sites]

>  An estimated 5,000 motorcyclists have joined today's "bikoi" protesting ACC levy rises, according to organisers. <i>(Nov 17, 2009,NZ Herald,online ed.</i>)

>  Up to 3000 motorcyclists are expected to roll into the capital on November 17 for a "bikoi", over proposed rises in ACC levies. <i>(Oct 23, 2009,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

### biky
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Christchurch is a very biky place, and it was not surprising to find that the next claim was in respect of a machine also. <i>(Apr 16, 1910,NZ Truth,251/6/2</i>)

### BIL
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Brierley Investments Ltd

>  BIL left New Zealand three years ago, when it moved its head office to Singapore... <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  Perhaps that is why Brierley Investments Ltd, which has done so much to change the face of pushier cities, is holding its annual meeting here today... "The BIL annual meeting turned into a love-in yesterday." <i>(Dec 18, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

### bilge free
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... the casks [of whale oil] ... are carefully lodged in the hold, the bung being upwards, and the body of the casks kept apart from touching each other, bedded and quoined with small blocks of wood; this is called [418]. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/417</i>)

### Bill Massey's bludgeon carriers
 
 <i>phr.</i> special constables in the 1913 strike

>  <i>(Sep 23, 1916,Hutt Valley Independent</i>)

### Bill Massey's Cockits
 
 <i>phr.</i> spec. policemen or `specials', recruited mainly from farmers who `kept order' during the 1913 strike, (from cocky @n, if not a misprint).

>  <b>HYDE</b> And the strike was no baby. 1913 brought Prime Minister Bill Massey's Cockits riding into town - strikebreakers armed with pick-handles and ready to smash any head that came in their way. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,51</i>)

### Bill Massey's district
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  It is only in what the homely settlers speak of as "Bill Massey's district" that the character of the soil is revealed by the plough. <i>(Aug 24, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

### Bill Massey's stew
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  So "Bill Massey's stew," as it is called, has out-lived its former position as the stock camp joke. <i>(Oct 2, 1918,Poverty Bay Herald,9</i>)

### Bill Massey's tourists
 
 <i>n.</i> Volunteers with the NZEF to Egypt in WW2, cf. debt-dodgers etc.

>  <b>BURTON</b> Great days for "Bill Massey's tourists" - the most glorious picnic they had ever had. <i>(1935,Silent Division, The,25</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> (Preface), The story of my adventures as one of `Bill Massey's Tourists' ... <i>(1939,Camps, Tramps and Trenches</i>) [NOTES: c1914-1918]

>  Cyprus would be nice to see, just the thing for `Bill Massey's tourists', but I would rather be going to France .. <i>(Mar 4, 1915,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1915 Mar 4 letter in 1965 Malthus ANZAC 29]

>  <b>PUGSLEY</b> All the action was in France and `Bill Massey's Tourists' were stuck among the desert sands; letters home were now full of complaints. <i>(1984,Gallipoli: the New Zealand Story,85</i>)

### Bill Masseys
 
 <i>n.</i> [(schoolboys', St Patrick's College, Wellington) School-cadets' khaki uniforms. -->ca1925- to ? slang]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  For many years, Dad kept an old Army overcoat... He affectionately called it his 'Bill Massey', as William Massey was Prime Minister of New Zealand at the time that these coats were issued during the First World War. <i>(Feb 2004,NZ Memories,No. 46/44</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (A pair of) NZ army boots [Aka Bill Massey's boots and Bill Massey boots; see also Masseys.]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> As one squad clumped along in their newly-acquired "Bill Masseys", the instructor gave the command, "Right turn!" <i>(1990,Soldier Country,2</i>)

>  Many of our party had already.. provided themselves with more or less comfortable boots of the type known amongst the Diggers as "Bill Masseys". <i>(Dec 1, 1928,NZ Free Lance,11</i>)

>  They are New Zealanders; and they wear Bill Massey's boots. <i>(1918,Ionicall Magazine, The,48</i>)

>  Just now "Bill Massey's boots" are in high favor. <i>(Oct 2, 1918,Poverty Bay Herald,9</i>)

>  The long march was made even more unbearable by the ill-fitting "Bill Massey" boots the soldiers wore. <i>(Sep 7, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  [Slang of the NZEF] Issue boots were `Bill Masseys.' <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bill Massey's</i>. NZ army-boots: New Zealand soldiers': in GW Ex the late Wm. Massey, who was the NZ War Minister. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,52</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Among numerous terms originated in the first Great War we may note <i>Bill Masseys</i> for army boots issued in this country. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,50</i>)

>  <i>Bill Masseys n</i>. army boots in World War 1 [From name of N.Z. Prime Minister]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) hobnailed farm boots, later adopted by military [World War I].

>  <b>BOYACK</b> We have boots which weight [sic] about a stone, regular Bill Massey's farm yard brand with nails in the soles very handy for fighting. <i>(1916,Behind the Lines,89</i>) [NOTES: in BOYACK Behind the Lines (1989)]

>  <b>GORDON</b> These [mustering] gangs were made up of men who'd taken up the "burnt chops", ate "hockey sticks", wore "Bill Masseys" slept in a "scratcher" and rode pigskin .. hockey sticks are chops, Bill Masseys, boots. <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,1</i>)

### bill-can
 
 <i>n.</i> [Is this an error for, or a colloquial shortening of, billy-can?]

>  Having rolled my swag up in regulation style, and having provided myself with bill-can and nosebag, I made a start from Woodville on June 22 for fresh fields and pastures new. <i>(Jan 7, 1911,NZ Truth,289/4/5</i>)

### bill-hook
 
 <i>n.</i> a long-handled tool with a hooked blade for trimming or pruning trees, seems to be the gen. Eng. term (OED 1611) for what in NZ would usu. be called a <i>slasher</i>, but freq. used in 19C. esp. for a sort of short-handled slasher with a hooked point and phs distinguished from the various long-handled slashers [check illustrations before defining]. [[get Hursthouse quot]]

>  The latter [sc. Maori] were armed only with bill hooks, but no dangerous wounds were inflicted. <i>(Dec 9, 1854,ANZ Gazette,163/570</i>)

>  <b>WOOD</b> The fern is fired; if it burns well, all the thick and matted dead stuff at the bottom.. will be consumed, leaving only the shrivelled 'Tutu'; and the cane-like fern stalks.. should be cut at once with a strong hook, or better still, with a short scythe, and the 'Tutu' slashed down with a bill-hook. <i>(1959,From New Plymouth to New Plymouth,138</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from HURSTHOUSE 1849]

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> He was much struck with the convenient form of a common billhook <i>(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),65</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection), [referring to Moyhanger buying tools in London to take back home]]

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> The axes, billhooks, prints, etc., which I intended to give them were all got ready after breakfast. <i>(1814,Letters & Journals,90,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> Duaterra himself was in his native dress, ... brandishing a large bill-hook in his right hand, and carrying a spear in his left. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/19</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> He stood in silence leaning on the end of his musket, a bill-hook bright as silver in his belt in front, and a handsome dogskin mat thrown carelessly over his shoulders, ... <i>(Mar 6, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,231,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The colonist need hardly be told to provide himself with <i>bill-hooks, pruning-knives</i>, &c., ... fitted into belts for the waist. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,447</i>)

>  Harry and I went down to the Beach House and opened our tool cask ...[sic] we bought a large camp oven ..., 3 English-American axes 7/- each and billhooks 5/-. <i>(Oct 22, 1853,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/136</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>MONEY</b> We recognised these at once as belonging to Howitt, for there were bill-hooks with the government mark on them, surveying materials, and other things, in the recess; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 129 The line of survey has to be taken across a certain country ...; though it should be ... thick tangled and heavy bush and undergrowth, the party with their bill-hooks and fern slashers must cut their way. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,28</i>) [NOTES: [cp slasher]]

>  Cutting can be done with a bill-hook. <i>(1877,Country Journal,1/243</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN & FENWICK</b> Mr. F-'s man piloting the way with bill-hook and slasher. <i>(1892,Holiday Trip to the Catlins District, A,12</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> The half-axes were necessary in case we had to cut a tree down to `spar' the river or a bad creek; the bill-hooks were for blazing a track. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,182</i>)

>  ... a country impossible for settlement, impenetrable to anybody save a trained bushman, armed with axe and billhook. <i>(1930,NZ Short Stories,13</i>) [NOTES: Arthur H. Adams [sc. as used by a bushman],]

>  <b>HARPER</b> A bill-hook, or short-handled slasher is essential in bush country. <i>(1945,Camping and Bushcraft in NZ,20</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The billhooks supplied to them [the bushfalling contractors] proved to be made of dishonest steel - `the edge would be turning around to look at you and you to smack a pound of butter'. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,10</i>)

### billet
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>PICARD</b> [I] got a billet hash throwing ... <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,30</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> food and lodging

>  <b>BUTLER</b> When a man takes a cadet's billet, it is a tolerably sure symptom that he means half-and-half work, in which case he is much worse than useless. <i>Ibid.</i> 127 [Workers] will ask and get from one pound to thirty shillings a week besides their board and billet. <i>(Jun 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,6/75</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> OED: verb is gen. Eng. (with preps <i>at, in, on, upon, with:</i> but not, <i>billet out</i>, which is a common NZ verbal usage in this sense; in gen. Eng. and NZ usage, esp. sports etc., tournaments etc, `temporary private lodgings for visitors'; or as a verb, to provide such lodgings. [ca1945 Victoria University College, We found a hundred billets for the Auckland competitors. (cf The Auckland competitors were all billeted out. (or) We billeted them out on ten people)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> position (permanent), appointment`he's got a good billet'. [1905 very frequent in <i>Truth passim</i> for `job']

>  <b>BAINES</b> I have been looking for an easy billet for the last week. <i>(1847,Edward Crewe,62</i>)

>  George Draper refused a billett as manager three hundred per an. a house and married couple to himself ... <i>(Aug 21, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,142,1950 ed.</i>)

>  An elderly digger, Tommy Smith,/ For a billet to them came, <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...,29</i>) [NOTES: Thatcher Lucky Hit Machine in Local Songs]

>  <b>MONEY</b> The owner of the machine ... gave me the billet of outside porter to a wholesale store ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,17</i>)

>  <b>HOPEFUL</b> If you are out of place, out of work, or out of situation as we say, here it is called `out of billet'. <i>(1887,Taken In,134</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> ... what the colonial calls `a billet'. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,221</i>)

>  <b>DAVIDSON</b> His first billet was looking after a mob of sheep in the wilds of Nelson Province. <i>(1889,Stories of NZ Life,41</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> an appointment, a position; a very common expression in Australia, but not confined to Australia; adapted from the meaning, `an official order requiring the person to whom it is addressed to provide board and lodging for the soldier bearing it.' (`O.E.D.'). <i>(1898,Austral English,30</i>)

>  he couldn't get a billet, tho' he tramped it up and down <i>(1903,King Bill's Philosophy,23</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> [a (? Government) position, job], After a lot of waiting and jawing you [a farmer] get what the chap with a billet to keep [sc a departmental valuer] shies at you; but what about your spent years. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,295,[1928/300] ed.</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> Do you know of a billet knocking about anywhere that 'ud suit him, boss? <i>(1908,Anchorage,45</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> I was a fairly competent `stockman' by that time, but had taken no permanent place or billet yet. <i>(1910,Reminiscences,30</i>)

>  That was the reason [sc he was a cadger] that I had besought him to get a `billet' - ... The post of `exchange clerk' in a bank was about his level. <i>(Sep 11, 1911,Triad, The,45</i>)

>  <i>Billet</i>. In the 'nineties station hands spoke of `a permanent b.', where they now [1933] speak of a `job'. In those days a man's job was his allotted task, and his billet was his appointment. (C.) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> You know that cook? I believe he is a bit of a pointer because he asked me what billet I was coming to and I wouldn't be surprised if he came along any minute. <i>(1935,Slang Today,288</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> ?a sinecure; a political appointment

>  <b>SMALL</b> For I've promised each [friend] a billet should I get elected./ <i>(1866,NZ & Australian Songster,33,1970 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (`Address to the Christchurch Electors')]

>  [The Premier, Seddon] does all the billetting ... Dick never gives a billet to any but his friends ... Why I heard of him giving a billet of [pounds]4 a week to a fellow. <i>(Aug 10, 1894,Argus, The,3</i>) [NOTES: 1894 Aug 10 The Argus and Newtown Chronicle 3]

### billied
 
 <i>adj.</i> equipped with a billy.

>  <b>FANNING</b> I was tramping with a mate ... to Westland, and it was our whim to be slouch-hatted, and `blueyed', `billied', unshaven, and otherwise orthodox swaggers. <i>(1910,Players and Slayers,81</i>)

### Billjim
 
 <i>n.</i> an Australian soldier

>  The following (only much better expressed) was told by an Australian Billjim in France, in a letter home... <i>(1918,Ionicall Magazine, The,41</i>)

### billy
 
 <i>n.</i> a vessel (orig. tin can) with a lid and a swinging wire handle used first for boiling water or food over open fires and later as a receptacle for milk and other liquids, billyful, noun; a tin can (or enamelled or, later, aluminium) vessel with a swinging wire handle, and often a lid, used orig. for boiling water or cooking in the open, and later for holding milk and other liquids; a billy can cf tin billy [AND TIN- from tinplate 1881] phs to tin-; cf billy-boiling, boil the billy, milk-billy see also swing the billy, swing 3. [tin pot (occas. tin can or bully pot) and bouilli tin are the the early congeners of billy attested in NZ in lexis and praxis]

>  <b>IHIMAERA & LONG</b> I sat on a log and swung my tea billy. I swung the billy lots of times, that night. <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,162</i>)

>  He makes me tea by throwing a handful of it into his black billy. <i>(Oct 19, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> Presently Jim ... placed on the table ... two tin pannikins; and a small tin boiler, called a `billy', filled with steaming tea. <i>(1881,White Hood and Blue Cap,12</i>)

>  <b>OVERTON</b> Having pitched their tent, they were sitting by the fire boiling the billy when, without any warning, a shot rang out and a bullet went right through the billy. <i>(1949,Fifty Years Sheep Farming,57</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> The water swirled through the bottom of the buggy and, to our great regret, carried away a `billy' which had been trusted to be heavy enough to withstand its violence. <i>(1895,Pictorial NZ,24</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <i>Bil&#769;.ly</i> (b&ibreve.l&#769;.l&ybreve.), <i>n. [Add.] 3.</i> A bushman's kettle; a cylindrical tin vessel with detached lid and arched wire handle; - sometimes called <i>billy-can</i>. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> There was no one with whom to argue; at three o'clock, beef tea came up in billies. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,31</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>Billy</i> - borrowed from Australia; where it was used of the can in which the swagmen boiled water and made tea - is now also applied to any can which will contain household milk. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,86</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> billy: Tin can used as or jug or kettle <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,302</i>) [NOTES: [Colonial-NZ glossary]]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> billy: tin with lid and swinging handle used mainly for milk. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,297</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> Ehtel [sic] made a very good brew; four tablespoons of oatmeal and a little water into a seven pound treacle billy where it was squeezed to a cream with the back of the spoon, and the billy filled water. <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,107</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> We left early to carry the billies of milk ... to the settlement to the homes of children. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,15</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Billy</i>. Round tin pot with a bucket-type handle, widely used for making tea. Usual capacity 1 1/2-2 pints. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)

>  <b>WASHBOURNE</b> The term billy for tin can also originated on the digging [sc. Collingwood 1856-1857] and is still in general use. <i>(1933,Reminders of Early Days,5</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> How Claude's taking a billy because it won't be too dry for a fire in the bush and Claude's not having any G-men in his squad who can't make a decent mug of billy tea. <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,159</i>)

>  The rustle of the trees, the shrill note of the <i>tui</i>, interspersed now and again by the gurgle of the boiling"billy," were the only sounds. <i>Ibid.</i> 27 Stanton had to boil his billy of tea with a few lumps of kauri gum. <i>(1900,Auckland Weekly News,Xmas No. / 19</i>)

>  Thinking of going camping in Antarctica? If so, your number one kitchen task will be filling your billy with snow and melting it over a Primus. <i>(Feb 9, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  The milk was dispensed into billies by way of various dippers and payment made. <i>(2004,Onslow Historian,34(1/2): 4</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... a cake I have been making of Taro is baking; W. is kneading a damper in a tin pot; ... <i>(Apr 30, 1839,Journal,2/122</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> When I came to get my evening meal I found I had left my tin pot at Wangaroa ... I had only a quart pot left for all purposes cooking and washing ... <i>(Nov 4, 1839,Journal,161</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> In excursions of this nature [a journey in Auckland bush], the `menage' is necessarily very limited. A tin pot serves as tea-kettle and tea-pot, and each man carries a `pannikin' for a cup and drinking vessel, a few tin plates, etc. <i>(1847,Notes from Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes from Journal in 1959 Taylor Early Travels 119]

>  <b>GODLEY</b> We hung [on three flax-sticks] just such a tin can and cover as one of your Welsh women ... would bring for broth [and made tea in it] <i>(Nov 1850,Letters,143,1951 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Canterbury)]

>  [On equipping soldiers for the bush] ...each man will carry a hatchet, a tomahawk, a strong jack-knife, a tin pot, his cloak rolled, a new oilskin haversack, ... and a tin pot to boil his tea in a bivouc [sic]. ... With his knife he will tripod a few skewers over the fire, and hang his pot thereon with stew, or soup, or tea; ... <i>(Jan 19, 1861,Otago Witness,Supplement 2</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> Tin pots of water dipped up from a neighbouring creek are stuck against the quickly-dying flames ...] <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,185</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND</b> At the Five-mile diggers carried the gold dust to Okarito to sell in billies, their ordinary chamois leather gold-bags being too small to contain their rich harvests. <i>(1906,Life & Work of Seddon,12</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> First of all, he [sc. a swaggy] had to have a good tea billy. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,71</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> The billy was ninety-nine times out of a hundred an old 'cocky's joy' tin - the old golden syrup or treacle tin was the ideal brewing billy. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,71</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Kevin had managed to light a fire and was poking sticks under the billy, one-handed. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,188</i>)

>  <b>PRINCE</b> Having spent an hour ... on the Pass, and after a `billy' of tea ... the journey was resumed ... <i>(1889,Diary of a Trip,9</i>) [NOTES: [nb quotes]]

>  <b>ELKINGTON</b> Each swagman carries his "billy" (a tin can for boiling tea) and a "tucker-bag," in which he keeps his lunch. <i>(1906,Adrift in NZ,127</i>)

>  Another filled the "billy" and got out the tea and sugar. <i>(Feb 12, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  He made a billy of tea and cooked a flapjack in the camp oven. <i>(Feb 12, 1924,Otago Witness,73</i>)

>  Again the billy is boiled and after tea we set our faces homeward. <i>(Dec 18, 1926,NZ Dairy Exporter,39</i>)

>  <b>HEBERLEY</b> [We] boiled the Billy and made some Tea out of tawa bark. <i>(1839,Autobiography,87</i>)

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> We must needs purchase a `billy' (a tin pot for boiling tea, coffee, meat or anything you may have the luck to get) ... <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,63</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> We are now existing in the manner of savages, boiling our flesh or fowl in our tea-can (called a <i>billy</i>) <i>(1858,Reconnaissance Survey  S.Otago</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Thomson Reconnaissance Survey Southern Otago in 1959 Taylor Early Travels 33]

>  We picked up enough wood to boil a small billy <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and ReSources,36</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> It is true that there were large tins of boeuf bouilli containing not very palatable boiled beef, with a good deal of liquid, which were obtainable and used for ships stores. The contents were commonly called soup and bully, and the cannisters bully cans, from which the popular name of `billy' for a tin pitcher is undoubtedly derived. [Bathgate is possibly following Morris's <i>Austral English</i> surmise.] <i>(1925,Random Recollections,12</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection),]

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> This billy is an article much like an outsize in preserved-peach cans; indeed its real name is billy-can. It has a wire handle on it as a bucket has, and a lid as a saucepan has. ... The billy is found in almost every home throughout Australia and New Zealand. Where milk is not delivered in bottles, the billy is `put out' at night to receive the morning supply. <i>(1945,If You'd Care to Know,99</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> Monday, got in stores at the following rates and pitched tent. Flour 9d. tea 4/6 a good handful ... Pick 15/6 Shovel 10/- Tin dish 6/- Billy 3/6 small swing pot for boiling <i>(Jan 8, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>) [NOTES: (Dunstan Diggings)]

>  <b>PASCOE</b> It was then arranged what else we would take with us, viz., a hatchet, a billy for tea, no tent ..., panikins, and some tea; ... <i>(1960,Over the Whitcombe Pass,25</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Lauper in 1960 Pascoe (ed) Over the Whitcombe Pass 25]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> On to other D's, with which his saddle was amply provided, he strapped his Erewhonian boots, a tin pannikin, and a billy that would hold about a quart. I should, perhaps, explain to English readers that a billy is a tin can, the name for which (doubtless of French-Canadian origin) is derived from the words `<i>faire bouillir</i>.' frying pan to cook his flapjacks. <i>(1901,Erewhon Revisited,ch ii [Everyman edn 1932,(1960) 207], ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> I'll send the kid down with the things and a billy of milk. Shall I knock up a few extry scones to take with you ter-morrow? c1920s Eliza McKenzie in <i>Outlook</i> in 1975 Knight Poyntzfield 20 [reference Wellington 1850s] Meantime the Mothers had again boiled the `tin can', (the word billy was not used then[sc Wellington, 1850s]) which did duty for a kettle as well, as a tea pot, ... . <i>(1912,Stories,112,(1984 Alpers) ed.</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> A new chum, of course very silly,/ ... We're told lost the run of his billy,/ <i>(1864,Songs of the War,6</i>)

>  <b>BURGESS</b> We also killed a young cock fowl, which we stewed in the billy. <i>(1866,Confessions,112,19i83 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CHEVALIER</b> ... the billey (a tin pot for boiling the water) and three pannikins) (tin cups). <i>(1866,Reminiscences of a Journey,10</i>)

>  Our domestic economy was at first unique, the cooking gear comprised a couple of three-legged iron pots called go-ashores, ditto cans named billy's, a frying-pan ... <i>(1870,Tale of NZ,32</i>) [NOTES: [pioneer Otago run c1848-1849]]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> ... every packer [c1850] generally provided himself with a tin pot (billy), so the billy had to be filled with wheat and boiled for food. <i>(1871,Autobiography,44</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... to earn a `damper and a billy of tea' by hard work is better than to eat the bread of idleness at the expence of his friends ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,viii</i>)

>  [A boatman of Waitaroa, West Coast of Fiordland] says he heaved the eggs away `because they would not go in the billy.' <i>(1872,TrNZI,5/196</i>) [NOTES: (Potts)]

>  <b>WOOD</b> Well ... the chief thing in my recollection is the billy - a large, black, strong one it was - that gave us tea when we were thirsty, and food when we were hungry; ... <i>(1875,Waiting for the Mail,30</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a billy goat, used in NZ (from 1849 and earlier in Cook?) as elsewhere in English; (See [billy goat](../B#billy-goat))

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Chaffey came over one day to Les Waghorn's dad saying: `I've brought you over a bit of young billy.' <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,81</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>26/620</b> ... its buttressed roots offer a too-convenient place for the road-contractor to `sling his billy,' ... <i>(1894,TrNZI</i>)

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> The billy he would sling afresh./ That they might have .../ `A snack before they went to bed'. <i>(1883,Castle Gay,178</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> [[Poss. move this to boil: or keep here to emphasise the connection between <i>billy</i> and <i>boil</i>.] 1839 Heberly [see 1. above]]

>  <b>PEACHE</b> There was plenty of scrub about so we soon had a fire going and boiled our `billy'. <i>(Dec 26, 1875,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1875 Dec 26 Peache Jnl in 1970 Gray Quiet with the Hills 38]

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> He gathered sticks and boiled the billy, while Elizabeth Mary unpacked the luncheon. <i>(1935,Little Country, The,290,1937 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> We got in rather late, ... it was as much as Bill could do to bake some bread, cook some bacon and boil the billy while I dug a trench around the tent ... <i>(Jan 19, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>) [NOTES: (Central Otago)]

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> We boiled the billy, and had breakfast. <i>(1870,Martin's Bay Settlement,27</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> We ... set to work to boil the billy as quickly as possible. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,37</i>)

>  <b>STUD</b> ... one of them should knock off ten minutes before time to boil the billy. <i>(1985,Coldstream,100</i>) [NOTES: 1909 Mar 13 in Studholme Coldstream (1985) 100]

>  <b>PFAFF</b> There was no firewood to be had, so we boiled our billy on a long stick we carried with us. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,115</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> He moved on till he found a safe place to make a fire to boil the billy for tea. <i>(1925,Allen Adair,91,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> [Speargrass] was of no use as a rule, but when dead its stalks were useful to boil the billy. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,120</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> There are now several bonnie burns coming down the mountain sides to the river, the largest of which are the famous Roaring Meg and Gentle Annie; and a visitor ... may wash his colours of gold from almost anywhere while his wee wife boils the billy. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,20</i>)

>  <i>billy </i>... 3. <i>boil the billy. Austral. inf.</i> to make tea. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,105</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 78; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 48],]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ROGERS</b> [express <i>billy's boiling</i>, it's smoko time, time for a cup of tea] `Looks like the billy's boiling,' Broker said, nodding toward the kitchen ... `Come on mate. Put your knittin' away.' <i>(1960,Long White Cloud, The,12</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> [Check cits. at billy tea [?black b. t. ?made in a balck billy??], now x-ref only at black billy.]

>  Oh you [bush-felling] contractors in black singlets and bowyangs, fuelled on spuds and mutton and pumpkin and rice and spotted dog or deadman's arm and black billy tea: perfect toast sopping with butter and Cocky's Joy (treacle): come into our art galleries, onto our stamps, wrap within songs, ... <i>(Feb 3, 1985,NZ Times,5</i>) [NOTES: (Jim Henderson)]

>  Heavily swagged diggers and laden horses whose swags consist of all the household goods of a mining party, from the black billy which boils the tea, to the tom cat which kills the rats and mice. <i>(Apr 16, 1864,Otago Witness</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Apr 16 Otago Witness in 1976 Veitch Clyde on the Dunstan 29-30]

>  <b>CRUMPTON</b> He awoke about midday and called loudly for a crack o' the whip, but when told he had made short work of it the night before, demanded a quart of black billy tea, ... <i>(1979,Spencer: the Gold Seeker,43</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> Black billy tea, boy,/ Black as it can be,/ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,6</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SEVERINSEN</b> Our evening meal was a sparse affair consisting of copious supplies of strong black billy tea and little else. <i>(1962,Hunter Climb High,23</i>)

### Billy Boy
 
 <i>n.</i> a clumsy ship ?? [recorded earliest in NZ]

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> On awaking in the morning we were disappointed to find a light head wind blowing, and a number of ships of all sorts and sizes, from the noble `East Indiaman' to the clumsy `Billy boy,' wind-bound like ourselves; ... <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,6</i>)

### billy boy
