 <i>n.</i> a boy, or cadet, who carries, the billy, makes tea, and does the rouseabout work for field parties, shearing gangs, etc.

>  Starting as a billy-boy, he worked at just about every job in the next 3 and a half years - on the skids, loading timber; breaking down logs in the sawmill; driving logging trucks, a TD18 dozer and chainsaw felling of native trees. <i>(Aug 27, 1982,Dominion,8</i>)

>  The only point I want to make is that my experience of mountains is lengthy although it may not be deep - billy boy to the botanists of the `twenties', mountaineering and field collecting for geologists in the `thirties'. <i>(1960,Report of Ninth Science Congress,144</i>) [NOTES: 1960 Report of Ninth Science Congress May 12-17: Proceedings of Royal Soc. of NZ 144]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> As the "billy boy," it was my job to get to camp before dark, light the fire and start the dinner. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,60</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) 1. a worker who mixes and distributes billies of burgoo or Ship's Lime to harvesting teams, working in hot, dry conditions [1850s to 1930s]. 2. a person who collects/delivers smoko materials or makes tea in shearing sheds. (See [billy time](../B#billy-time))

>  Here you find boys of scarce fourteen eager to take on a job as billy-boy, a by no means enviable task. <i>(May 1909,NZ Farmer,338</i>)

>  <b>COULSON</b> Cutting grass seed was dry work, and there was a regular break for a drink. It fell to the lot of the Billy Boy to provide this. 2. <i>(1979,Golden Harvest,37</i>)

>  <b>ALLEN</b> I was fleeco, billy-boy and rouseabout. <i>(1980,Farm on the Wanganui River, A,39</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> As the billy-boy, it was my job to get back to camp before dark, get the water, light the fire and start the dinner. [billy boy] <i>(1991,Off the Sheep's Back,60</i>)

### billy bread
 
 <i>n.</i> bread baked in a billy over a fire, bread baked orig. in a billy using it as a kind of camp oven (See [cf. billy loaf](../B#billy-loaf))

>  <b>McKAY</b> <i>BROWN BILLY BREAD </i>wheatmeal ... baking powder ... salt ... treacle ... cream and milk ... Let the mixture rise in billy ten minutes (on the rack) before baking. <i>(1929,Practical Home Cookery,176</i>)

>  <i>BILLY BREAD</i> ... flour ... wheatmeal ... salt ... B.P. ... golden syrup ... milk. Mix ... into a wet dough, grease and flour the billy, bake for 2 hours with lid on. <i>(1934,Marigold Book of Recipes, The,35</i>)

>  Regarding the usage ... `billy bread or loaf' you can use either, some say `billy bread'. I have made it for years past. <i>(1950,Letter</i>) [NOTES: ca1950 Letter from Wanganui]

### billy brew
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Brian occasionally made a billy brew but mostly there was thermos tea and a loaf of white bread, so soft. <i>(Feb 4, 1999,Dominion,11</i>)

### billy cake
 
 <i>n.</i> a cake baked in a billy (See [cf. billy sponge](../B#billy-sponge))

>  <i>BILLY CAKE</i> ... flour ... sugar ... butter ... sultanas or currants ... baking powder ... milk ... egg ... place in billy, and bake ... until cooked. <i>(1924,Help the Babies Cookery Book,113</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> `Pooh! I'd rather have billy cake with raspberry jam. Scones aren't very special; we often have them.' <i>(1948,Dusty Road,56,1949 ed.</i>)

### billy can
 
 <i>n.</i> a billy (See [billy](../B#billy))

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> A request for water to fill his billy-can. <i>(1907,Return of Joe,13</i>)

>  <b>DARE</b> ... I had to carry down to the woolshed an enormous billy can of tea ... <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,20</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Or even back in Forli in the billet drinking plonk from chipped mugs with Mick pouring it out from the section [93] billycan. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,92</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Quite a few were present ... and Jim, with Mollie, ... sauntered off together, armed with billy cans and baskets [to gather blackberries]. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,125</i>)

>  <b>HOGAN</b> Days of Billy-can and Pikau,/ Horse and Buggy, sleet and snow,/ Railway camp and crowded `Boozer',/ <i>(1962,Billy-Can Ballads,1</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> Wherever men worked or played ... they brewed tea. And why call it a billycan? I have never found a satisfactory answer to that one. <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,6</i>)

>  <b>TRAIL</b> `Where is the billy-can and the gooseberries?' our mother asked. <i>(1984,Child of the Arrow,96</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> The shrapnel could be pretty thick ... once we got a shower of it just when we were brewing up tea. It riddled our billy-can and we lost all our tea. <i>(1988,Voices of Gallipoli,39</i>)

>  <b>HUDSON</b> ... watching the billycan boil. <i>(1908,Song of the Manly Men,28</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> Here, in the summer, we would go with our billies and pull the soft, reluctant fruit off the bushes putting one in the can for every three we ate. ... One afternoon ... four of us ... took our billycans to the mudflats and began to fill them. <i>(1981,Solo,121</i>) [NOTES: `Not Christmas']

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> This billy is an article much like an outsize in preserved-peach cans; indeed, its real name is billy-can. <i>(1945,If You'd Care to Know,99</i>)

>  In many suburbs billy cans are still used for deliveries, the milk being ladled out of a container that is open to germs and dust. <i>(May 27, 1951,Here & Now,1</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> Mr.McParland sent to Dunedin for 100 billy cans, but ... abbreviated by writing 100 billies, first boat. The boat duly arrived with the 100 billies, but ... they turned out to be goats. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,113</i>)

### billy fire
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It may be pointed out that in the matter of billy-boiling along roadsides, drovers are a class apart and there is little prospect of suppressing what has been a universal custom, since the earliest days of settlement ... Permission to light billy-fires should be granted. <i>(Sep 23, 1947,Official Advice Note: Forest Service</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> ... a cluster of stones by a bushland river still charred by a billyfire of long ago, ... <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,240,1963 ed.</i>)

### billy goat
 
 <i>n.</i> a male goat

>  <b>WAITE</b> You could not do better than bring the cattle and some rabbits, another billy goat, etc. <i>(Jul 6, 1840,Port Molyneux,12</i>) [NOTES: 1840 July 6 T.W. Bessant in 1940 Waite Port Molyneux 12]

### billy hook
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PICKERING</b> Fires would have been substantial and built with a dry-walling technique that wouldn't have disgraced a craftsman. Strong Y-supports were set in the ground either side of the fire and from the cross bar would hang billy-hooks, which everyone carried. <i>(1988,Hills, The,80</i>)

### Billy Lid
 
 <i>n.</i> a kid, a child [ORIGIN: rhyming slang, (prison use)]

>  There are many examples of rhyming slang in use at Paparua:..<i>Billy Lid</i> for a kid (child). <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 2</i>)

### billy lid
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Oh, the days of Hughey's tribute and the doings that they did! You had to drink your grog those times from out a billy lid. <i>(1985,Penguin Book of NZ Verse,93</i>) [NOTES: c1890-1900 Edwin Edwards `Waitekauri Every Time' in 1985 Penguin Book of NZ Verse 93]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Often I was driven to frying myself a steak in the billy-lid. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,46</i>)

>  <b>MUELLER</b> We have no plates - only pannikins, so I ate my stew out of one, while Price had a billy lid for a plate, ... <i>(Oct 1, 1865,My Dear Bannie,55,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WOOD</b> ... some other facetious person had crowned him with a billy lid. <i>(1875,Waiting for the Mail,23</i>)

### billy loaf
 
 <i>n.</i> home-made bread baked in a billy; billy bread. [ca1900]

>  <i>BILLY CURRANT LOAF </i>... flour ... sugar ... Edmonds' Baking Powder ... 1 egg ... butter ... milk ... salt ... Bake 1 hour not removing the lid till cooked. <i>(1916,Edmonds Cookery Book,8,4th ed.</i>)

>  <i>BILLY LOAF</i> ... flour ... wheatmeal ... goldem syrup ... salt ... baking powder ... Bake in No. 3 billy with lid on. <i>(1924,Help the Babies Cookery Book,90</i>)

>  <i>WHEATMEAL BILLY LOAF</i> ... wheatmeal ... salt ... golden syrup ... `Silverdust' flour ... baking powder ... milk ... Can be baked in baking powder tins, well greased, and with lids on. <i>(1929,Ideal Cookery Book,135</i>)

>  Regarding the usage ... `billy bread or loaf' you can use either, some say `billy bread'. I have made it for years past. <i>(1950,Letter</i>) [NOTES: ca1950 Letter from Wanganui]

### billy milk
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  In New Zealand about 85 per cent. of all town milk sold in urban areas is pasteurised. ... Too much `billy' milk is still sold. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,306</i>)

### billy sponge
 
 <i>n.</i> originally made in a billy (See [cf. billy cake](../B#billy-cake))

>  <b>McCREDIE</b> BILLY SPONGE CAKE ... Have ready a 3-quart billy with buttered paper; cook with lid on 1 1/2 hours. Do not have fire too fierce and do not take lid off till the cake comes out. <i>(1915,Bungalow Recipe Book, The,67</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> <i>BILLY SPONGE</i>. Four eggs, 1/2 lb. sugar, 3/4 lb. flour, 1/2 teaspoon baking powder, few drops of rosewater. ... Bake in a bread tin. <i>(1915,Amuri Cookery Book,116,1927 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FINLAY</b> BILLY SPONGE. Ingredients:- 4ozs. Flour 8ozs.Sugar 4 Eggs No.2 Billy. Time, about 3/4-hour. <i>(1933,Cookery,123 No. 442</i>)

>  <i>BILLY SPONGE</i> ... eggs ... sugar ... flour ... put in a greased billy sprinkled with sugar, bake ... not removing the lid of the billy. <i>(1916,Edmonds Cookery Book,28,4th ed.</i>)

>  <b>CHERRILL</b> And in true New Zealand style, I suppose I had better send her the recipe of a billy-sponge. <i>(1950,Sheepfarm,106</i>)

>  <b>R MASON</b> A sponge cake of a particular shape and type. Not made with the usual sponge-cake mixture'. <i>(1954,Personal Communication</i>)

>  Billy Sponge. 4 eggs, 8 oz. sugar, 4 oz. flour, pinch of salt. Whisk the eggs. Gradually beat in the sugar. Pour into a greased and floured billy or loaf tin. Sprinkle with castor sugar. Bake at 325[degree sign] for 3/4-1 hour. <i>(1964,Atlas Cookery Book,44</i>)

### billy tea
 
 <i>n.</i> tea made in a billy [--[Cf description in 1945 Simpson If You'd Care to Know 99-100 The billy is filled with water and suspended over a fire ... When the water boils, the tea is popped in, the lid is clamped down to confine the flavour, and the billy is removed from the fire. A few minutes is allowed for the brew to `draw', and there is your tea in the Australian bush fashion. Maybe there is no sugar in it, and of a certainty there is no milk or lemon; you take this liquor straight and you take it hot, although the temperature of the day may be a hundred in the shade.] W. Ramson: common in Aust., also a trade name; nf W3.] Tea made in a billy over a fire, tea made by tossing a handful of tea-leaves into a billy of water on <i>the boil (used often with the epithet</i> black,<i> either defining</i> tea from the strength of the tea made judged by its colour); or <i>billy</i> from its open-fire blackened appearance: cf [1864 Apr 16 Otago Witness in 1976 Veitch Clyde on the Dunstan 29-30 Heavily swagged diggers and laden horses whose swags consist of all the household goods of a mining party, from the black billy which boils the tea, to the tom cat which kills the rats and mice.]] (See [also boil the billy](../A#also-boil-the-billy))

>  <b>HYDE</b> Starkie ... was off again - not entirely discouraged, for he had eggs, bacon, and billy tea in his stomach and [pounds]26 in his pocket. <i>Ibid.</i> 70 Rannock fed his prison labour on honest bush food: fresh meat, billy tea, enormous flat damper scones cooked on the three-legged camp oven. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,50</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> The workmen were having a spell ... The foreman nodded as we came near, squinting up at us from where he sat nursing a mug of black tea. `Goodday, mates. Like a mug of brew?' ... `Yeah, there's plenty in the billy.' <i>(1963,In the Sticks,84</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> But after a good strong brew of billy-tea I was able to carry on, ... <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,149</i>)

>  <b>HALEY</b> Red tea it used to be called ...[sic]somewhere. Billy tea. <i>(1986,Settlement, The,60</i>)

>  <b>SLIGO</b> The fire was going ... The black billy tea stewed. <i>(1987,Final Things,187</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> Of billy tea. Waiting until the billy boils, throwing in half a handful of tea, trying to hook the billy off the fire before it boils over, and tapping the side with a spoon to settle the leaves. Then demonstrating a touch of skill by pouring the tea over the billy handle and into your chipped enamel cup and swallowing the brew in hot blowy gulps. <i>(1988,Hills, The,8</i>)

>  Nowadays, musters are completed in days using helicopters and 4WDs. Hob-nailed boots, billy tea and camp ovens have been replaced by GPS locators, energy drinks and Vibram rubber soles. <i>(Feb 20, 2010,Your Weekend,8</i>)

>  With a cushion to our backs and a generous plate of good things on our knees and a cup of steaming hot "billy tea" we feel it is indeed a happy and restful Christmas Day... Again the billy is boiled and after tea we set our faces homeward. <i>(Dec 18, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,39</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> In a few minutes we were grouped about, sitting and standing, lunching off girdle scones, current cakes, pineapple, and 'billy tea'. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,106</i>)

>  <i>BILLY TEA</i> A clean billy, good water that has percolated through pumice ... Have ready a white linen rag in which is placed 1/4 oz of tea, loosely tied ... Let the water simmer ... take out the bag. Drink without milk ... <i>(1906,Tetaka Kai: A Book of Well Tried Recipes,138</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tea ... Billy-tea</i>, or <i>Bush-tea.</i> Tea made in a <i>billy</i> (q.v.) There is a belief that in order to bring out the full flavour it should be stirred with a gum-stick.<i> New Zealand tea</i>. Tea made of the leaves of the <i>manuka </i>(q.v.). See <i>Tea-tree. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,462</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> The spuds and meat were nicely done,/ The billy tea was made. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,27</i>) [NOTES: (The Duff)]

>  Pigeon and kaka stew, ... and ... several large pannikins each of choice billy tea. <i>(Sep 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,408</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The billy tea was soon ready. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,164</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> She thought Hugh's boyish enthusiasm for billy tea delicious ... <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,320</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> Pannikin after pannikin of `billy tea.' <i>(1936,Hunted, The,169</i>)

>  <b>MYERS</b> `Come down ... where we'll be clearing the gorse this afternoon, Tara.' `All right! and I'll bring `Billy' tea. ...' <i>(1941,Valiant Love,33</i>)

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> Again we drank our billy tea and talked of books. <i>(1950,Otago Interval,104</i>)

>  <b>CHERRILL</b> Is that what you call `billy tea'? She had watched him throwing in tea by the fistful. <i>(1950,Sheepfarm,54</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> We had been in the habit of getting water for the billy tea at a little waterfall above a mountain pool, in which there swam a lordly and and outsize kokopu, a little more than a foot long. <i>(1952,Faring South,186</i>)

>  <b>JOSEPH</b> We baked our own bread in a camp-oven, and lived mainly on that and fish and billy-tea. <i>(1962,Pound of Saffron, A,12</i>)

>  He built a fire and showed us how to make `billy tea', so the thermos flasks were left untouched. <i>(1962,Landfall Country,77</i>) [NOTES: Stead `A Race Apart']

>  <b>SEVERINSEN</b> Our evening meal was a sparse affair consisting of copious supplies of strong black billy tea and little else. <i>(1962,Hunter Climb High,23</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> ... we sat ... outside the hut and enjoyed our billy-tea and thick sandwiches. <i>(1965,Skippers Road,25</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> `Years since what?' Hubert repeated. `Since I had a mug of billy tea. Just smell it, will you? Nothing to beat it. Billy tea boiled over manuka.' <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,139</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> Porridge was eaten with treacle and the billy tea was well sweetened with sugar - ... <i>Ibid.</i> 108 Fill [a treacle tin] with water, punch a hole in the lid, hang it on a pot hook in the chimney or on a stick over a fire in the paddock, drop in the tea when boiling ... This is `Billy Tea'.(?) <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,41</i>)

>  <i>billy-tea</i>, tea made in a billy [Fr. <i>bouiller</i>, to boil]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> His technique for making billy tea was correct. He waited for the water to boil, then dropped into it a little less tea than would be used in a teapot and allowed it to boil just briefly before taking the billy off the fire. <i>(1970,Way Up North,106</i>)

>  <b>ROWAN</b> We boiled the kettle [and] made some `Billy' tea. <i>(1898,Flower Hunter,247</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> They drank their `billy' tea. <i>(1914,Sale of Timbertown,83</i>)

>  <b>BRUCE</b> After a breakfast of chops, bread and billy tea, we drafted up our different lots of sheep. <i>(1977,Life in the Hinterland of the South Island,31</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> We supplemented this fare with all manner of pies and cakes, `washed down with billy tea' to quote the boys. <i>(1982,Myrtle and Sophia,17</i>)

>  <b>HOLCROFT</b> No tea ... tasted like billy tea, the water boiled over a fire of manuka cut from the scrub with dew still on the branches. <i>(1984,Way of a Writer, The,56</i>)

### billy time
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a call made by a harvesting boss to indicate that a billy boy's services are needed.

>  <b>COULSON</b> When the boss called 'Billy time' the boy would have to trudge down the hill to where the billy had been left in the shade, carry it up again, and go along the line of reapers. <i>(1979,Golden Harvest,37</i>)

### billy tin
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also tin billy](../A#also-tin-billy))

>  <b>MYERS</b> ... she was on her way across the paddocks, carrying [35] the `Billy'-tin and the basket of good things for tea. <i>(1941,Valiant Love,34</i>) [NOTES: [Mrs Myers spells always with a cap B., probably mistakenly thinking thinking it derived from the proper noun, Billy.]]

### billy-be-damned
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> A cook once said to the manager, `I wish you would speak to that there bullocky. He walks about with as much sangfroid as billy-be-damned. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,75</i>)

### billy-boiled
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> The sole comforts of this miserable night were boiled ducks and billy-boiled tea, unless one might add that the soaking clothes ... were dried ... by frost. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,54</i>)

### billy-boiling
 
 <i>n.</i> [See boil(ing) for phrases keep the billy boiling, billy boils over, boil the billy, etc.]

>  <b>COWAN</b> Grateful were the spells for rest and smoke and the midday billy-boiling half-hour; more grateful still the sundown halt for rest ... <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,10</i>)

>  It may be pointed out that in the matter of billy-boiling along roadsides, drovers are a class apart and there is little prospect of suppressing what has been a universal custom, since the earliest days of settlement ... Permission to light billy-fires should be granted. <i>(Sep 23, 1947,Official Advice Note: Forest Service</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> And so remembers all a lively store/ Of billy-boilings to the evening star;/ outdoor tea-making <i>(1951,Swagger Jack,19</i>) [NOTES: [x-ref only at BOIL]]

### billy-can farm
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small, subsistence dairy farm.

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> The 1939-40 era of the numerous small dairy farms, some being little more than Billy-can farms, is also gone. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,iv</i>)

### billy-made
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>SCOTT</b> Ned Ryan and Pat McPhee.. Sat down to Christmas dinner of pork, doughboys and billy-made tea. <i>(1900,Tales of the Colonial Turf,230</i>)

### billy-o
 
 <i>n.</i> intens. an action: `he went like billy-oh and couldn't be caught', go to billy-o(h)!, go to hell! [->ca1900-1910 W.H.B. Orsman, used as a child; 'like billy-o' is general English]

>  <b>HUNT</b> One night our house burned right down, way to billio up there. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,78</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> He's typical of the modern generation. I'm all right Jack and to Billy-oh with responsibility. <i>(1981,Fish Factory,83</i>)

### billycart
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's trolley or go-cart, (?obs.) a trolley, a boys' go-cart, push-cart.

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Billy carts were absolutely essential for a boy. Originally this was a box - butterbox or slightly larger - set on an axle and with, preferably, a pair of high wheels off a discarded pram, about an 18-to-20-inch diameter, and having long shafts. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,269</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> And then we had [c1913] a little `billy-cart' - a box with a couple of wheels on it and a shaft - <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,101</i>) [NOTES: (George Davies, Wellington)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GIBBONS</b> Then we had to catch the billy cart... <i>Ibid.</i> 21We had several billy carts in our time, all made from benzine boxes. <i>(1989,Recollections (various authors/titles),17</i>)

>  [Caption] We had a little 'billy-cart' - a box with a couple of wheels on it and a shaft - and we used to go down to the wharf when the coal boats were coming in, and take a length of four-by-two with us <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,24</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>billycart</i> trolley home-made from cannibalised prams and leftover bits of lumber, possibly once associated with carts pulled by billy goats. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,15</i>)

### billycock
 
 <i>n.</i> an old word for a bowler hat, any of various low round-crowned hats worn by men (occas. used of a `bowler' hat (contrast ? hard-hitter'); -also billycock hat

>  <b>FARJEON</b> All are alike attired in rough jackets, moleskin trousers, and billy-cock hats. <i>(1865,Shadows on the Snow,61</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> I levied contribution on a publican for a billy-cock-hat. <i>(1866,Twenty-Five Years in NZ,21</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> Only cherubs don't wear `billy-cock' hats, nor carry `swags', as a general rule. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/iii/14,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>SKEY</b> So my head-gear I'll have a billycock hat. ... Now this your stiff belltoppers won't do,/ One kick and their day is done;/ So the billycock soft my looks shall renew,/ And let off my innocent fun. <i>(1889,Pirate Chief and the Mummy's Complaint..., The,48</i>) [NOTES: (`On the Choice of a Hat')]

>  <b>COTTLE</b> ... billy-cock hat <i>(1890,Frank Melton's Luck,32</i>)

>  She saw a man standing at the foot of the bed, dressed in a check coat and a billycock (or bowler) hat. <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,77</i>) [NOTES: [possibly Jenkins's error]]

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> Hunting goats. Robin shot a Billy. <i>(1849,Journal,281</i>) [NOTES: (?137 OED2 check)]

### billyful
 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1938 Havelock (Jane Morison)]

>  I myself put away nearly a whole `billy' full of new potatoes. <i>(1881,TrNZI</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN & FENWICK</b> We had a hurried lunch and a billyful of tea. <i>(1892,Holiday Trip to the Catlins District, A,14</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> `Here you are, old fellow,' said my father coming through the stunted manuka scrub with a billy-ful of spring water. <i>(1981,Black Beech & Honeydew,86</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> ... Mr. Burnett went down for a billy-full of water ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,14</i>)

>  Nothing but one loaf of bread left, could hardly get firewood enough to boil one billy full of tea. Met some mourays who told us of the new digins. <i>(May 9, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,37,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MUELLER</b> I also turned out Bob and ordered him to boil a billy-full of tea. <i>(May 22, 1866,My Dear Bannie,174,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMSON</b> Gathered a billyful of mussels, which we had for supper. [chk] <i>(1866,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1866 Williamson Diary in 1870 Whitworth Martin's Bay Settlement 23]

>  <b>MONEY</b> Here was a grand field for our sharpened appetites, and billy-ful after billy-ful did we consume of the raw fish with great gusto. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,62</i>)

### bin
 
 <i>n.</i> a wool-bin [main def. there].

>  The T wing of the shed, where the wool press and bins are located.. has a large cellar underneath, into which the daggs and such of the wool as requires handpicking is passed through the hatchway in the floor above. <i>(Sep 16, 1890,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Meanwhile the `pickers up' were busy gathering up the fleeces as they fell from the bereft sheep and carrying them to the sorting table, where they [173] were stripped of the `pieces', which were thrown aside, while the best of the fleece was quickly classified, rolled together, and deposited in a kind of bin, according to its quality, whether `clothing' or `combing.' <i>(1881,Waitaruna,172</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> At the main entrance of the shearing-shed, which is a long building with pens down the centre, and an outlet at each end, stands a wool-press, and behind this are the bins into which the fleeces, after having been classed, are placed preparatory to being pressed. <i>Ibid.</i> 244 Meanwhile the shorn fleeces are carried to a large table in the wool-shed by a man appropriately named `Fleecy', and are spread out by four wool-pickers, who tear away the bad parts and fold the fleeces square, passing them to the classer, who, in turn, after a rapid examination hands them on to his attendant, and he places them in their respective bins according to their quality. <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,242</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> No more mighty fleeces crown the bins like drifted snow,/ <i>(1897,Station Ballads,46,1945 ed.</i>) [NOTES: `Arlington']

>  <b>CARR</b> Dag Cutting. - The wool rollers take off the dirty portion of the fleece and throw it into a bin, the dag cutter ... with a pair of shears cuts off all the wool he can, which he throws into another bin. It is then called `second pieces'. <i>(1913,Country Work,15</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> On no account must a man go to the bin of fleeces and, taking up as many as he can gather in his arms, rush to the press putting the whole into the bale in a confused mass. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,124</i>)

>  <i>(1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland, see wool bin]

>  <i>Bin</i>: Receptacle for holding loose wool prior to pressing in either a shearing shed or woolstore. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,52</i>)

>  <i>bin ... NZ & Austral.</i> receptacle for wool in wool-shed. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,69</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> A steam whistle ... blew for the commencement and finish of each run. The wool tables for rolling and skirting were in the centre of the woolboard. Close by stood the classer's table, where he sat and classed each fleece before it was carried by a shed hand to its respective bin. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,113</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> [Fleeces] laid on the tables before the wool-sorters ... who pronounced ... to which bin they belonged. <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,33,1874 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large open structure used to store fertiliser for topdressing at a farm airstrip.

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> Landing strips and top-dressing bins were built in all sorts of unusual places in the hills and on the downs and terraces. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,200</i>)

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> There's an airstrip and bin where we rode long ago. <i>(2000,Country Bloke and Other Verse, The,228</i>)

>  Hill country unit auction .. concrete killing house, sheep and cattle yards and airstrip with 49 tonne fertiliser bin. [fertiliser bin] <i>(Jul 26, 2002,Dominion Post,C7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an ore hopper

>  <i>Bins</i>: Ore hoppers. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),312</i>) [NOTES: [nf spec. OED2]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a chilly-bin

>  The security man at the gate inspected the bin and took away their grog. I can understand their stopping young men toting a couple of dozen coldies on their back, but two respectable women? What could they possibly do? As they were able to buy two cans inside the Park at inflated prices, I wonder where avarice comes in and commonsense goes out the window? <i>(Mar 23, 1983,NZ Truth,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a very large open container for rubbish and spoil, designed to be picked up by a special truck. Also called a skip; a dump-bin

>  As the skinny joker from/ the centre curses the thing/ into gear and collects/ a dump bin on his way/ to temporary safety <i>(Nov 30, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>) [NOTES: (Beyer)]



 
 <i>n.</i> the bin, the mental hospital. .



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high open structure in a woolshed where graded wool is placed awaiting baling.

>  Doggedly, the shearers went back to the boards ... <i>(May 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,86</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> The shed had accommodation for twenty-five shearers, but at a pinch room could be found for twenty-seven on the board. <i>(1952,History of Northern Southland,95</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> All along the 'board' are doors into the sheep pens - catching pens they call them - and opposite them are other doors called portholes, leading out of the shed into counting-out pens. <i>(1954,On the Hill,11</i>)

>  A mellow finishing touch inside has resulted from using varnished taraire on the wall and gates facing the board. <i>(Nov 17, 1975,NZ Farmer,14</i>)

>  <b>CAIRD</b> Immediately behind the [wool] tables come the bins labelled for the different sorts of wool. <i>(1874,Notes on Sheepfarming in NZ,23</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> At the main entrance of the shearing shed .. stands a wool-press and behind this are the bins ... <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,242</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Put up two temporary wool bins in the woolshed for the bellies and pieces, then started to plane boards to go round the bottom of the wooltable. <i>(Oct 14, 1914,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> We picked the locks and stains, we filled the tins and swished the bins ... <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,94</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> Bins for holding wool after it has been skirted and classed are of two types, fixed and movable ... <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,353</i>)

>  <b>DOMINY</b> Fleeces are expertly folded and tossed into 'bins'. In addition to the main line of fleece, there are bins and bales for belly wool, topknots ... <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,190</i>)

### bin bale
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a woolshed collection of wool containing a mixture of types, such as skirtings, crutchings, odd fleeces etc.

>  <b>SQUIRE</b> bin bale - a bale of wool of mixed contents from a grower. <i>(1991,A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms,10</i>)

>  <b>WOOLPRO</b> Bin bale - a woolgrower's mixed bale from which each type of wool will be taken and placed in the brokers' bins with similar types from other bin bales received from other woolgrowers. <i>(2001,Woolpro Glossary of Wool Terms,4</i>)

### bin boy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A series of unskilled, low-paid jobs followed [for her] ... as bin boy in a woolstore, ... <i>(Apr 1987,More Magazine,43</i>)

### bin room
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  When a clip is received by the store for classing, all the fleece wool goes to the fleece classing-room which in most stores is on a different floor from the `bin room' where all the pieces and oddments are sent. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,711</i>)

### binder
 
 <i>n.</i> a solid meal, or a meal after some fast e.g. a cooked breakfast). [[P: a (good, satisfying) meal: NZers': 20C. OED2 9. A large quantity, esp. of food; a satisfying meal. Dial and NZ coll.: 1881 Isle of Wight Words; cf EDD n2 Large quantity, esp. of food. IW, Hmp; nf W3]]

>  I was hungry so I turned my eyes away from the promising binder. <i>(Sep 19, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,55</i>)

>  If you hunger there is the `binder for a bob'; <i>(1925,Auckland Capping Book,13</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ...<i>binder</i>, a feed, because Army food is, inevitably at the front, somewhat constipating; <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> He absolutely dropped his bundle, and, to make matters worse, I had started off with a duck's breakfast, but I saw a cookhouse and decided to give it a pop for a binder. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Binder.</i> ... 2. A meal, esp. a good, satisfying one: NZders' 20C. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,54</i>)

>  [The traveller to New Zealand] might be invited to have `a good binder,' ... <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  I shouted him a bob dinner and I could tell by the way he ate he was in need of a binder. <i>(1943,Penguin New Writing,17/59</i>) [NOTES: Sargeson]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> I could tell by the way he ate he was in need of a binder. <i>(1946,That Summer,58</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Well, there she is, sir, and a pretty good binder, too. ... Curried sausages. ... [The cook] swore he'd give them some good manga to go off with. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,75</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He still talks of the binder I ate that day. ... [Glossary] Binder : a meal. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,93</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> No, he didn't want a feed, he said; he's just got up from an `almighty binder,' thanks just the same. <i>Ibid.</i> 113 Here's me, down and out and cadging a binder, and you, with tons of sugar and sitting right on top of the world - the two extremes, you might say.' <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,32,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HELMER</b> `This little hori's damned hungry,' Crump said lifting the tea billy from the single stick. `I could scoff a real binder, just quietly!' <i>(1964,Stag Party,80</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> binder: solid meal. <i>(1964,Collected Stories (Duggan),297</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> And the proper form and ceremony after having a few wets was to have a binder and they stumbled by instinct to the hash house ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,165</i>)

>  <b>McINNES</b> Skinny as a rake. You look as if you could do with a good binder. <i>(1969,Castle on the Run,122</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> So Jack scratched round in the kitchen and before long I [120] had my entrenching tools into a fine binder of bully fritters and fried tinned potatoes, ... <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,119</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> Better give your pony a binder and let her go in the horse paddock. <i>(1981,Sunrise,109</i>)

>  <i>binder ... NZ & Austral. sl.</i> a satisfying meal. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,69</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>binder</i> meal; originally tramp slang for a satisfying meal, associated in army with constipation. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,15</i>)

>  <b>SARGENT</b> The horses look in need of a decent binder. <i>(1944,Palms Bend Down,26</i>)

>  We kicked off at 12.30 with turkey, ham and vegetables, followed by some delicious plum pudding and real beer... Altogether, some "binder"! <i>(Jan 2, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,III/34-35 : 20</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large, 'square' meal, especially with a high meat content, served to farm and station workers.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> After my morning binder, camp duties would occupy me until perhaps 10 o'clock. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,131</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> We had a good binder - I remember Spot had made a duff. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,48</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He could dish up a fair sort of binder, old Flour Bags. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,87</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Judging by the binder she set in front of me, I'd say the men won't mind if they stay there another sixteen years. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,136</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS & TURNER</b> Meals are hoed into; real binders are expected and dished up. <i>(1983,NZ High Country Four Seasons,16</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Breakfasts are the usual rural binders of chops, mince, sausages - and eggs to accompany all of them. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,80</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> ?a special name for bushmen's binders.

>  <b>CARR</b> Stooking and Leading in. - The oat, wheat, and barley crops are ready ... Driving the binder requires experience. Men are wanted ... for stooking, i.e., picking up two sheaves and placing their heads together at an angle of about 45 degrees, 10 to 12 sheaves are generally put in one lot. ... There are ... good and bad stookers after years of experience. <i>(1913,Country Work,13</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> They suffered agony with backache and cramp [in learning to shear]. Each wore a wide red flannel `binder' to keep their back warm. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,55</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> wool fibres running more or less obliquely between two or more staples thereby holding a fleece of wool together.

>  <i>Binders</i>. Wool fibres running more or less obliquely between two or more staples thereby holding a fleece of wool together. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,52</i>)

### binder twine
 
 <i>n.</i> [The twine used in hay balers to tie up hay bales. This was also used by farmers as an even more flexible No. 8 wire for repairs and emergencies and for keeping up trousers as a substitute for belt or braces.]

>  You reach out a careless hand for the next five yards if binder-twine that came wrapped around Aunt Jemima's fruit cake. <i>(Oct 20, 1941,NZEF Times,I/17 : 6</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural, informal) a traditional farmer who is reliant on binder twine, having many and varied uses for it.

>  <b>BARNES</b> "Binder Twine Maxwell," they called him. He loved the stuff - used it around the farm, used it to tie his shoes, used it to keep his trousers up. <i>(1987,Country Calendar (TVNZ),18</i>)

### bindweed
 
 <i>n.</i> [OED2 1. The English name for the species of the N[atural] O[rder] <i>Convolvulus</i>; as Greater Bindweed (<i>C. sepium</i>), Lesser Bindweed (<i>C. arvensis</i>), Seaside Bindweed (<i>C. Soldanella</i>). 2. Applied dialectally or vaguely to various other climbing plants ... 3. <i>black, corn</i>, or<i> ivy bindweed</i> Polygonum Convolvulus; <i>Blue Bindweed </i>Bittersweet or Woody Nightshade. [17-18c.].] In NZ applied to various genera of climbing or .... plants, chiefly of the family Convolvulaceae, esp <i>Calystegia sepium </i> see also greater bindweed [check that this is still admitted]. [Note from ed. AND in response to query are there native bindweeds in Aust. 1986. Yes, three native (2 of which endemic) spp. of <i>Convolvulus</i>. However OED definition (`The English name for the species of the N[atural] O[rder] <i>Convolvulus'</i>) covers our use of the word, so we haven't needed to make an entry. (1 quot only Mitchell 1848; 1 fig 1965).] (See [convolvulus](../C#convolvulus))



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> sea bindweed, shore bindweed

>  Cockayne Report on Dune-areas <i>(1911,AJHR,C-13</i>)

>  <i>bindweed, sea [=] shore bindweed</i> [s] <i>... bindweed, shore<i> </i> [s] <i>Calystegia soldanella</i> (L.) R.Br. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,5</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (also black bindweed, field bindweed)(1926 headword <i>convolvulus</i>; 1967 headword <i>bindweed</i>) convolvulus.

>  <i>bindweed, black [=] cornbind</i> [s] ... <i>bindweed, field</i> [s] <i>Convolvulus arvensis</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,5</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> CONVOLVULUS [BINDWEED] (<i>Convolvulus arvensis</i>), is also called bindweed and black bindweed, cornbind, and morning glory. It is an introduced weed found in fields, gardens and waste places in many localities. It has a perennial creeping underground stem, trailing or twining [aerial] stems with leaves 1 to 3 inches long, shaped like a spear head, and handsome <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,135,1967 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> bell-climber, pohue; (also greater bindweed)

>  Amongst the known kinds of plants are common and rough Bindweed, [ed. fn 4 Probably <i>Calystegia sepium</i> and the smaller <i>C. turguriorum</i>.] Nightshade and Nettles, both which grow to the size of small trees; a shruby [sic] Speedwell found near all the Beeches, Sow thistles, ... <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/804</i>) [NOTES: (Queen Charlotte Sound) 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/804-805]

>  <b>POLACK</b> The clematis, campanula trachelium, or bell flower, whose hues, white, red, and yellow, with the convolvulus, or bind-weed, and immumerable liands, [106] hung around in pleasing disorder. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/105</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> Convolvulus sepium, our common English bind-weed is common and is almost the only flower that we see in the bush. <i>(Feb 14, 1853,Traveller under Concern,79,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Calystegia <i>sepium,</i> Br. ... Nat. name, `Panake and Pohue', <i>Col</i>. ... This beautiful plant, the English `Bind-weed', is as common in the Southern Hemisphere as it is in the Northern, being found in Chili, Australia, Tasmania, and Java ... <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 183</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> (Roots used by Maoris as food, and called <i>pohue</i>.) <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui</i>)

>  Convolvulus sepium Bindweed <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/125</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>Vol I 1877-XXII 1898</b> <i>(1886,NZ Country Journal</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Calystegia sepium, R.Br.] Pohue; Panahe</i>; Bindweed. ... Widely dispersed in most temperate countries and everywhere highly variable. note Cheeseman has <i>panahe</i>: Williams 256 gives: <i>Panahi </i>1. <i>Calystegia sepium</i> and <i>C. soldanella; bindweed.</i>; and <i>Panake, Calystegia sepium; wild convolvulus. =poohue</i>.  <i>v.</i>
