### bingy
 
 <i>n.</i> stomach, belly [1972 known to W.H.B. Orsman, but not heard since c1920s. slang]

>  If the wowsers got their way boxing championships of the future will [sic] be decided by the candidates being required to answer certain questions, as, for instance: The best way to avoid an uppercut; what to do upon receipt of a prod in the bingie, or the probable effect of a 600lb blow upon the listening organ. <i>(Jul 23, 1910,NZ Truth,265/1/3</i>)

>  Not Understood! Yer cop one in the bingie, jis' when yer think you got `im dead to rights; yer claim a foul, but referees are stingy; <i>(Feb 8, 1913,NZ Bulletin,16</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> The following [slang terms], which I have noted in constant use by our youngsters, [include] binjey, skite, sheilah] ... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,51</i>)

>  `My oath,' I said. `Many a Jap binjie is going to get a hoodickey stuck in it before sparrow-hiccough. ...' <i>(1945,Sixes & Sevens,7</i>)

>  <i>bingey</i>, bingy n. <i>stomach, belly. [Abo]. </i> <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

>  <i>bingey, bingy ...n</i>. stomach, belly. [Abo.] <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> I like a little kip after my crib, but there's no time for that, not even forty winks, and I tell you there's times my tucker burns in my bingy all afternoon. <i>Ibid.</i> 242 bingy stomach <i>(1984,Blackball 08,33</i>)

### binning
 
 <i>n.</i> broker service for the disposal of small or mixed lots of wool in which each grower's wool is classed and weighed to bins with similar types from other growers, cf bin.

>  The wool removed by crutching is of low value and the amount per clip is usually small. For these reasons `crutchings' are seldom if ever classed except by the wool brokers when the wool is sent to them for binning. <i>Ibid.</i> 711 In `Binning,' the bales are broken up and classed into lines. This method of building up saleable lines is generally used in the case of small parcels of fleece wool and bales of oddments which cannot be included in the main lines without destroying their evenness. As each vendor's wool [712] is classed in the binning-room, the wool in each class is weighed and credited to the owner before being placed in its appropriate bin. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,710</i>)

>  <i>Binning</i>: Broker service for the disposal of small or mixed lots of wool. Each grower's wool is classed and weighed to bins with similar types from other. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,52</i>)

>  <b>WINTER</b> Binning [sc wool-binning] <i>(1939,King Country, The,15</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The broke has a good binning system and can handle most of the oddments. If a line of four bales cannot be made, send the wool in for binning. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,313</i>)

### birch
 
 <i>n.</i> (any of a group of deciduous, Northern Hemisphere trees with slender branches and smooth bark, used for timber.) In NZ commonly, but incorrectly, applied to the native beech spp.; a common popular name c1795-1970s for the trees and timber of <i>Nothofagus</i> spp., preferred common name beech q.v. Also occas. used for species of other general thought to resemble Nothofagus. occas. (usu. overseas usage) New Zealand birch. As with beech, vernacular naming tries to distinguish var. and spp. by ref. to colour of bark, texture of wood or timber, appearance of leaf, etc., but with similar confusion and local variation. [cf the use of birch by Cook of Nothofagus in Tierra del Fuego (=? beech <i>Nothofagus antarctica</i> or <i>N. pumilio</i> or both [fn]) 1769 January 25 Cook's Journals (1955) 1/51 [Tierra del Fuego] <i>`The wood is of the Birch kind... wild celery' [fn3</i>Apium <i>graveolens</i>].]

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> On the Hollyford job the road went through beech-forest, but this was locally called birch. Woe betide us if we referred to birch trees in our correspondence with Head Office: we were reminded that it was <i>beech</i> and given all sorts of advice telling us that the botanical name was something like <i>Fagus fagusti</i>, and so on. Some people are allergic to beech trees and the local name for this allergic condition was birch itch. On one occasion a compensatory claim was sent to Wellington advising that the condition suffered was birch itch. Back came a reprimand about this use of the term birch - it should be <i>beech</i>, Head Office pointed out. Nothing was done about this until one day it came to the notice of our chief. He was quick to reply and acknowledge that the trees were beech, not birch, but the itch suffered by the men was birch itch not beech itch. It was locally named by them, and as far as his staff were concerned they used the term birch itch to describe the allergy caused by beech trees. There was no further comment from the Moguls in Head Office. <i>(1975,Men of the Milford Road,93</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  1. A tree with a silvery bark. The name of `birch' was applied in error by early New Zealand settlers to different kinds of beech and is still often so used by bushmen. ... <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),40</i>)

>  <i>Birch (b&etilde.rch),<i> n. 1. [Add.] </i> In New Zealand the name <i>birch</i> is applied to all the native beeches (see under BEECH) and to all small-leaved forest trees; it is, however, qualified by various adjectives, - <i>white, black, brown, red, silver</i>, etc. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> In New Zealand, the trees called birches are really <i>beeches</i> (q.v.), but the term birch is used very vaguely; see quotation 1889. In Tasmania, the name is applied to <i>Dodonoea ericifolia</i>, Don., N.O. <i>Sapindaceae.</i> <i>(1898,Austral English,31</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> ... our camp was in a small bay surrounded by beech trees - always called `birch' trees - and very big matagouris or Irishman, some more than a foot through. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,38</i>)

>  <i>new zealand birch n, usu cap N & Z</i> : NEW ZEALAND BEECH <i>(1981,Musterer on Molesworth,1524</i>)

>  <i>birch n</i> -es <i>1 b Austral</i> : PIRIPIRI <i>1 c NewZeal </i>: any of various trees resembling those of the genus<i> Betula</i> (as the kamahi or the native beeches) <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,219</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> English names of animals and plants are sometimes mistakenly applied. The native beech, <i>Nothofagus</i>, has been called <i>birch</i> since the early days and is still often so called by bushmen; ... <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

>  <i> ~ n</i>. name given in New Zealand to many varieties of beech trees. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

>  <i>birch ...n.</i> N.Z. name for several varieties of beech. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>MURRY</b> Capt. Cook has given so good a description of the Spruce Fir, that it is impossible to mistake it. But he has not taken any notice of the Pitch Pine - Birch - And large Myrtle. ... The Birch is only fit for fuel - Its uncommon whiteness would cause it to be preferred for decks, &c. but it splits with the smallest blow, and, of all the woods at Duskey Bay it is the least durable - It grows from a large stump about 50-60 & even 70 feet with branches included. The boughs spread more than any other tree and the bark is generally white, somewhat resembling the hazel in England. <i>(1796,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1796 [chck ?1795]Jan Murry's Journal of the `Britannia' in 1908 McNab Hist. Records 2/534]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Diary (Wellington) ... toi tois ... birches ... <i>(Apr 8, 1840,Diary</i>) [NOTES: ATLMS]

>  ... birch grows. <i>(1842,NZ Journal,93/185,1843 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Wellington Surveyor)]

>  <b>SAM STEPHENS</b> ... birch, mai etc. <i>(1845,Journal,390</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,125</i>) [NOTES: [Morris 31; date given - 1853 - is incorrect]]

>  Birch of New Zealand. <i>Fagus. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),764</i>)

>  The flat land and low spurs are covered with the common species of Pines and Birch, such as Rimu, ...Weinmannia (Karmahi), and Fagus (Tawai) ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/4</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  ... at the higher levels, one genus of trees, the <i>Fagus</i> or `birch' of the colonists, occupies the ground ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/9</i>) [NOTES: (Monro)]

>  new zealand birch n, usu cap N & Z : NEW ZEALAND BEECH <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1524</i>)

>  <i>birch ... 2 N.Z</i>. native beech. -->[nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; compare NZPOD 70] <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,49</i>)

>  As is well known, the Fagus family occupy one of the most important positions among the forest trees of the Middle Island ... Four species are recognized by botanists; these are known to settlers [of Otago and Canterbury] by the names of Black, White or Red<i> Birch</i>, not <i>Beech</i>, which would be their more correct designation. <i>(1871,TrNZI,3/200</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  In fact, the term `birch' may be regarded as a generic name applied by bushmen to any small-leaved tree, and qualified with the prefixes `black', `white', or `red', at the caprice of the individual, or as may be suggested by the colour of the foliage, bark, or timber. <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,16</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> [Ferns] contrasting ... with the black stems of the birch-trees around them. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,94,1870 ed.</i>)

>  The higher portion of the hills and steep spurs are clothed with the various species and variety of birch (beech), to which along the shores of the [Marlborough] sounds were added pukatea and kohekohe, the latter locally called cedar. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,17</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> [The species of] the genus <i>Nothofagus</i> ... in New Zealand, all forest trees [, are] known to bushmen as `birch,' but [should] more correctly [be] termed beech. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,132,1940 p. 136 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Tawai</i> ...the birch of the colonists. [1957 ... beech-tree species] <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,475</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> [caption facing] `BUSH' SHOWING BIRCH TREES AND FERNS <i>(1930,NZ Memories,150</i>)

>  <i>Birch</i>. Various species of Nothofagus. There are various kinds (white, red, black etc.) of trees in New Zealand called birches. They are all really beeches and the newspapers and forestry people are trying to get them so called, but up country they are still always called birches. Birch (creeks, hill, etc.) is one of the commonest components of local names in the South Island. ([1898 Morris Austral-English], and an unpublished letter to Hunter Brown from Dr Hodgkinson, August 9, 1854.) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Fagus Solandri.</i> - The most common name of this tree is `black-birch,' but it has several other names. In the Wellington and Marlborough districts it is called `black-birch'; in Canterbury it is called `black-birch,' `white-birch,' `red-birch,' and `brown-birch'; in Otago it is known as `white-birch,' `black-birch,' or black-heart birch.' Young specimens of this tree that have no dark heart-wood are often called `white-birch.' This tree is a beech, and it would do away with confusion if it was called `black-beech'. <i>Fagus Menziesii. </i>- The commonest name of this tree is `silver-birch,' but it is often called `brown-birch,' `red-birch,' and `white-birch' [308] in different localities. It would be best to call it `silver-beech,'though it is gaining an Australasian reputation as `Southland beech.' <i>Fagus fusca. </i>- In Auckland this tree is called `black-birch'; in Wellington `red-birch'; in Nelson, Marlborough and Westland it is called `brown-birch'; and in Otago it is called both `red-birch' and `bull-birch.' The most suitable name is `red-beech.' <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/307</i>)

>  The utmost confusion prevails among the common names of the birches. There are scarcely two districts, a few miles apart, in which the same name is applied to the same tree, and a similar result may be obtained by consulting two bushmen in the same bush. With a view to obviating this difficulty Mr Kirk suggests `the adoption of new names based of the obvious characteristics of foliage [e.g.] for Fagus fusca `tooth-leaved beech' ... <i>(1876,TrNZI,64</i>) [NOTES: (Blair)]

>  [this cit. not spec. repeated in individual entries] [In Oxford Forest] I learned that the tree [F. solandri, entire-leaved beech ] was termed [red, brown, white, black, and yellow] birch at different stages of its growth, but the application of these terms differed greatly; perhaps Black Birch was most generally applied to the mature condition before decay commenced, and White birch to the young state; but there were too many exceptions to allow the names to be anything but misleading. <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/300</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk, NZ Beeches)]

>  The perversity with which the true `beeches' have become popularly known as `birches,' and different kinds of `mapau' as `maples - a popular name which includes small trees of widely distinct Natural Order, is enough to fill with despair any botanist who desires to foster a system of popular names that will be free from misleading and inaccurate suggestions. <i>(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/50</i>)

>  '... the term birch may be regarded as a generic name applied by bushmen to any small-leaved tree, and qualified by the prefixes `black', `white' or `red' at the caprice of the individual or as may be suggested by the colour of its foliage, bark or timber.' So the term `black birch' was in many districts applied to Pittosporum tenuifolium [ ] and to Weinmannia racemosa [Kamahi] in Otago - the latter tree being also known as `brown birch' or `red birch.' Myrsine Urvillei [ ] was known as `red birch', and Carpodetus serratus [ ] and Quintinia serrata [ ] as `white birch.' It was principally to the trees of the species Nothofagus (then known a Fagus), that the term `birch' was applied, and a distinguishing of the various kinds by the prefixing of a descriptive word was a step in the right direction; but in this instance the step was on a path that led to a quagmire. To take only the instance of N. Solandri (formerly Fagus Solandri). [v. 1885 Kirk] ... The confusion created has been endless ; it has at times even resulted in commercial loss ; [v. (Morris) 1889 Kirk `Forest Flora of NZ p.91] yet so strong is habit that confusion was preferred before a change of name. And, indeed, the bushmen might well ask what name they should adopt ; for the scientists came and added to the confusion by pointing out that the trees usually called `birch' were not birch at all, but `beech' - when a fresh crop of names resulted.: `black beech', `red beech', `dusky beech', etc. The scientists themselves, however, in calling the species Nothofagus, `beech', took care - that is, usually took care - to make the distinguishing prefix one that would not lead to a repetition of the old confusion. N. Solanderi was accordingly called `entire-leaved beech' ; N. Cliffortioides, `mountain beech' ; N. Menziesii, `round-leaved beech' ; N. fusca `tooth-leaved beech'. It was some time before even these names were agreed upon ; and `silver beech' finally took the place of `round-leaved beech' for N. Menziesii. A note may here be made of the fact that, whilst the entire-leaved beech was Fagus Solandri, on Fagus being changed to Nothofagus, Solandri was altered to Solanderi ; but this has been altered back again to Solandri ; for that was the original and not incorrect spelling of the word. <i>(1926,TrNZI,56-57/663</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Popular Names ff Kirk in 1875 [Reports on the Durability of N.Z. Timber 16]]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> ... it is perhaps futile to hope that the more exact name [beech] will become popular ; but we might at least expect some consistent nomenclature to be adopted through the islands. At present the various species are designated almost indifferently, white, red, black, silver birch etc. What is white birch in one part of the country may be black in another ... The use of the name <i>birch</i> in place of <i>beech</i> is inexcusable, though ... N. Menziesii ... [shows] some external resemblance to the English birch. <i>(1940,NZ Plants,136</i>)

>  Colour Coding [heading] ... St Patrick's Day sports organiser, Mr Lyn Thompson yesterday inquired about the difference between red, black and brown birch. It was `clearly' explained for him by a prominent axeman, Mr Bill Evans. The bark of the red birch is silver, but the timber is pink when it's green. Brown birch often has black bark, but the green timber is red. Sometimes the bark of the black birch is white, but the timber is yellow and sometimes it's brown when it's green. <i>(Mar 11, 1972,Otago Daily Times,1</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) native beech, spp. Nothofagus. [used in error by farmers.]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> After cutting off a leg for the evening meal, he tied the mutton sheep in a sack and hauled it high among the branches of a birch tree. <i>(1949,High Country Days,43</i>)

>  <b>DEANS</b> The areas that originally consisted of a few black beeches, or birches as they were called in those days.. <i>(1964,Pioneers on Port Cooper Plains,175</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMSON</b> Tawa bush country will make good grass country, whereas birch bush country will usually turn out to be poor. <i>(1981,Sheep Farming in the NZ Bush,1</i>) [NOTES: Citaion named title as : Sheepfarming in the NZ Bush]

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> birch: Many of the farming pursuit, especially when in the presence of over-earnest and better-informed purists, cling obstinately and often with glee to this word rather than use the correct term 'beech.' <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,7</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> Our bunks in a mud whare were built with birch saplings to support wire netting. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,69</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> The settlers call them all [sc. southern beech] 'birch'. And merely distinguish them as 'white', 'black' or 'red', according to the wood. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,206</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> applied to other than <i>Nothofagus</i> genera (see esp. black birch below. Kirk 1875 (quoted by Anderson) states that birch is to be regarded as a generic name applied by bushmen to any small-leaved tree ... so the term black birch was in many districts applied to <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i> (black) matipo and to <i>Weinmannia racemosa</i> in Otago. <i>Myrsine Urvillei</i> [ ] was known as `red birch', and <i>Carpodetus serratus</i> [ ] and <i>Quintinia serrata</i> [ ] as `white birch'.

>  <b>FEATON</b> Kamai [Maori name] .. Birch [Settlers' name] .. Weinmannia racemosa .. Tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,177</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> [Gloss] birch: Common West Coast bushman's name for two kinds of tree, the <i>kaamahi</i> and the New Zealand beech. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,420</i>)

>  The hills along the shores of the [Pelorus] sound will, for many years, furnish birch sleepers. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,17</i>)

### birch country
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PASCOE</b> At one time the Kakapo screamed in the birch country from the sea beach to near the snow line. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,259</i>) [NOTES: c1899 Douglas Birds of South Westland in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 259]

### birch fever
 
 <i>n.</i> ?birch sickness, also beech fever [ca1951 Otago and Southland (R. Mason)]

### birch forest
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The birch forests (Fagus), which are so important in the South, are absent from the North... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/241</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan and Kirk Northern plants)]

### birch itch
 
 <i>n.</i> an allergy affliction (see cit.): phs the same as birch fever, birch sickness, [cf. tauhinu rash ca1914-1918 Marlborough bushmen p.c. W.H.B. Orsman `You catch it off birch trees'] (See [cf. tauhinu rash](../T#tauhinu-rash))

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Cliff developed a kind of skin rash, sometimes called `birch itch'. This rash usually occurs with people who are allergic to bush localities, especially when the pollen is released from the trees in the spring. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,119</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> On the Hollyford job the road went through beech-forest, but this was locally called birch. Woe betide us if we referred to birch trees in our correspondence with Head Office: we were reminded that it was <i>beech</i> and given all sorts of advice telling us that the botanical name was something like <i>Fagus fagusti,</i> and so on. Some people are allergic to beech trees and the local name for this allergic condition was birch itch. On one occasion a compensatory claim was sent to Wellington advising that the condition suffered was birch itch. Back came a reprimand about this use of the term birch - it should be <i>beech</i>, Head Office pointed out. Nothing was done about this until one day it came to the notice of our chief. He was quick to reply and acknowledge that the trees were beech, not birch, but the itch suffered by the men was birch itch not beech itch. It was locally named by them, and as far as his staff were concerned they used the term birch itch to describe the allergy caused by beech trees. <i>(1975,Men of the Milford Road,93</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a form of dermatitis experienced by bush settlers and hunters.

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Cliff developed a kind of skin rash, sometimes called 'birch itch.' <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,119</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Arawhata Bill was finally forced to leave the bush when he developed 'birch itch' probably caused by a diet deficiency. <i>(1974,Open Country Muster,181</i>)

>  <b>CHANDLER</b> Afflicted with birch itch, a form of dermatitis, he claimed that camping at that spot provided prompt relief. <i>(1996,Land of the Mountain and the Flood,98</i>)

### birch sickness
 
 <i>n.</i> ? Birch fever, ? birch itch [ca1951 Otago and Southland (R. Mason)]

### birch strawberry
 
 <i>n.</i> a fungus [ca1951 Nov R. Mason. (C.M.S.)]

### Birchitis
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It is Rogernomics, Ruthenasia, Jennycide and Birchitis on a regional scale. <i>(Jul 6, 1999,Evening Post,4</i>)

### bird
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> The old birders said there was always a queer unfriendly feeling about these places, as though unseen eyes were watching them and they were reluctant to `bird' there, and during the torching season (at night) kept well away from the area is possible. <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,64</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> elliptical for muttonbird

>  We went a few yards and made a fire and ate our dinner of birds and potatoes: ... <i>IBid.</i> 81 [At Ruaboka (Ruapuke Island)] were crowds of natives collected together ... who were on their way to the islands in the Straits in search of birds. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,77,1986 ed.</i>)

>  Out on line by door bull-kelp bags blown up and drying. Under the table finished pohas stacked. Birds in kelp bags with totara bark wrapping, looking fine in their new flax baskets. <i>(Apr 10, 1952,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1952 Apr 20 diary in 1982 Sansom In the Grip of an Island 153]



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Got letters from the Chathams ... Hood has been delayed birding [[ed] killing fledgling albatross and mutton birds] with the natives ... <i>(Sep 25, 1878,Chudleigh Diary,272,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> prisoner, jailbird (See [cf. budgie cage](../B#budgie-cage))

>  The `Harmy' [sc. Salvation Army] always hangs around `Prison Gates' to get hold of discharged `birds' to give them a helping hand, and by the elderly Henry if the released prisoner accepts their help he pays for it with the sweat of his brow. <i>(May 2, 1908,NZ Truth,150/1/5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Slang of the N.Z.E.F.] A `blue duck' was a rumour; but a `bird' was a prisoner in a cage. <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bird</i>. 2. A prisoner: New Zealand military: 1915-18. Ex <i>cage,</i> a detention-camp. Prob. ex earlier<i> cage</i>, a prison. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,54</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a certainty [2. make a bird of abbrev. of dead bird [. [P8 10. a certainty Aust ca1910 (B1943).]]

>  <b>PEARSON</b> Well, you'll get a clear run this time, son, and you make a bird of it and get ahead in your job. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,12</i>)

### bird strike
 
 <i>n.</i> [also (general English) meaning when an aircraft hits a bird or a bird is sucked into the engine]

>  One trialist who had a good crop with an excellent production of seed and excellent growth lost 99 per cent of the seed to bird strike. <i>(Sep 20, 2002,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

### bird's eye
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MASSEY</b> <i>Bird's eye figure </i>Small rounded grain figure resembling a bird's eye. <i>(1987,Woodturning in New Zealand,93</i>)

### bird's eye rot
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fungal disease Anthracnose or black spot.

>  It [Anthracnose or black-spot] is also known as the 'bird's eye rot' which aptly describes the appearance of the round spot on the berry with its rosy-grey centre and dark edges. <i>(1927,NZ Journal of Agriculture,302</i>)

### bird's eye totara
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MANDER</b> Besides this, Peter picked out as a gift mottled kauri for the front room and the ceiling and some fine bits of bird's-eye totara for the doors and cupboards. <i>(1925,Allen Adair,61,1971 ed.</i>)

### bird-catcher (plant)
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The third ... is <i>Pisonia Brunoniana,</i> the parapara, or `bird-catcher' of the settlers. ... As is well known, the seed-pods of the<i> Pisonia</i> exude a sticky substance, which it is stated will ensnare the smaller birds that come into contact with the gluey mass - hence the name `bird-catcher plant'. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/186</i>) [NOTES: (Sladden Karewa Island)]

### bird-catching plant
 
 <i>n.</i> parapara, <i>Heimerliodendron brunonianum</i> (See [parapara](../P#parapara),[bird-catcher](../B#bird-catcher))

>  Some mysterious person every year at this season breaks off the branches of the bird-catching tree in Pukekura Park.. so that it cannot trap birds or insects. <i>(Jul 26, 1943,NZEF Times,III/109 : 11</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> ~ a New Zealand shrub or tree, <i>Pisonia brunoniana, </i>Endl., <i>N.O. Nyctagineae;</i> Maori name, <i>Parapara. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,31</i>)

>  `A Bird-killing Tree.... In a shrub growing in my father's garden at New Plymouth, two Silver-eyes (<i>Zosterops</i>) and an English Sparrow has been found with their wings so glued by the sticky seed-vessels that they were unable to move, and could only fly away after having been carefully washed.' <i>(1883,TrNZI,14/364</i>) [NOTES: R.H. Govett]

>  <b>KIRK</b> It is sometimes termed the `bird-catching plant' by settlers and bushmen ... It will always be a plant of special interest, as small birds are often found captured by its viscid fruits, to which their feathers become attached as effectively as if they were glued. <i>(1899,Forest Flora,293</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Pisonia Brunoniana (The Parapara)</i>. ... Maori name<i> para-para</i>, sometimes called by the settlers the <i>Bird-catching plant</i>. North Island: Auckland province chiefly. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,159,1940, p. 165 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Pisonia Brunoniana </i> <i>(1917,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>(1940,NZ Plants,165</i>) [NOTES: [North Island: Auckland province chiefly]]

>  <i> ~ n.</i> New Zealand shrub, so called because the wings of small birds are often caught in the sticky seed-vessels. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

>  <i>bird-catching plant n.</i> N.Z. shrub, so called because the wings of small birds are often caught in the sticky seed-vessels. The Maori name is <i>parapara. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>CROWE</b> A few <i>rare native</i> coastal plants have become well known as cultivated plants: tawapou, creeping fuchsia, puka (<i>Meryta sinclairii</i>), parapara/bird-catcher tree, <i>Tecomanthe speciosa</i> and Poor Knights lily. <i>(1995,Which Coastal Plant?,62</i>)

### bird-catching tree
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>parapara</i>, a tree (Pisonia Brunoniana), bird-catching tree: its sticky leaves are liable to entrap small birds; the tree does not eat them as the sundew does entrapped insects. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/155</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>WODZICKI</b> Such trees [on Three Kings' Islands] as the Wharangi (<i>Melicope terrata</i>) and the bird-catching tree (<i>Myoporum laetum</i>) were ringbarked. <i>(1950,Introduced Mammals of NZ,163</i>)

>  <i>Parapara</i>, ... n. <i>1. Heimerliodendron brunonianum (Pisonia brunoniana), the Andersen Maori Words in English . </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,262</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> On the mainland as well as on islands, and extending to East Cape and beyond, are the small trees parapara or the `bird catching tree' (<i>Pisonia brunoniana</i>) and tawapou (<i>Planchonella costata</i>). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,110</i>)

### bird-stone
 
 <i>n.</i> gizzard-stone (?of moa); moa-stone.

>  Many small stones, well-worn - unquestionably `bird-stones' - can be picked out of the clay immediately beneath the ashes of the burned peat-bed. <i>(1889,TrNZI,410</i>)

### birdcage
 
 <i>n.</i> strongly-fenced enclosure where used-car dealers displayed their wares (c1950).

>  Clearly the days of `bird-cage' dealers (so called because their lots are usually surrounded by wire netting) are on the way out. <i>(Nov 13, 1976,Evening Post,6/6</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>birdcage</i> used car dealer's lot post-war; died out as dealers got rid of the chicken wire; may revive along with new security fences. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> security exercise yard

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BIRDCAGE (n) Security exercise yard. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,245</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(racing)]

>  From the birdcage, the people would walk to the totalisator. <i>(Nov 18, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,3</i>)

>  Coats and costumes were much affected by the womenfolk, the only noticeable colours being a few spring flowers inside the birdcage and the jackets of the jockeys. <i>(Nov 18, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,3</i>)

>  Take over the birdcage gate - the pay is higher. <i>(1957,Racing in Rangitikei,44</i>)

>  Watching the horses in the birdcage was the only thing Mrs Betham enjoyed at the races. <i>(Jun 1950,Landfall,13(2) No. 50 / 133</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The road dies to an excited buzz and a smattering of applause as the trotters come back to the birdcage. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,63</i>)

>  "Oh! 'Ere they come," as the horses filed out of the bird-cage to do their preliminary. <i>(Dec 21, 1914,Public Service Journal,17</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> You gather around the birdcage... watch the horses being saddled up and led out for the preliminaries. <i>(1949,From N to Z,108</i>)

>  After contacting the Auckland Trotting Club, the National Racing Museum and even turf historian Patrick Finnegan, we are all stumped. What [Finnegan] can tell us is that "birdcage" is a New Zealand term dating back to the 1880s... "The only thing I can think of," Finnegan says, "is that it looks like a bit of a birdcage." <i>(Jan 13, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  [Caption] In the 'birdcage' before the National Election Stakes (Nominations now closed). <i>(Nov 13, 1935,NZ Free Lance,1</i>)

>  When photographed with a cup in the birdcage, they look as if a visit to a good tailor is well overdue. <i>(Jan 28, 1953,NZ Observer,6</i>)

>  Not knowing, as many New Zealanders would, that.. the birdcage in the poem is the term used for where the horses are saddled, Private Eye found the poem incomprehensible. <i>(1987,Antipodes New Writing,22</i>)

>  270. The person responsible for any horse not being in the Birdcage by the time [fixed for starting] may be fined. <i>(1986,NZ Rules of Racing,109</i>)

>  [Caption] Cup candidates parading in the birdcage before the race. <i>(Nov 16, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,40</i>)

>  Obviously backward, she attracted much attention in the birdcage. <i>(Feb 16, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,64</i>)

>  No jockey shall, after leaving the Bird-cage, speak to, or hold any communication with, any person other than a Steward, the Starter, or his assistant, of the Clerk of the Course. <i>(Nov 23, 1920,Otago Witness,39</i>)

>  He saw the horse.. go out of the birdcage for the Introductory Handicap. <i>(May 20, 1924,Otago Witness,24</i>)

>  Witness was the birdcage steward at the meeting... Jury had the evidence of Wilson, "the birdcage artist." <i>(May 20, 1924,Otago Witness,24</i>)

>  A Melbourne writer says that those who looked at [Aurum] in the birdcage at Caulfield would recognise that his portraiture.. was tolerably faithful. <i>(Oct 30, 1897,NZ Times,Suppl. [6]</i>) [NOTES: ?Australian usage]

>  I will bet that the horse finishes close to the tail of the field, and the first thing he will do when he comes back to the birdcage is to yawn. <i>(Apr 18, 1934,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  It was also decided.. to enlarge the birdcage by at least 15 yards. <i>(Oct 12, 1937,Morrinsville Star,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Well, there's three bird-cage compartments, that's sleeping quarters at night. <i>(Jul 17, 1912,Otago Witness,89</i>)

>  The refreshment and bird-cage cars were both off the line, lying at an uncomfortable angle. <i>(Feb 8, 1905,Otago Witness,75</i>)

>  He [sc. the Minister of Lands] received a deputation in one compartment of a "bird-cage" carriage. <i>(Jan 2, 1904,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  <b>LEITCH</b> In the 1890s the unusual 'birdcage' cars were built... They featured an open saloon in one half of the car, and two small compartments in the other half - opening onto an open corridor or passage protected by heavy grillwork. <i>(1972,Railways of NZ,198</i>)

### birdcage boy
 
 <i>n.</i> a used-car dealer, descriptive of their outdoor `showrooms' in the 1950s, surrounded by heavy wire-mesh and barbed wire for security against thieves of the highly sought-after used cars

>  That dealing in the sale of second-hand cars is a lucrative profession can be gauged by the very numerous `bird-cage' businesses that now abound. ... [21] ... These used car dealers, perhaps more colloquially known as the `bird-cage boys' <i>(May 1954,Public Service Journal,20</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>birdcage boy</i> used car dealer. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,15</i>)

### birder
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori or Chatham Islander who snares or takes birds for food.

>  Rail and pukeka were caught by snares set in their sphagnum moss runs, ... Tui and mako (bell birds) were killed by the birder hutching beneath a cone of fern fronds ... <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,9(5)/362</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> one who catches seabirds for food. (See [cf. muttonbirder](../M#muttonbirder))

>  Birders were going "torching" - searching for their prey in the dark - and were walking long distances to find anything. <i>(Apr 29, 2009,Southland Times,3</i>)

>  Last year a party of 'birders returned to the mainland by air. <i>(Aug 16, 1953,NZ Observer,16</i>)

>  These sea birdings [on the Chathams] were preluded and attended by special rituals; for instance, no birder must coite with a female the night before the expedition, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 372 When we were younger [Chatham Island] birders grumbled at delays ... a finger pointed at some hoary ancient ... was a sufficient rebuke to silence us. <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,9/5:362</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> European wives or husbands of legitimate `birders' may ... accompany their partners to the islets. <i>(1940,Rakiura,211</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> In March, while in the burrows, the chicks are pulled out and killed by the birders, split, salted, roasted over a fire ... and then put hot into bags of split kelp ... <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,84</i>)

>  If the burrow is short, the bird is caught by hand and dragged out to receive a sharp rap over the head from a club, or a crushing bite from the birder's strong teeth which puts a swift end to its struggle. <i>(Mar 5, 1966,Dominion,8</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... (in latter years the black cloud of departing birds has been getting progressively thinner according to some birders who maintain the birds are getting less plentiful) ... <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,25</i>)

>  Muttonbirding begins on 1 April. Formerly the birders travelled by sea, but now many hire a helicopter. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,97</i>)

>  Some of the families or tribes [of Maoris] hold the right of `birding' on certain islands. <i>(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,2/920</i>)

>  These sea birdings [on the Chathams] were preluded and attended by special rituals; for instance, no birder must coite with a female the night before the expedition, ... <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,9(5)/362</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 362]

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> Very few folks know anything about the birding, especially up in the North Island. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,37</i>)

### birding
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Hence those repeatedly successful birding expeditions, almost incredible to such as know ... the fierce currents between the main island and the Motuhara - birding rock. <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,9(5)/372</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 372]

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> During one long vacation, he [Maui Pomare] returned to the Chathams, and one day went out with a birding party. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,39</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Birding season' means a period commencing on the 1st day of April in any year and ending with the 31st day of May in the same year: <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,174</i>) [NOTES: 1949 Land Act Regulations in 1979 Wilson Titi Heritage 174]

>  <b>WILSON</b> Over the years since 1864 they had controlled their islands during the birding seasons with stern and strict discipline. <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,38</i>)

>  <i>Birdsmouth joint (metal)</i> ... [15]<i> Birdsmouth joint (timber)</i> - An angle checked near or at the lower end of a rafter for connection to the wall plate. <i>(1979,Glossary of Building Terminology,15</i>)

### birding island
 
 <i>n.</i>

### birdseed
 
 <i>n.</i> broad-leaved plantain.

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> BROAD-LEAVED PLANTAIN (<i>Plantago major</i>), also called ratstail, birdseed, lamb's tongue, and greater plantain, is common on roadsides and trodden places throughout the country and in pastures in the King Country. ... From the middle of the rosette ascends the flower stalk ..., which afterwards give[s] place to seeds, and produce[s] the ratstail appearance that gives the plant one of its trivial names. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,161,1967, p. 61 ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [trefoil](../T#trefoil))

 <i>n.</i>

### birl
