### biscuit
 
 <i>n.</i> a floatation device.

>  Quietly paddling the Devonport-Takapuna coastline this summer I was nearly run over by an otherwise sane-looking Dad in a tinny pulling his child on an inflatable "biscuit". Only trouble was he was driving fast forward while facing backwards, waving at his child. <i>(Feb 2004,North & South,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a thin layer of hay [Jim Cameron writes: as part of a [?square] bale. In NZPOD and Macquarie 1981 but not in DNZE or other dictionaries I have seen.]



 
 <i>n.</i> boasting [[? A dvt of `that takes the biscuit'.] 1984 16 M E95 Pakuranga Coll. 17 BISCUIT [M2] b. biscuit eater a boaster, skite. 1984 17 M 101 Pakuranga Coll. 17 BISCUIT EATER]



 
 <i>n.</i> a railways tablet

>  <b>McGILL</b> Inside the station they had the big red metal boxes that work the old-fashioned tablet signalling system. If you ever wonder what drivers are picking up or unloading on the end of a curved piece of wood, it is the tablet or biscuit. A train cannot get through a section of rail without the biscuit matching the one in the red metal box, which is connected to the manually operated signals. Automatic signals are phasing this system in to rural museums. <i>(1985,G'day Country,32</i>)

>  <b>F M O'BRIEN (Invercargill)</b> <i>The Biscuit:</i> The tablet used in N.Z. railways. <i>(Dec 28, 1950,Personal Communication</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> in early use often is `ship's biscuit'; (See [cabin biscuit](../C#cabin-biscuit),[cabin bread](../C#cabin-bread))

>  <b>LELAND</b> Any sort or COOKIE or CRACKER is a biscuit. What you think is a biscuit is a scone. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,14</i>)

>  A remark of Manuka's was rather good - Maori chiefs (leaders) were toas & went out at the head of their men, Pakeha chiefs stayed in Town and ate biscuit. [fn.] Their notion of luxury. <i>(Dec 25, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/671,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]



 
 <i>n.</i> be done out, be exhausted. [ca1940 Havelock an old lady says (?as an excuse for) coming in for a cup of tea with a friend: `I feel I've had [?done] the biscuit today, Jane.]

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> means the same thing [as<i> have the snake</i>] 95] <i>Snake, have the</i> To be exhausted, or at the end of one's resources. `I've had the snake. I've been loading all day without a break, barely stopped for lunch and hot as hell.' [not heard in NZ] <i>(1974,Australian & NZ Gazette,496</i>)

### biscuit class
 
 <i>n.</i> nickname for a class of economy air travel

>  Spokesman Bob Wallace confirmed to <i>NBR</i> that passengers would still have to pay top dollars to fly the Friendships on provincial routes, dubbed `biscuit class' because of the crackers and cheese snacks and cramped legroom. <i>(Jul 20, 1987,National Business Review,4</i>)

### bish
 
 <i>v.</i> throw, biff [ca1938 Havelock esp. to bish stones]

>  <b>MARSH</b> They'd just sort of bished them [sc. the things used for charades] into the cupboard and they were bulging out ... <i>(1941,Surfeit of Lampreys,142,1951 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> Fine. Dined at Bishopscorte at one. <i>(Feb 22, 1875,Thames Journals,159,1975 ed.</i>)

>  Arch and I could hit anything with a stone. The term was to chuck or "bish" a rock. <i>(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 59/19</i>)

### bish-bash
 
 <i>n.</i> tennis [1951 15 F 9 Marlborough C. 20 BISH-BASH (= TENNIS) slang]

### Bishopscourt
 
 <i>n.</i> In NZ, the name of the residences of Anglican bishops, prob. since Selwyn's day (?mid or late 19cent)

>  Fine. Dined at Bishopscorte at one. <i>(Aug 22, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,99,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BOREHAM</b> The house of a Mr Clarke had been secured as a `Bishopscourt' for the present; ... <i>(1911,Selwyn,57</i>)

>  Bishop Selwyn lived at Waimate, where his house, the first Bishopscourt, still stands as the second oldest wooden building in New Zealand. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,7</i>)

>  <b>ARNOLD</b> To a pious and dutiful daughter of Bishopscourt, Tom Arnold ... must have indeed seemed a dangerous suitor. [reference to Archbishop's Palace, Dublin- Whately: JB (6/7/89 recollects using it in an Australasian sense: doesn't recall seeing it in Britain] <i>(1966,Letters,xxx [Intro]</i>) [NOTES: 1966 ed Bertram Arnold Letters]

>  <b>HARPER</b> Plans for the future were discussed at Bishop's Court [at Christchurch] when Ellen went there early in the next year, 1862. <i>(1967,Kettle on the Fuchsia, The,40</i>)

### bit
 
 <i>n.</i> in phr. a bit of (a) [1984 14 F E143Wgton Girls C. 7 BIT OF A BUMMER -->1984 17 M E107Pakuranga Coll. 34 BIT OF A LAUGH ca1935 Havelock e.g. She's only a bituva kid. It was bituva hot in there.]

>  <b>HUNT</b> I just lived up there in a bit of a bach with another chap ... <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,82</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> He was a bit of a one for going on the booze. <i>(1946,That Summer,31</i>) [NOTES: (`Pair of Socks')]

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> as common in NZ in a variety of senses and uses as it is in gen. Eng. - esp. bit of a, pronounced [ ] and often spelt <i>bituva</i>, used adjectively for `small, little', and also ironically in understatement as `large' (e.g. 1944 `I must 'er made a bituva mistake, and opened up yer nose'): also used (esp. by children) adverbially as `rather': e.g. `It's bituva hot in here'. These and other uses are attested in OED(S) and EDD; cf hang of a; collocations (A) BIT OF (A) [to go to file 3BI5 BIT 1.]. The expression is in constant use. It has different meanings, but each is clear from the circumstances of the conversation. For example: <i>A bit of luck, A bit of a break</i> The event is praised, its quality is genuine. <i>A bit of a lark, A bit of fun</i> There is no intense quality in the event. <i>A bit of an ass, aren't you?</i> The quality of the topic is weakened so that the remark will not sting as much. <i>A bit of a hold-up (i.e. check to progress)</i> The delay is brief. <i>A bit of risk attached to it</i> The proportion of risk is not great, possibly slight. <i>A bit of a muddler, A bit of a no-hoper, A bit of a poor show </i> The quality referred to is present in full force. <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],60</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Exaggeration is not an Australian characteristic and appears mainly in slang ...; understatement shows in style and unemphatic intonation rather than in vocabulary, except for the ubiquitous <i>a bit. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 126 [qoting a Queensland survey] The word <i>bit</i> is common because it occurs in<i> a bit</i> (169) ...,<i> a bit of</i> (142) ... The word <i>bit</i> (1,241) is thus very frequent; compare <i>two (1,253).. </i> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,48</i>)

>  <b>STEWARD</b> Not wishing a time-honoured custom to efface,/ I prefix my book with `a bit of a Preface'. <i>(1867,Carmina Varia,9</i>)

>  I must 'er made a bituva mistake, and opened up yer nose. <i>(1944,Hospitiki,3/11</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The luckiest player that ever pulled on a jersey, said old Bert. He's been a bit of a tin-bum, said Erik. ... Not like me. I'm always shit out of luck. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,158</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> short for gen. Eng. a bit on the side.

>  <b>MASON</b> JUNE: ... Then we had to get dug in again to that bloody pigsty in town - ... and he got all snaky again. Then, when I had to take it quiet an' he couldn't get his bit, he just cleared out, six weeks ago. <i>(1974,Hand on the Rail, The,38,1981 ed.</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> a girlfriend (or boyfriend), esp. my bit [1984 16-17 F E24 Pakuranga Coll. 28 MY BIT [F2M5] -->1984 16-17 M E108Pakuranga Coll. 28 (A) BIT [M6]]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bit of skirt</i>. A girl; a woman: coll: from ca1900; esp. military, Australian, New Zealand. [P8 merely Mil.] <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,56</i>)

### bit of dirt
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm, or land. (See [bit of dirt](../B#bit-of-dirt),[get one's own](../G#get-one's-own))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. and claimed by the people of that locality to be the best bit of dirt in the country. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,240</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> He worked freezing work yards and shepherded around the traps, then got a bit of dirt of his own south of Invercargill. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,48</i>)

>  I settled into my 50-acre bit of retirement dirt down country in Windsor near Oamaru. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,155</i>)

>  Ever wanted to make your living off the land but don't have the finance to own your own piece of dirt? <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,April/May 40</i>)

>  Too often a beautiful piece of dirt is spoiled by a monoculture crop. <i>(2001,Organic NZ,July/Aug 29</i>)

>  I get so excited about a tonne of ideas for my own bit of dirt ... <i>(Nov 2001,NZ House & Garden,22</i>)

>  Once again it was a pretty rundown property with no fences. But it was a pretty good piece of dirt. [dirt; patch/piece of dirt] <i>(Nov 29, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### bit on, a
 
 <i>phr.</i> (that's) a bit on, from the longer expressions on the cuff (q.v.) <i>nose</i>, the <i>snorer,</i> that's tough; that's a bit much; see also cuff. [ca1945 e.g's `I want four pounds for it.' `Gee, that's a bit on' `He got three years hard for bookying.' `That was a bit on, wasn't it?'; also meaning 'rather drunk' in citation below]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The doctor came to the north bank and left his horse, and he and [Jimmy] the Needle double-banked across. The Needle was washed off and the doctor rode to the hut. He must have been `a bit on' to leave the other in the river. ... he was asked `Where's the Needle?' He replied `In Hell!' <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,178</i>)

### bitch
 
 <i>v.</i> to play the female part

>  With two guys you can bomb the other into drag by turning into `bitch' i.e. reversing the roles. see <i>Bomb Someone Out</i> <i>(Sep 1973,Salient,11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> It interested him, after nightfall, to see two boys with flaring painted cheeks ... He had seen plenty of them in Sydney, but not before in Auckland. `Place is lousy with them,' explained a labourer, ... `We call 'em bitches,' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,235</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <i>Bitch</i>.-(1) Spoil or botch; (2) pour out tea. I thought these were colonial expressions, if ever there were any; but an erudite friend points out that (1) is used by Surtees in chapter XLII of `Handley Cross,' perhaps the most English book ever written, and that (2) is an old Cambridge University expression quoted in Gradus ad Cantabrigiam - a dictionary of Cambridge expressions, published in 1824. Let us hope that Samuel Butler introduced it to Canterbury. <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss]

>  ~ Slang. To spoil or make a mess of, e.g., `You've bitched the whole thing.' (C.) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (ca1890-1910) 1933 Acland Glossary Press 16 Sept 15]



 
 <i>n.</i> to pour out tea, a coarse expression which I haven't heard for years; e.g., `Pass up your pannikins, I'll be bitch.' (C.)

### bitch-house
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a secure kennel, usually built on stilts, where a female working dog in oestrus is separately housed.

>  <b>STAPLES</b> Next time Nellie was in season she was put in the bitch-house away from the dogs. <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,78</i>)

### bitcherine
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>wils</b> `Poor beggar be blowed! He took his money with him, and that bitcherine he married can't lay her hands on it. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,180</i>)

### bite
 
 <i>n.</i> an angry response

>  A fellow can't look at him now without risking a "bite". <i>(Jun 7, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/45 : 199</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> sp. of bight

>  that night we reached the Southern Station the bite [or entrance [ed addition]] runs like a corkscrew between two ... swamps that cannot be passed. <i>(Mar 16, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,28,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Breakfast before daylight, cutting out till 4 o'clock when we drove five miles along the sea shore, jamed [sic] the cattle in a bite and pitched our tent, boiled some of my cocoa <i>(Apr 16, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,32,1950 ed.</i>)

>  ... took a lot of cows out of the paddoc into the horse bite, ... <i>(Sep 4, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,56,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> accost and beg successfully from: `the old man bit me for a dollar outside the town hall'; put the bite on, (informal) to ask a favour; to cadge, borrow; to accost and borrow (esp. money)

>  If you ask a pal for many/ When you cannot `strap a beer',/ Then you `chew his lug', how funny,/ And what's more you `bite his ear.'] <i>(Mar 31, 1906,NZ Truth,41/6/4</i>)

>  <b>BRUNO</b> They had to bite a Red-cap for the fare home. <i>(1948,Maleesh George,38</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> I'd feel the same way if I had a mug biting me for money all the time. <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,53</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> I'd been bitten for money often enough before, but this was the best I'd come across yet. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,146</i>)

>  <i>bite v.</i> beg, particularly for money; scrounge. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <i>bite ... 10. (tr.</i>; often foll. by <i>for) Austral. & N.Z. sl.</i> to ask (for); scrounge from. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,108</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

>  `Sing a song of sixpence.' That is the unmelodious chant of the beer-chewer when he's biting your lug for a sprat. <i>(Mar 10, 1906,NZ Truth,38/1/6</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a crotchetty (old) person; an irritable nag - and usu. a woman (esp. old bite). [ca1935 Havelock, frequently of women.]

>  Two [Sergeants] were universally known as `C.B. Kings', and were the worst `bites' in their respective battalions. <i>(Sep 5, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,28</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> ?To defraud, ?cheat, trick [still in ?obs. use in NZ].

>  The fellow could hardly conceal his chagrin at my having done him and his knavery [in trying to get Chudleigh's land away from him] saying that though he would not have bitten me he should have been able to do so had his lawyers been half smart with his deed and not let mine get in first. <i>(Jul 23, 1977,Chudleigh Diary,258,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> The other chap who had been taking it all in had a good laugh at the expense of this shrewd head who I bit for a sixpenny bit [sc tricked the trickster out of sixpence]. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,28</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> copy another graffiti painter's style

>  Graffiti Speak... Bite: To copy another writer's style. <i>(Nov 30, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere but sign. in NZ pastoralism] cover or sole of pasture.

>  The sheep bite had become considerably shortened over the greater part of the East Coast country. <i>(Mar 1907,NZ Farmer,216</i>)

>  .. its presence is unfavourable to the ready development of the fresh leafy 'bite' ... <i>(Mar 20, 1931,NZ Journal of Agriculture,202</i>)

>  .. cut out the lambed ewes quietly ... into a sheltered paddock which has a good clean bite of grass ... <i>(Aug 5, 1936,Weekly News,82</i>)

>  <b>LESLIE</b> A striking feature of pregnancy disease in average aged ewes is that it rarely occurs when the sheep have access to a green 'bite' or 'pick.' <i>(1938,Diseases of Breeding Ewes,39</i>)

>  When grass was sown in the ashes after the burning off of the scrub, the strike usually was excellent and the first season a good bite of feed was thrown. <i>(Jul 15, 1947,NZ Journal of Agriculture,24</i>)

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> The farm looked well despite the frosts and there was bite of good grass over most of the paddocks. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,122</i>)

### bite rate
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the extent to which stock can graze pasture.

>  The bite rate was greatly reduced on spring pasture containing potassium at concentrations of more than 3.7 per cent. <i>(Oct 26, 2001,Dominion Post,26</i>)

### bite the big kumara
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be too ambitious, fail [cf. suck the kumara]

>  I had to say to myself, "wow - he educated, wow - he heard of Robyn Hyde." As a novelist, though, he bites the big kumara. <i>(Apr 28, 1997,Salient,18</i>)

### bite your bum(, go)
 
 <i>exclam.</i> expressing contemptuous dismissal

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bite your bum get lost! eg `I'm sick of you, go bite your bum!' NZA. <i>Ibid.</i> 51<i> go bite your bum! </i>get lost! You're not wanted here. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,15</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> &#65279;"Go bite your bum." <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,239</i>)

### bitsa
 
 <i>v.</i> (See [bitzer](../B#bitzer))

### bitter cress
 
 <i>n.</i> native bitter cress, New Zealand bitter cress? <i>Cardamine debilis.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> <i>Cardamine heterophylla </i> <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>(1916,Dunedin Field Club Plant Catalogues</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> BITTER CRESS (<i>Cardamine heterophylla </i>), also called lady's smock, is found in native pastures and cultivated gardens throughout the islands, Zealand in gardens and driveways, but not important in field crops. <i>(1927,Weeds of New Zealand,95</i>) [NOTES: [wording nf 1967]]

>  <b>TUWHARE</b> No one comes/ ... through straggly tea tree bush/ and gorse, past the hidden spring/ and bitter cress. <i>(1977,No Ordinary Sun,21,1964 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (`The Old Place')]

>  <b>TUWHARE</b> No one comes/ ... through straggly tea tree bush/ and gorse, past the hidden spring/ and bitter cress. <i>(1977,No Ordinary Sun,21,1964 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (`The Old Place')]

>  <i>bitter cress</i> [s]<i> Cardamine hirsuta </i>L. .<i>...bitter cress, native [=] New Zealand bitter cress [s] - ... bitter cress, New Zealand</i> [s] <i>Cardamine debilis</i> Banks ex DC. <i>Ibid.</i> 52 <i>native bitter cress [=] New Zealand bitter cress</i> [s] <i>New Zealand bitter cress</i> [s] <i>Cardamine debilis </i>Banks ex DC. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,5</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> BITTER CRESS (<i>Cardamine hirsuta</i>). Small annual native of Eurasia and North America ... Bitter cress is common throughout New Zealand in gardens and driveways, but not important in field crops. <i>(1980,Weeds of Crops,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cardamine heterophylla</i> var. <i>uniflora. </i>

>  <i>(1909,AJHR,C-12</i>) [NOTES: (And) Cockayne Botanical Survey Stewart Island in AJHR]



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cardamine heterophylla.</i>

>  <b>C-8</b> Cockayne Report on Botanical Survey of Kapiti Island <i>(1907,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>(1908,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: (And) Cockayne Botanical Survey Tongariro National Park]

>  <b>C-11</b> <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: (And) Turner Botanical Examination Higher Waimarino]

>  <i>(1915,AJHR,C-6</i>) [NOTES: (And). Cockayne Plant Covering Kennedy's Bush (Canterbury)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>bitter cress, wavy</i> [s] <i>Cardamine flexuosa</i> With. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,5</i>)

### bittern
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... in the chill, peaty water of this habitat [sambar deer] keep company with pukeko and crakes, fernbirds and bitterns, mad-eyed birds whose randy booming echoes around the hillsides. <i>(Jul 16, 2005,Dominion Post,E18</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The Sultan-hen of brilliant plumage (<i>Porphyris melanotus</i>) [sic], or the Pukeko of the natives; the New Zealand bittern or Matuku (<i>Botaurus melanotus</i>), and the wild-duck Parena [sic] (<i>Spatula rhynchotis</i>). <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),293fn</i>)

>  The solitary bitterns supply a subdued bass to the performance. <i>(Oct 11, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  The bittern (botaurus paciloptilus), apparently stiff in the legs, rises from its haunt in the cat's-reed mace. <i>(Nov 5, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  At the bottom of the nest lay four young bitterns. <i>(Jan 15, 1924,Otago Witness,63</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Secretive Australian brown bitterns nest in the raupo at its edge... <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/96</i>)

### bitterns
 
 <i>n.</i> spent brine

>  The salt gradually forms big chunk crystals that fall to the bottom of the pond, forming a crust. The spent brine, or "bitterns", by now too bitter to be of further use, is drained off and sent back to sea, leaving the salt crust to dry out and be scooped up by a bulldozer-like machine, its blade tipped with a series of fork-like tines. <i>(Aug 7, 2004,Dominion Post,E8</i>)

### bittock
 
 <i>n.</i> mattock, potato axe

>  The best tool for clearing the fern-root is what our labourers call `bittocks' or `mattocks', and similar to those sent out under the name of `potatoe-axes'[sic]. <i>(1842,Latest Inf from New Plymouth,29</i>) [NOTES: (Cutler)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McHUTCHESON</b> My own impression, however, is that in hot weather it is at least twenty miles and `a bittock'. <i>(1888,New Zealander Abroad, The,151</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection) 1888 McHutcheson handwritten addition to New Zealander Abroad 151]

>  <b>A TRAMP</b> Adamson's is 9 miles - and a `bittock', I fancy <i>(1891,Casual Ramblings,49</i>) [NOTES: [Scotch a bit q.v.] [relation to NZ use of `a bit' - Scottish refce]]

>  <b>McHUTCHESON</b> [Scotchman says] `I estimate the distance at 7 miles and a bittock' <i>(1892,Camp-Life in Fiordland,96</i>) [NOTES: [Scotch but NB how Scots dial is used jocularly] OED Scots 1882]

### bitty grass
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [grass](../G#grass))

### bitumen
 
 <i>n.</i> tar-seal, f. ca1950.

>  <b>SHARPE</b> But on bitumen, cattle could get no grip with their straining hooves ... <i>(1952,Country Occasions,172</i>)

>  <b>WATT</b> They [boys aged 9 to 13] played the game of side skidding on the bitumen. <i>(1971,Centenary of Invercargill,70</i>)

>  <b>PINFOLD</b> ... there are now good new roads : the main ones being finished with bitumen and the side ones macadamized.] <i>(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,15</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> She almost ran to her father's side through the flat streets, ... and the lighter bread-carts slapped the bitumen as young fellows in white aprons shouted `Giddap, giddap'. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,101</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... and the dust of the sidestreet was a relief after the bitumen. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,247,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> `Those autumn trees,' Arthur said, as they turned back [87] [from the metal] towards the bitumen. <i>(1960,Word for Word,86</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Then the big black taxi accelerated fast and drove away with a squeal of its tyres on the bitumen. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,59</i>)

>  <b>JONES</b> They sat at one end of the [bus] shelter looking out at the rain dancing on the bitumen. <i>(1985,Gilmore's Dairy,60</i>)

>  The remote receiver for 2760 K.C. used last year was a trial `bitzer.' <i>(Jul 16, 1928,Official Advice Note: Forest Service</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  New Zealand has developed many phrases that depend on local interpretation e.g. ... `off the bitumen', `motor camp', `to farewell', ... <i>(1950,NZ Post Office English Course,14</i>)

### bitumen man
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Bitumen Man</i>: A town-dweller - one who has spent his time in the bitumen-sealed streets. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,185</i>)

### bitzer
 
 <i>n.</i> shortened form of "bits of this and bits of that"; usu. any old and ramshackle mechanical thing or vehicle appearing to be made up of odd bits and pieces; used esp. of cars and (by schoolchildren) of bicycles, but in extended or transferred use, of the non-mechanical. [ca1920 (at least) Havelock `old car made up of parts of others']

>  It is a perfectly ordinary machine - a "bitza" in fact, being made up of parts from four different machines. <i>(May 30, 1946,NZ Farmers' Weekly,8</i>)

>  The bitzer that attacked Carolina was reported as three different types of dog in various places. <i>(May 3, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  Better an honest four-legged bitzer than a pedigreed whelp of your own that doesn't know its place. <i>(Feb 14, 1999,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  A snow vehicle with a difference made its appearance at Scott Base early this month. It has been called a "sno-traveller". More correctly it is an Antarctic "bitzer," for it has been made from items salvaged from an old Tucker snocat, a crashed helicopter, various foreign snow vehicles, and locally-manufactured parts... The "bitzer's" steering sysrtem is believed to be novel. <i>(Sep 1970,Antarctic,5(11) : 446</i>)

>  <b>C. NICHOLL</b> motor mechanic' usage `a sturdy bitzer' [of a car] <i>(Apr 26, 1979,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1929-35 p.c. C. Nicholl (letter 26 April 1979)]

>  [R. Mason, for a milking machine made out of bits and pieces. (Not uncommon for any machine made out of pieces of others).] <i>(May 30, 1946,NZ Farmers' Weekly,8</i>)

>  Title <i>Bitzers:</i> Bits of this and that for the motorist. <i>(1962,NZ Truth</i>)

>  He acquired a succession of grids ... One he called the Herk Bitsa, being mostly Hercules with bits of stolen B.S.A. and all painted a startling silvery blue. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,230</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bitser </i>made from bits and pieces, such as mongrel or home-made trolley. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,15</i>)

>  <b>MONRO</b> After an uncomfortable bivouac ... we trudged on again, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 257 We found a nice grove of wood on the banks of the Waihola lake, and had a most comfortable bivouac. <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey,246</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 246]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [1989 Rockliffe's Folly TV programme: English use for mongrel dog]

>  A WELLINGTON fireman is the toast of Upland Road dog lovers, but he wants to remain anonymous. He received a standing ovation when he was pulled by a rope from the bottom of a 16-metre cliff yesterday with an ageing grateful bitser in his arms. <i>(Mar 5, 1985,Dominion,4</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> Although his official description is humiliatingly described as `bitsa' he is passed off by us ... as a `Surrey setter'. <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,32</i>)

### biv
 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport, informal) a hut, or tent etc. built or used as a temporary shelter; and esp. as bivvy (or biv) freq. among outdoors people for a purpose-built rough shelter or sheltered camping place. [ORIGIN: abbreviation, Google search Nov 2006: NZ 20 , Austral 0, UK 0 hits.] (See [bivouac](../B#bivouac),[bivvy](../B#bivvy))

>  From the biv, easy travel through increasingly attractive beech forest leads down to old river terraces on the true right of the Hurunui. <i>(Sep 2008,NZ Geographic,28</i>)

>  A new two-person hut will replace the old "dog -box" Biv at Aokaparangi during the summer .. Prices are being sought for the replacement of Arete Biv on the main Tararua Range. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 5</i>)

>  Colin Giddy, of DoC Waikanae says "It'd have to be a real rugged night before you'd have considered sleeping in the old biv!" <i>(Jul 2000,NZ Wilderness,10</i>)

>  The new bivwas airlifted onto site by helicopter, where DoC staff from Kapiti Area Office fixed the biv in place with wire stays, installed a new water tank and erected a deck all in a couple of hours. <i>(Jul 2000,NZ Wilderness,10</i>)

>  Climbers to the Sefton Biv in the Aoraki/Mt Cook National Park can now have the best seat in the country - on an open air loo. <i>(Jan 22, 2011,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

### bivouac
 
 <i>v.</i> (Sport) to camp in the open

>  <b>FORSAITH</b> The natives ... in fine weather bivouacking in the open air or under sail-made tents. <i>(1856,Handbook for Emigrants,19</i>)

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> ... the climate is too uncertain for bivouacing in the open air. To travel in the bush with any degree of comfort, a tent is indispensable; ... <i>(1853,Auckland,123</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> FOREST BIVOUAC. ... Strengthened by the usual New Zealand food of roasted potatoes and `damper', we set off ... but soon found ourselves bewildered ... in the surveyor's lines which crossed one another in all directions in the wood. We then resorted to the usual expedient of torches made of `karehao' (supple Jack) or `katoa'; ... it was [agreed] ... that we should light a fire and bivouac for the night. <i>(Aug 30, 1848,New Zealand (various authors/titles),NZ Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 76</i>)

>  <b>BREES</b> A party of surveyors and men bivouacking in the bush. <i>(1849,Panorama of NZ,31</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a camping-out; an outdoor camp or camping place; a camping-hut; ? a base-camp (usu. now bivvy).

>  <b>PICKERING</b> [specialist use also BIV] [caption] <i>Bivouacs are shrunken versions of huts and affectionately called dog kennels. Winchcombe Biv, Tararuas. </i> <i>(1988,Hills, The,82</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> A first bivouac must always be united with something strange and even impressive to the feelings. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,40</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> [ordinary text] <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,40</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  [A picture of a frame-tent with the legend] `Building the Hutt Valley Tramping Club's Bivouac in the Otaki Valley.' <i>(1936,Hills and Valleys,2/3</i>)

>  <b>GILKISON</b> We were back at the bivouac. <i>(1940,Peaks, Packs & Mountain Tracks,54</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  My bivvie door swings open with a slam,/ <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,33</i>)

>  Anzac was absolutely dark except for ... the candlelights left purposely burning in the `bivvies' <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,15</i>)

>  We arrived at our allotted spot, somewhere in Palestine, and erected our bivvies. <i>(Feb 13, 1918,NZEF Chronicles,9</i>)

>  ... it was not possible to make habitable `bivvies' in either the support or the front line ... <i>(1921,NZ Division,243</i>)

>  any small tent <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in the Middle East</i>)

>  <b>SARGE</b> (abbrev) bivouac tent. <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!</i>)

### bivvy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GILKISON</b> <i>(1940,Peaks, Packs & Mountain Tracks</i>) [NOTES: [frequent passim.]]



 
 <i>n.</i> bivouac [EOD has ANZAC ref 1947]

>  We found a good bivvy in a rock about as large as a church, riddled with cracks and caves complete with upholstered sleeping bench, ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,34</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> Hubert and I slept in a bivvy made of manuka and fern; [check punct.] there was just enough room for us both to crawl inside. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,144</i>)

>  <i>bivvy n. NZ colloq</i>. bivouac. [abbr.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,71</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  For the first three months they gravitated between ... a pup tent camp up the valley, where they built brush bivvys [sic]- their first homes. <i>(Dec 30, 1988,Dominion,11</i>)

>  The `Bivi' is a good starting point. <i>(1936,Hills and Valleys,2/4</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Snow and me were sitting outside the bivvy. <i>(1947,Gorse Blooms Pale, The,199</i>)

>  Figure 5: Doug Campbell and Harold Wellman outside a drafty bivvy, with snow on the ground. <i>(Jul 1998,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,116/20</i>)

>  He bridged the era of the late 1960s when bohemian Wellington shoocked the grey suit brigade through to the 1990s when self-reliance was less to do with the ability to foot it in a bush bivvy for weeks on end than on acquisition of piles of money. <i>(Feb 1, 2001,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  <b>INGRAM</b> There are not enough `bivvies' about here to house us all ... I and two pals dug a possie out of the rim of a large shell hole and covered the top with odd pieces of corrugated iron. <i>(Oct 12, 1917,Anzac Diary,59,1987 ed.</i>)

>  Early on they were washed out of their camp, a leaky bivvy on the beach near Whangaroa. <i>(Nov 1989,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,86/74</i>)

>  Battery Stream bivvy in Rimutaka Forest Park has been closed after an inspection by a DOC engineer revealed major structural repairs were needed. <i>(Nov 2002,Footnotes,No. 18/6</i>)

>  So on to our bivy, which was reached at 11.30. <i>(1934,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,III/77</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to bivouac; to camp in a bivvy or shelter

>  A yodel ahead announced Conway and Co.,.. who had been forced to bivvy. <i>(1938,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,7/28</i>)

>  I would just keep going until it got dark and bivvy wherever I happened to be .. I was bivvying on Trudge Col and a nor-west storm came through completely unforecasted, and it was incredibly windy and raining. <i>(1999,NZ Wilderness,2</i>)

>  We bivvied ... below the summit rocks. <i>(May 31, 2003,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

### bivvy bag
 


>  Shaking with shock, he was wrapped in an emergency blanket by his companions and put inside a bivvy bag as the group waited for the Westpac Rescue Helicopter to arrive and take him to Christchurch Hospital. <i>(Mar 14, 2012,Bay of Plenty Times</i>)

>  I have been thrown out of the local botanical gardens on three occasions as staff have found me in my bivvy bag camping in the naative plants display between rata and rimu. <i>(Mar 2001,NZ Wilderness,10</i>)

>  I'd been lying flat on the ground in a river valley near Dip Flat for about four hours, wrapped in a thick sleeping bag and a wind-proof "bivvy bag" and covered by a glorified tarpaulin tied to a tree. I was curled in a ball and my knees, which were crammed against the walls of the sleeping bag, were even more numb than my nose. <i>(Jul 30, 2008,Marlborough Express</i>)

>  "In the trolley I have my bedroom -- a bivvy bag and sleeping bag -- my kitchen, a small camera and some books," he said. <i>(Apr 7, 2004,Daily News,3</i>)

### bizzie
 
 <i>n.</i> ? alteration of biddy-(bid) (perhaps nickname for `bug').

>  <b>LAWLOR</b> ... there would be `bizzies' on his stockings. <i>(1938,House of Templemore, The,131</i>)

### bizzo
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) business

>  I left and went back later. I did the bizzo that time, on the fourth attempt. <i>(Jul 9, 1999,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  You've scored yourself a home run though you can't help thinking the animal kingdom are onto a good thing when they exchange a few grunts and get down to the bizzo. <i>(Oct 5, 1998,Salient,7</i>)

>  We're going to have to front-up and do the bizzo again. <i>(Oct 23, 1998,Evening Post,23</i>)

>  It's not a long hop from there to saying, "To hell with this election bizzo. You love me. You know you do. ..." <i>(Apr 6, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  You leave the bizzo side to me, cuz. <i>(Aug 29, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  ...I just hate the fact she started Amigene Metcalf when she could have had the vastly experienced Adine Wilson doing the bizzo. <i>(Nov 24, 2004,Capital Times,19</i>)

>  He says he's just doing whatever keeps him in the "bizzo". <i>(Feb 1993,North & South,86</i>)

### bla
 
 <i>n.</i> hype, jingoism, skite

>  Her parents "hated what they called the bla, and they particularly hated Yankee bla. Yankee bla was found in American war films. British bla was slightly better, but skiting about the war was something my mother particularly hated." <i>(May 6, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

### black
 
 <i>adj.</i> As a distinguishing epithet in names of plants, see akeake, ash, beech, southern; bent, bindweed, birch, fern, bristle fern; fern, daisy fern; fern, hard fern; fern, shield fern; fern, tree-fern; grass, blackgrass; hinau, hydrolagus, maire, mapau (mapou), maple, matipo, medick, mustard, nightshade, oak, pine; pine, rue; potato, punga, rata, rue (pine), scrub, shrub, stinkroach, tomato, vine, weed.



 
 <i>n.</i> black flounder.

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>FLOUNDER</i> (four species) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i> (ii) <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> (iii) <i>Rhombosolea tapirina</i> (iv) <i>Rhombosolea leporina</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) Flounder (ii) Freshwater Flounder (iii) Flounder (iv) Yellowbelly Flounder. (c) Other common names: (i) Square, Dab, Plaice, Diamond or Diamond-back, Tin-plate, Three Corners. (ii) Blacks, Black Flounder (iii) Greenback [25 `Not common but occasionally taken in southern waters.'] (iv) Yellow Flounder. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,24</i>)

>  [species name] black flounder .. [scientific name] <i>Rhombosolea retiaria </i>.. [other name(s)] blacks,freshwater flounder, river flounder <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> black sheep

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> I remember shortly after arrival in New Zealand watching the well-known dealer, Andrew Grant, picking `fats.' When asked by me for his hesitancy and discrimination in regard to several apparently prime blacks, his answer was that they `killed' badly. So many statements, nevertheless, continue to be accepted because it is nobody's interest to disprove them, that this dealer's belief is given for what it was worth. On the other hand, what Sandy Grant did not know of sheep was not worth knowing <i>(1926,Tutira,362 fn 1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Black Power gang (member).

>  `We thought it was the blacks.' -->The day before the shooting the couple had seen a car belonging to the Black Power gang in the street below their flat. `That's why Paul decided to barricade the door that night. There'd been trouble once before with the blacks ... <i>(May 10, 1983,Dominion,1</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <i>black ... 18. (tr.) Brit. & N.Z.</i> (of trade unionists) to organize a boycott of (specified goods, jobs, work, etc.). <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,109</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 50]]



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. blue](../B#blue))

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I couldn't go back to the goods shed either. They wouldn't have me after the black I put up. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,192</i>) [NOTES: Perhaps from P5 3. `A black mark for doing something badly' -->H and P services since ca1955 `I have put up a black' PS RAF officers]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an Aberdeen Angus cattle beast.

>  The blacks are great doers on the hills. <i>(Sep 1, 1925,NZ Farmer,1361</i>)

>  He likes the black. It's a small cow which is ideal on the hill, requiring minimum tucker for maintenance. The down-country cockies like nothing better than a nice line of blacks and will pay accordingly. <i>(Sep 10, 1970,NZ Farmer,35</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> As an epithet in the names of fish, see: bass, bream, cod, .bd;angelfish, angler fish, bass, bream, cod, dory, eel flounder, javelinfish, marlin, oreo (dory), parore, perch, physeter, ray, rock-cod, rock-fish, shark, slickhead, snapper, snipe eel, stingray whale(r), whalefish, whaling. See also blackfish.



 
<b>3</b> <i>adj.</i> As an epithet in the names of birds, see: cormorant, creeper, diver, oystercatcher, petrel, robin, scaup, shag, stilt, teal, tern; see also blackcap.



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  `We want <i>you!</i> ... we can understand you!' `No, I am going to the islands [sc Melanesia as Missionary, later Bishop] to the blacks there.' (N.B. The Maoris speak of the <i>Blacks</i> with a little touch of contempt.) `You are wanted here! Never mind the blacks.' <i>(1874,Life of John Coleridge Patteson,1/234</i>) [NOTES: 1855 J.C. Patteson in 1874 Yonge Life of John Coleridge Patteson 1/234 [Picton Maoris to J.C. Patteson]]



 
<b>4</b> <i>adj.</i> As an epithet in the names of insects, see: locust, stinkroach; see also blackbeetle, black-bug.

### Black
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori or Islander.

>  <b>WHISKER</b> The Blacks or Mowreys acted very well. ... The Natives ware [sic] no clothes at all but some old shirt tied around there weastes. [sic] <i>(1845,Memorandum Book</i>) [NOTES: 1845 Whisker Memorandum Book in 1979 Barthorp To Face the Daring Maori 71]

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> [The Waikato] will eventually fall into the Hands of the Whites when the Blacks go to the wall. <i>(Nov 18, 1863,Journal</i>)

>  A further charge, which Murray admitted, was of robbing Ariki Akamoeau of a transistor radio valued at $25. ... Sergeant Mowatt-Wilson said Murray was in a car with other youths when they saw four schoolboys walking along the footpath. Murray is reported to have said: `I'm going to get a black tonight.' He then asked for the radio from one of the boys who, in fear of his safety, gave it to Murray. Returning to the car, Murray said: `Look what I've got - I didn't even have to beat him up for it.' <i>(Aug 31, 1971,Otago Daily Times,7</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]



 
 <i>n.</i> an Australian Aborigine

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Mr. Marsden entered with much apparent interest, upon the idea of a New Zealand settlement in Port Jackson; that is to say, within the Colony. ... [We think] that it should not be within one hundred miles of Sydney; that the natives should not be subject to visits from the curious intruders, ... [May 5 54] The natives have had much conversation, relative to the projected settlement in New Holland; ... They wish to be distinct from the whites or blacks, to retain their own teachers ... <i>(Apr 9, 1827,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,51,1961 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (usu. pl.) an All Black

>  The first Rugby test [was] one in which the heavier New Zealand forwards ... `bullocked' their way to victory. ... A penalty try ... was awarded for obstruction to Stanley who was racing ... for the ball, which had been kicked over the Blacks' line. <i>(Sep 11, 1922,Quick March,20</i>)

>  [a possible early descriptive term = ALL BLACK] see All Black <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/33</i>)

### black akeake
 
 <i>n.</i> Dodonoea viscosa, a var. of akeake. -->[1919 Jul 29] Ver. (WB).= Vernacular names used by settlers supplied by W. Best, Otaki (And) black akeake Dodonoea viscosa [1961 W3 for D. viscosa; O. avicenniaefolia, O. Traversii]

### black as a bull's backside
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Sergeant Wilkey, speaking on behalf of the team ... said that there were four vessels at the scene 38km north of Westport. It was `as black as a bull's backside' and the Westport dredge they were in was rolling heavily in the swell. <i>(May 10, 1983,Press, The,1</i>)

### black as a goat-hair tent
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>W. GRIFFITHS</b> black as a goat-hair tent in McNeish <i>Joy </i> <i>(Feb 28, 1983,Letter</i>) [NOTES: (Auck)]

### black as a musterer's billy
 
 <i>phr.</i> (of a night)

>  All through a night as black as a musterer's billy we had seen nothing but ... the black form of black hills, ... <i>(1948,Our Own Country,132</i>) [NOTES: (`Central Otago')]

### black as Maori
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>NICHOLL</b> By the time we got back we were as black as Maoris from the dust. <i>(Apr 27, 1864,Journal,200</i>)

### black as the inside of a cow
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  The men were all in good spirits and he was keeping contact by shouting to them. 'It's as black as the inside of a cow out here.' <i>(Oct 28, 1991,Dominion,1</i>)

>  We like our gummies as black as the inside of a cow but, more often than not, with a red band. <i>(May 2007,NZ Geographic,60</i>)

### black beech
 
 <i>n.</i> Nothofagus solandri, tawhai-rauriki, also popularly called black birch,a beech with dark bark, or bark blackened by fungus.

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Beech ... Black .. tawhairauriki <i>Nothofagus solandri </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/710</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>NOTHOFAGUS SOLANDRI</i> variety <i>SOLANDRI, black beech.</i> A tall tree of lowland sites whose precise distribution is uncertain because of crossing with mountain beech; ... <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,80</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> Black beech (<i>N. solandri var. solandri</i> ) is the main species on occasional sites in the east [of Westland]. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,159</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Five species of <i>Nothofagus</i> were formerly recognised in New Zealand, but more recently two of these have been combined as varieties of one species. ... <i>Nothofagus solandri</i> var.<i> solandri:</i> black beech (the bark is very dark partly due to the growth of a sooty mould). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,121</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Black birch or beech tree - Tawai. <i>(1855,Leaf from Natural History,444</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>The Black Beech. </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,140</i>)

>  <b>LINKLATER</b> Ferns and tree-ferns flourish [on Stewart Island], ... supple-jack entwines your heels, and stinkwood is garlanded with little red flowers. There is red or black beech, there is mutton-bird scrub with thick green leaves that have white undersides, there are, perhaps, a dozen others. <i>(1953,Year of Space, A,203,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SEVERINSEN</b> <i>(1962,Hunter Climb High,21</i>) [NOTES: see Leatherwood]

>  <i>black beech </i>(erron. <i>black birch) NZ & Austral.</i> variety of nothofagus. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,71</i>) [NOTES: [nf. CCON],]

>  <i>Nothofagus Solanderi</i> <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The</i>) [NOTES: (And) [black b.]]

>  <i>(1917,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>) [NOTES: (And) [black b.]]

>  <i>Nothofagus Solanderi</i> black-beech <i>(1909,AJHR,C-11</i>) [NOTES: (And)Turner Botanical Examination Higher Waimarino]

>  The chief indigenous trees of commercial value are: Kauri (<i>Agathis Australis</i> ), Kahikatea or white pine ... red and hard beech (<i>Nothofagus fusca</i> and <i>Nothofagus truncata</i> ), silver beech ... (<i>Nothofagus Menziesii</i> ), black beech (<i>Nothofagus Solandri) ... N. Solandri (Solander's Beech).</i> .. North Island: mountain forests; South Island: alt. sea-level to 2,500 ft. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,578</i>)

>  <i>black beech n 2 NewZeal </i> : a forest tree (<i>Nothofagus solanderi</i> ) having entire leaves <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,225</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> <i>(1890,Descriptive & Priced List</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>1/III/32</b> Black Birch of Otago, Beech, etc. (<i>Fagus fusca</i>), another valuable timber tree, attaining a great size; ... H.H. Allan <i>Flora</i> (1982) pp. 397ff, 1035 assigns the popular name black beech to <i>Nothofagus solandri</i>, and renames <i>N. Cliffortioides</i> as <i>N. solandri</i> var. <i>cliffortioides: N. fusca</i> is assigned red beech - these three have dark bark; the epithet <i>black</i> is taken from the bark or from a sooty fungus growing on the bark <i>(1869,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Nothofagus Solanderi</i> black southern beech <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And),]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>(1924,Cultivation of NZ Plants, The</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>Crowe</b> Black beech timber was...once the major building material in Canterbury for bridges, beams and decking, flooring and wall panelling. <i>(2009,Which Native Tree?,42</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Nothofagus fusca (aka red beech) [1909 T H W (Waimarino) (Andersen) [black beech] 1917 D D D (Andersen) [black beech]]

>  <b>HAAST</b> The evening setting in, we camped ... where, owing to the altitude ..., the black beech (<i>Fagus fusca, Hook.f.</i>) was now the predominent [sic] tree. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,112</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Along the rather wet track appeared, besides the Totara pine, white pines (Kahikatea, <i>Podocarpus dacrydioides</i>), and black pines (<i>Rimu</i> or <i>Dacrydium cupressinum</i>), which ... towered high above the other forest foliage; although here and there black beeches (<i>Fagus fusca</i>), 100 heet high ... were also to be seen. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,81</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>) [NOTES: [And black beech]]

### black beetle
 
 <i>n.</i> a native cockroach; also called Maori bug or black bug

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> one kind of Black Beetle is worthy of particular notice on account of its troublesome nature, and known by the appellation of Kekerere, by the natives. If molested throws off the most disgusting smell imaginable, but not a venomous insect. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,111,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> You have no idea what a hubbub there is here some times, it is infinitely worse than `Blackbeetles, Bluebottles and Flies'. <i>(May 4, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>) [NOTES: (ATB:TS) (Molyneux) [refce to ?song or poem]]

>  Of course the `black beetle' denied that he had done so [i.e. `aspersed the moral character of the young people of Motueka'] =parson. <i>(Jul 14, 1906,NZ Truth,56/1/6</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> `To dream of four black beetles dragging a hearse is bad. ...' <i>(1916,Aloe, The,121,(1982 O'Sullivan) ed.</i>)

>  <b>DARE</b> The place swarmed with black beetles ... <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,71</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> ~ Maori bug, kererengu, cock roach <i>Platyzosteria novae</i> - see <i>landiae </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> The town Christchurch having become a photograph of modern towns, and a ball being imminent, out late guest ... had to reduce himself to the black-beetle appearance of the evening exquisite of the nineteenth century,- ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,209</i>)

### black billy tea
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [billy](../B#billy))

>  Anybody who stopped by his camp was always sure of a good feed and a cup of black billy tea. <i>(1955,Authentic NZ Ballads,00</i>) [NOTES: Sleeve notes]

>  You can sip hot black billy tea beside the fire in great-grandfather's worker's hut, and listen to local yarns, giving the past and present cohesion. <i>(Nov 1999,AA Directions,30</i>)

### black birch
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Nothofagus fusca,</i> Hook. Tawhai Tawhairaunui, Black-birch of Auckland and Otago (from colour of bark), Red-birch of Wellington and Nelson (from colour of timber). --This is a noble tree 60-90 feet high the trunk 5-8 feet in diameter. The timber is excessively tough and hard to cut. It is highly valued in Nelson and Wellington. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,125,3rd ed.</i>)

>  <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/307</i>) [NOTES: (E. Phillips Turner)see cit. 1 b. above.]

>  <i>Fagus cliffortioides </i>Hook. f. .. Mountain beech or birch. <i>[Fagus] fusca</i> Hook. f. .. Tawhai, black-birch. <i>[Fagus] Menziesii </i>Hook. f. .. Silver or red birch. <i>[Fagus] Solandri</i> Hook. f. .. White-birch. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/144</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> ... I found the river ... flowing ... through a black birch country. [fn] A species of <i>Notofagus</i>, probably <i>N. fusca </i>or red beech. [On 27 Jan Brunner speaks of the `bark of the tawai, or black birch'] <i>(Jan 25, 1847,Account of an Exploring Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Jan 25 Brunner Exped. Southwest Nelson in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 266]

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> Passing over about a mile of pine forest, but again came to our black birch country - precipices and granite rocks. <i>(Feb 1847,Account of an Exploring Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Feb 26 Brunner Exped. Southwest Nelson in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 268]

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> Before, it [the river] abounded in eels, but we found none ... or anything else fit to eat in the black birch forest. [<i>N. fusca</i>] <i>(Jan 25, 1847,Account of an Exploring Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Jan 25 Brunner Exped. Southwest Nelson in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 266]

>  <b>JOLLIE</b> Clearing the road through black birch bush [c1852]. <i>(1878,Reminiscences,35</i>) [NOTES: (Marlborough) [??transfer to BUSH]/FOREST birch bush is at BUSH x ref here?]]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The forest was of black birch, almost the only timber in these high elevations. Southwest Nelson in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 193]

>  Fagus <i>fusca</i>, Hook. fil. ... HAB. Mountains of the Northern Island ... Common in the Middle Island ... Nat. name, `Tawai', <i>Bidwill</i>. `Black Birch' of the colonists. ... [230] A lofty, handsome forest tree, 80-100 feet high ... <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 229</i>)

>  <b>1/32</b> Red Birch of Otago, Black Birch of Nelson, Beech etc (Fagus Menziesii); Black Birch of Otago (F. fusca) White Birch of Otago (F. Solandri)[get full citation] <i>(1869,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Beech, or Black Birch [Fagus fusca] <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/90</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk Botany Thames)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> BLACK BIRCH of Auckland and Otago, RED BIRCH of Wellington and Nelson: HUTU-TAWHAI.- <i>(Fagus fusca.)</i> The true black birch is a noble tree, found from Kaitaia ... to Otago ... <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,15</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> <i>Fagus fusca</i>, the black birch of the colonists, soon makes its appearance together with the two conifers <i>Podocarpus Totara</i> and <i>Libocedrus Doniana</i>, the <i>Kawhaka</i> of the natives ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,79</i>)

>  [<i>Notho]fagus fusca</i>, Hook. f. ... Tooth-leaved Beech. Black Birch of Auckland and in part of Otago and Southland. Black or Bull Birch of Lake Wakatipu. Red Birch of Wellington, Nelson, and in part of Otago and Southland. It is not so easy to see why any difficulty should have occured in the identification of this fine timber-tree apart from the misleading tendency of the common names generally applied. The thin yet firm texture of the leaf, the prominent veins, the sharply-toothed margins, are characters that can only be confused with those afforded by a careless observer. Yet merely owing to the use of common names based upon colour, and applied or rather misapplied to the leaves, bark, or wood at the fancy of the bushman, no species has been more misunderstood. <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/300</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk, NZ Beeches)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Tawairaunui (<i>Fagus fusca</i>) is a species of the former, known as `black birch'. ... [It] is not so plentiful in the North. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/190</i>)

>  <b>HUDSON</b> <i>Nothofagus fusca</i> Black Birch <i>(1898,NZ Moths & Butterflies</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  The timber on the western side consists of red- and white-pine, matai (or black-pine), totara, kawhaka (or cedar), rata, and occasional silver-pine, besides black- and red-birch (<i>Fagus fusca</i>). <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,19</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>F. fusca, Hook.f.</i> ... A noble forest-tree 60-100 ft. high; ... bark dark-brown or black in old plants, deeply furrowed, smooth and greyish-white on young trees; ... rare in Canterbury and eastern Otago. ... <i>Tawhai; Tawhai-rau-nui; Black-birch; Red-birch. </i>... [642] Wood bark-red, ... more durable than that of other species, ... <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,641</i>)

>  The Tawhai, and Tawhai-rau-nui, or Black and Red Birches, (<i>Fagus Solandri</i> and <i>F. Fusca</i>,) often form large ... trees. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/44</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

White, Black, Red, and Brown Birch of Oxford and Alford Forests. A fine tree, sometimes attaining the height of 100 feet, but usually 60-80 feet, with a trunk from 2 to 4 feet or more in diameter. <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/301</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk, NZ Beeches)]

Barely distinguished by the woodman from Entire-leaved beech. The mountain beech bears a close resemblance to the entire-leaved beech in general appearance, although it never attains the extreme dimensions of that species. <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/304</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk, NZ Beeches)]

 <i>n.</i> (applied to non-Nothofagus spp.) kamahi or tawhero

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> 9. Tawai, (<i>Leiospermum racemosum.</i>) This tree has a rough, dark-brown bark, and a small round leaf, in shape like that of an elm. The wood is particularly well adapted for ship-building, and for the staves of barrels, and is commonly called `black birch' by the sailors and other Europeans acquainted with it. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,142</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> TAWHERO.- (<i>Weinmannia racemosa.</i>) ... Often called black birch, and substituted for that timber, to which it is greatly inferior in strength and durability. <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,19</i>)

>  The kamai is called black birch in the Callin River District and Southland, which name is given on account of a supposed resemblance to the `birches', or more correctly, `beeches', a number of which occur in that locality ... Whatever the reason, the misapplication of names is complete, for the birches are still commonly called kamai in Southland. Kamai is little known on the East Coast north of the Clutha River. <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/148</i>) [NOTES: (Blair)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> WEINMANNIA RACEMOSA. (<i>Forst.</i>) ... The `TAWHERO' OR ``KAMAI'. This handsome and useful tree is found in both Islands; ... In Otago the tree is popularly known as the `Black Birch'. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,136</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> Its [<i>Quintinnia serrata</i>, native lilac] durability is probably about equal to the wood of the Tawhero - <i>Weinmannia racemosa </i>- or Black Birch of Otago, to which it is indeed closely allied. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,131</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> In many parts of the colony, the small-leaved tarata (<i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>) is called black birch; in others the name is applied to the tawhero (<i>Weinmannia racemosa</i>). ... The timber [of tawhero] greatly resembles that of the true black birch, but may be at once distinguished by its lighter specific gravity. <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Black birch or beech tree - Tawai. <i>(1855,Leaf from Natural History,444</i>)

>  274 Black birch burr (entire leaved beech). <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,21</i>)

>  Thick growth of kalo and talamea near the bush which was ... a black birch forest with an occasional red pine. <i>(Apr 14, 1849,Torlesse Papers,74,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WELD</b> [The Maori] ... said that formerly he had often been there to catch kakapos (green night parrots) in a black birch wood above the Awatere pass. <i>(Jan 1851,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Jan Weld Letter in 1851 Feb 21 NZGG 31]

>  <b>HAAST</b> The spur was covered with black birch forest, ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,4</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... noticing a clump of black birch trees which might shelter my horse for the night ... [Canterbury] <i>(Oct 1, 1863,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,72,1914 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It was covered with shingle and a heavy growth of black birch. <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/28/47</i>) [NOTES: (Nelson)]

>  <b>BARNICOAT</b> The timber of the hills is chiefly of a kind called black birch which is despised. <i>(1845,Journal,149</i>) [NOTES: (Wellington)]

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> [I] looked after black birch for hurdles. <i>(1846,Journal,27</i>) [NOTES: (Wairarapa)]

>  ... the towkhai [sic] (or black birch). [reference is to a beech] <i>(1849,Notes on NZ</i>) [NOTES: (Otago)]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [The towai] is used for ship-building and is called by Englishmen `black birch.' [Although Morris quotes this under towai, Andersen apparently regards it as <i>N. solandri</i>. It is significant that in his 1848 Handbook of NZ 142, Wakefield replaces the 1845 <i>Towai</i> above with <i>Tawai</i>: thus, `Tawai ... is particularly well adapted for ship-building ... and is commonly called `black birch' by the sailors and other Europeans acquainted with it'.] <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,95</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> The settlers call them all [sc. southern beech] 'birch'. And merely distinguish them as 'white', 'black' or 'red', according to the wood. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,206</i>)

>  <b>WELD</b> [Marlborough] ... - the grass land [near Kaikoura] gave place to bushes, grass, and scrub, - the frequent patches of black birch on the sides of the hills told of inferior soil and a greater height above sea level; ... <i>(Jan 1851,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Jan Weld Letter in 1851 Feb 21 NZGG 30]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> In some places a few patches of black birch are found in the gullies, and manuka scrub on the ranges; but towards the Manuherikia only a few patches of matakaru, and a dark small-leaved shrub are to be met with. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,19</i>) [NOTES: c1857 Alex Garvie [Report on Otago] in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 19]

>  <b>HAAST</b> The evergreen Fagus [is] commonly known to the settlers [of the South Island] as black and white birch. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,146</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> But the black birch of this country [Wairarapa] takes the cake for toughness and shortness of grain and stem. It is tough to fall and tough to saw, and ... it has to be blasted with powder to get a crack in it to put a wedge in. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,33,1935 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1872 in c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 33]

>  <b>MACKIE</b> The forest consists almost entirely of `Blk. Birch' as it is called. The trees resemble some of our smaller leaved English elms. <i>(Mar 18, 1853,Traveller under Concern,89,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> [=wood] There is also the rata tree, the prince of firewoods, ... miki-miki, kamahi, or so-called red-birch, white and black birches (which are really beeches), the mountain broom, ribbon wood, broad leaf, totara, and many others, ... and always available in the Ranges [sc. Alps]. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,35</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Several species of Loranthus (misletoe) [sic]) also adorn the woods, growing principally upon the evergreen Fagus, commonly known to the settlers as black and white birch. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,146</i>)

>  <b>DRURY</b> TREES [MET WITH IN PELORUS]. Black Birch - exceedingly common ... <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,66</i>)

>  The wood ... is from that variety of Fagus termed emphatically by the colonists [of Nelson] the <i>Black-</i>Birch, a tree with a sooty rough stem, and minute heart-shaped leaves, growing at low levels. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/14</i>) [NOTES: (Monro)]

>  Red Birch of Otago, Black Birch of Nelson, Beech, etc. (<i>Fagus Menziesi</i>), a valuable timber tree, found from the sea level to 4000 feet altitude, sometimes attaining a diameter of 12 feet. ... Black Birch of Otago, Beech, etc. (<i>Fagus fusca</i>), another valuable timber tree, attaining a great size; ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/32</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

### Black Budget
 
 <i>n.</i> The name given first by opponents, and then generally, to Finance Minister Nordmeyer's deflationary budget of 1958 and thence to any such budget (also used of an earlier budget); Charles Ransom comments (Nov 1986): referred to occas. by journalists but does not seem to have entered the language as meaning `deflationary budget'.

>  June 26 [1958]. Minister of Finance Arnold Nordmeyer presented his first Budget, increasing taxes on beer, spirits, cigarettes and tobacco. Known ever after as the Black Budget, it was a response to a balance of payments crisis and introduced cheap housing loans and the Pay-As-You-Earn (Paye) tax system. <i>(Aug 2, 1999,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  The 1958 budget was labelled "black" by one-time communist and union leader F.P. Walsh. <i>(Feb 11, 1999,Dominion,6</i>)

>  The `BLACK BUDGET' 1958 [heading] In his Budget speech, 26 June 1958, the Labour Minister of Finance, Arnold Nordmeyer, increased taxes in order to reduce demand for imports, encourage local manufacturing and conserve foreign exchange ... <i>(1971,Speeches & Documents,434 185.</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> In 1930 J.G. Coates had called Ward's budget `the Black Budget' [PD 1931, 229, 88] - but that was long forgotten. This time the label stuck. Nordmeyer's budget [of June 1958] was remembered as `the black budget' for many years. It can be argued that it was good for `the country'. For the government it was a suicidal mistake. It was the biggest blunder in Nash's political career. Although he was not immediately and directly responsible, he was the leader and did not say `No'. <i>(1976,Nash,309</i>)

>  Mr Lake and the government are lucky that the country's condition permitted them to avoid a Black Budget. <i>(Oct 1963,Comment,175</i>)

>  <b>NEAVE</b> It would not be an exaggeration to say that [Mr Nordmeyer] was respected as much outside the Labour Party, as he was within it, ... and although his `Black Budget' was known as the cause of the fall of the Labour Government, it was recognised as the anti-dote for the economic situation at that time. <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,26</i>)

>  he speculates that a second term for Labour will - at this moment - be trickling a tear from the eye of Black Budget author Arnold Nordmeyer. <i>(Aug 17, 1987,Dominion,5</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> Would the action we had to take prove to be as fatal as Nordy's Black Budget, when a public eager for handouts got a good kick in the wallet? <i>(1987,Hard Labour,108</i>)

>  he speculates that a second term for Labour will - at this moment - be trickling a tear from the eye of Black Budget author Arnold Nordmeyer. <i>(Aug 17, 1987,Dominion,2</i>)

>  We had a contribution to this debate to-day from one of the Ministers of the Crown.. about the so-called "black" Budget. <i> Ibid. </i> 229/88 When the last Budget was introduced, the Leader of the Opposition described it as the " Black Budget". <i>(1930,NZ Parliamentary Debates,224/843</i>)

>  Nordmeyer became better known as a minister of another kind, producing the notorious Black Budget as finance minister of the second Labour Government in 1957. <i>(May 2001,NZ Geographic,51/48</i>)

>  On June 26, 1958, Minister of Finance Arnold Nordmeyer presented his first budget. It has gone down in political history as the "black budget" and it helped consign its author to political purgatory... In a statement issued on the night of the budget speech the two presidents of the North and South Island Motor Unions called it "black Thursday" and Federation of Labour strongman Fintan Patrick Walsh was soon labelling it the "black budget". <i>(Jun 22, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

### black bug
 
 <i>n.</i> black beetle, Maori bug, stink bug

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> And he's sick of black-bugs. Just leave your coat hanging on the wall and ther'll be a black-bug in it ... <i>Ibid.</i> 87 A real cow-spanker, he reckons he's sick of the black-bugs. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,67,1979 ed.</i>)

### black burn
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an incomplete forest fire where the heat is insufficient to reduce timber to a small residue of white ash.

>  <b>LEVY</b> A white ash, called a white burn was the objective; a black burn resulted from a less intense fire where the heat was not sufficient to burn the timber, leaf mould, seeds and spores on the forest floor. <i>(1970,Grasslands of NZ,241</i>)

>  <b>BIBBY</b> The fire made a horrible mess, the material not being dry enough to get rid of small trees - to the bushmen, a 'blackburn'. [blackburn] <i>(1990,Lunesdale: A Bush Farm Community from 1871,12</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> It was not so difficult to replace the forest with grass where the climate made intense white-ash burns possible after the felling of the forest. ... But in higher rain-fall belts, when poor burns (black burns) only occur, much forest litter remained unconsumed ... <i>Ibid.</i> 241 A steady, hot fire was desired in order to burn all fallen timber and leaf mould, seeds, and fern spores lying dormant on the forest floor. A white ash, called a <i>white burn</i>, was the objective; a black burn resulted from a less intense fire where the heat was not sufficient to burn the timber, leaf mould, seeds, and spores on the forest floor. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BLACK COD ...Notothenia magellanica </i>... The black cod is similar to the maori chief but has a deeper, less rounded body ... and does not reach such a large size. ... Black cod are common in inshore rocky areas south of Cook Strait ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,276,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Maori chief Paranotothenia angustata</i> Family Notothenidae (southern cods, ice cods). Sometimes called black cod. Also present off southern South America. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,117</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BLACK COD ... <i>Other names: Notothenia microlepidota</i>; southern rock cod, nototheniid, Maori chief. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,265</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> COD, BLACK A close relative of the Maori Chief with which he is often confused the Black Cod looks like the Blue Cod in shape but is slightly more slender and is a blackish-brown in colour. ... Another name is Southern Rock Cod. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,39</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> Pied and Black Fantail. <i>Ibid.</i> 146 {1888 2nd edn 1/72} RHIPIDURA FULIGINOSA. (BLACK FANTAIL.) ... This dark-coloured species is restricted to the South island, ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,LIX</i>)

>  36. <i>Rhipidura fuliginosa</i>, Sparrm. Black Fantail, Black Flycatcher, Ti-waka-waka. <i>(Mar 1885,NZ Journal of Science,375</i>)

>  <i>Rhipidura fuliginosa Sparr. Black Fantail. Remaining plentiful and widespread in the South Island, this species still occurs fairly frequently in the North Island. </i> <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/91</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <i>pi[i]wakawaka</i>, a bird (Rhipidura flabellifera), pied fantail: the so-called black fantail, which is chocolate-colour (R. fuliginosa) is ti[i]wakawaka. The former is common throughout Newzealand; the latter is common in the South Island but rather rare in the North and is thought to be a mutant of the former. Both names were applied to both birds by the Maori, but for distinction pi[i]wakawaka for the pied and ti[i]wakawaka for the black have been adopted by the Pakeha. The bird is noted for the disaster it caused to Maui in his struggle with the mother of night, Hine-nui-te-po, Death personified. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/156</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> FANTAIL <i>Rhipidura fuliginosa Maori name</i>: Piwakawaka. ... Birds of normal colour pattern (`Pied Fantail') make up most of the population in North Island, but the `Black Fantail' (i.e. dark phase) accounts for perhaps a quarter of the population of the South Island subpecies; ... <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,198</i>)

>  Fantail <i>Rhipidura fuliginosa</i> (SPARRMAN, 1787) North Island Fantail <i>R.f. placabilis </i>BANGS, 1921. South Island Fantail <i>R.f. fulginosa</i> (SPARRMAN, 1787). Chatham Island Fantail <i>R.f. penitus</i> BANGS, 1911. OTHER NAMES: <i>Piwakawaka, pied fantail, black fantail. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,280</i>)

### Black Ferns
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Our women's rugby team the Black Ferns, retained the Canada Cup and their unbeaten record, while the Kiwi Ferns league team were unbeaten on its way to winning the world championship. <i>(Dec 14, 2000,Evening Post,32</i>)

>  Annaleah is the goalkicking centre in the Black ferns who are playing the curtain-raiser at next weekend's Bledisloe Cup test at the Sydney Football Stadium. <i>(Aug 24, 1998,Dominion,1</i>)

>  New Zealand's only successful national rugby team this year, the triumphant women's World Cup winners, will now be known as the Black Ferns. <i>(Aug 5, 1998,Dominion,40</i>)

>  She plays on the wing for the Black Ferns, as the New Zealand women's rugby team is now called after rejecting labels like Gal Blacks, Elle Blacks and She Bs. <i>(Aug 1998,Mana,28</i>)

>  Better or not the officially sanctioned substitute was announced early in August and the Gal Blacks of brief notoriety (along with other canvassed names such as Elle Blacks and She Bs - after ABs) were finally history. The team was henceforth to be known as the Black Ferns. Here the colour adjective fittingly remains, and the noun echoes the name sported (since the early 1990s) by another black-clad international New Zealand women's team, the netballers or Silver Ferns. Since that title itself derives from the famous fernleaf-cum-national symbol of the All Black jersey (to wear the silver fern is a common variant of to be an All Black), there is an obvious appropriateness in reapplying the fern to the rugby union context. Black Ferns is further legitimised bv the presence in the natural world of a black (tree-) fern, in Maori the mamaku. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2(1) : 5</i>)

>  An off-duty police commander in his Jandals and a Black Fern helped chase a man who led police on a 40km pursuit through rush-hour motorway traffic in Auckland yesterday. <i>(Jun 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>) [NOTES: Original in NZ Herald, 24/01/1998]

>  New Zealand's Black Ferns lifted the World cup for remarkable fourth successive time in what is being hailed as the greatest game of women's rugby ever . . the Black Ferns' only try came from Carla Hohepa ... <i>(Sep 9, 2010,Rugby News,32</i>)

### black flounder
 
 <i>n.</i> Rhombosolea retiaria, aka patiki mohoao [also river flounder]

>  <b>WAITE</b> Black Flounder - Patiki - <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i>. <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/197</i>)

>  RHOMBOSOLEA RETIARIA Hutton. <i>Black Flounder; Patiki-mohoao</i>. This species, known generally as `river-flounder,' is occasionally taken in the sea. Common in the estuaries of most large rivers, and often taken many miles inland. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/122</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The four species of Rhombosolea are distinguished by form and colour. The best known is the Sand flounder, or simply Flounder, (<i>R. plebeia</i>) (Plate II. fig. 2) wider across than the other, while the Greenback (<i>R. tapirina</i>) with white undersurface, the Yellowbelly (<i>R. leporina</i>) and the Black flounder (<i>R. retiaria</i>) have their distinguishing colours recorded in their common names. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,201</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 201]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> Hutton. RIVER-FLOUNDER, BLACK FLOUNDER; PATIKI-MOHOAO. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,29,1971 ed.</i>)

>  19. <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> Hutton. Black Flounder. Maori: Patiki. <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/142</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> Hutton. River flounder (black flounder). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/408</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Mohoao (i), n. Rhombosolea retiaria, black flounder,</i> a fish. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,206</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Patiki mohao, Rhombosolea retiara, river or black flounder. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,271</i>)

>  <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i>: Black or fresh-water flounder. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BLACK FLOUNDER ... <i>Other names: Rhombosolea retiaria</i>; mud flounder, river flounder, estuary flounder; patiki-mahoao (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,215</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BLACK FLOUNDER [<i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i>] ... River flounder and mud flounder are widely used alternative names. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,32</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> FLOUNDER (four species) (a) Scientific name: <i>(i) Rhombosolea plebeia (ii) Rhombosolea retiaria (iii) Rhombosolea tapirina (iv) Rhombosolea leporina</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) Flounder (ii) Freshwater Flounder (iii) Flounder (iv) Yellowbelly Flounder. (c) Other common names: (i) Square, Dab, Plaice, Diamond or Diamond-back, Tin-plate, Three Corners. (ii) Blacks, Black Flounder (iii) Greenback [25 `Not common but occasionally taken in southern waters.'] (iv) Yellow Flounder. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,24</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> FLOUNDER, BLACK More sole-like in shape than his cousin flounders this fellow also has small, brick-red spots on his back and has the ability - unlike the other flatfish - of being able to live in pure, fresh water. Other names are `River Flounder' and `Mud or Freshwater Flounder'. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,72</i>)

>  Another rare species is the black flounder, which may be available in the lower North Island because a few are netted at Lake Onoke in the Wairarapa and sold locally. It has a dark green back with darker mottling and is dotted with beautiful brick-red spots. <i>(Dec 8, 1982,Evening Post,37</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> These much sought-after but relatively rare flatfish [flounder and sole] are generally found on sandy bottoms of shallow inshore areas. There are eight commercial species in New Zealand : the sand flounder, yellowbelly flounder, greenback flounder, black flounder, lemon sole, common sole, brill and turbot. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,73</i>)

>  [species name] black flounder .. [scientific name] <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> .. [other name(s)] blacks, freshwater flounder, river flounder <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Black flounder Rhombosolea retiaria</i> Restricted to New Zealand. Alternative names include river flounder, freshwater flounder, or estuary flounder, derived from its habitat. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,144</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Black or River Flounder - patiki-mohoao, <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] flounder, black 2287-90, 2296 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> BLACK FLOUNDER (Mud flounder, patiki-mahoao) <i> Rhombosolea retiaria</i> ... Coloration is distinctive; the back is usually dark grey-green with darker mottling and a variable number of brick red spots. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,310,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> NEW ZEALAND BLACK FLOUNDER or RIVER FLOUNDER (PATIKI-MOHOAO) <i>Rhombosolea (Adamsoma) retiaria adams </i>Whitley and Phillipps. It is known to fishermen and those in the fishing industry as the River Flounder as it is caught mostly inside or near the mouths of rivers and enters lakes where it lives chiefly among weeds. To some it is known as the Maori Flounder. I have helped to catch these fish in various rivers in New Zealand, Both in the North and South Islands, from the Bay of Islands, Bay of Plenty, Canterbury and Otago. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,204,2nd ed. ed.</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> The four New Zealand species were long placed with the true olives in <i>Olea</i>; more recently they fell temporarily into <i>Gymnelaea</i> but now are all attributed to <i>Nestegis, ... NESTEGIS CUNNINGHAMII, black maire</i>. A tall, stout-trunked tree of North Island mixed forests, recorded also from Nelson and marlborough but now common in only a few districts. >  <b>DAWSON</b> Three of the four species of <i>Nestegis</i> or `native olives' have narrow adult leaves. In the case of two species, black maire (<i>N. cunninghamii</i>) and white maire (<i>N. lanceolata</i>), the juveniles are much narrower than the adults ... Jasmineae</i>; also Maire-rau-nui, <i>Olea Cunninghamii.</i> Hook., fil., Black M., forty to fifty feet high, three to four feet in diameter, timber close-grained, heavy, and very durable. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,132</i>)
